# Master App Design Taxonomy

> Part of a frontier/craft reference set for steering coding agents and designers past the default. Default reach: one-size web patterns bolted onto an app. This maps what each platform actually owns — lean on the per-entry **trap** and platform-divergence notes.

A working reference for **application** design direction — native iOS/iPadOS & Android, desktop (macOS/Windows), web apps & PWAs, wearables (watchOS/Wear OS), and spatial (visionOS). Sibling to `design-style-taxonomy.md` (which covers the *web/marketing* surface); this doc covers everything that lives behind a login, inside an app shell, or on a device home screen. Covers: **platform foundations & design languages**, **app aesthetic styles**, **navigation & IA**, **adaptive layout**, **components** (bars, controls, containers, overlays — by axis), **forms & keyboards**, **gestures & input**, **haptics & sensory feedback**, **motion**, **the full app state set**, **onboarding**, **notifications**, **permissions & privacy**, **color & materials**, **type & icons**, **accessibility**, **performance**, **offline & sync**, **platform extensions** (widgets, Live Activities, App Intents…), **desktop patterns**, **monetization & store UX**, **AI-era app interfaces**, and **app design systems & tokens**. Each entry explains what it is, which platform owns it, how the platforms diverge, when to reach for it, and the trap to avoid.

Compiled June 2026. Platform-version-sensitive entries are dated (iOS 26 Liquid Glass, Material 3 / Material You, Android 13–15, WCAG 2.2). Foundational entries are timeless.

---

## How to use this doc

- **The platform sets the defaults; deviate deliberately.** Apps live inside an OS with strong conventions (Jakob's Law bites harder here than on the web). Start from the platform-native pattern and break it only with a reason — users punish novelty in the controls they use every day.
- **Foundations → navigation → layout → components → behavior is the stack.** Pick the platform language first (§1), then the IA (§3), then let components and motion inherit from it.
- **Every pattern has a platform split.** iOS, Android, desktop, and web-app each name and build the same idea differently. The divergence *is* the content — a tab bar is not a bottom nav is not a navigation rail.
- **Accessibility is a veto.** VoiceOver/TalkBack operability, Dynamic Type, Reduce Motion, and 44pt/48dp targets outrank taste (§19). A pattern that can't pass loses.
- **Perceived performance beats raw speed.** Launch tricks, skeletons, and optimistic UI (§13, §20) shape the felt experience more than milliseconds do.
- **"Native-feel" and "on-brand" are a dial, not a switch.** Decide per surface how much platform convention to keep vs. brand identity to assert (§26).

# 1. Platform foundations & design languages

Every app is built on a platform that already has opinions — about navigation, gesture, type, color, and motion — encoded in an official design language and enforced (loosely) by store review. This section maps those languages and the cardinal rule that follows from them: **respect the platform.** Governing principle: users spend almost all their time in *other* apps, so your app should mostly behave like those apps (Jakob's Law for apps); deviate only where your brand or function genuinely earns it, and never deviate on the things the OS owns (back behavior, system gestures, accessibility, hardware affordances).

## 1.1 The "respect the platform" principle

- **Jakob's Law (for apps)** — users build a mental model from every other app on their device, not yours. A back swipe, a tab bar, a long-press menu, a pull-to-refresh should work *here* the way they work *everywhere else* on that OS. Trap: "innovative" navigation is almost always a tax users pay to relearn what they already knew; spend novelty budget on your core value, not on relocating the back button.
- **Native vs branded** — the spectrum from "indistinguishable from a system app" (Files, Settings clones) to "fully custom canvas" (games, creative tools, Spotify, Headspace). Native buys you free accessibility, free OS updates, lower build cost, and instant familiarity; branded buys you differentiation and cross-platform consistency at the cost of re-implementing (and re-testing) everything the OS gave you for free. Choose per-surface, not per-app: a custom hero screen with native settings/sheets is the common sweet spot.
- **The cost of cross-platform sameness** — shipping one identical UI to iOS and Android means *both* platforms get an app that feels foreign. The Android user gets iOS's back-button-in-a-chrome; the iOS user gets Material ripples and a hamburger. "Write once, feel wrong twice." Adaptive (per-platform conventions, shared logic) beats uniform (one look everywhere) for consumer apps; uniform is defensible only for tools where the brand *is* the product (Figma, Linear, games).
- **What the OS owns — never override** — the system back gesture/button, edge swipes, the status bar, the home indicator / gesture-nav area, Dynamic Island and notch safe areas, system text-size (Dynamic Type / font scale), Reduce Motion, Increase Contrast, screen-reader focus order, and the share sheet. Reskinning or intercepting these breaks muscle memory and frequently fails accessibility (→ §19). Trap: a full-screen custom canvas that swallows the edge-back swipe — users feel trapped.
- **Adaptive over uniform** — "adaptive" means same information architecture, platform-correct components: an iOS build uses a bottom tab bar + nav stack + action sheets; the Android build of the same product uses a navigation bar + top app bar + bottom sheets + Material FAB. The user never notices the difference because each feels native. This is the default posture for any app shipping to more than one platform.
- **Respect the hardware, not just the OS** — safe areas, notches/Dynamic Island, rounded display corners, foldable hinges and fold/unfold posture changes (Android `WindowSizeClass`, `displayFeatures`), home-button vs gesture nav, the Action Button / Camera Control (iPhone), the Digital Crown (Apple Watch), and pointer/keyboard on tablets all change layout. Trap: hardcoding a status-bar height or assuming one contiguous rectangle — your UI lands under the camera cutout or across a fold seam.

## 1.2 Apple — HIG, iOS/iPadOS & the Liquid Glass era

- **Apple Human Interface Guidelines (HIG)** — Apple's canonical design doc, spanning iOS, iPadOS, macOS, watchOS, tvOS, and visionOS. Prescriptive on layout, SF Symbols, Dynamic Type, safe areas, and component behavior; enforced partly by App Review. Reach for it as ground truth on any Apple platform. Trap: HIG examples assume San Francisco (SF) type + SF Symbols + system controls — fight those and you lose the free accessibility and the native feel.
- **Liquid Glass (iOS 26 / iPadOS 26 / macOS 26 Tahoe / watchOS 26 / tvOS 26, 2025)** — Apple's biggest visual overhaul since iOS 7 (2013), announced at WWDC25. A translucent system *material* that reflects and refracts content behind it ("lensing"), used for the controls/navigation layer — tab bars, toolbars, sidebars, sheets — that floats above content. The principle is *hierarchy through depth*: the chrome reads as a distinct glass plane over the content plane. In SwiftUI it's the `.glassEffect()` / `glassEffectContainer` API, not a manual blur. **Date-flagged (2025–26): load-bearing, not hype** — it's the shipping default across all Apple OSes, but the look is still settling (Apple dialed back transparency in betas over legibility complaints). Trap: see the two-variant rule below.
- **Regular vs Clear (Liquid Glass variants)** — **Regular** is the default: fully adaptive (adjusts to background brightness, size, content), legible over anything, safe to put any content on top of. **Clear** is permanently more transparent, *non-adaptive*, and demands all three conditions: it sits over media-rich content, that content tolerates a dimming scrim beneath the glass, and the labels/symbols above are bold and bright. Never mix the two in one design. Trap: reaching for Clear for "premium" feel over arbitrary content tanks contrast (→ §19) — the scrim is mandatory, not optional, and even then verify against WCAG.
- **"Glass belongs to the navigation layer"** — reserve Liquid Glass for the floating chrome above content; do **not** apply it to content layers (lists, tables, cards) and **never stack glass-on-glass**. For overlays within content, use fills, vibrancy, and transparency instead. Trap: a screen where everything is glass has no depth hierarchy — the entire point of the material collapses.
- **SF Symbols** — Apple's 6,900+ icon library (SF Symbols 7, 2025), weight- and scale-matched to San Francisco, with built-in rendering modes (monochrome, hierarchical, palette, multicolor), variable-color, Draw animations, and gradients. Using them gets you Dynamic Type alignment, RTL mirroring, and accessibility labels for free. Trap: mixing custom icons with SF Symbols at the same optical weight looks broken — match stroke and baseline or commit fully to a custom set.
- **Dynamic Type** — Apple's user-controlled text-scaling system: design to *text styles* (`largeTitle`, `body`, `caption`) not fixed point sizes, and the system scales them across the standard range plus five larger Accessibility sizes. Honoring it is a baseline accessibility requirement (→ §19). Trap: fixed 14pt labels in truncating fixed-height rows — copy clips at AX5; design for reflow and test at the largest size.
- **iPadOS specifics** — not "big iPhone": expects multitasking (Stage Manager, Split View, Slide Over), pointer/trackpad hover + the morphing pointer that snaps to controls, full keyboard shortcuts (⌘-based menu, discoverable via the hold-⌘ overlay), drag-and-drop, and sidebar-based navigation on wide layouts. iPadOS 26 adds a real menu bar and resizable overlapping windows, pushing it toward desktop. Trap: shipping a stretched-phone single-column layout on a 13″ iPad wastes the canvas and fails pointer-precision and keyboard expectations.
- **tvOS** — the **focus engine**, not touch: navigation is directional focus driven by Siri Remote swipes/clicks, with parallax-layered focused artwork and 10-foot legibility (large type, generous safe-area overscan margins). No hover, no cursor, no precise pointing. Trap: porting a phone's dense grid and small tap targets to the living room — focus must land predictably on large, well-separated elements.

## 1.3 Google — Material Design 3 / Material You (Android)

- **Material Design 3 (M3) / Material You** — Google's design system and Android's default since Android 12. Built on **dynamic color** (a tonal palette algorithmically derived from the user's wallpaper, `ColorScheme` roles like `primary` / `surface` / `surfaceContainer`), large-radius shapes, and an elevation model that uses *tonal* surface color (and optional shadow) rather than shadow alone. Canonical reference: m3.material.io. Trap: hardcoding brand hex values defeats Material You — users expect the system to retint your app; design your tokens to *accept* dynamic color or deliberately opt out, don't accidentally half-adopt it.
- **Material 3 Expressive (May 2025; Android 16 / Pixel, Wear OS)** — an additive evolution of M3 emphasizing emotion and motion: a **35-shape** morph library (square↔squircle and beyond) with built-in shape-morph animation, emphasized typography, a **spring-based (physics) motion system** replacing duration curves, and **15 new/updated Compose components**. **Date-flagged (2025–26): rolling out, semi-hype** — the research framing is real and components are landing, but full availability is phased across devices; don't assume every Android user sees it yet. Trap: cranking "expressive" motion everywhere fights Reduce Motion (→ §11, §19) and slows task flows — reserve the flourish for moments that matter.
- **Material components & their iOS counterparts** — map below. The FAB (floating action button) has no native iOS equivalent; a hamburger navigation drawer is *discouraged* on iOS where a tab bar belongs. Trap: porting a FAB or a hamburger drawer to iOS instantly reads as "Android app on iPhone."

  | Material (Android) | Apple analog | Note |
  |---|---|---|
  | Top app bar | Navigation bar | title + actions |
  | Navigation bar (bottom) | Tab bar | primary destinations |
  | Navigation drawer (hamburger) | Sidebar | drawer discouraged on iOS |
  | FAB | (toolbar/nav-bar button) | no native iOS FAB |
  | Snackbar | (no exact analog; ~toast) | transient, bottom |
  | Bottom sheet | Sheet / action sheet | modal or persistent |
  | Ripple (touch feedback) | (highlight/dim) | Material-specific tell |

- **System back & predictive back** — Android's back is a global, OS-level affordance (gesture or button) you must honor; **predictive back** (opt-in Android 14, default for apps targeting Android 15+) previews the destination with an animated peek as the user drags. Trap: maintaining your own in-app back button *instead of* honoring system back, or breaking the back stack so the peek animates to the wrong screen.
- **Type & spacing units** — Android measures layout in **dp** (density-independent pixels) and text in **sp** (scalable pixels, which track the user's font-size setting). Use sp for text so font-scale works; use dp for everything else. Material's minimum touch target is **48×48 dp** (vs iOS's 44×44 pt) (→ §19). Trap: sizing text in dp — it ignores the accessibility font-scale and fails users who scaled up.
- **Android version behavior deltas that hit design** — themed (monochrome) app icons (13+), per-app language (13+), notification runtime permission (13+ — you must *ask*, can't assume), predictive back (14 opt-in / 15 default), and **edge-to-edge enforced by default** for apps targeting Android 15+ (handle window insets or content hides behind the status/nav bars). Trap: ignoring edge-to-edge on Android 15 — your content scrolls under the system bars.

## 1.4 Microsoft — Fluent 2 (Windows) & macOS HIG (desktop)

- **Fluent 2 (Windows 11)** — Microsoft's cross-platform design system; on Windows it governs WinUI 3 / Windows App SDK apps. Signature traits: a layered elevation model (a base material + content layers on top), rounded window corners, and the Segoe UI Variable type ramp. Reference: fluent2.microsoft.design + Microsoft Learn (Windows app design). Trap: Win32 legacy apps don't get Fluent materials automatically — opting in requires WinUI/Windows App SDK or explicit material brushes.
- **Mica vs Acrylic (Windows materials)** — **Mica** is *opaque*, tinted once by the desktop wallpaper, cheap to render, and used for long-lived base surfaces (window backgrounds, title bars). **Acrylic** is *semi-transparent* frosted glass for *transient, light-dismiss* surfaces (flyouts, menus, command bars). Material map below. Trap: using Acrylic for a primary always-on background — it's GPU-expensive and visually noisy; that's Mica's job.

  | Surface | Windows material | Apple analog |
  |---|---|---|
  | Window / page base | Mica (opaque, wallpaper-tinted) | (opaque system background) |
  | Transient flyout / menu | Acrylic (translucent) | Liquid Glass / `.regularMaterial` |
  | Tinted-window base | Mica Alt | — |
  | Immersive scrim | Smoke | dimming layer |

- **macOS HIG** — Apple's desktop conventions: the global menu bar (top of screen, *not* per-window), traffic-light window controls, sidebars, toolbars, the dock, full keyboard navigation, and — as of macOS 26 Tahoe — Liquid Glass sidebars and toolbars. Trap: a Windows-style in-window menu bar, or a hamburger menu, on macOS; Mac users expect the function in the system menu bar and ⌘-shortcuts.
- **Windows vs macOS divergence cheat-sheet** — menu bar: per-window (Win) vs global (Mac); window controls: top-right minimize/maximize/close (Win) vs top-left traffic lights (Mac); primary modifier: Ctrl (Win) vs ⌘ (Mac); close-window ≠ quit-app on Mac (the app stays running, ⌘Q quits). Scrollbars, default fonts (Segoe UI Variable vs SF), and title-bar conventions also differ. Trap: a cross-platform desktop app that hardcodes Ctrl-shortcuts and right-side window controls feels alien on Mac — adapt per-OS.

## 1.5 Spatial & wearables — visionOS, watchOS, Wear OS

- **visionOS (Apple Vision Pro)** — spatial computing built on three container types: **Windows** (flat, resizable, glass-backed panels), **Volumes** (bounded 3D content via RealityKit/`RealityView`), and **Spaces** (Shared Space, where apps coexist in your room, vs a Full Space your app owns exclusively). Default material is system **glass**, so the physical environment shows through. Primary input is **gaze + pinch** (eyes target, fingers confirm). Trap: opaque backgrounds in the Shared Space punch "holes" in the room — use translucent glass; and because eye-targeting is imprecise, give every interactive element **≥60 pt of target space** (vs 44 pt on iOS), with hover feedback on gaze (→ §19).
- **Ornaments (visionOS)** — UI controls that attach to and float just outside a window's edges (toolbars, tab bars, contextual actions), e.g. a bottom toolbar overlapping the window edge or a vertical tab bar on the leading edge — keeping chrome off the content surface. Trap: re-creating an iOS bottom tab bar *inside* the window wastes content space and ignores the platform's depth model.
- **watchOS** — glanceable-first; the canonical surfaces are **complications** (tiny, watch-face-tinted data points), the **Smart Stack** (scrollable widget stack summoned by an upward Digital Crown turn, watchOS 10+), notifications, and short, vertically-scrolling app views. The **Digital Crown** is the precision scroll/zoom input; the Action Button (Ultra) and double-tap gesture add hardware affordances. Trap: porting a phone's information density to a watch — design for a sub-two-second glance, one primary action per screen.
- **Wear OS (Material 3 Expressive for Wear, 2025)** — Google's watch platform; the headline surface is **Tiles** (full-screen, swipeable slices of app function — heavier and more interactive than watchOS complications), plus complications, ongoing activities, and notifications, all now under Material 3 Expressive with watch-tuned components and round-screen layouts. The rotating side button / bezel is the Crown analog. Trap: Tiles ≠ watchOS Smart Stack widgets — Tiles do more and are swiped horizontally; don't assume a 1:1 port of your watchOS layout.
- **Complication vs Tile vs Smart Stack widget** — complication = tiny face-embedded glyph/value (both platforms); Smart Stack widget (watchOS) = small rectangular card, limited interaction, Crown-summoned; Tile (Wear OS) = full-screen interactive panel, swipe-navigated. Trap: conflating these three when planning a wearable feature — they have different sizes, interaction models, and content budgets.

## 1.6 In-car & other embedded surfaces

- **CarPlay (iOS) / Android Auto** — projected, *template-driven* UIs: you don't draw freely; you populate Apple's CarPlay templates (list, grid, map, now-playing, tab bar) or Android's `CarAppLibrary` / Auto templates, and the system renders driver-safe, distraction-limited layouts. Categories are gated (audio, navigation, communication, EV, parking…). Trap: assuming you can ship your phone UI to the dash — only approved template-based apps pass review, and free-form custom drawing is disallowed for safety (→ §19).
- **Android Automotive OS (AAOS)** vs **Android Auto** — Auto *projects* from the phone; Automotive OS *runs natively in the car's head unit*. Same template discipline, different lifecycle and distribution. Trap: conflating the two — AAOS apps are installed in the vehicle and must handle car-specific hardware/permissions, not a phone tether.

## 1.7 Web apps & PWAs

- **Progressive Web App (PWA)** — a website that, with a web app manifest + HTTPS + (usually) a service worker, becomes installable and runs in an app-like window. The cross-platform escape hatch: one codebase, no app store (mostly), instant updates. Trap: PWAs hit a *capabilities gap* — no/limited access to many native APIs, weaker iOS support (Safari gates installability; web push on iOS arrived only in 16.4, and only for Home-Screen-installed PWAs), and discoverability lives outside the App Store / Play Store.
- **Web app manifest** — the JSON (`manifest.json` / `.webmanifest`) declaring `name`, `icons`, `theme_color`, `background_color`, `start_url`, and `display`. Required for installability (alongside HTTPS — `localhost` excepted). Reference: web.dev + MDN. Trap: missing **maskable** icons (the `purpose: "maskable"` set) or a too-small icon set means a degraded install prompt or a letterboxed/clipped icon on Android adaptive-icon masks.
- **Display modes** — `fullscreen` → `standalone` → `minimal-ui` → `browser`, a defined fallback chain (override the order via `display_override`). `standalone` (no browser chrome, app-like) is the common target; `minimal-ui` keeps a slim back/reload affordance. Experimental: **`window-controls-overlay`** (draw into the title-bar area on desktop) and **`tabbed`** (a browser-rendered tab strip for multi-context PWAs) — **date-flagged (2025–26): experimental**, Chromium-leaning, only honored via `display_override` and only when `display` is also set; don't depend on them cross-browser yet. Trap: assuming `standalone` everywhere — iOS honors a subset and you lose the URL bar's affordances (share, reload, security indicator) without replacing them.
- **Web-app vs native trade-off** — PWA wins on reach, update speed, and single-codebase cost; native wins on deep OS integration (background work, hardware, home-screen widgets, deep haptics, App Store trust), performance ceiling, and platform-correct feel. Trap: choosing PWA for an app whose value *is* OS integration (a camera-heavy tool, a fitness tracker needing background sensors), then fighting the capabilities gap forever.
- **Responsive web app ≠ adaptive native** — a responsive site that merely reflows columns is not a platform-correct app; it won't grow native navigation, gestures, or controls. Trap: shipping a responsive marketing layout in a PWA shell and calling it an "app" — it reads as a website in a frame, because it is one.

## 1.8 Cross-platform frameworks & their design models

- **SwiftUI** — Apple's declarative UI framework across all Apple platforms; adopts Liquid Glass, Dynamic Type, and SF Symbols natively and adapts a single view hierarchy to iOS/iPadOS/macOS/watchOS/visionOS. Apple-only. Trap: "adapts" ≠ "free desktop app" — `List`, navigation, toolbars, and keyboard handling still need per-platform tuning (sidebars, multi-column, shortcuts).
- **Jetpack Compose** — Android's modern declarative toolkit; ships Material 3 / Material You and the tightest Android-fidelity of any framework. Pairs with **Compose Multiplatform** (JetBrains) to share Compose UI to desktop/iOS/web — but shared UI then carries *Material* onto iOS, i.e. uniform-not-adaptive unless you branch. Trap: Compose Multiplatform on iOS gives you Material-flavored iOS unless you deliberately style for Cupertino — know whether you're choosing uniform or adaptive.
- **Flutter** — Google's framework rendering its *own* widgets via the Impeller engine (pixel-identical everywhere), with two design libraries: **Material** and **Cupertino** (iOS-look widgets). Because Flutter paints rather than wraps native controls, you must *choose* fidelity, and Cupertino lags real UIKit/SwiftUI (it won't auto-inherit Liquid Glass). Trap: shipping Material on iOS "to save time" — it's the canonical "Android app on my iPhone" tell; either adapt with Cupertino or own a fully custom brand canvas.
- **React Native** — JS/TS framework that renders to *actual native* components (with the New Architecture / Fabric renderer), so controls inherit real platform look/feel; design adaptation is on you (platform-split files, `Platform.select`). Trap: assuming native rendering means native *feel* automatically — navigation patterns, gestures, and back behavior still need per-platform design decisions.
- **Kotlin Multiplatform (KMP)** — shares *business logic*, not UI, by design — the deliberately adaptive choice: native SwiftUI on iOS + Compose on Android, one shared core. Highest native fidelity of the cross-platform options, highest UI cost. Trap: expecting KMP to share screens — it won't; that's Compose Multiplatform's job.
- **.NET MAUI** — Microsoft's evolution of Xamarin.Forms, mapping a single XAML/C# UI to native controls on iOS/Android/macOS (Catalyst)/Windows (WinUI/Fluent). Strong on Windows + enterprise/line-of-business; thinner on cutting-edge mobile fidelity. Trap: leaning on the single-UI abstraction for a consumer app where platform feel matters — MAUI shines for internal tools, not for apps competing on polish against native incumbents.

  | Framework | UI model | Native feel default | Best when |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | SwiftUI | Native, Apple-only | High (Apple) | All-Apple, latest HIG |
  | Jetpack Compose | Native Android (+ CMP shared) | High (Android) | Android-first; CMP for shared UI |
  | Flutter | Self-rendered (Material/Cupertino) | Custom (you choose) | Brand-canvas apps, pixel parity |
  | React Native | Maps to native components | Medium (adapt yourself) | JS teams, mostly-native feel |
  | KMP | Shared logic, native UI per platform | Highest | Max fidelity, shared core |
  | .NET MAUI | Single UI → native controls | Medium | Windows + enterprise LOB |

*Sources: [Apple Human Interface Guidelines](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/); [Meet Liquid Glass — WWDC25](https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2025/219/); [Designing for visionOS — Apple HIG](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/designing-for-visionos); [Material 3 (m3.material.io)](https://m3.material.io/); [Start building with Material 3 Expressive — Material Design](https://m3.material.io/blog/building-with-m3-expressive); [Mica material — Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/style/mica); [display_override — Web app manifest, MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Progressive_web_apps/Manifest/Reference/display_override).*

# 2. App visual & aesthetic styles

App aesthetics are not free choices the way web styles are: each OS ships a design language with a system component kit, and shipping against it costs you familiarity, free accessibility behaviors, and OS-update durability. This section catalogues the app-flavored styles, names what each platform calls its default, and flags where a borrowed aesthetic fights the platform. Governing principle: **on the web you paint on a blank canvas; in an app you paint inside someone else's frame — diverge deliberately, never by accident.**

## 2.1 Platform-native defaults

The single highest-leverage aesthetic decision is whether to adopt the platform look at all. Adopt it and you inherit motion, haptics, accessibility, and Dynamic Type for free; reject it and you own all of that yourself, forever.

- **Native iOS look (Cupertino, "HIG-native")** — Apple's system aesthetic: SF Pro / SF Pro Rounded type, large navigation titles, generous whitespace, grouped inset tables, full-width buttons, SF Symbols iconography, deference to content. Owned by Apple HIG; rendered by SwiftUI / UIKit. Reach for it for any app where "feels like an iPhone app" is a virtue (utilities, productivity, finance). Trap: a pixel-perfect Android-style UI shipped on iOS reads as a cheap port — App Store reviewers and users notice. As of **iOS 26 (2025)** the native look is Liquid Glass (→ §2.2); pre-26 it's the flat-but-layered post-iOS 7 language.
- **Material You / Material 3 (Android default)** — Google's system aesthetic: Roboto/Google Sans type, tonal elevation (color-tint instead of drop-shadow), large touch targets, FAB, dynamic color from wallpaper. Owned by **m3.material.io**; rendered by Jetpack Compose Material3. **Material 3 Expressive** (announced May 2025, shipping on Pixel via Android 16 QPR1 from Sept 2025) layers on vibrant color, spring-physics motion, a 35-shape library, and emphasized type — Google's most user-researched update (46 studies, 18,000+ participants). Reach for it when Android-first or when dynamic-color personalization is a feature. Trap: Material's elevation-by-tint and FAB are load-bearing affordances — strip them for a custom brand and you lose the "where do I tap" legibility Material buys you.
- **Fluent (Windows / Microsoft 365)** — Microsoft's system aesthetic: Segoe UI Variable, Mica and Acrylic materials, rounded corners (WinUI 3), reveal highlight, depth via layering. Owned by Microsoft Fluent 2. Reach for it on Windows desktop apps and cross-Microsoft surfaces. Trap: Acrylic (the blur material) is GPU-expensive and Microsoft itself walked it back toward the opaque **Mica** for app backgrounds — don't acrylic-everything.
- **macOS-native (AppKit/SwiftUI desktop)** — vibrancy sidebars, traffic-light controls, toolbar+content split, menu bar as primary command surface, sheets anchored to windows. As of **macOS Tahoe 26** it shares Liquid Glass with iOS. Reach for it for pro desktop tools. Trap: an iPad app stretched to a Mac window (Catalyst done lazily) feels wrong — Mac users expect a real menu bar, keyboard shortcuts, hover states, and resize behavior, not touch ergonomics scaled up.
- **Web-app / PWA "neutral-native"** — no OS owns the canvas, so PWAs pick a cross-platform system (often Material-flavored, or a house design system) and lean on it everywhere. Reach for it when one codebase serves all platforms. Trap: "looks native nowhere" — a PWA that mimics iOS chrome on Android (or vice versa) lands in the uncanny valley on both; and iOS Safari throttles install prompts, push, and `100vh`/safe-area behavior, so faked native chrome breaks at the edges (→ §1 app shells). Pick a confident neutral identity instead of half-emulating each host.
- **watchOS / Wear OS** — wrist platforms each ship a constrained aesthetic: watchOS uses SF Compact, full-bleed cards, edge-hugging rounded layouts, and a dark default (OLED); Wear OS 5 uses a Material-derived **Compose for Wear OS** kit with curved/edge-aligned components and tiles. Reach for the platform kit verbatim — glance-time is ~2–4s, so custom chrome is pure cost. Trap: porting a phone layout to the wrist (dense lists, multi-field forms) is unusable; design for one decision per screen and complication/tile entry points (→ §1).

| Platform | Default language | Type | Signature material/affordance |
|---|---|---|---|
| iOS / iPadOS 26 | Liquid Glass | SF Pro | Translucent navigation glass |
| Android | Material 3 Expressive | Roboto / Google Sans | Tonal elevation, dynamic color, FAB |
| Windows | Fluent 2 | Segoe UI Variable | Mica / Acrylic, reveal |
| macOS 26 | Liquid Glass / AppKit | SF Pro | Vibrancy sidebar, menu bar |
| watchOS / Wear OS | Compact native | SF Compact / Roboto | Full-bleed, edge-curved cards/tiles |
| visionOS | Spatial glass | SF Pro | Translucent windows, ornaments, depth |

## 2.2 Translucency & glass

Frosted translucency is the defining app-era material — but apps constrain it far more tightly than the web, because the OS defines exactly where it's allowed.

- **Liquid Glass (iOS/iPadOS/macOS/watchOS/tvOS 26, 2025)** — Apple's real-time-rendered translucent material: refracts and tints from content behind it, shows specular highlights, reacts to motion, adapts light/dark. Two variants: **Regular** (adaptive, versatile) and **Clear** (permanently transparent, requires a dimming layer for legibility). Owned by Apple; free via SwiftUI's `.glassEffect()` / `GlassEffectContainer` and system bars. Reach for it on the **navigation layer only** — tab bars, toolbars, sidebars, sheets. Trap (Apple's own rule): never put glass on the content layer, and never stack glass on glass — it destroys hierarchy and shreds contrast. *Volatile/load-bearing: this is the live iOS default, not hype — design for it.*
- **System materials (iOS) — ultraThin / thin / regular / thick / chrome** — the five named blur materials beneath Liquid Glass, ordered by opacity. Pick by how much background context you want to bleed through (thin = more context, thick = more legible). Trap: thinner material = lower text contrast; vibrancy helps but doesn't guarantee WCAG — verify, don't assume.
- **Acrylic / Mica (Windows Fluent)** — Acrylic is the iOS-blur analogue (translucent, in-app or behind-window); **Mica** is a low-cost opaque material tinted by the desktop wallpaper, intended for app backgrounds and title bars. Reach for Mica as the default backdrop, Acrylic for transient flyouts/command surfaces. Trap: Acrylic over scrolling content is a GPU and battery cost on laptops — Microsoft restricts it deliberately; follow suit.
- **Spatial translucency (visionOS)** — Apple's spatial OS renders windows as frosted glass floating in the user's room, with **ornaments** (controls anchored outside the window edge) and depth/Z-layering as primary affordances. Owned by Apple; rendered by SwiftUI/RealityKit. Reach for it only when building for Vision Pro. Trap: glass-on-passthrough contrast varies with whatever real wall is behind it — you cannot assume a background, so lean on the system material and test in bright + dark rooms; eye-tracking targets need ~60pt minimums (→ §19). *Volatile: visionOS is a small install base; spatial is real but a niche surface, not a mainstream app target — date any spatial bet.*
- **Glassmorphism (cross-platform borrowed style)** — the web/Big-Sur frosted-panel aesthetic ported into custom app skins. Looks premium; reads modern. Trap: outside the OS material APIs you get none of the automatic accessibility fallbacks — text-over-blur contrast is fragile and routinely fails **WCAG 2.2 SC 1.4.3 / 1.4.11**; you must hand-build a scrim and honor Reduce Transparency yourself (→ §19 accessibility).
- **Vibrancy** — the system treatment that adapts foreground text/icons against translucent materials so they stay legible. Owned by Apple (and echoed by Fluent). Use system vibrancy rather than hardcoding label colors over glass. Trap: custom-colored text over a system material bypasses vibrancy and breaks in dark mode.
- **Accessibility veto for all glass** — Reduce Transparency frosts/opaques the material, Increase Contrast adds borders and pushes toward black/white, Reduce Motion kills the elastic/specular response. On iOS 26 these fire automatically *only* when you use the real material; roll your own glass and you must replicate all three (→ §19, §11 haptics for the motion coupling).

## 2.3 Flat, semi-flat & content-first

The dominant app aesthetic of the last decade: recede the chrome, foreground the content.

- **Flat / semi-flat app UI** — minimal ornament, solid fills, type-and-color hierarchy, no skeuomorphic texture. iOS went flat in iOS 7; Material is "flat with elevation" (shadow/tint conveys depth without gloss); Fluent is "flat with material" (Mica/Acrylic stand in for shadow). Reach for it as the safe, legible default for almost any app. Trap: pure flat can erase affordance — a flat-colored rectangle reads as a label, not a button — and **flat affordance is a documented usability cost (NN/g)**. Material solves it with tonal elevation; flat-iOS solves it with SF Symbols + tint color + position. Don't flatten away the *tap-here* signal.
- **Bold-minimal / content-first** — one dominant element per screen, oversized type, ruthless whitespace, color used sparingly as accent. The aesthetic of reading, music, and photo apps. Reach for it when the content *is* the product (a track, a photo, an article). Trap: minimalism hides navigation — if the only way forward is an unlabeled gesture or a hidden hamburger, discoverability collapses; pair every gesture with a visible affordance (→ §19, §1).
- **Data-dense pro-tool aesthetic** — high information density, compact rows, multi-pane layouts, monospaced numerics, keyboard-first. Bloomberg, Linear, trading and DAW apps, pro creative tools. Reach for it when the user lives in the app all day and density = speed. Trap: density is hostile to touch (**44pt iOS / 48dp Android minimum targets, ~7–9mm physical**) and to first-run users — pro density on a phone, or with no progressive disclosure, is unusable. Tune density to input modality (pointer vs touch), not to how much you can cram in (→ §19).

## 2.4 Expressive, playful & brand-forward

When the app's *job* is to feel like a personality, not a utility.

- **Vibrant gradient / brand app** — saturated gradients, bold brand color, large rounded shapes, energetic motion. Fintech, social, fitness onboarding (Robinhood, Duolingo, Cash App). Reach for it when emotional differentiation beats system familiarity. Trap: brand gradients fight the OS's adaptive color and dark mode — design both themes explicitly, ensure text-on-gradient clears **WCAG 1.4.3 (4.5:1 / 3:1 large)** at *every* stop, and don't let a gradient bleed under translucent system bars where vibrancy can't correct it.
- **Claymorphic / playful consumer** — soft 3D "clay" shapes, puffy rounded forms, candy color, bouncy spring motion, mascots. Kids', wellness, casual-game, and habit apps. Material 3 Expressive's spring motion + extra shape options lean this way natively. Reach for it for warmth and approachability. Trap: clay's soft inner/outer shadows drift toward the same low-contrast pit as neumorphism — keep interactive elements on hard, accessible contrast and reserve clay for decoration (→ §19).
- **Material 3 Expressive as a brand vector (2025)** — Google now ships expressiveness *as the default*: vibrant tonal palettes, two spring systems (spatial + effects), a 35-shape library, emphasized type, and 15 updated components (button groups, FAB menu, split button, toolbars). Reach for it to be playful while staying fully native on Android. Trap: expressive shape/motion still answers to the M3 token system — improvising outside the tokens loses dynamic color and theming. *Dated: Expressive landed on Pixel via Android 16 QPR1 (Sept 2025) and is still rolling across Wear OS and OEM skins — treat shape and motion specifics as still-settling.*
- **AMOLED-black** — pure `#000000` backgrounds that switch OLED pixels fully off. Distinct from standard dark theme's near-black (`#121212` in Material). Reach for it on OLED hardware for contrast drama and battery: pure black can cut display power **~39–47% at high brightness** and ~10–15% beyond what near-black grays save (Purdue 2020; ~21% average across common apps at default brightness). Trap: pure-black edges disappear against device bezels and lose all elevation cues — Material deliberately uses `#121212` so shadows still read; offer AMOLED-black as an *option*, not the only dark theme. Smearing/black-crush on cheaper OLEDs is real. (LCD hardware sees zero battery benefit — gate it on a panel check.)
- **Dark-first apps** — designed dark as the primary theme, light as the afterthought (developer tools, media, night-use apps). Reach for it when the usage context is dark (cinema, bed, IDE). Trap: dark UI needs *desaturated* accent colors — fully saturated hues vibrate painfully on dark backgrounds — and elevation must come from lighter surfaces, since drop-shadows vanish on black; dark mode still owes **WCAG 1.4.3** contrast (light-gray-on-black often fails) (→ §19).

## 2.5 Utility, density & the failure cases

Styles that are either deliberately raw, or that look great in a mockup and break on a real device.

- **Brutalist / utility apps** — raw system controls, system font, zero custom chrome, monochrome, function over polish. Indie utilities, dev tools, "anti-design" statements. Reach for it to ship fast, signal seriousness/no-nonsense, or as a brand stance. Trap: "no design" still has to clear accessibility and touch-target minimums — raw isn't an excuse for sub-44pt tap targets or 2:1 text contrast (→ §19).
- **Super-app / everything-app density** — many services packed into one shell: grid of mini-app icons, dense home feed, persistent bottom nav, in-app browser (WeChat, Grab, Alipay, X's ambitions). Reach for it only with genuine multi-service breadth and the scale to justify it. Trap: Western platform conventions and App Store/Play policies fight the model (mini-app review, in-app-payment rules, the iOS notarization requirement for app-within-app stores); density also crushes discoverability — a wall of equal-weight entry points has no hierarchy, so users find nothing.
- **Neumorphism (Soft UI) — usually a trap in apps** — elements extruded from one base color via paired light/dark soft shadows, no hard borders. Beautiful in a static comp. Reach for it almost never in production: it categorically fails **WCAG 2.2 SC 1.4.11 non-text contrast** — the shadow-against-base ratio is often ~1.7:1 vs the 3:1 required, so users literally can't tell what's interactive, and `:focus`/`:pressed` feedback is too subtle to register. If you must, confine it to decorative, non-interactive surfaces and give every control a real contrast border (→ §19). Apps make this worse than the web: no OS gives you neumorphism as a system material, so you forfeit every automatic accessibility fallback.
- **Skeuomorphism (selective revival)** — real-world texture/metaphor (leather, felt, paper, knobs). Killed wholesale in iOS 7, but tactile metaphors survive where they aid comprehension: synth/DAW knobs, level meters, e-reader page curl, Apple's depth cues in Journal/Weather. Reach for it when the physical metaphor *teaches* the control. Trap: decorative skeuomorphism (stitched leather for its own sake) reads as dated and wastes pixels — use the metaphor only when it carries function; a rotary knob also fails as a touch target unless it accepts a linear drag fallback (→ §19).
- **AI-native / generative-surface aesthetics (2025–26)** — chat-first or canvas-first shells with streaming text, shimmer/skeleton "thinking" states, citation chips, and inline tool-call cards (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, Copilot). The emerging convention is a translucent input dock + a scrolling transcript, often over the platform's native material. Reach for it when the product *is* the model. Trap: streaming + non-deterministic output is hostile to screen readers (live-region churn) and to "where am I" orientation; gate animation behind Reduce Motion, mark streaming regions `aria-live="polite"` / accessibility-announce sparingly, and never hide the stop/regenerate control (→ §19). *Volatile: conventions here are <2 years old and shifting fast — treat any specific chrome as provisional.*

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Materials](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/materials); [Apple Developer — Adopting Liquid Glass](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/TechnologyOverviews/adopting-liquid-glass); [Material 3 — Start building with M3 Expressive](https://m3.material.io/blog/building-with-m3-expressive); [Microsoft Fluent 2 — Materials (Acrylic/Mica)](https://fluent2.microsoft.design/material); [Android Accessibility — Touch target size (48dp)](https://support.google.com/accessibility/android/answer/7101858); [Axess Lab — Neumorphism accessibility](https://axesslab.com/neumorphism/).*

All facts confirmed. The draft's specs are accurate. Now I'll produce the revised, strictly-more-complete section, adding missing patterns (back-to-home vs back-to-app, breadcrumbs/wayfinding, segmented control as in-screen nav, pull-to-refresh-adjacent gestures, scroll-to-top tab tap, large title behavior, command palette, modal vs modeless, sheet detents target sizes, tab badges, search as navigation, "Now" tab/launcher patterns, swipe-between-tabs anti-pattern, nested-doll depth limits) and filling platform halves and a11y vetoes.

# 3. Navigation architecture & information architecture

How users locate themselves and move between destinations. Navigation is the skeleton every other pattern hangs on — get the IA model and the back stack wrong and no amount of visual polish saves the app. Governing principle: **match the platform's native model**, because the OS already trained the user (gesture-back, tab persistence, up-vs-back), and an app that fights muscle memory feels broken before the user can articulate why.

## 3.1 Top-level navigation (the persistent chrome)

- **Tab bar (iOS) / bottom navigation bar (Android, Material 3)** — the persistent row of 3–5 top-level destinations at the bottom of a phone screen. The default for flat, peer-level sections (Home, Search, Profile). iOS HIG caps at 3–5 and reserves tabs for **navigation, not verbs** — no "Compose" or "Share" tab. Android M3 calls it the *navigation bar* and likewise wants 3–5 destinations, each with icon + short label, the active item highlighted by color and a pill-shaped active indicator. Trap: stuffing 6+ items and hiding the overflow behind a **"More" tab** (iOS) — it buries destinations behind an extra tap and reads as an IA failure. Accessibility: keep labels visible, not icon-only guesswork; the tab is a single touch target sized ≥44×44pt (iOS) / 48×48dp (Android) and must announce its selected state to the screen reader (→ §19).
- **iOS 26 floating Liquid Glass tab bar** *(dated: iOS 26, 2025–2026; load-bearing, not hype)* — the tab bar detaches from the screen edge, becomes a capsule inset from the margins, renders in translucent **Liquid Glass**, and **minimizes on scroll-down** (collapsing toward just the active tab) then re-expands on scroll-up via the `tabBarMinimizeBehavior(_:)` modifier. A **tab view bottom accessory** (`tabViewBottomAccessory`) "shelf" sits above the tabs and persists across tab switches — the canonical use is a Now-Playing mini-player, which animates down inline with the bar when it minimizes. Trap: the floating capsule overlaps scrollable content — your scroll view needs the right safe-area inset or the last row hides under the glass. Contrast over the blur is fragile; don't trust the system to scrim custom overlay content (→ §19).
- **Navigation rail (Android M3 / iPadOS)** — a vertical strip of the same top-level destinations pinned to the leading edge, used when the window gets wide. M3's rule is explicit by **window size class**: *compact* (<600dp, phone portrait) → navigation bar; *medium* (600–839dp) → rail (or bar if few destinations); *expanded* (≥840dp) → rail, **never** a bottom bar. The collapsed rail holds **3–7 items** plus an optional FAB; past five destinations, switch to a **modal expanded rail** (a labeled drawer) rather than cramming. Trap: keeping a bottom bar on a tablet/desktop window — it strands navigation a thumb-reach away from content that's now mouse-or-far-thumb driven.
- **Navigation drawer / hamburger menu (Android) / sidebar (iOS, iPadOS, macOS)** — an off-screen panel of destinations revealed by a menu (☰) button or edge swipe. Holds *many* or *hierarchical* destinations that don't fit a tab bar. **Discoverability cost is the defining trap**: hidden nav is used roughly half as much as visible nav (NN/g) — anything important earns a tab, not a drawer slot. M3 distinguishes *standard* (inline, persistent on wide screens) from *modal* (overlay + scrim, dismiss-on-outside-tap) drawers; Material 3 Expressive deprioritized the modal drawer in favor of the rail. macOS/iPadOS *sidebars* are the desktop-class equivalent — persistent, resizable, often the primary nav for productivity apps. Accessibility: a modal drawer must trap focus and be escapable by Esc / screen-reader back gesture (→ §19).
- **Tab-bar-to-sidebar adaptation (iPadOS 18+)** *(dated: iPadOS 18, 2024)* — one declared `UITab` set renders as a **bottom/top tab bar in compact width and a sidebar in regular width**, and the user can drag items between a customizable sidebar and a pinned tab-bar section. The modern answer to "do I build a tab bar or a sidebar?" — you build one adaptive structure (`UITabBarController` with `sidebar` mode / SwiftUI `TabView` with `.tabViewStyle(.sidebarAdaptable)`). Trap: assuming this is automatic; you must adopt the adaptable tab API, not hand-roll two navigation trees.
- **Top app bar / navigation bar / title bar** — the header strip carrying the screen title, the back/up affordance, and 1–3 contextual actions. iOS calls it the *navigation bar* (the **large-title** variant collapses to an inline title on scroll); Android M3 calls it the *top app bar* (small / center-aligned / medium / large, the tall ones collapsing on scroll); macOS/Windows use a *title bar* + toolbar. Trap: overloading it with actions — it's for the current screen's *primary* one or two actions, with overflow in a "⋯" / "⋮" menu, not a toolbar dumping ground.
- **FAB (floating action button, Android/Material)** — a circular elevated button for the screen's single most important *constructive* action (compose, add, new). Material-native; iOS has **no FAB** — the equivalent action lives in the nav-bar trailing slot or a toolbar. M3 sizes: small (40dp), standard (56dp), large (96dp), plus the extended FAB (icon + label). Trap: porting a FAB straight into an iOS app (it reads as off-platform), having more than one FAB competing per screen, or letting it occlude the last list row (pad the scroll view's bottom inset).
- **Bottom toolbar (iOS) / bottom app bar (Android M3)** — a bottom strip of *actions* (not destinations) for the current context. Distinct from the tab bar: tabs navigate, toolbars act. M3's bottom app bar can host a FAB and a handful of icon actions; iOS bottom toolbars carry contextual verbs in editing/detail screens. Trap: confusing it with a tab bar — users tap it expecting to change sections.

## 3.2 IA models (the shape of the tree)

- **Flat / tabs** — a small set of co-equal top-level sections, each its own stack, switched via the tab bar/rail. Best when sections are peers and users hop between them (Instagram, Phone). Cheap orientation, shallow depth. Trap: forcing genuinely hierarchical content into 5 flat buckets.
- **Hub-and-spoke** — a central home/launcher (the hub) from which users dive into a task (a spoke) and return to the hub to start another. The classic phone springboard, the Apple Watch app grid, and most single-purpose utilities. Best when tasks are discrete and you want users to fully exit one before another. Trap: deep spokes with no lateral movement — users bounce back to the hub for everything.
- **Nested doll / drill-down** — linear push-deeper-pop-back hierarchy (list → detail → sub-detail). The native `UINavigationController` / Android back-stack idiom. Best for browsing taxonomies (Settings, Mail folders). Trap: burying frequent destinations 4+ levels deep with no shortcut, and giving no wayfinding cue for *where* in the tree the user is (the parent's title in the back chevron is the minimum).
- **Bento / dashboard** — a single scrollable surface of heterogeneous tiles/cards, each a window into a sub-area (modern Settings landing pages, fitness/finance home screens, the Apple Watch grid). Best for at-a-glance status + jump-off. Plays to wide screens and glanceable wearables. Trap: a wall of equal-weight tiles with no hierarchy — the AI-dashboard cliché; weight tiles by importance, vary their size, lead with what changed.
- **Combination (the real world)** — most non-trivial apps are tabs-of-stacks: a flat tab bar where each tab owns an independent nested-doll stack, occasionally with a modal flow layered on top. State this explicitly when designing so the back-stack rules below are unambiguous.

## 3.3 The stack, push/pop, and up-vs-back

- **Navigation stack (push / pop)** — the LIFO history of screens within a section. *Push* slides a child on (iOS: right-to-left, with the title-bar back chevron showing the parent's title); *pop* reverses it. Material uses a comparable shared-axis forward/back motion. The fundamental drill-down primitive on every platform; SwiftUI expresses it as `NavigationStack` (data-driven `path`), Android as the Navigation component back stack.
- **Up vs. Back (Android's defining distinction)** — **Up** (the title-bar arrow) moves to the *hierarchical parent* within your app and never leaves it; **Back** (system gesture/button) moves to the *previous screen in time*, which may be another app entirely. They often coincide but diverge after a deep link (Back exits to the referrer; Up climbs into your app's hierarchy). iOS historically has **only Back** (no Up concept) — the nav-bar chevron *is* temporal back, scoped to the app. Trap: wiring Android's Up to behave like Back (or vice versa) — it silently breaks the deep-link return path.
- **Per-tab stacks & tab persistence** — each tab maintains its **own independent navigation stack**, and re-selecting the *already-active* tab **pops that tab to its root** (iOS HIG; Android Navigation component mirrors it with `saveState`/`restoreState`). Switching tabs preserves where you were in each. A double-tap-to-scroll-to-top convention layers on iOS (tap the active tab once at root to scroll the content view to the top). Trap: a single shared global stack across tabs (state bleeds between sections); or losing a tab's scroll position and drill-down on switch — users expect it frozen, not reset.
- **Title-bar back affordance vs. system back** — iOS shows an explicit on-screen back chevron **and** supports the edge-swipe; Android relies primarily on the **system gesture/button** and shows Up only when there's a hierarchical parent. Don't draw a custom in-content back button on Android that duplicates and contradicts the system one. Accessibility: never rely on the gesture alone — the on-screen back/up control is the screen-reader-reachable affordance (→ §19).

## 3.4 Gestural back & predictive back

- **Interactive edge-swipe back (iOS)** — swipe from the **left screen edge** to pop the current screen, with the underlying parent dragging in live and the gesture cancelable mid-swipe (release past threshold to commit). On by default for pushed screens (`interactivePopGestureRecognizer`). Trap: a full-bleed left-edge interactive element (a carousel, a drawing canvas) that swallows the swipe — you've disabled back without telling anyone; reserve a gutter or opt that screen out deliberately. For RTL locales the edge mirrors to the right (→ §19 RTL).
- **Predictive back (Android)** *(dated: opt-in Android 13/API 33; system cross-activity / back-to-home animations shipped Android 15/API 35; load-bearing)* — swiping back reveals a **live preview of the destination** (the home screen, or the previous screen) peeking behind the current one before you commit, so users see where back goes and can cancel. Requires opting in via `android:enableOnBackInvokedCallback="true"` in the manifest and migrating to the `OnBackInvokedCallback` / `OnBackPressedDispatcher` API; legacy `onBackPressed` overrides **block** the animation. Material 3 components (≥1.3.0) — bottom sheet, side sheet, search, navigation drawer — animate with it automatically. Trap: an un-migrated custom back handler, which on Android 15+ produces a janky no-preview snap and increasingly looks broken as the platform leans in.
- **Back-to-home vs. back-to-app (Android)** — predictive back distinguishes *in-app* back (preview the previous screen) from *back-at-the-root* (preview the launcher / the app that referred you). At a tab's root, M3 guidance is to let Back exit the app (after popping to the start destination) rather than cycling tabs. Trap: trapping the user in a back loop between tabs so Back never escapes — a common Compose `NavHost` misconfiguration.
- **Confirm-before-exit** — intercepting back/up to guard unsaved work. Legitimate, but on Android route it through `OnBackPressedDispatcher` (so predictive-back can show the "stay/leave" preview) rather than swallowing the event; on iOS use the sheet's `interactiveDismissDisabled` + a confirmation. Trap: a "press back again to exit" toast as a substitute for actually saving a draft.
- **Swipe-back vs. carousels/sliders** — left-edge horizontal gestures collide with horizontally-scrolling content. Resolve by giving the system gesture the screen-edge gutter and starting custom horizontal scroll inset from it. Related anti-pattern: **swipe-between-tabs** (`ViewPager`-style horizontal paging across top-level tabs) competes with edge-back and with in-content carousels — acceptable for a known finite set (Material tab rows) but fragile as primary nav.

## 3.5 Detail panes, modals & overlay navigation

- **Split view / master-detail (iOS, `UISplitViewController` / SwiftUI `NavigationSplitView`) / supporting pane (Android M3 list-detail)** — a two-(or three-)column layout: a list/primary column beside a detail column, collapsing to a single navigable stack on narrow widths. The default for wide screens (iPad, desktop, foldables open). M3 formalizes it as the *list-detail* and *supporting-pane* canonical layouts driven by window size class (`ListDetailPaneScaffold`). Trap: not collapsing gracefully to a push/pop stack on phone width — or losing the selected item when the window resizes across the breakpoint (preserve selection state across the fold).
- **Modal flow / full-screen takeover (sheet / modal)** — a self-contained, interrupting task layered over everything, demanding completion or explicit cancel (compose, onboarding, purchase). iOS *sheets* (the iOS 13+ card that partially covers and dims the parent) signal "temporary, swipe-down to dismiss"; *full-screen cover* (`fullScreenCover`) for serious multi-step flows. Android uses full-screen dialogs / dedicated modal destinations. **Modal vs. modeless**: a true modal blocks the parent and demands resolution; don't make routine, reversible actions modal. Trap: a modal with **no visible escape** (no Cancel/✕), or putting *navigation* in a modal — modals are for one focused task, not for browsing.
- **Bottom sheet (Android M3 / iOS sheet detents)** — a panel rising from the bottom edge. *Modal* bottom sheet = scrim + blocks interaction (a menu/picker); *standard/persistent* = coexists with content. iOS expresses the same idea with **sheet detents** (`.medium`/`.large` and custom fractions, `presentationDetents`). Increasingly used as lightweight **navigation** (a map app's pull-up place card → details). Trap: a bottom sheet so tall it's really a full screen — at that point push a screen; and a drag handle / its hit target below the ~44pt/48dp minimum, which fails touch-target sizing and is unreachable by switch control (→ §19).
- **Action sheet (iOS) / modal bottom sheet menu (Android)** — a contextual menu of choices anchored to the bottom (iOS action sheet) or a Material bottom-sheet list. For 2–6 contextual actions, including destructive ones (red, separated, and on iOS placed away from the cancel button). On iPad an iOS action sheet presents as a **popover** anchored to its trigger. Trap: using it for a yes/no that belongs in an alert, or for navigation that belongs in the nav structure.
- **Popover (iPadOS/macOS) / menu (Android) / context menu (iOS long-press)** — a small transient panel anchored to its trigger with a pointer, for secondary actions/options without leaving context. On iPhone, popovers *adapt down* to sheets automatically. iOS long-press **context menus** and Android long-press menus surface item-scoped actions inline. Trap: cramming a whole sub-flow into a popover; hiding a *primary* action behind a long-press with no visible affordance (discoverability + motor-accessibility failure → §19).
- **Alert / dialog** — a small, focused, **system-modal** interruption demanding a decision (destructive confirmation, error that blocks progress). iOS *alert* / Android M3 *dialog* (basic / full-screen). Two or three buttons max; the destructive option is visually distinct. Trap: alert fatigue — using a blocking dialog for anything a non-blocking snackbar/toast (→ §7) could carry, training users to dismiss without reading.

## 3.6 In-screen & secondary navigation

- **Segmented control (iOS) / tabs (Material primary/secondary tabs)** — an in-content switch between *views of the same screen* (e.g. "Following / For You"), distinct from top-level tabs. iOS `UISegmentedControl` / SwiftUI `Picker(.segmented)`; Material top tabs (fixed or scrollable). Trap: 5+ segments crammed edge-to-edge (use scrollable Material tabs), or confusing a segmented control with section navigation.
- **Search as navigation** — for broad or deep IA, search is often the fastest path. iOS surfaces it as a nav-bar `searchable` field or a dedicated Search tab; Android M3 has the **search bar → search view** expansion (predictive-back aware). Trap: search that only matches exact strings — users expect fuzzy/synonym matching, and an empty search tab should offer recents/suggestions, not a blank void.
- **Breadcrumbs & wayfinding (desktop/web; rare on phone)** — a path trail showing depth in a deep hierarchy. Standard on desktop/web file-and-settings trees; phones substitute the back chevron's parent title plus a clear screen title. Trap: porting web breadcrumbs onto a phone where there's no room — lean on titles and Up instead.
- **Command palette (desktop / pro web apps)** *(⌘K / Ctrl-K)* — a fuzzy-searchable launcher for any command or destination, bypassing the nav tree entirely. Power-user navigation on desktop-class apps (VS Code, Linear, Slack). Trap: making it the *only* path to a feature — it's an accelerator layered over discoverable nav, never a replacement.
- **Badges & nav signaling** — unread counts / dots on tabs and drawer items pull attention to a section. iOS `UITabBarItem.badgeValue`; Android M3 badges. Trap: a permanent badge that never clears (cry-wolf), or a badge as the *only* indication of state for color-blind/low-vision users — pair count with text (→ §19).

## 3.7 Deep linking, state restoration & continuity

- **Universal Links (iOS) / Android App Links** — **HTTPS** URLs that open directly into the right in-app screen with **no disambiguation prompt**, because the OS cryptographically verified domain↔app ownership: iOS via an `apple-app-site-association` file served from `/.well-known/` on the domain; Android via Digital Asset Links (`assetlinks.json`) + `android:autoVerify="true"` on the intent filter. The correct, secure way to deep-link. Trap: relying on these without a **web fallback** for users who don't have the app — a verified link that lands on nothing is worse than a website.
- **Custom URL scheme (`myapp://`)** — the legacy deep-link mechanism. Still useful intra-app/inter-app, but it triggers a confirmation prompt, **fails silently if the app isn't installed**, is hijackable by any app claiming the scheme, and is blocked in many in-app browsers and Chrome Custom Tabs. Trap: shipping `myapp://` as your *primary* marketing/share link — use verified HTTPS links and keep schemes for internal plumbing.
- **Synthetic back stack on deep-link entry** — landing deep via a link, the app must **build a plausible back stack** so Up/Back climb sensibly into the hierarchy (Android `NavDeepLinkBuilder` / `TaskStackBuilder`; iOS reconstruct the nav stack / set the `NavigationStack` path). Trap: a deep link that drops the user on a leaf with a Back that ejects them straight to the home screen, stranding them with no way up (the Up-vs-Back divergence in §3.3 is exactly this case).
- **State restoration** — after the OS kills a backgrounded app to reclaim memory, relaunch should **restore the prior screen, scroll position, and in-progress input**, not dump the user at the root. iOS *state restoration* / `UIScene` state + SwiftUI `@SceneStorage`; Android `onSaveInstanceState` + `SavedStateHandle` (and `rememberSaveable` in Compose). Trap: persisting only the top-level tab and losing the deep position and the half-typed form — the single most common "where did my place go" complaint.
- **Handoff / Continuity (Apple) & cross-device deep links** — resuming the same activity across the user's devices (iPhone → Mac → iPad) via `NSUserActivity`. A continuity layer over the same deep-link/state machinery. Useful for multi-device users; don't over-invest before the IA itself is sound.

## 3.8 Cross-platform & emerging surfaces

- **Web-app / PWA navigation** — the browser owns a **back button and URL bar you don't control**, so the URL *is* your navigation state: every meaningful screen needs a route (History API / framework router), and the browser Back must do the sane thing. PWAs added to the home screen run in `standalone` display mode and **lose the browser chrome** — then you must supply your own back affordance. Trap: an SPA where Back blows away the whole app or desyncs the URL from the view; and intercepting Back so the user can't escape. **Consistent Help (WCAG 2.2 SC 3.2.6)** wants help/support entry points in a consistent location across pages, and focus must move to newly-revealed views for screen-reader users (→ §19).
- **Desktop navigation (macOS / Windows Fluent)** — the **menu bar** (macOS top-screen / Windows in-window or NavigationView) is the exhaustive command surface; a **sidebar / navigation pane** carries primary nav; **tabs** are document tabs, not section nav. Keyboard navigation is first-class (⌘1/Ctrl+1 to switch sections, ⌘[ / Alt+← for back, full Tab focus order). Trap: porting a phone tab bar to desktop and ignoring the menu bar and keyboard model users expect; orphaning a command that exists *only* in a toolbar with no menu-bar equivalent.
- **watchOS / Wear OS navigation** — radically compressed. watchOS uses a **vertical scroll + page-based** model (the Digital Crown scrolls; swipe horizontally between pages), plus the grid/list app launcher and **Smart Stack** widgets; Wear OS (Compose for Wear) favors **swipe-to-dismiss** (left-edge right-swipe = back) and curved `ScalingLazyColumn` lists. No room for tab bars or drawers. Trap: porting phone IA down — a watch screen is one glanceable task, reachable in ≤2 interactions, and Wear's swipe-to-dismiss collides with any custom horizontal swipe.
- **visionOS / spatial navigation** *(dated: visionOS, 2024–2026; emerging, partly speculative)* — windows float in space; an **ornament** (a control strip attached just outside a window's edge) carries navigation; the **tab bar sits as a vertical ornament on the window's leading side** and expands on gaze/hover, while toolbars sit as bottom ornaments. Eyes target, pinch selects (gaze + pinch), so hit targets and spacing must be generous — Apple's guidance is a minimum ~60pt target with ample separation. Trap: assuming flat-screen tab metrics translate — gaze targeting needs larger, better-separated targets, and depth must not bury navigation behind content.
- **Floating / contextual navigation (emerging)** *(dated: 2025–2026; the iOS 26 floating bar is the load-bearing instance, "floating nav" generally is still trend-leaning)* — detached, capsule, blur-backed nav that hovers over content rather than docking to an edge (iOS 26 tab bar; many Compose/KMP apps cloning it). Looks premium and frees vertical space. Trap: the same contrast-over-blur fragility as glassmorphism, plus content occlusion under the floating element — budget safe-area insets and verify the bottom of every scroll view clears the capsule (→ §19).

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Tab bars](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/tab-bars); [Apple Developer — Elevating your iPad app with a tab bar and sidebar](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/uikit/elevating-your-ipad-app-with-a-tab-bar-and-sidebar); [Material 3 — Navigation rail guidelines](https://m3.material.io/components/navigation-rail/guidelines); [Android Developers — Predictive back gesture](https://developer.android.com/guide/navigation/custom-back/predictive-back-gesture); [Material 3 — List-detail / canonical layouts](https://m3.material.io/foundations/layout/canonical-layouts/list-detail); [WCAG 2.2](https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/).*
