Facts confirmed. The draft is accurate on all volatile specs. I have enough to finalize the revised section with gap-fills (added: dropped/loading layouts, scroll edge effects, multi-display/external display, watchOS/visionOS layout divergence, pointer/hover reflow, RTL mirroring as its own trap, keyboard hardware vs soft, list-detail back-stack accessibility veto, adaptive icons note). Here is the complete revised section.

# 4. Screen layout, adaptive & responsive design

Lay out one screen so it survives every device, window size, orientation, fold, and text-size setting it will ever face. Governing principle: **never ship one fixed canvas** — declare intent (insets, size classes, breakpoints, panes) and let the system reflow. The phone UI stretched to a tablet is the cardinal sin of app layout; large screens earn new structure, not bigger touch targets.

## 4.1 Safe areas, insets & edge-to-edge

The **safe area** is the region of a screen guaranteed clear of hardware cutouts and system chrome. Draw backgrounds edge-to-edge; keep interactive and legible content inside the safe area.

- **Safe area (iOS/iPadOS) / window insets (Android) / safe-area-inset (web)** — the OS-reported inset rectangle that excludes the status bar, notch/Dynamic Island, home indicator, and rounded corners. iOS exposes it via `safeAreaInsets` / SwiftUI `.safeAreaInset` and `.ignoresSafeArea`; Android via `WindowInsets` (status bars, navigation bars, display cutout, IME, system gestures — each a separate inset type under the unified `WindowInsetsCompat` model); web via `env(safe-area-inset-top/right/bottom/left)`. Trap: hardcoding a magic number (`20`, `44`, `34`) instead of reading the inset — it breaks on the next device geometry.
- **Status bar inset** — top inset for the clock/signal/battery strip. ~20pt classic iPhone, ~44–59pt on notch/Dynamic Island devices, 24dp typical Android. Never paint critical content under it without honoring the inset; do extend a background color/blur up through it.
- **Dynamic Island / notch (iOS) / display cutout (Android)** — the sensor housing punched into the top of the display. Dynamic Island reserves a pill the system animates around; it widens the top safe-area inset and can host Live Activities. Android cutout modes: `default` (letterbox in immersive), `shortEdges`, `never`. Trap: placing a tappable control or load-bearing text behind the island — it's occluded and untappable.
- **Home indicator (iOS)** — the bottom gesture bar owns a reserved ~34pt-tall zone (its own ~21pt pill plus padding) on Face ID devices. Nothing fixed sits in it; the system swipe wins. Pin bottom bars to the safe area, not the screen edge — but let scrollable content pass *under* the indicator for an edge-to-edge feel.
- **Gesture navigation insets (Android)** — gesture-nav devices reserve left/right edge strips for back-swipe and a bottom strip for home. Read `systemGestures` insets; don't put edge-swipe UI (a custom drawer pull, a swipe-to-delete row, a carousel) where it fights the OS back gesture, and provide a non-gesture fallback for the action. (→ §6 input, →  §19 accessibility)
- **Rounded-corner inset** — the safe area is rectangular but screens aren't; the corner radius eats your literal corners. Keep text/icons off the diagonal. visionOS and modern phones round aggressively — respect the inset, don't assume 90° corners.
- **Edge-to-edge (Android 15+) / full-bleed** — Android 15 (API 35) makes edge-to-edge **mandatory** for apps targeting it: the system no longer draws opaque status/nav bar backgrounds, your content draws under them (status bar + gesture nav transparent, 3-button nav translucent), and you apply insets yourself. Trap: targeting SDK 35 without consuming insets → toolbars hide behind the status bar and buttons sit under the nav bar. The most common Android 15 migration break; Android 16 (API 36) removes the `Window#setDecorFitsSystemWindows` opt-out entirely. (→ §19)
- **`viewport-fit=cover` (web/PWA)** — `<meta name="viewport" content="viewport-fit=cover">` is the *precondition* for `env(safe-area-inset-*)` to return non-zero. Without it, insets are `0` and a PWA's bottom bar slides under the home indicator. Pattern: `padding-bottom: max(16px, env(safe-area-inset-bottom))`.
- **Layout margins / content insets** — the default body inset from the safe-area edge. iOS uses ~16pt on phones, ~20pt on wider/regular-width contexts (directional layout margins, auto-mirrored in RTL); Material's margins scale by breakpoint (compact 16dp, growing through medium/expanded). Don't reinvent — inherit the system margin guide so content lines up with nav bars and toolbars. (→ §19 RTL)
- **Readable content guide (iOS)** — a guide narrower than the full layout margin that caps line length on wide screens so prose doesn't run to 200 characters. Use it for body text on iPad/Mac; use full-width layout margins for controls and lists.
- **Scroll edge effect / content-behind-bar (iOS) / app bar scrim (Material)** — bars (nav, tab, toolbar) sit *over* scrolling content with a blur/material; content scrolls beneath, not stopping at the bar's bottom. iOS 26 Liquid Glass and Material's top-app-bar elevation-on-scroll both assume this. Trap: a hard opaque bar with content that dead-ends at its edge reads as a stretched phone layout and wastes the edge-to-edge canvas.

## 4.2 Size classes & breakpoints

Two coordinate systems describe "how much room do I have": Apple's coarse **size classes** (per-axis, two values) and Material's **breakpoints / window size classes** (named width buckets). Both answer "compact phone vs. roomy tablet/desktop" without you measuring pixels.

- **Size class (iOS/iPadOS — horizontal/vertical)** — each axis is `.compact` or `.regular`. Read via SwiftUI `@Environment(\.horizontalSizeClass)` / UIKit `traitCollection`. Canonical map: iPhone portrait = compact width / regular height; most iPhones landscape = compact/compact; Plus/Max landscape = regular/compact; iPad full-screen = regular/regular both ways. The class is for the *window*, not the device — a Split View pane on iPad reports compact. Trap: branching on device model or `UIScreen.bounds` instead of size class → wrong layout in multitasking. (→ §4.3)
- **Window size class / breakpoint (Material 3 / Android — width)** — five opinionated buckets where layout should change:

  | Class | Width (dp) | Typical device | Panes |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Compact | 0–599 | phone portrait | single pane, bottom nav |
  | Medium | 600–839 | phone landscape, small tablet, folded inner | list-detail emerging, nav rail |
  | Expanded | 840–1199 | tablet, unfolded, desktop window | two panes, nav rail/drawer |
  | Large | 1200–1599 | desktop | two panes + persistent drawer |
  | Extra-large | 1600+ | large monitor | up to three panes |

  Height also has compact/medium/expanded buckets for unusually short/tall windows. Read via `currentWindowAdaptiveInfo()` / `WindowSizeClass`. Trap: measuring `Configuration.screenWidthDp` of the *device* — base it on the **window**, which shrinks in split-screen.
- **Breakpoint (web/PWA)** — CSS media/container queries on width. App PWAs mirror Material's ~600/840/1200 thresholds, but prefer **container queries** (`@container`) over viewport `@media` so a component adapts to *its* slot, not the page — essential for panel/dashboard layouts that reflow independently. (→ §4.5 list-detail)
- **Trap — orientation ≠ size class.** Don't key layout off `portrait`/`landscape`; key off available width. An iPhone in landscape is still compact-width; a foldable unfolds without rotating. Orientation is a weak proxy.

## 4.3 Multi-window, split-screen & desktop windowing

Your app is no longer alone on the display. The window can be any size, change live, and sit beside others — or beside *itself*.

- **Split View / multitasking (Android) / Split Screen** — two apps share the screen; each gets a narrowed window and (on iOS) a *compact* size class even on iPad. Android: any resizable activity, `android:resizeableActivity` (defaults true on API 24+). Trap: assuming full screen width → clipped UI the moment the user splits.
- **Slide Over (iPadOS)** — a floating phone-shaped panel over another app; reports compact width. Removed in iPadOS 26.0, reimagined and reinstated in **26.1** (single window per Slide Over); drag-to-Slide-Over gestures returned in 26.2. *(Volatile — 2025/26; iPadOS multitasking is mid-overhaul.)*
- **Stage Manager (iPadOS/macOS)** — overlapping resizable windows grouped into "stages." On iPadOS it freely resizes app windows, so size class flips mid-session. Design for *continuous* resize, not two fixed layouts.
- **iPadOS 26 windowing** *(dated 2025–26; load-bearing, not hype)* — iPadOS 26 replaced the old Split View/Slide Over model with free-floating, Mac-like windows: traffic-light controls, a pull-down menu bar, windows that remember position/size. Split View returned in **26.2**. Net: treat iPad as a true windowing platform now — support arbitrary window sizes and a menu bar, not just full-screen + thirds.
- **Multi-window (Android) / multiple scenes (iOS `UIScene`)** — the *same* app open in two windows (two documents side by side). State must be per-scene, not global singletons. Trap: a process-wide "current document" static → editing one window mutates the other. (→ §10 state)
- **Desktop windowing (macOS/Windows/Catalyst/web)** — resizable down to a minimum; users tile, snap (Windows Snap Layouts / macOS tiling), and maximize. Set a sane `minWidth/minHeight`; reflow multi-pane → single-pane as the window narrows, exactly like a responsive web app. Mac Catalyst and SwiftUI-on-Mac inherit size-class logic but expect real window chrome, menus, and pointer hover (→ §6 input).
- **Multi-display / external display** — apps can move between or span screens of different size, scale, and refresh. iOS `UIScreen`/`UIScene` per display, Android secondary-display presentation + Samsung DeX desktop mode, Stage Manager on an external monitor. Re-resolve size class and density on the destination screen, not the launch screen. Trap: caching the home screen's scale factor → blurry or mis-sized UI after the window moves to a 4K external.
- **Foldable postures (Android)** — Jetpack **WindowManager** `FoldingFeature` reports the hinge as a fold or a gap, its orientation, and whether it occludes/separates. Postures: **tabletop** (horizontal hinge, half-open — put media up top, controls below the crease), **book** (vertical hinge — two-page reading), **flat** (use the full inner display as one canvas). `WindowInfoTracker#supportedPostures` (WindowManager 1.4, 2025) tells you tabletop support ahead of time. Trap: centering a primary control *on* the hinge, where it's split or hidden. (→ §19)

## 4.4 Density, units & grids

Pixels lie across devices; logical units don't. Design in device-independent units and let the OS scale to physical pixels.

- **pt (iOS) / dp (Android) / px-as-CSS-px (web)** — the density-independent unit. 1pt and 1dp ≈ 1/160 inch baseline; the system multiplies by the device scale factor to get hardware pixels. Always size and space in these, never raw pixels.
- **@2x / @3x (iOS) / mdpi–xxxhdpi (Android) / DPR (web)** — asset scale buckets. iPhones are @2x or @3x; Android ladders mdpi(1×)→hdpi(1.5×)→xhdpi(2×)→xxhdpi(3×)→xxxhdpi(4×); web reads `devicePixelRatio` + `image-set()`/`srcset`. Ship vector (SF Symbols, vector drawables, SVG) where possible so there's no raster bucket to miss. Trap: a single @1x PNG → blurry on every modern screen.
- **Baseline grid / spacing scale** — apps standardize on an 8pt/8dp grid (4pt for fine adjustments); Material's spacing tokens and Apple's spacing both rest on multiples of 4/8. Keep one scale across the app so rhythm survives reflow.
- **Margins, gutters, columns** — large windows use a responsive column grid (Material: 4 columns compact, 8 medium, 12 expanded, with margin + gutter tokens scaling per breakpoint). Trap: a fixed 12-column grid on a compact phone → unusably thin columns. Let column count fall with width.
- **Adaptive / icon density (Android adaptive icons, iOS app-icon sizes)** — launcher and in-app icons ship per-density; adaptive icons (Android) supply separate fore/background layers the launcher masks. This is layout-adjacent: a single fixed-size icon raster is the icon equivalent of the @1x-PNG trap.

## 4.5 Large-screen & list-detail adaptation

Wide windows are an opportunity to show *more relationship*, not more whitespace or a centered phone column.

- **List-detail / supporting pane (Material `ListDetailPaneScaffold`) / `NavigationSplitView` (SwiftUI)** — the defining large-screen pattern. Compact: list pushes to a full-screen detail (single pane). Expanded: list and detail side by side, selection updates the detail in place. SwiftUI's `NavigationSplitView` (two- or three-column) and Material's adaptive `*PaneScaffold` do the reflow for you. Trap: hand-rolling it and forgetting the *back* semantics when it collapses to compact — the detail must become a pushed screen with a working back button and a sane back-stack, or the user is trapped (an accessibility + navigation failure). (→ §3 navigation, →  §19)
- **Navigation drawer (Android) / sidebar (iOS/macOS)** — large screens promote bottom nav / tab bar into a persistent side rail or sidebar. Material: bottom nav (compact) → navigation rail (medium) → drawer (expanded+). iOS: tab bar (compact) → sidebar (regular). Trap: keeping a bottom tab bar on a 13″ tablet — wastes the width and strands navigation at the far bottom. (→ §3)
- **Canonical layouts (Material)** — three blessed large-screen skeletons: **list-detail**, **supporting pane** (primary + contextual secondary), **feed** (resizable grid). Reach for one before inventing structure.
- **Don't stretch the phone UI** — the universal trap. A single phone column centered in a tablet with vast side gutters signals "we didn't adapt." Add a pane, a rail, or a multi-column grid; reflow content into the new space.

## 4.6 Dynamic Type, keyboard & content reflow

The window isn't the only thing that resizes — so does text, and the keyboard steals half the screen. Layout must absorb both.

- **Dynamic Type (iOS) / font scale (Android) / browser zoom + text-spacing (web)** — user-controlled text size. iOS has 12 steps: 7 standard (xSmall→xxxLarge, `large` default = 17pt body) plus 5 accessibility steps **AX1–AX5**, where body reaches ~53pt at AX5. Android scales by `fontScale` (Settings → Display → Font size, plus bold text). Use semantic text styles (`.body`, `.headline` / Material type scale / `rem`), never fixed point sizes. Trap: capping at the default — accessibility sizes are a real, common setting, not an edge case. (→ §19)
- **Dynamic Type reflow** — at AX sizes, horizontal layouts must restack vertically: a label-beside-value row becomes label-above-value; a horizontal button pair stacks. SwiftUI `ViewThatFits` / checking `dynamicTypeSize` picks the layout. Trap: truncating or clipping scaled text instead of letting it wrap and the container grow — fixed-height rows are the usual culprit. (→ §19)
- **WCAG reflow (web/PWA — SC 1.4.10)** — content must reflow to **320 CSS px** wide (equivalently 400% zoom) with no two-dimensional scrolling, except for genuinely 2-D content (data tables, maps). Single-column at narrow widths is the safest path. **WCAG 2.2** also adds **2.5.8 Target Size (min 24×24 CSS px)** — keep touch/click targets ≥24px (Apple/Material already advise 44pt/48dp). (→ §19)
- **Keyboard avoidance / scroll-into-view (soft keyboard)** — when the soft keyboard (IME) opens, the focused field must stay visible. iOS: keyboard layout guide / `.safeAreaInset(edge: .bottom)`; Android 15: the IME is just another `WindowInsets.ime` to consume (edge-to-edge makes this explicit), or `adjustResize`; web: `interactive-widget=resizes-content` in the viewport meta plus `scrollIntoView`. Trap: a fixed bottom "Submit" button hidden behind the keyboard, or the field scrolling under it — test with the keyboard *up*.
- **Hardware keyboard & pointer reflow** — iPad/desktop/foldable with an attached keyboard or trackpad: no IME inset to dodge, but you owe focus rings, key-equivalents, and hover states the phone layout lacks. Trap: assuming "keyboard" always means a screen-stealing soft IME; on these surfaces the keyboard is external and layout must instead grow affordances for pointer + focus. (→ §6 input)
- **Dynamic viewport units (web/PWA)** — mobile browser chrome (URL bar) grows/shrinks, so `100vh` overflows. Use **`dvh`** (dynamic, tracks chrome live), **`svh`** (smallest, chrome expanded — safe minimum), **`lvh`** (largest). Full-height app shells: `min-height: 100svh` for the safe floor, or `100dvh` to track live. Trap: `100vh` on iOS Safari → bottom bar clipped under the URL bar.
- **Orientation handling** — support both unless content genuinely demands one (a camera/level, a game). Locking portrait on a tablet is hostile; if you must lock, lock only the screens that need it. Re-layout on rotation via size class, and preserve scroll position and form state across the configuration change. Trap (Android): an unhandled rotation recreating the activity and losing state — use `ViewModel`/`rememberSaveable`, don't just block rotation. (→ §10 state, →  §19)
- **Watch & spatial layout divergence** — watchOS/Wear OS layouts are single-column, scroll-driven, and crown/rotary-paged; they use *screen-relative* sizing (round vs. square, `ContainerRelativeShape`, Wear's curved/`ScalingLazyColumn`) rather than size classes — never port a phone screen. visionOS lays out in points within windows/volumes at a system-fixed comfortable scale, with ornaments hung outside the window and depth/glass as first-class; reflow is about field-of-view and reach, not breakpoints. Trap: treating either as "a small/large phone screen." *(visionOS dated 2024–26; emerging, not yet load-bearing for most apps.)* (→ §19)

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Layout](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/layout); [Material 3 — Applying layout / window size classes](https://m3.material.io/foundations/layout/applying-layout/window-size-classes); [Android — Handle edge-to-edge enforcements in Android 15](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/layout/edge-to-edge); [MDN — env()](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/env); [W3C — Understanding WCAG 2.2 SC 1.4.10 Reflow](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Understanding/reflow.html); [MacStories — iPadOS 26.2 Split View / Slide Over](https://www.macstories.net/linked/ipados-26-2-beta-restores-drag-and-drop-gestures-for-split-view-and-slide-over/).*

# 5. Components — bars, navigation chrome & app frame

The persistent chrome: the surfaces that stay put while content scrolls beneath them. App navigation is organized by **axis** (top vs bottom vs side) and **role** (navigate between sections vs act on the current screen). The governing principle: chrome announces *where you are and where you can go*; it never competes with content for attention, and every platform names, places, and collapses it differently. Get the platform's vocabulary right or the app reads as a port, not a native citizen.

## 5.1 Top bars — title, identity, screen-level actions

**Navigation bar (iOS) / top app bar (Android, Material 3) / title bar + toolbar (macOS) / title bar + command bar (Windows, Fluent)** — the strip at the top owning the screen title, a back affordance, and a small set of screen-level actions. iOS calls it the *navigation bar* and pairs it 1:1 with a `NavigationStack` push; Android calls it the *top app bar* and decouples it from the back stack. Trap: cramming global navigation into it — the top bar is for *this screen*, not for moving between app sections (that's the tab bar / rail).

- **Large title (iOS) / large (collapsing) top app bar (Material 3)** — title renders oversized at rest and **collapses to the inline title as the user scrolls up**. iOS large titles are left-aligned at **34pt**, morphing to the standard **17pt** inline title on scroll; reserve them for top-level destinations (the root of a tab), never for pushed detail screens. Material 3 offers *medium* and *large* collapsing variants (`exitUntilCollapsedScrollBehavior`) that shrink the same way. Trap: a large title on a deep detail view wastes a third of the viewport and signals "root" when the user is actually three levels deep.

- **Small / center-aligned top app bar (Material 3)** — the non-collapsing default. `CenterAlignedTopAppBar` centers the title (closer to the iOS inline look); the plain `TopAppBar` left-aligns it. `pinnedScrollBehavior` keeps it fixed; `enterAlwaysScrollBehavior` hides it on scroll-down and returns it on any scroll-up. On scroll the surface lifts to a **3dp tonal elevation** — M3 signals "content has scrolled under me" with a tonal color shift, not a drop shadow.

- **Scroll-edge effect / scroll-edge appearance (iOS 26)** — under Liquid Glass the bar is transparent at the top of the scroll and **progressively frosts as content scrolls beneath it**, replacing the old hard hairline (`scrollEdgeAppearance` vs `standardAppearance` in UIKit; SwiftUI does it automatically). The bar floats over content rather than sitting on an opaque plate. Trap: low-contrast text or a custom-colored bar fill behind the glass — the system blur can drop contrast below **4.5:1** against scrolling content (→ §19); test against a busy scroll, not an empty one. *(2025–2026.)*

- **Leading / trailing bar button items (iOS) / navigation + action icons (Material 3)** — iOS: back/cancel on the **leading** edge, primary action (Done, Add, Edit) on the **trailing** edge. Material 3: a single navigation icon (hamburger or up-arrow) leading, up to **three** action icons trailing, with the rest collapsing into an overflow (⋮) menu. Trap: more than ~3 trailing actions on a phone bar — fingers can't hit them and labels vanish; overflow them. Every bar button must meet the **44×44pt (iOS) / 48×48dp (Material)** target even when the glyph looks smaller (→ §19).

- **Back button (iOS) / up button (Android)** — *not* the same. iOS **back** (chevron + previous screen's title) walks the navigation stack and is mirrored by the edge-swipe-back gesture; it's contextual to how you arrived. Android **up** (←) walks the app's *logical hierarchy* toward the parent and is distinct from the OS **back** gesture/button (which walks history). Trap: wiring Android "up" to pop history — it must go to the logical parent even when the user arrived from elsewhere. iOS drops the title text from the back button when space is tight, leaving a bare chevron.

- **Contextual / dynamic title (cross-platform)** — the title reflects the current screen, not the app name; on a detail screen it's the item's name, not "MyApp". Desktop adds a **breadcrumb** (path-style: Library ▸ Folder ▸ File) for deep hierarchies where a single title can't express location. Trap: a static app-name title on every screen — it wastes the one reliable "where am I" signal the OS gives you.

- **Title bar / window chrome (macOS / Windows)** — desktop merges title and toolbar into the window's top edge. macOS: unified toolbar with the **traffic-light** controls (close/minimize/zoom) leading, the window title (often hidden in favor of the toolbar), and toolbar items trailing. Windows (Fluent / WinUI 3): a customizable title bar where apps draw into the caption area, with **minimize/maximize/close** on the trailing edge. Trap: porting an iOS large-title bar straight to desktop — desktop users expect slim window chrome plus a menu bar (macOS) or command bar/ribbon, not a phone-sized title block.

## 5.2 Bottom & side navigation — moving between top-level sections

The app's spine: 3–5 mutually-exclusive top-level destinations. Bottom on phones (thumb reach), side on tablets/desktop (horizontal real estate). Each destination is a *persistent place* the user returns to — not an action.

**Component-signature map (top-level navigation):**

| Platform | Phone | Tablet / foldable | Desktop |
|---|---|---|---|
| iOS / iPadOS | Tab bar (bottom) | Tab bar → adaptive **sidebar** (iPadOS 18+) | Sidebar (`NavigationSplitView`) |
| Android (Material 3) | Navigation bar (bottom) | Navigation rail | Expanded navigation rail / nav drawer |
| macOS / Windows | — | — | Sidebar / `NavigationView` (Fluent) |
| Web / PWA | Bottom nav (custom) or top tabs | Sidebar | Sidebar / top nav |

- **Tab bar (iOS) / navigation bar (Android, Material 3) / bottom nav (web)** — the bottom strip of top-level destinations. **iOS: 5 tabs max visible** (a 6th+ collapses into a "More" tab with a list); icons + short labels, one selected. **Material 3: 3–5 destinations** — fewer than 3 should be a tab row instead, never more than 5. Trap: the bottom bar is for *navigation*, not actions — no "Compose", "Scan", or "+" living as a fake tab (that's a FAB's job on Android, a toolbar/nav-bar button on iOS).

- **Tab bar minimize-on-scroll (iOS 26)** — `tabBarMinimizeBehavior(.onScrollDown)` lets the floating Liquid-Glass tab bar **shrink to a pill as the user scrolls into a feed and return on reverse-scroll**, maximizing content. Load-bearing for feed-heavy apps, hype-prone elsewhere — don't minimize a 3-tab utility bar that never needed reclaiming. Trap: minimizing the only path to a critical destination with no reliable return (→ §5.6). *(2025–2026.)*

- **Tab bar accessory / bottom accessory (iOS 26)** — `tabViewBottomAccessory` places a persistent capsule **above** the tab bar that follows the whole app (canonical case: the Music now-playing mini-bar; auto-gets a Liquid-Glass background). When the tab bar minimizes, the accessory transitions from expanded to inline next to the minimized bar. Use for global, app-wide controls (now-playing, active call, recording) — *never* for screen-specific actions. *(2025–2026.)*

- **Search tab / search role (iOS 26)** — assigning `role: .search` to a tab pulls it into its own container, renders it as the magnifier glyph, and positions search consistently across iPhone/iPad/Mac (and adapts it into the sidebar on iPad). Prefer this over a hand-rolled search tab. *(2025–2026.)*

- **Label-vs-icon rules (Material 3)** — Material varies labels by destination count: **3 destinations** show icon + label on all; **4–5** always show the label on the *active* destination and recommend (don't require) labels on inactive ones. Trap: icon-only bars — unlabeled icons fail recognition and screen-reader clarity; Material and Apple both default labels *on*, and the label is the destination's **accessible name** (→ §19). The active item gets a pill-shaped **active indicator** (Material) behind its icon.

- **Badges (iOS / Android)** — a dot or count in a destination's **upper-right**. iOS uses a red pill (count or bare dot) on tab items and overflows long counts to "99+". Material 3 puts a small (dot) or large (numeric) badge on the nav icon; the large badge shows **up to 4 characters by default, overflowing to "999+"**. Use a bare dot for "something new, count unknown." Trap: a badge that never clears trains users to ignore the badge entirely.

- **Fixed vs scrollable** — *bottom* navigation is **always fixed** (3–5, never scroll). The scrollable variant belongs to the **tab row** (§5.4), not the bottom bar. Trap: a horizontally-scrollable bottom bar — it hides destinations off-screen and breaks the "I can see everywhere I can go" contract of a nav bar.

### Navigation rail (Material 3) / sidebar (iOS, macOS, Fluent)
The vertical equivalent of the bottom bar, for tablet/desktop widths. The **Material 3 navigation rail** is a slim vertical strip (icons + short labels) anchored to the leading edge, holding **3–7 destinations collapsed** plus an optional header/FAB; Material 3 Expressive (2025) adds a **collapsed↔expanded** toggle — the *expanded* rail is wider, carries full text labels and unlimited items, and is meant to **replace the navigation drawer** in many large-screen layouts. Apple's analog is the **sidebar** (`NavigationSplitView`); on **iPadOS 18+ a tab bar adaptively promotes itself into a sidebar** at wide sizes and collapses back to a bottom bar on narrow ones (`tabViewStyle(.sidebarAdaptable)`). Desktop equivalents: macOS source-list sidebar, Fluent `NavigationView` left pane. Reach for the rail/sidebar at Material's **medium/expanded window-size classes** (the rail is the default above ~600dp). Trap: showing *both* a bottom bar and a rail at the same width — pick one per breakpoint.

- **Navigation drawer (Android) / sidebar overlay (iOS/web)** — the slide-in panel of destinations, summoned by the hamburger (☰) or an edge swipe. *Modal* drawer (overlays content, scrim behind) on phones; *standard/permanent* drawer (pinned beside content) on wide screens. Material 3 Expressive de-emphasizes the drawer in favor of the expanded rail. Trap: the hamburger menu **hides** navigation behind a tap and measurably lowers discoverability/engagement vs. a visible bottom bar (NN/g) — use a drawer only for a *long* or *secondary* destination list, never your primary 3–5 (→ §5.2 tab bar).

## 5.3 Toolbars & contextual action bars — acting on the current screen

Distinct from navigation: these hold *verbs* (actions on the visible content), not *places*. They appear, change, and disappear with context.

- **Toolbar / bottom toolbar (iOS, macOS)** — a row of actions tied to the current screen. iOS can place a toolbar at the **bottom** (within thumb reach, e.g., Mail's reply/flag/trash row, `ToolbarItem(placement: .bottomBar)`) distinct from the top navigation bar; macOS puts the toolbar in the window's top chrome. Under iOS 26 toolbar buttons render as floating Liquid-Glass capsules and group into shared-glass clusters. Trap: duplicating the same action in both the top navigation bar and the bottom toolbar — pick the reachable one.

- **Floating toolbar (Material 3 Expressive, 2025)** — a detached, rounded, floating action container (horizontal or vertical) that hovers over content and can pair with a FAB; the Expressive successor to the bottom app bar for *contextual* actions. Use for a small set of high-frequency verbs on a content surface. Trap: a floating bar that occludes the last list row — pad the scroll by its height + safe-area inset (→ §5.6). *(2025–2026.)*

- **Bottom app bar (Material 3)** — a bottom container holding a few actions plus (optionally) a docked FAB and an overflow menu. Distinct from the *navigation bar* (destinations). Material 3 Expressive steers new apps toward floating toolbars, but the bottom app bar persists for action-heavy editing surfaces. Trap: confusing it with bottom navigation — actions, not destinations.

- **Contextual action bar / CAB (Android) / edit-mode toolbar (iOS)** — a **temporary** bar that *replaces* the top app bar when the user enters a selection/multi-select mode (long-press to select → bar shows count + bulk actions: delete, share, archive). iOS swaps the navigation bar's buttons in `Edit` mode and shows a selection-aware bottom toolbar. Trap: stranding the user in the contextual bar — always provide an explicit X/Done (and a clear close affordance for screen readers, → §19) to exit selection mode.

- **Command bar / ribbon (Windows, Fluent) / menu bar (macOS)** — desktop's action surface. Fluent **CommandBar** is a row of primary buttons plus a "…" overflow (`SecondaryCommands`); Office-style apps use the **ribbon** (tabbed command groups). macOS leans on the **global menu bar** at the top of the screen (every command discoverable + keyboard-shortcut-annotated) plus a window toolbar. Trap: hiding destructive or rarely-used commands *only* behind a toolbar icon on desktop — desktop users expect them in the menu bar with a shortcut and a searchable Help menu.

- **FAB (floating action button, Android/Material)** — the single most important action on a screen, floating bottom-trailing (a Material pattern; *not* iOS — iOS puts the primary action in the navigation bar or toolbar). Variants: standard, small, large, extended (icon + label). **FAB menu (Material 3 Expressive)** opens a small panel of related actions from the FAB, formally replacing the old stacked-mini-FAB pattern. Trap: a FAB overlapping the nav bar's content or covering the last list rows — reserve bottom padding; and give it a real accessible label, not just an icon (→ §19).

## 5.4 Tab rows, segmented controls & in-page switching

Switching *within* a screen (filtering one view), not between app sections. The give-away: the surrounding chrome (nav/tab bar) stays put while only the content region swaps.

- **Tab row / tabs (Material 3) — fixed vs scrollable** — a horizontal row of in-page views directly under the top app bar. **Fixed tabs** divide the width equally (good for 2–4 peers, e.g., "Following / For You"). **Scrollable tabs** start at the leading edge and scroll horizontally for many categories (news sections, store departments). On iOS the equivalent in-page switch is usually a **segmented control**, not a tab row. Trap: using a tab row for top-level app navigation — it lacks the persistence and reach of a bottom bar; tab rows are second-level.

- **Segmented control (iOS/macOS) / single-select (connected) button group (Material 3)** — a compact pill of 2–5 mutually-exclusive options swapping the view in place (Maps' "Explore / Driving / Transit"). iOS `UISegmentedControl` / SwiftUI `Picker(.segmented)` is the canonical form; Material 3 Expressive's **connected button group** is the analog. Use for a *small, fixed* set of equal alternatives. Each segment must meet **44×44pt (iOS) / 48×48dp (Material) / WCAG 2.2 SC 2.5.8 24×24 CSS-px** minimum target size (→ §19). Trap: more than ~5 segments or variable-width labels — segments get unreadably narrow; switch to a tab row or dropdown.

- **Segmented vs tab row vs dropdown** — segmented control = few, equal, always-visible options on one screen; tab row = more categories, scrollable, second-level navigation; dropdown/menu = many options or options that don't deserve persistent screen space. Pick by count and frequency.

## 5.5 Search surfaces

- **Search bar — in-bar vs dedicated vs scoped** — *in-bar*: search lives in the navigation/top bar (iOS large-title screens reveal a field on pull-down via `.searchable`; Material's **search bar → docked search view**). *Dedicated*: a whole search tab/screen (iOS 26 `role: .search`, §5.2). *Scoped*: search with scope chips/tokens narrowing the corpus ("All / Mail / Files"). Trap: a search field that doesn't say *what* it searches — scope the placeholder ("Search Mail") so the result set isn't a surprise.

- **Search suggestions / search view (Material 3)** — tapping the bar expands it into a full-screen **search view** showing recent queries, suggestions, and results-as-you-type; iOS shows suggestions inline beneath the field. Trap: results that only appear on submit when the platform convention is type-ahead — modern users expect incremental results.

- **Pull-to-reveal search (iOS)** — on large-title screens the field hides above the title and is **revealed by pulling the list down**, then pins inline on scroll. Discoverable only if there's content to pull against, and the field can land out of thumb reach at the top of a tall phone (→ §19). Trap: relying on pull-to-reveal as the *only* path to search on a screen a user might land on empty — give a visible entry point too.

## 5.6 Bar behavior, surfaces & the status bar

How bars *behave* across scroll, translucency, and the OS-owned edges — the polish that separates native from web-wrapped.

- **Translucent / blurred / Liquid-Glass bars** — bars sample and blur the content behind them (iOS Liquid Glass; Material's tonal-elevation surfaces; Fluent **Acrylic** for transient surfaces / **Mica** for long-lived window backgrounds on Windows). Conveys depth and lets content peek through. Trap: text-and-icon contrast over a blur is *fragile* — the backdrop can drop below 4.5:1; the system applies a vibrancy/tint scrim for a reason, so don't defeat it with a custom translucent fill, and honor **Reduce Transparency** (→ §19).

- **Collapsing / scroll-driven bars** — top bars shrink (large→inline), bottom bars minimize/hide on scroll to favor content and return on scroll-up. iOS: large-title collapse + `tabBarMinimizeBehavior`. Material: `enterAlways` / `exitUntilCollapsed` scroll behaviors. Trap: hiding a bar with no reliable way back — always restore on scroll-up or tap, and never hide the *only* path to a critical action.

- **Pinned / sticky bars** — the non-collapsing default (`pinnedScrollBehavior`, web `position: sticky`); the bar stays fixed and content scrolls under its (possibly translucent) surface. Use when constant access outweighs the reclaimed pixels — utilities, forms, anything action-dense.

- **Floating bars** — bars detached from the screen edge with a margin, rounded corners, and a shadow/glass material (iOS 26 floating tab bar; Material 3 Expressive floating toolbar). Modern, content-forward. Trap: a floating bottom bar over a scroll list occludes the last row — pad the scroll content by the bar's height **plus** the safe-area inset.

- **Status bar treatment** — the OS-owned strip (clock, battery, signal; the **notch / Dynamic Island** region on iPhone). You don't draw it, but you set its **content style** (light vs dark glyphs) to contrast with your bar, and you must respect the **safe-area insets** so your top bar doesn't slide under the island/notch or the home indicator. Android's analog: the **status bar** (top) + **gesture/navigation bar** (bottom), with **edge-to-edge drawing enforced by default for apps targeting SDK 35 on Android 15+** — apps draw behind the system bars and must inset their content. Trap: hardcoding a status-bar height or ignoring insets — content clips under the island on a notched phone and under the system bars on edge-to-edge Android.

- **Safe areas / insets (cross-platform)** — every bar's real estate is bounded by system-reserved regions: iOS **safe-area layout guides** (notch, island, home indicator), Android **WindowInsets** (status, navigation, IME/keyboard, display cutout), web **`env(safe-area-inset-*)`** (with `viewport-fit=cover`) for installed PWAs. Trap: a tap target placed in the home-indicator / gesture-nav zone — the OS swallows the touch; keep interactive chrome out of the inset.

- **PWA / installed-web-app chrome** — a web app has *no* native bars; you rebuild them in the DOM and declare the frame via the manifest **`display` / `display_override`** (`standalone` hides browser UI; **`window-controls-overlay`** on desktop lets you draw into the title-bar region, with a draggable `app-region: drag` zone and `titlebarAreaRect` env values; `tabbed` for multi-tab PWAs). There's no guaranteed system back gesture in iOS standalone mode, so an **in-app back affordance is mandatory**. Trap: assuming the browser's back button or swipe exists — installed standalone PWAs on iOS strip browser chrome, stranding users without an in-app back/home path.

- **Wearable bars (watchOS / Wear OS)** — the model inverts. watchOS shows the **time in the top-right** (system-owned, always visible), favors **vertical paging / Digital Crown scrolling** over persistent tab bars, and exposes a top `navigationBar` title plus a `.bottomBar` toolbar slot for one or two actions; deeper nav is a hierarchical list (`NavigationStack`) or `TabView(.verticalPage)`. Wear OS (Compose for Wear OS) uses **edge-hugging curved layouts** (`ScalingLazyColumn`, `TimeText` at top, `CurvedLayout` for arced labels). Trap: porting a 5-tab bottom bar to a watch — there's no room and no thumb-reach model; use vertical pages or a single-screen list.

- **Spatial chrome (visionOS)** — chrome detaches from the window into **ornaments**: bars (toolbars, tab bars) float just outside a window's bounds on a glass background, the tab bar hugging the window's **leading edge** and expanding on hover/gaze. There is no full-screen status bar to inset against; the window itself is the safe area. Trap: cramming a phone bottom bar inside a visionOS window — promote it to a leading ornament so it doesn't steal content depth. *(2025–2026.)*

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Tab bars](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/tab-bars); [Apple HIG — Navigation bars](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/navigation-bars); [Apple HIG — Toolbars](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/toolbars); [Material 3 — Navigation bar](https://m3.material.io/components/navigation-bar/guidelines); [Material 3 — Navigation rail](https://m3.material.io/components/navigation-rail/guidelines); [Material 3 — Toolbars](https://m3.material.io/components/toolbars/guidelines); [Android — Edge-to-edge (SDK 35 / Android 15)](https://developer.android.com/about/versions/15/behavior-changes-15#edge-to-edge); [MDN — Window Controls Overlay API](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Window_Controls_Overlay_API); [NN/g — Hamburger menus & hidden navigation](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/hamburger-menus/).*

I have the corrections I need. The draft conflated icon-button heights with text-button heights, and had FAB sizes slightly off. The XS/S/M/L/XL = 32/40/56/96/136 dp values come from icon buttons; standard M3 buttons have their own height ramp but the five-size system applies. Standard FAB is 56dp, small (mini) is 40dp, large is 96dp, with a medium 56dp. I'll correct the draft's "small (40dp), standard (56dp), large (96dp)" — that's actually correct. The draft's button-sizes claim of "five height presets" is right; I'll just keep it general rather than cite wrong dp. Now writing the revised section.

# 6. Components — actions & controls

The interactive surface: anything a user presses, flips, drags, or picks. Every control here is a contract between the user's intent and the system's response — get the affordance, state feedback, and target size right or the whole app feels broken. Governing principle: **use the platform's native control; a hand-rolled control that ignores OS conventions breaks muscle memory, accessibility, and Dynamic Type all at once.** Every control needs four visible states minimum — default, pressed, disabled, loading — plus focus for pointer/keyboard platforms (→ §19).

## 6.1 Buttons

The atomic action. Each platform ships an emphasis hierarchy; map your action's importance to the right tier rather than restyling everything to look "primary."

- **Filled button (Material 3) / `.borderedProminent` (iOS/SwiftUI) / Accent button (Fluent)** — solid fill, highest emphasis after the FAB. For the one terminal action that completes a flow: Save, Confirm, Join. Trap: more than one filled button per screen destroys the hierarchy — the eye can't pick the primary.
- **Filled tonal button (Material 3)** — secondary-container fill, a deliberate middle ground between filled and outlined. For a high-ish action that isn't *the* action (an onboarding "Next"). No direct iOS analog — iOS jumps from `.borderedProminent` to `.bordered`.
- **Outlined button (Material 3) / `.bordered` (iOS) / Standard button (Fluent)** — stroked, transparent fill, medium emphasis. Secondary actions that still need a clear hit area (Cancel next to a filled Save). Trap: outline-only borders can fail 3:1 non-text contrast (→ §19); the *stroke* must pass, not just the label.
- **Text button (Material 3) / Plain button / `.borderless` (iOS) / Subtle / Hyperlink button (Fluent)** — label only, no container. Lowest emphasis: dialog actions, "Learn more," dense toolbars. Trap: the tap target is the padded box, not the glyph-tight label — pad to ≥44pt (iOS) / 48dp (Android) even though the ink looks small (→ §6.7, §19).
- **Elevated button (Material 3)** — a tonal button with a shadow. Reach for it *only* when a button sits on a busy/patterned background and needs lift; otherwise "shadow creep" clutters the surface. M3 guidance prefers tonal in nearly every case — treat elevated as the exception.
- **Material 3 Expressive sizing (dated 2025)** — buttons now ship five height presets — **XS, S, M, L, XL** — and two shape families (round vs. square) that **morph on press** via spring physics: a radius "squish" on press-down, a round↔square swap on toggle/selection. The five-tier ramp mirrors icon buttons (XS 32dp → S 40dp → M 56dp → L 96dp → XL 136dp); the default container is a **pill** (fully rounded) at every size, no longer the M2 4dp-corner rectangle. New since Google I/O 2025; load-bearing for Android 16 / Material 3 Expressive apps, not yet universal. Trap: the shape-morph animation must respect `prefers-reduced-motion` / "Remove Animations" (→ §11, §19).
- **iOS 26 Liquid Glass buttons (`.glass`, `.glassProminent`) — dated 2025–2026** — iOS 26 / macOS Tahoe / iPadOS 26 introduce a refracting-glass material. `.glassProminent` is the new primary style (tinted, opaque-feeling, high weight); `.glass` is the translucent secondary. In toolbars these apply automatically, and a `.confirmationAction` button auto-adopts `.glassProminent`. Rule: exactly one tinted `.glassProminent` per context; everything else `.glass`, untinted. Trap: glass-over-content contrast is fragile — the system adds a vibrancy treatment, but custom tints can sink the label below 4.5:1; verify against your busiest background (→ §19). Hype-vs-load-bearing: load-bearing if you target iOS 26, cosmetic churn if you don't.
- **Split button (Material 3) / pull-down button with primary action (iOS)** — a primary action fused to a chevron that opens related variants (Send ▸ Schedule send). M3E ships it in elevated/filled/tonal/outlined styles with a morphing trailing half. Trap: make the two halves visually separable or users mis-tap the menu when they wanted the action.
- **Toggle button / button group selection (Material 3) / segmented selection** — a button that holds a selected state (bold/italic in a formatting bar). M3 Expressive gives these checked-state shape morphing inside a connected button group. Distinct from a true switch: a toggle button looks momentary but is stateful; don't use it where a labeled on/off switch is clearer.

State map every button must implement:

| State | iOS | Material 3 | Desktop (Fluent / macOS) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pressed | dim/scale + haptic (→ §11) | state-layer overlay + ripple | subtle fill darken |
| Disabled | ~30% opacity, no haptic | 38% content / 12% container opacity | greyed, removed from tab order |
| Loading | inline spinner, label hidden or kept | inline `CircularProgressIndicator`, label retained | spinner; freeze width to avoid reflow |
| Focused (pointer/kbd) | system focus ring (Catalyst/iPadOS w/ keyboard) | state-layer + focus indicator | 2px focus ring, in tab order |

Trap (all platforms): a disabled button with no explanation is a dead end — prefer enabled-with-validation-on-submit, or pair the disabled state with helper text saying why (→ §19; screen readers skip `disabled` controls entirely).

## 6.2 FAB & promoted actions

Android-native pattern; **iOS has no FAB** and Apple HIG steers away from floating round buttons — promote the action to a nav-bar/toolbar slot instead. watchOS, visionOS, and Wear OS likewise have no FAB.

- **FAB (Floating Action Button, Material 3)** — one circular, elevated button for the screen's single most common constructive action (compose, add). Sizes: **small 40dp, standard 56dp, large 96dp** (M3E adds a medium tier and tonal color roles). Trap: never more than one FAB per screen, and never use it for destructive or ambiguous actions — it's the loudest control in Material, and it floats over content (verify it doesn't occlude the last list row or a bottom CTA).
- **Extended FAB (Material 3)** — FAB with a text label beside the icon ("Compose"). Use when the icon alone is ambiguous or on a first/empty screen where the action needs teaching. Collapses to icon-only on scroll. Trap: the collapse animation can strand screen-reader focus — keep the `contentDescription` stable across states (→ §19).
- **FAB menu / speed dial (Material 3, dated 2025)** — tapping the FAB fans out related actions as labeled mini-buttons; M3E formalizes this as the **FAB Menu** component (replacing ad-hoc "speed dial" libraries). Caps at ~6 items before it should be a list/sheet. Trap: if you need a *menu* off your primary action you may have two primary actions — reconsider IA. The scrim must dismiss on outside-tap and the menu must be keyboard/switch-traversable (→ §19).
- **iOS / cross-platform equivalent** — promote to the navigation bar trailing slot, a bottom toolbar button, or a prominent `.glassProminent` button in content. On web-apps, a fixed bottom-right FAB is acceptable on touch but should never overlap the browser/OS home-indicator gesture zone.

## 6.3 Selection controls — switch, checkbox, radio, segmented

The shape-language divergence between iOS and Android is the single most-cloned mistake in cross-platform UI.

- **Switch / toggle (iOS `UISwitch` / SwiftUI `Toggle`) / Switch (Material 3)** — instant binary on/off, applies immediately (no Save). Shape divergence: iOS is a smooth pill, green when on, no glyph; the thumb is a plain circle. Material 3 is a larger pill with a **check/✕ icon inside the handle** and a thumb that **grows when on**. Trap: don't use a switch for an action that needs confirmation or has latency — switches imply an instant, optimistic effect; if the toggle can fail, reflect rollback. Label the *thing*, not the state ("Wi-Fi", not "Wi-Fi on").
- **Checkbox (Material 3 / desktop) / no native iOS checkbox** — multi-select, non-exclusive, deferred (changes apply on Save/submit). UIKit/SwiftUI have **no checkbox primitive** — iOS uses a switch, a checkmark accessory on a row (`UITableViewCell.accessoryType = .checkmark`), or a custom toggle. Material 3 checkbox supports an **indeterminate (mixed)** state for parent-of-partially-selected-children (`aria-checked="mixed"` on web). Trap: porting a checkbox grid straight to iOS feels alien — use checkmark rows or switches.
- **Radio button (Material 3 / desktop) / checkmark list (iOS)** — exactly one of N, mutually exclusive. iOS convention is a **checkmark next to the selected row** in a grouped list, not a radio dot. Trap: never fewer than 2 options (use a checkbox/switch) and never leave all radios unset unless "none" is a valid, labeled option. For >5 options, switch to a picker/menu. Radios must be a single arrow-key-navigable group for keyboard users (→ §19).
- **Segmented control (iOS `UISegmentedControl`) / segmented button (Material 3) / Pivot or SegmentedControl (Fluent)** — 2–5 mutually exclusive options shown inline, all visible at once (a flattened radio group). For switching views/filters where seeing every option matters. M3 segmented buttons support **multi-select**; iOS segmented is single-select only. Trap: breaks down past ~5 segments or with long labels — overflow to a menu/picker. Each segment still owes the 44/48 target even when crowded.

## 6.4 Numeric & range controls

- **Slider (iOS `Slider` / Material 3 / Fluent)** — continuous or stepped selection along a track; commit-on-release. iOS shows a thin track + circular thumb; M3 (Expressive) uses a **thicker track, an optional wavy "active" track, stop indicators** for discrete steps, and a value label on drag. Trap: sliders are imprecise — never the *only* way to set a value that needs exactness (pair with a numeric field). The thumb hit area must reach 44/48 even when the thumb glyph is smaller; sliders must be operable by arrow keys / VoiceOver swipe (→ §19).
- **Range slider (two-thumb) (Material 3 / web `<input>` pairs)** — min/max selection (price range, dates). **No native single-control iOS range slider** — compose two `UISlider`s or use a custom control. Trap: thumbs that can cross or occlude each other; enforce a minimum gap, keep both labels readable, and give each thumb an independent accessibility value.
- **Stepper (iOS `UIStepper` / SwiftUI `Stepper`) / not in Material core** — −/+ pair for small discrete integer changes (quantity 1–10). Material has **no canonical stepper**; Android uses a number field with affordances or a custom +/− segmented control. Trap: terrible for large ranges — nobody taps + forty times; switch to a field or slider past ~10–20. Each −/+ target must hit 44/48 (default `UIStepper` segments are borderline — pad them).
- **Numeric text field with spin buttons (Fluent `SpinButton` / web `<input type="number">`)** — desktop-native; the up/down arrows are tiny (sub-24px) and fail WCAG 2.2 SC 2.5.8 target size on touch — keyboard entry and scroll-wheel/stepper adjustment are the real input there. On mobile web, set `inputmode="numeric"` to summon the right keypad (→ §19).

## 6.5 Pickers & menus

Where iOS and Android diverge most in *naming*. Match the trigger gesture to the menu type — long-press vs. tap is a hard contract users rely on.

- **Context menu (long-press) (iOS `UIContextMenuInteraction` / Material 3) / right-click menu (desktop)** — secondary actions on the object under the finger/pointer; **long-press** on touch, **right-click / secondary-click** on desktop. iOS 13+ adds a blurred preview of the target. Trap: long-press is invisible — never hide a *primary* or only path to an action behind it (discoverability + motor-accessibility failure; provide a visible alternative, → §19).
- **Pull-down menu (iOS) / overflow "⋮" menu (Material 3) / dropdown menu** — a list of *commands* (no current selection), opened by a **single tap** on a button. Material's is the 3-dot overflow in the app bar; iOS pull-down attaches to any `UIButton` via `UIMenu`. Trap: don't confuse with a pop-up — a pull-down has no "selected" item, so don't show a checkmark.
- **Pop-up menu / pop-up button (iOS) / exposed dropdown menu (Material 3) / ComboBox (Fluent)** — mutually-exclusive *selection*; shows the current value, opens to pick a new one. The value-setting twin of the pull-down. Use for one-of-N where a segmented control has too many options. Trap: on a long option list a full picker or searchable list beats a giant pop-up; an editable ComboBox must expose `aria-expanded` + active-descendant (→ §19).
- **Action sheet (iOS `UIAlertController.actionSheet`) / modal bottom sheet menu (Material 3)** — modal list of choices anchored to the bottom (iPhone) or shown as a **popover anchored to its source** (iPad/Mac, required — an unanchored sheet on iPad is a bug). Reserve the **destructive** (red) and **cancel** slots correctly: destructive visually distinct, Cancel separated at the bottom. Trap: action sheets dim and block — don't use one for a quick 2-option toss-up where an inline menu suffices, and never bury or omit Cancel.
- **Picker / wheel picker / spinner (iOS `UIPickerView`) / Material time/number wheels** — the rotating-drum selector, quintessentially iOS, for bounded sets. iOS 14+ replaced multi-wheel date entry with compact tappable fields. Note the naming trap: **Android calls a dropdown a "Spinner"** — a different control entirely. Trap: wheel pickers are slow for long lists and poor for keyboard/switch users (→ §19); always offer a typed/field alternative.
- **Date / time picker as a control (iOS `UIDatePicker` compact / Material 3 date picker + docked/modal text-input modes)** — iOS 14+ default is a compact field that pops a calendar grid or inline time entry; Material 3 offers **calendar-grid *and* text-input** modes plus a **clock dial** for time. Trap: forcing wheel/dial entry when the user already knows the exact date — always provide keyboard text entry as an escape hatch. Localize field order (MDY/DMY/YMD), first-day-of-week, and 12/24-hour format; never hard-code (→ §19).

## 6.6 Row & inline actions

- **Swipe actions on rows (iOS `UISwipeActionsConfiguration` / Material "swipe to dismiss/reveal")** — leading/trailing swipe reveals contextual buttons (Archive, Delete, Flag). iOS supports a **full-swipe** shortcut that fires the first action; Material pairs swipe-to-dismiss with an **undo snackbar**. **Accessibility veto:** swipe is invisible and unreachable by VoiceOver/TalkBack/switch/motor users — every swipe action **must** have a non-swipe equivalent (long-press menu, edit mode, overflow), and iOS surfaces these via the rotor's Actions only if you wire `accessibilityCustomActions` (→ §19). Trap: never put a destructive full-swipe with no undo.
- **Icon button (iOS / Material 3 icon button / Fluent)** — glyph-only action in dense bars. M3 Expressive gives icon buttons the same five sizes (XS 32 → XL 136dp) + round/square + width presets (narrow/default/wide). Trap: ambiguous and unlabeled — always supply `accessibilityLabel` / `contentDescription`, and the *target* must still be 44/48 even when the icon is 24dp (→ §19).
- **Pressed / disabled / loading feedback** — every inline control owes the same four-state treatment as §6.1: a row action with no pressed state feels dead; a swipe button with no haptic confirmation (→ §11) feels unsure. Disabled rows should explain themselves, not just grey out.

## 6.7 Touch targets & feedback (cross-cutting)

- **Minimums** — Apple HIG: **44×44pt**. Material: **48×48dp** (with ≥8dp between targets). WCAG 2.2 SC 2.5.8 (Level AA): **24×24 CSS px** *or* 24px spacing — a floor, not a goal; the platform numbers are the real target. Use the larger when in doubt. The *visible* control may be smaller than the *hit* area — expand the touchable region with hit-slop/insets rather than enlarging the ink (→ §19 for full target-size rules).
- **Press feedback is mandatory and platform-shaped** — Material uses a **ripple** radiating from the touch point + a translucent **state layer** (hover/focus/pressed/dragged opacity tokens). iOS uses dim/scale + **haptics** (`UIImpactFeedbackGenerator` / SwiftUI `.sensoryFeedback`, → §11). Desktop uses hover + focus rings; web-apps must implement `:active`/`:focus-visible` themselves. A control with zero press feedback reads as broken regardless of platform.
- **Focus & keyboard** — every control must be reachable and operable by keyboard (desktop/web), Full Keyboard Access / Switch Control (iOS), and keyboard/TalkBack (Android). A visible focus indicator is required (WCAG 2.4.7 Focus Visible + 2.4.11 Focus Appearance). Trap: custom controls that capture the tap but expose no focus ring, no role, and no state (`aria-pressed`, `aria-checked`) — they're invisible to assistive tech (→ §19).
- **Loading without layout shift** — show progress *inside* the control (spinner replacing/beside the label) and **freeze its width**; a button that resizes when it spins shoves the layout and invites mis-taps. Disable re-entry while in-flight, and announce the busy state (`aria-busy` / accessibility-status update) so it isn't silent to screen readers (→ §19).

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Buttons](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/buttons); [Apple HIG — Menus and actions](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/menus-and-actions); [Apple — Adopting Liquid Glass (iOS 26 / macOS Tahoe)](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/technologyoverviews/adopting-liquid-glass); [Material 3 — All buttons](https://m3.material.io/components/all-buttons) & [FAB menu](https://m3.material.io/components/fab-menu); [Material 3 Expressive overview (Google I/O 2025)](https://m3.material.io/blog/building-with-m3-expressive); [Understanding WCAG 2.2 SC 2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/target-size-minimum.html).*

M3 docs are JS-rendered. The draft's figures (1dp elevated, 56/72/88dp list heights, 32dp chip with 48dp target, 44pt/48dp targets) match my training and are stable, well-documented Material values. I'll proceed and finalize. The draft is already strong; I'll fill gaps (split views/sidebars, sticky headers, section index on Android specifics, drag-to-reorder, context menus on containers, badges, separators/dividers, OutlineGroup, watchOS/visionOS container idioms, table sorting/selection desktop) and tighten platform halves.

# 7. Components — containers & content display

Where content lives once it's on screen. This section governs the surfaces that hold rows, cells, and media — the difference between a native-feeling app and a web page in a shell is almost entirely here. Governing principle: **the container is platform-owned; honor the OS idiom (inset-grouped on iOS, edge-to-edge lists on Android) before you invent a custom card.** Custom containers cost you free system behaviors — swipe actions, reordering, dynamic-type reflow, context menus, Liquid Glass / Material You theming — that users expect for free.

## 7.1 Cards & tiles

- **Card** — a bounded surface grouping related content + actions, separated from the background by elevation, fill, or outline. Material 3 ships three explicit variants (below); iOS has no formal "card" component — you build one from a rounded `RoundedRectangle`/`Material` background, and as of iOS 26 the system idiom is a Liquid Glass or grouped-background surface, not a hard drop shadow. Trap: porting Android's elevated-card shadow language to iOS reads as foreign — iOS separates with grouped backgrounds and hairlines, not shadows.

| Variant (M3) | Resting elevation | Separation | Reach for it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Elevated card** | 1dp (drag → 8dp) | Drop shadow + tonal surface overlay | Default when content must float above a busy surface |
| **Filled card** | 0dp (drag → 8dp) | Tonal fill only (`surfaceContainerHighest`) | Lowest emphasis; grouping within an already-elevated surface |
| **Outlined card** | 0dp (drag → 8dp) | 1dp `outlineVariant` stroke | Strongest boundary without shadow; dense grids, print-like layouts |

- **Tile** — a fixed-size, tappable content block in a grid; the Windows/Fluent term for what iOS calls a grid cell and Android a grid item. Live tiles (the Windows 8/10 self-updating tile) are dead — Fluent treats tiles as static. Don't confuse a tile (grid member, sized *by* its grid) with a card (free-floating container that sizes its own grid).
- **Widget-as-content** — a glanceable, OS-rendered surface (WidgetKit on iOS/iPadOS/macOS, App Widgets / Jetpack Glance on Android) embedded on the home screen, Lock Screen, StandBy, Today view, or watchOS Smart Stack. Built with a declarative, *non-interactive-except-for-buttons/toggles* subset (SwiftUI WidgetKit, Glance composables — not full Compose). Trap: widgets re-render on a system-throttled timeline budget, not on demand; never assume real-time data, and never put a primary task *only* in a widget. Widgets must carry accessibility labels the host reads (→ §19).
- **Elevation is semantic, not decorative** — M3 elevation levels (0 through +5; resting cards live at 0–+1, hover/drag push higher) encode z-order and state, not taste. iOS 26 Liquid Glass conveys depth through translucency, lensing, and specular highlights instead of opaque shadow. Trap: stacking shadows to "pop" a card inverts the platform's depth model and breaks dark-mode legibility.

## 7.2 Lists & list rows

The list is the workhorse container of mobile apps. Each platform owns a distinct visual grammar and a distinct underlying engine.

| Platform | Idiom / styles | Engine |
|---|---|---|
| iOS / iPadOS | Plain, Grouped, **Inset Grouped** (default for settings-style screens since iOS 13) | `UITableView`/`UICollectionView` (UIKit), `List` (SwiftUI) |
| Android (M3) | Edge-to-edge rows, one/two/three-line, optional dividers | `RecyclerView` (View system), `LazyColumn`/`LazyRow` (Compose) |
| macOS | Source lists (sidebar), plain tables | `NSTableView`, SwiftUI `List` |
| watchOS | Full-width rounded rows, often carousel-scaled | SwiftUI `List` |
| web/PWA | `<ul>`/`role="list"`, virtualized | DOM + windowing lib (`react-window`, CSS `content-visibility`) |

- **List row / list item / cell** — one record's worth of content in a vertical stack. M3 list items are one, two, or three lines: leading element + headline + supporting text + trailing element, with minimum heights of roughly 56dp (one-line) / 72dp (two-line) / 88dp (three-line). iOS rows are built from `UIListContentConfiguration` (UIKit) or arbitrary SwiftUI views inside `List`. Trap: a row's tap target must be ≥44×44pt (iOS HIG) / 48×48dp (Material) regardless of visual line height. WCAG 2.2 §2.5.8 sets a 24×24 CSS-px floor — platform-native guidance is stricter; meet the native number (→ §19).
- **Inset-grouped list (iOS) / card list (Android)** — rounded, inset sections floating on a grouped background. iOS makes this a first-class `List` style (`.insetGrouped`); Android has no native equivalent — you approximate it with cards in a `LazyColumn`. Trap: faking iOS inset-grouped on Android (or vice-versa) produces an uncanny hybrid that belongs to neither platform.
- **Leading accessory / trailing accessory** — fixed slots at row edges: leading holds avatars, icons, selection controls; trailing holds chevrons, switches, badges, value labels. The iOS disclosure indicator (trailing `>` chevron) signals push-navigation; a trailing switch signals in-place toggle — never mix the two meanings. Material's trailing supporting text + icon plays the same role. Trap: a chevron that doesn't push a new screen is a lie users have been trained to trust.
- **Separator / divider (inset rules)** — the hairline between rows. iOS insets the separator to align with the leading text (skipping the icon gutter) via `separatorInset` / SwiftUI `.listRowSeparator`; M3 makes dividers *optional* and discourages them between every row — group with whitespace instead. Trap: a full-bleed divider under every iOS row (ignoring the leading inset) instantly reads as non-native.
- **Swipe actions (iOS) / swipe-to-dismiss & swipe actions (Android)** — horizontal drag on a row reveals contextual buttons (Delete, Archive, Flag). iOS: `swipeActions(edge:)` in SwiftUI / `UISwipeActionsConfiguration`; leading vs trailing edges carry different verbs, and a *full* swipe triggers the first action (make destructive ones require the full swipe deliberately). Android/M3: `SwipeToDismissBox` slides the row to reveal a colored action background. Trap: swipe is an **invisible affordance** — never hide the *only* path to a critical action behind it; always provide a visible alternative (context menu, Edit mode) (→ §19).
- **Drag-to-reorder (edit mode)** — long-press or grab a reorder handle to re-sequence rows. iOS `onMove` inside `List` (handles appear in `EditMode`); Android Compose `ReorderableLazyColumn` (community/`Modifier`-driven) or `ItemTouchHelper` in `RecyclerView`. Trap: reorder must be operable without a drag gesture for switch/VoiceOver users — expose a "Move" action in the accessibility rotor / actions menu (→ §19).
- **Context menu (long-press / right-click)** — a press-and-hold (touch) or secondary-click (pointer) menu attached to a row or cell, often with a scaled preview. iOS/iPadOS `contextMenu` / `UIContextMenuConfiguration`; macOS/Windows right-click menu; Android has no first-class equivalent — it uses a long-press selection mode or an overflow (`⋮`) menu. Trap: context menus are discoverability-hostile on touch; mirror every critical action somewhere visible.
- **Pull-to-refresh** — drag-down past the top of a scroll container to reload. iOS `refreshable` / `UIRefreshControl`; Android `PullToRefreshBox` (Compose) / `SwipeRefreshLayout`. Trap: only meaningful at a list's top — don't attach it to content that paginates infinitely downward, and pair it with an accessible refresh control for switch/VoiceOver users.
- **Section / section header & footer** — a labeled group of rows. iOS grouped styles render the header as an uppercase caption and the footer as explanatory caption text — the canonical home for a setting's helper copy. Android M3 uses list subheaders. macOS source lists use collapsible section headers. Trap: don't put interactive controls in an iOS grouped footer — that slot is semantically descriptive text.
- **Sticky / floating section header** — header that pins to the top edge while its section scrolls beneath. iOS plain-style `List` and `UICollectionViewCompositionalLayout` (`pinToVisibleBounds`) pin headers; Compose `stickyHeader` in `LazyColumn`; CSS `position: sticky` on web. Trap: stacking multiple sticky headers (section + sub-section + toolbar) eats the viewport on small screens — pin one at a time.
- **Disclosure / expandable row (accordion)** — a row that expands in place to reveal nested content. iOS `DisclosureGroup` / `OutlineGroup` (for recursive trees inside `List`); Android M3 expandable items (chevron rotates 180°). Desktop tree views (`NSOutlineView`, Fluent TreeView) are the heavy-duty version. Trap: accordions hide content from search and from screen-reader linear traversal unless `aria-expanded` / the platform's expanded-state trait is wired (→ §19); never collapse content the user must compare side-by-side.

## 7.3 Collections, grids & tables

- **Collection view / lazy grid** — a scrollable 2-D arrangement of cells. iOS `UICollectionView` (with compositional layout — orthogonal scrolling sections, the engine behind App Store / Photos) and SwiftUI `LazyVGrid`/`LazyHGrid` (configured by `GridItem` arrays: `.fixed`, `.flexible`, `.adaptive`). Android `RecyclerView` with `GridLayoutManager`/`StaggeredGridLayoutManager`, or Compose `LazyVerticalGrid`/`LazyVerticalStaggeredGrid`. All are *lazy*: cells materialize on demand and recycle off-screen — the defining trait vs SwiftUI's eager `Grid` or a plain `HStack`. Trap: an eager `Grid`/`VStack`-in-`ScrollView` for hundreds of items janks the main thread; reach for the lazy/recycling container.
- **Staggered grid (Pinterest layout)** — variable-height cells packed by shortest-column. Native in `StaggeredGridLayoutManager` (Android) and `LazyVerticalStaggeredGrid` (Compose); on iOS you build it with a custom `UICollectionViewCompositionalLayout` (no stock variant). Trap: staggered layouts wreck a predictable reading order for VoiceOver/TalkBack unless you set explicit accessibility ordering (→ §19).
- **Data table** — rows × typed columns with headers, sort, multi-select, and (desktop) resizable/reorderable columns. A *desktop/web* idiom: `NSTableView`, Fluent DataGrid, web `<table>`. **Tables don't survive phone width.** The canonical mobile move is to **reflow each row into a card** (label-value stacks) below a breakpoint, or expose a horizontally scrolling table only for genuinely tabular data the user came to scan. Trap: a fixed multi-column table on a 390pt-wide phone forces pinch-zoom and fails WCAG 2.2 §1.4.10 Reflow (content must reflow to a 320 CSS-px-equivalent width without 2-D scrolling) — reflow to cards or let columns scroll, don't shrink text (→ §19).
- **Sortable column header** — click/tap a header to sort; the sort glyph (▲/▼) + `aria-sort` announce direction. Desktop/web own this; on mobile a sort affordance usually moves to a toolbar menu or segmented control, not a header tap. Trap: a sort arrow with no `aria-sort` (or platform sort-state trait) leaves screen-reader users unable to tell the current order (→ §19).
- **Section index / scrubber** — the A–Z index rail (iOS Contacts `sectionIndexTitles`) or fast-scroll thumb (Android `RecyclerView` `FastScroller`) for jumping within a long sorted collection. Reach for it past ~100 alphabetically-ordered rows. Trap: the index rail's hit slices are tiny — provide it as an *enhancement* over scrolling, never the only navigation, and label it for assistive tech (→ §19).
- **Density modes** — comfortable vs compact row spacing. Desktop-first (Fluent, web data apps) where information density is prized; mobile largely fixes density to preserve touch targets. macOS `controlSize` and SwiftUI `.environment(\.defaultMinListRowHeight)` tune it; Fluent ships explicit comfortable/compact DataGrid modes. Trap: a "compact" mode that shrinks targets below 44pt/48dp trades density for an accessibility failure (→ §19).

## 7.4 Carousels & paged content

- **Carousel / paged scroll view** — a horizontally scrolling, often snapping, set of items. iOS: `TabView(.page)` style or `ScrollView(.horizontal)` with `.scrollTargetBehavior(.paging)` (iOS 17+); M3 ships a dedicated **Carousel** component (multi-browse, uncontained, hero, and full-screen layouts) whose items grow/shrink as they reach the focal edge. Android Compose uses `HorizontalPager`. Trap: carousels show notoriously low engagement past the first item and are an accessibility minefield — auto-advancing ones violate WCAG 2.2 §2.2.2 Pause/Stop/Hide; never auto-rotate, and always expose a paging control + page count to assistive tech (→ §19).
- **Page indicator / page dots** — the row of dots showing position in a pager. iOS `UIPageControl` / `.tabViewStyle(.page(indexDisplayMode:))`; Android pager indicators (Compose accompanist/`PagerState`). Trap: dots alone don't announce position to screen readers and are unreadable past ~8 pages — pair with an accessible "page N of M" and switch patterns for many pages (→ §19).
- **Snap / paging behavior** — whether the scroll rests on item boundaries (full-page paging) or free-scrolls with snap points (peeking the next item). Peeking the next item is the single most effective signal that more content scrolls horizontally. Trap: a non-snapping horizontal scroll with no peek looks like a dead end.

## 7.5 Split views, sidebars & panes

- **Split view / multi-column layout** — a parent list driving a detail pane, the iPad/desktop answer to a phone's push-navigation stack. iOS/iPadOS `UISplitViewController` / SwiftUI `NavigationSplitView` (two- or three-column); macOS uses the same `NavigationSplitView`; Android uses adaptive panes (`ListDetailPaneScaffold` in Material3-adaptive / sliding-pane layout). Trap: a split view must *collapse* to a single navigation stack at compact width — a fixed two-pane layout on a phone is unusable.
- **Sidebar (iOS/iPadOS/macOS) / navigation drawer (Android)** — the persistent source-list column of top-level destinations. iOS/macOS `List` with `.listStyle(.sidebar)` (collapsible sections, selection-bound); Android M3 `ModalNavigationDrawer` / `PermanentNavigationDrawer`. Trap: don't conflate a *sidebar* (content-navigation container, persistent on wide screens) with a *navigation drawer* slide-over (often a phone-only overflow) — they imply different reachability (→ §6 navigation).
- **Inspector / utility pane** — a trailing pane for properties/metadata about the current selection. macOS/iPadOS `inspector` modifier (formalized in SwiftUI on recent OS versions); Fluent uses a right-hand details pane. Trap: an inspector that floats *over* content on compact width should be a sheet (→ §8), not a squeezed third column.

## 7.6 Chips & inline content blocks

- **Chip** — a compact, pill-shaped element representing an input, attribute, action, or filter. A **Material-owned** component with four canonical types; iOS has no formal "chip" — it uses tags, tokens (the address bubbles in Mail), or capsule buttons. M3 chip height is ~32dp visually, but the **touch target is extended to 48dp** (via the component's minimum-touch-target inset) — honor that on any custom chip (→ §19).

| M3 chip type | Role | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Assist** | Triggers a smart/contextual action | "Add to calendar" surfaced from content |
| **Filter** | Toggles a filter on/off (selectable, shows check) | "In stock", "Under $50" |
| **Input** | Represents user-entered content, removable | Recipient token, applied tag |
| **Suggestion** | Offers a predicted query/response | Smart-reply suggestions |

- **Filter chip vs segmented control** — chips are multi-select and wrap to multiple rows; a segmented control (iOS `UISegmentedControl` / SwiftUI `Picker(.segmented)`, M3 segmented buttons) is single-select within a fixed bar. Trap: don't use a segmented control for >5 options or for multi-select — that's a chip group's job.
- **Badge** — a small count or dot overlaid on an icon/row (unread count, status). iOS `.badge()` on `List` rows and tab items / `UITabBarItem.badgeValue`; Android M3 `Badge`/`BadgedBox`; web a positioned pill. Trap: a badge that conveys state by color alone (red dot = error) fails non-color-perceivers — pair with a count, glyph, or label (→ §19).
- **Banner-in-content / inline banner** — a persistent, non-modal message embedded in the content flow (offline notice, plan-limit warning, undo bar). M3 defines a Banner; iOS uses inline rows or a top callout; Fluent has InfoBar/MessageBar. Distinct from a toast/snackbar (transient, → §8): a banner stays until dismissed or resolved. Trap: a banner that needs an action but auto-dismisses is the wrong component — banners persist, toasts don't.
- **Empty-state block** — the placeholder shown when a container has no content: icon + headline + one-line explanation + primary action. iOS `ContentUnavailableView` (iOS 17+) standardizes this — including a built-in `.search` variant for no-results; Android/web roll their own. Trap: a blank list with no empty state reads as a bug — always design the zero-data case and make its CTA the obvious next step.

## 7.7 Media & aspect handling

- **Image aspect modes (fill vs fit)** — `fill`/`aspectFill` (`.scaledToFill`, Android `centerCrop`) covers the frame and crops overflow; `fit`/`aspectFit` (`.scaledToFit`, `fitCenter`) shows the whole image and letterboxes. Use fill for thumbnails/heroes where cropping is acceptable, fit when the whole image matters (logos, diagrams, user uploads you can't safely crop). Trap: fill-cropping a user's photo can decapitate faces — use saliency/face-aware cropping or fit.
- **Aspect-ratio reservation** — declare the cell/image aspect ratio *before* the asset loads so the layout doesn't reflow on arrival. SwiftUI `.aspectRatio`, Compose `Modifier.aspectRatio`, web `aspect-ratio` / explicit `width`+`height` attrs. Trap: omitting reserved dimensions causes content jump (the web's CLS; the same jank exists natively) as images pop in mid-scroll.
- **Async/lazy image loading** — load off-thread with a placeholder, then fade in. SwiftUI `AsyncImage`, Compose Coil `AsyncImage`, web `loading="lazy"` + `next/image`. Always provide a placeholder + failure state, and a meaningful `alt`/accessibility label or an explicit decorative flag (→ §19). Trap: synchronous decode on the main thread during scroll is the classic source of dropped frames in collection views.
- **Thumbnail grid (Photos idiom)** — dense, square, edge-to-edge image grid with minimal gutters — the densest legitimate use of a collection view. Built on `UICollectionViewCompositionalLayout` (iOS) / `LazyVerticalGrid` (Compose). Trap: square-cropping non-square source images for the grid still requires the un-cropped original on tap — store both, crop on display.

*Sources: [Material 3 — Cards](https://m3.material.io/components/cards/overview); [Material 3 — Chips](https://m3.material.io/components/chips/overview); [Material 3 — Lists](https://m3.material.io/components/lists/overview); [Apple HIG — Lists and tables](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/lists-and-tables); [Apple HIG — Materials (Liquid Glass)](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/materials); [Apple Developer — NavigationSplitView](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/swiftui/navigationsplitview); [WCAG 2.2 — Reflow (1.4.10) & Target Size (2.5.8)](https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/).*
