All facts confirmed. One correction to incorporate: TipKit was introduced at WWDC 2023 / iOS 17 (my draft says "iOS 17+" — correct), and the morph transition uses `matchedTransitionSource()` + `.navigationTransition(.zoom)`, not simply declaring a detent. I'll refine the iOS 26 sheet entry accordingly. Here is the complete revised section.

# 8. Components — overlays & transient surfaces

Everything that floats above the screen, classified by three axes: **how it enters** (morph from source, slide up, fade in), **how much it blocks** (modal/scrim vs. non-modal/passthrough), and **how it leaves** (tap-outside, swipe, timeout, explicit action). Governing principle: the more a surface blocks, the more deliberate its dismissal must be — and the more it interrupts, the louder its job had better be. Get the blocking-vs-dismissal pairing wrong and you either trap the user or lose their data.

## 8.1 Sheets — modal & non-modal containers

The dominant mobile overlay since ~2018. A container that rises from an edge (usually the bottom) holding a self-contained task. iOS, Android, and the web have converged on the form but diverge sharply on detents, dismissal, and material.

- **Sheet / page sheet (iOS) / modal bottom sheet (Android)** — a card that covers part of the screen with the parent visible (dimmed) behind it. iOS `pageSheet` is the default modal presentation since iOS 13: rounded top corners, parent shrinks back in a stack, swipe-down to dismiss. As of **iOS 26 (Sept 2025)** sheets render in **Liquid Glass**; at a partial detent they float over content, and at `.large` the background turns opaque and attaches to the screen edges. A sheet can **morph out of the toolbar button that presented it** — but that's opt-in, not automatic: tag the source with `matchedTransitionSource()` and apply `.navigationTransition(.zoom(sourceID:in:))` to the sheet (requires a `NavigationStack`/`NavigationSplitView`). Android's equivalent is Material 3 `ModalBottomSheet`, capped at 50% screen height initially and expandable to full with internal scroll. Trap: iOS page sheets are interactively dismissible by default — a half-finished form swipes away and loses data unless you set `interactiveDismissDisabled()` / `isModalInPresentation` and handle the dismiss-attempt with a confirm (→ §8.7).
- **Form sheet (iOS)** — a centered, narrower modal card on iPad/large widths (vs. the edge-anchored page sheet). Same API family (`UIModalPresentationStyle.formSheet`), used for short focused input. On compact iPhone widths it falls back to a full-height sheet. Trap: don't use it for long scrolling content — it caps width and wastes the regular-width canvas.
- **Full-screen cover (iOS) / full-screen dialog (Android)** — opaque, edge-to-edge, no parent visible. iOS `fullScreenCover` / `.fullScreen`; Material 3 **full-screen dialog**. Reach for it when the task is a multi-step flow that owns the user's full attention (compose, onboarding, media editor). It does **not** swipe-to-dismiss — it needs an explicit Cancel/Done in the nav bar (iOS) or a top-app-bar close + confirm-on-discard (Android). Trap: using a full-screen cover for a one-field task; the heaviness signals "big commitment" the content doesn't earn.
- **Detents / snap points (iOS) / preset heights (Android)** — the discrete heights a sheet rests at. iOS `presentationDetents([.medium, .large])` plus `.fraction`/`.height`/`.custom`; `.large` is full-height, `.medium` ≈ half. Material 3 sheets cycle preset heights via the drag handle and must also be able to **close completely** without a drag. Use a partial detent as the default so the user keeps context (map, list) behind the sheet. Trap: a single `.large`-only detent throws away the sheet's whole advantage — it's just a full-screen modal that happens to swipe.
- **Drag handle / grabber (Android & iOS)** — the short centered pill at the top edge signaling "this drags." Material 3 makes it the canonical resize/dismiss control: selecting it should **toggle through preset heights or close**, and selecting the **scrim should always close**. iOS shows a grabber via `presentationDragIndicator(.visible)`. Accessibility veto: the handle must be a real focusable, labeled control with an action (not decorative) or VoiceOver/TalkBack users can't resize or dismiss (→ §19). Trap: a purely visual handle with no accessibility action.
- **Standard / persistent bottom sheet (Android)** — non-modal: **co-exists** with the main UI, no scrim, both regions interactive at once (Maps place card, Now Playing). Built in Compose with `BottomSheetScaffold`; it rests at a peek height and expands on drag. iOS has no first-class equivalent — you build it with a non-dimming sheet (`presentationBackgroundInteraction(.enabled)`) or a custom container. Reach for it to keep secondary content pinned while the user pans/scrolls the main region. Trap: adding a scrim makes it modal by accident and kills the "both at once" affordance.
- **Background interaction / passthrough** — whether taps reach content behind the sheet. iOS `presentationBackgroundInteraction(.enabled(upThrough:))` lets the parent stay live below a given detent (the pattern behind Apple Maps-style sheets). Material standard sheets are passthrough by definition; modal ones aren't. Decide this deliberately: passthrough = "companion panel," blocking = "do this first."

| Surface | iOS | Android (M3) | Blocks? | Dismiss |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Partial card, parent visible | Page sheet (`.pageSheet`) | Modal bottom sheet | Yes (scrim) | Swipe-down, scrim tap |
| Companion panel, both live | (custom / bg-interaction) | Standard bottom sheet | No | Drag to peek/collapse |
| Centered input card (regular width) | Form sheet | — (use basic dialog) | Yes | Cancel/Done |
| Edge-to-edge flow | Full-screen cover | Full-screen dialog | Yes (opaque) | Explicit Cancel/Done |

## 8.2 Dialogs, alerts & confirmations

The smallest, most blocking overlay: a centered modal that demands a decision before anything else proceeds. Reserve for genuinely interruptive moments — overuse trains users to dismiss reflexively.

- **Alert (iOS) / basic dialog (Android)** — centered, scrim-backed, 1–3 buttons, short title + optional body. iOS `UIAlertController(.alert)` / SwiftUI `.alert`; Material 3 **basic dialog**. iOS stacks buttons vertically past two; Material lays text buttons out at the bottom-end (confirming action right-most). macOS/Windows render an OS-styled alert/dialog with a default (highlighted) button bound to Return and Cancel bound to Esc. Reach for it only for must-answer-now decisions or errors that block progress. Trap: cramming a form into an alert — iOS alerts officially support only single-line text fields; anything richer belongs in a sheet.
- **Action sheet (iOS) / bottom sheet menu (Android)** — a list of contextual actions tied to a specific selection, with a destructive option styled red and Cancel separated. Pre-iOS 26 it slid from the bottom on iPhone and popped as a popover on iPad. **As of iOS 26 it anchors over the originating view on both** — set `sourceItem`/`sourceView`. Android uses a modal bottom sheet with a list of items for the same job; macOS/Windows surface the equivalent as a context menu or popover, not a bottom-anchored sheet. Trap: forgetting to set the source on iPad/iOS 26 — the action sheet then has no anchor and the morph/positioning breaks.
- **Destructive-action confirmation** — the second-step "Are you sure?" before an irreversible action. iOS uses `role: .destructive` (red label) and a `.cancel` role; Material 3 **confirmation dialogs omit the close (X) icon** so the only exits are the explicit affirmative/dismissive buttons. Rules: name the verb on the button ("Delete 3 photos," not "OK"), make the safe choice the default focus, and place the destructive action where habit won't auto-trigger it. Trap: destructive action as the right-most/default button where users tap-through; trap²: relying on a confirm dialog instead of an **undo** — a Snackbar with Undo is less interruptive and usually safer for reversible deletes (→ §8.5).
- **Full-screen dialog (Android)** — Material's heavyweight: fills the screen for a multi-field task (new calendar event), with a top app bar carrying Cancel/Save rather than bottom buttons. The Android analogue to iOS full-screen cover but explicitly a "dialog" in M3 taxonomy. Trap: using it where a basic dialog suffices.
- **Accessibility of dialogs** — a true modal must trap focus, expose `role="dialog"` + `aria-modal="true"` (web), move focus in on open and **restore it to the trigger on close**, and dismiss on **Esc** (desktop/web) or the platform back gesture (Android). Native `UIAlertController` / Material dialogs do this for you; hand-rolled web modals routinely don't (→ §19). The web platform's `<dialog>` element + `showModal()` gives focus-trap, Esc-close, `inert` backdrop, and top-layer rendering for free (Baseline since 2022) — prefer it over a div-with-overlay.

## 8.3 Popovers & inline contextual surfaces

Pointer-anchored overlays for larger screens. The defining trait vs. a sheet: a popover has a **beak/arrow** pointing at the element that spawned it and is positioned relative to that element, not the screen edge.

- **Popover (iPad / macOS / iPadOS)** — a floating panel with an arrow anchored to a control, holding secondary controls or info. iOS `UIPopoverPresentationController` / SwiftUI `.popover`; on **compact iPhone widths a popover auto-adapts to a sheet** unless you force `.popover` adaptation — design for both. macOS `NSPopover` (transient vs. semi-transient vs. detached behaviors). Reach for it on regular-width canvases to keep the user in place. Trap: assuming the popover form survives onto iPhone — it collapses to a sheet and your arrow-anchored mental model breaks.
- **Flyout / teaching tip (Windows / Fluent)** — Fluent's `Flyout` is the popover analogue (lightweight, light-dismiss on outside-tap); `TeachingTip` is a popover specialized for feature discovery with an optional tail pointing at a target (→ §8.6). Use Flyout for "more options on this control," TeachingTip for "here's a new thing."
- **Popover (web — `popover` attribute / Popover API)** — the platform primitive for non-modal overlays: `popover="auto"` gives light-dismiss + top-layer rendering + Esc-close for free, declaratively wired via `popovertarget` (Baseline 2024 — Chrome/Edge 114, Firefox 125, Safari 17). Unlike `<dialog showModal()>`, an auto popover does **not** make the page inert and allows only one open per ancestor chain. Reach for it for menus, tooltips, and disclosure panels; reach for `<dialog>` when you need a blocking modal. Trap: hand-rolling a `z-index` overlay when the platform now escapes clipping and manages the top layer for you (→ §8.7).
- **Menu vs. popover** — a menu is a list of commands; a popover is an arbitrary container that may hold controls, a mini-form, or rich content. When the payload is just "pick one action," use a menu (→ §6), not a popover. Trap: building a menu out of a popover full of buttons — you lose the platform's free keyboarding, type-ahead, and dismissal.
- **Context menu with preview (iOS) / long-press menu (Android)** — long-press surfaces a menu **plus a lifted, enlarged preview** of the target (iOS `UIContextMenuConfiguration` with `previewProvider`; the Liquid Glass-era version lifts the item onto a blurred backdrop). Android's long-press context menu is list-only (no preview by default); desktop right-click context menus are command lists with no preview. Use the preview to confirm "this is the item I'm acting on" for cards, photos, messages. Accessibility veto: long-press has no keyboard/switch equivalent on its own — every context-menu action must also be reachable via a visible affordance or the rotor/accessibility menu (→ §19).
- **HUD (heads-up display) / transient overlay** — a brief, centered, non-interactive status splash (the iOS volume/AirPods pill, a checkmark "Saved" puck). No buttons, auto-dismisses, doesn't block. Reach for it to confirm an in-place state change too minor for a toast bar. Trap: putting actionable or error content in a HUD — it vanishes before the user can respond (→ §8.5 accessibility).

## 8.4 Banners & inline alerts

Persistent, **non-modal** messages embedded in the layout (not floating above it). They don't block and don't auto-dismiss — they wait.

- **Banner / inline alert** — a strip pinned at the top of a content region (or inline within a form) carrying a status + optional action: offline mode, sync failure, "Verify your email." Material 2 had a formal **Banner** component; **Material 3 dropped it** — M3 routes persistent messages to inline content, cards, or (for transient) snackbars, so on Android you compose a banner from a surface + text + text-buttons rather than reaching for a named component. iOS has no system in-app banner component (the iOS "banner" word means the **OS notification** banner, below). macOS uses inline `NSBox`-style notices; Windows/Fluent has `InfoBar` for exactly this persistent-status job. Reach for a banner when the condition persists and the user may act on it later. Trap: using a banner for transient confirmation (that's a toast) or for a must-answer-now decision (that's a dialog).
- **Inline validation message** — the field-level error/warning that lives next to the input it describes, not in an overlay. Always prefer this to an alert for form errors: it's non-blocking, points at the cause, and survives on screen. Accessibility: tie it to the field with `aria-describedby` + `aria-invalid` and announce via `aria-live="polite"` (web) / accessibility announcement (native) so it's not silently visual (→ §19).
- **System notification banner (iOS/Android)** — the OS-drawn drop-down from the top for incoming push/local notifications (iOS banner/alert styles; Android heads-up notification). Owned by the OS, not your layout; you control content, not chrome. Don't confuse the iOS "banner" notification style with an in-app banner — different layers, different lifetimes, different code paths.

## 8.5 Snackbars, toasts & transient messages

Brief, self-dismissing feedback that doesn't block. The lightest-weight overlay — and the most abused.

- **Snackbar (Android / Material) / toast** — a short message at the **bottom** that auto-dismisses (Material spec: `LENGTH_SHORT` ≈ 1.5–2s, `LENGTH_LONG` ≈ 2.75s, or indefinite when it carries an action), optionally with **one** action (commonly Undo). M3 rules: single line preferred, at most one action button, never two, and it shouldn't cover the FAB/nav — it pushes them up. Reach for it to confirm a completed, reversible action ("Message archived — Undo"). Trap: putting a **critical error that needs action** in an auto-dismissing snackbar — it disappears before a slow or distracted user reacts; that belongs in a banner or dialog.
- **Toast (Android platform) vs. Snackbar** — Android's legacy `Toast` is a fire-and-forget, non-interactive, OS-styled blip with **no action and no dismissal control** — and on Android 12+ (API 31) it's restricted to two text lines, app-icon-prefixed, and rate-limited. Snackbar is the modern in-app choice because it can carry an action and respects your theme. Trap: reaching for `Toast` when you need an Undo — it can't have one.
- **iOS has no system toast** — Apple deliberately omits a toast component; the platform-blessed substitutes are a brief **HUD**, an inline status, or surfacing the result in-place. Rolling your own toast on iOS is common but off-spec — keep it bottom-anchored, brief, and never load-bearing. Trap: cloning Android's snackbar-with-action on iOS where users don't expect a transient action affordance and may miss it.
- **Accessibility of transient messages** — auto-dismiss is hostile to screen-reader, low-vision, and motor users who can't read/act in time. Mitigations: announce via `aria-live="polite"` / role `status` (web) or an accessibility announcement (native); **pause the timer on hover/focus**; give a manual close; and never make the toast the *only* path to an action. WCAG 2.2 reinforces this for hover/focus-revealed content under **1.4.13** (→ §8.6, §19). Trap: a 3-second toast as the sole way to Undo.

## 8.6 Tooltips, coach marks & feature discovery

Surfaces that *teach*. They ride on hover/focus or first-run timing and are the single most common source of overlay accessibility failures.

- **Tooltip (desktop/web) — plain vs. rich (Material 3)** — a small label revealing the name or purpose of a control. Material 3 splits them: **plain tooltips** (brief, for labeling icon-only/unlabeled controls) and **rich tooltips** (longer text, may include a title, a link, or a button — and stay open until dismissed). macOS/Windows have OS-native hover tooltips (`help` text / `ToolTip`). Reach for plain to name an icon button; rich to explain a concept. Trap: tooltips on touch — there's no hover, so a tooltip-only label is invisible on phones; give icon buttons a real accessible name and don't hide essential info in a hover-only tip.
- **WCAG 2.2 §1.4.13 (Content on Hover or Focus)** — any hover/focus-triggered overlay (tooltip, popover, submenu) **must** be: **Dismissible** (closeable via **Esc** without moving the pointer/focus), **Hoverable** (the pointer can move onto the tooltip without it vanishing — critical for screen-magnifier users panning to read it), and **Persistent** (stays until dismissed or the trigger is removed). Native tooltips often violate hoverable (they close the instant you leave the trigger). This is a Level AA veto — fail it and the pattern is non-compliant (→ §19).
- **Coach mark / callout / spotlight** — a one-time overlay that points at a UI element ("Tap here to scan") on first run, often with a dimmed scrim cutting a hole around the target. No system component — you build it, which means you own the accessibility (focus order, dismissibility, skip control). Reach for it to introduce a genuinely non-obvious affordance. Trap: a wall of sequential coach marks at first launch — users dismiss them blind; show one, in context, at the moment of relevance.
- **Feature discovery — TipKit (iOS/iPadOS/macOS 17+) / TeachingTip (Windows/Fluent)** — platform-blessed "here's something new" surfaces. **TipKit** (introduced WWDC 2023, iOS 17+) shows rule-based, frequency-capped tips as **inline (`TipView`)** or **popover (`popoverTip`)**, with eligibility/dismissal/display-history managed for you and optional CloudKit sync so a dismissed tip stays dismissed across devices. Fluent's `TeachingTip` is the Windows analogue with an optional tail to the target. Prefer these to hand-rolled coach marks — they get dismissal, persistence, and "don't show again" right. Trap: ignoring frequency caps and re-showing a tip the user already dismissed.
- **Onboarding tooltip vs. just-in-time tip** — discovery shown at first launch (low retention; user has no context) vs. shown the moment the feature becomes relevant (high retention). Default to just-in-time. Trap: front-loading every tip into an onboarding carousel the user swipes past.

## 8.7 Dismissal, scrims & cross-cutting rules

The mechanics shared by every overlay above. Pick the dismissal model from the surface's blocking level, then make every model reachable by every input.

- **Scrim / dim / obscuring overlay** — the translucent layer behind a modal that dims content and catches outside taps (web modals also mark the backdrop `inert`). Tapping the scrim ("light dismiss") closes **light** modals (sheets, popovers, non-destructive dialogs) but should **not** dismiss a surface holding unsaved input without a confirm. Material: scrim tap always closes a bottom sheet; iOS: tapping outside a popover dismisses it; a true alert has no light-dismiss. Trap: a scrim that silently discards a half-filled form on an errant tap.
- **Swipe-to-dismiss** — the drag-down (sheets) or drag-aside (cards, notifications) gesture. Fast and expected on mobile, but **gesture-only dismissal fails accessibility** — VoiceOver/TalkBack/switch users have no swipe; always pair it with a visible Close/Cancel control and the platform back action (→ §19). Trap: a sheet whose only exit is swipe-down.
- **Esc / back / explicit close** — desktop & web dismiss on **Esc**; Android dismisses overlays on the **back gesture/button** (and animates them under **predictive back** on Android 14+); iOS uses an explicit nav-bar Cancel/Done for non-swipeable covers. A modal that ignores Esc/back is broken on its platform. The web `<dialog>` `showModal()` and `popover="auto"` both wire Esc automatically; custom overlays must add a keydown handler.
- **Interactive-dismiss guard** — when a surface holds unsaved work, intercept the dismiss gesture and confirm. iOS: `interactiveDismissDisabled()` + a discard-changes confirmation action sheet; Android: handle predictive-back/scrim with a discard confirm; web: the `<dialog>` `cancel` event (preventable) or `beforeunload`. Trap: shipping a richly-editable sheet with default swipe-to-dismiss and no guard — the #1 data-loss bug in mobile forms.
- **Top layer / stacking** — overlays must render above all app content and own their own focus scope. Web: the **top layer** (`<dialog showModal()>`, the `popover` attribute, `::backdrop`) escapes `z-index`/`overflow` clipping (Popover API Baseline 2024); native frameworks present overlays in a window above the key window. Trap: a custom modal built with `z-index` inside a scrolling/overflow-clipped container — it gets cut off or trapped below other layers.
- **One overlay at a time** — stacking a dialog on a sheet on a popover is a smell and a focus-management nightmare. Resolve or dismiss the current surface before presenting the next; queue transient messages rather than overlapping them. Trap: a toast firing over an open alert — the screen reader announces both and the user loses the thread.
- **Liquid Glass note (iOS 26, Sept 2025 — load-bearing, not hype)** — Apple's guidance is to reserve Liquid Glass for the **navigation layer that floats above content** — sheets, popovers, menus, toolbars — not for content surfaces. Overusing the material on dense content tanks text contrast over the refractive backdrop. The contrast trap is real: always back copy with a solid scrim/material, and respect **Reduce Transparency** + **Increase Contrast** (→ §19). Don't sprinkle glass everywhere because it's new.

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Sheets](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/sheets); [Apple HIG — Action sheets](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/action-sheets); [Apple — TipKit](https://developer.apple.com/tipkit/); [Material 3 — Bottom sheets](https://m3.material.io/components/bottom-sheets/guidelines); [Material 3 — Dialogs](https://m3.material.io/components/dialogs/guidelines); [MDN — Popover API](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Popover_API); [W3C — Understanding WCAG 2.2 SC 1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/content-on-hover-or-focus.html).*

---

All facts confirmed. The draft is accurate on Android 15/SDK 35 edge-to-edge, IME insets, WebOTP format, and passkey grammar. I have enough grounding. Here is the revised, strictly-more-complete section.

# 9. Forms, text input & keyboards

Data entry is where apps win or lose. The on-screen keyboard eats up to half the viewport, validation is unforgiving on a touch target, and every platform models the text field, the keyboard, and autofill differently. Governing principle: **the field asks for the least, the keyboard matches the data, the system fills what it already knows, and errors arrive at the right moment — not before, not after submit.**

## 9.1 Text fields & labels

- **Filled text field (Material 3)** — container with a tonal fill, rounded top corners, square bottom, and a bottom-edge stroke that thickens + recolors to the primary on focus. Material's default; higher visual emphasis, suited to short forms and dialogs. Trap: the fill competes with surrounding surfaces — don't stack many filled fields in a long form (use outlined).
- **Outlined text field (Material 3)** — stroke around the full rounded container, label notched into the top border on focus/value. Lower emphasis; Material's recommendation for long, dense forms. Both variants share behavior; the choice is purely emphasis. iOS has no native equivalent — a "bordered" look on iOS is custom styling on a plain `UITextField`/SwiftUI `TextField`.
- **iOS text field** — single-line, fixed-height, rounded; SwiftUI `TextField` defaults to *no* border (`.textFieldStyle(.roundedBorder)` adds one). Apple leans on **section grouping** in `Form`/grouped lists rather than per-field borders — the row inset and separator do the visual work. Trap: porting Material's outlined look to iOS verbatim reads as off-platform.
- **Desktop text field (macOS/Windows)** — macOS `NSTextField` (AppKit) / SwiftUI `TextField` is a thin-bordered, focus-ringed inset box; Windows Fluent `TextBox` is a filled box with a bottom accent on focus (the WinUI lineage Material's filled field descends from). Both expect a visible focus ring, hover state, and a right-click context menu (Cut/Copy/Paste/spelling) that mobile lacks. Desktop forms are dense and multi-column; mobile forms are single-column.
- **Floating label (Material) vs. static label (iOS/macOS/Windows) vs. placeholder-as-label (anti-pattern)** — Material animates the label from inside the field to the notch on focus. iOS/macOS/Windows put a persistent label *outside/left/above* the field (or as a section header). Never use the placeholder as the only label: it vanishes on input, fails reflow + screen readers, and breaks recall (→ §19 accessibility). Placeholder is a *hint/example*, never the name.
- **Assistive/supporting text** — Material's `supportingText` slot below the field for hints, character counts, and errors (it swaps to the error string + recolors on validation). iOS uses a footer caption under a grouped section; Fluent uses an inline message below. Reserve the vertical space so the layout doesn't jump when an error appears.
- **Character counter** — Material renders `current/max` at the trailing end of supporting text. Show it only when a hard limit exists and the user is approaching it; a counter on an unbounded field is noise. Announce remaining count to screen readers via a polite live region, not on every keystroke (→ §19).
- **Multi-line / text area** — Material `TextField` with multiple lines auto-grows; iOS uses `UITextView`/SwiftUI `TextEditor` (no built-in placeholder — you draw one); web `<textarea>`. macOS/Windows expect a resize affordance and respect Enter-to-newline vs. Enter-to-submit conventions (single-line submits, multi-line inserts newline; ⌘/Ctrl+Enter submits).
- **Read-only vs. disabled** — disabled = not applicable now (dimmed, skipped by VoiceOver/TalkBack focus order, fails 1.4.3 contrast exemption but still must be perceivable); read-only = value matters but isn't editable (still focusable, copyable, announced). Don't disable a field to convey "locked" when the user needs to read it (→ §19).

## 9.2 On-screen keyboards: type, return key, toolbars

Match the keyboard to the data. Wrong keyboard type is the single most common mobile-form defect — a phone field that opens QWERTY costs taps and triggers autocorrect on digits.

| Intent | iOS `UIKeyboardType` / `textContentType` | Android `inputType` | Web `inputmode` + `type` |
|---|---|---|---|
| Email | `.emailAddress` | `textEmailAddress` | `inputmode="email"` + `type="email"` |
| Phone | `.phonePad` | `phone` | `inputmode="tel"` + `type="tel"` |
| Integer (PIN, qty) | `.numberPad` | `number` | `inputmode="numeric"` + `type="text"`* |
| Decimal / currency | `.decimalPad` | `numberDecimal` | `inputmode="decimal"` |
| URL | `.URL` | `textUri` | `inputmode="url"` + `type="url"` |
| Search | `.default` + `.search` return | `text` + IME action search | `type="search"`, `enterkeyhint="search"` |

*\* `type="number"` on web is hostile for IDs/ZIPs/card numbers (spinners, locale parsing, leading-zero loss, scroll-wheel mutation) — prefer `type="text"` + `inputmode="numeric"` + `pattern="[0-9]*"`.*

- **`inputmode` (web) vs. keyboard type (native)** — `inputmode` only changes the *on-screen keyboard layout*; it does **not** validate. `type="email"` validates and changes the keyboard. Use both. Trap: `inputmode="numeric"` still lets pasted letters through — validate regardless.
- **Return-key label / `enterkeyhint`** — set the action word on the key: iOS `returnKeyType` (`.go`, `.search`, `.send`, `.next`, `.done`), Android `imeOptions` (`actionNext`, `actionDone`, `actionSearch`), web `enterkeyhint`. "Next" advances focus to the next field; "Done"/"Go" submits. A "Next" on the last field is a dead end.
- **Keyboard toolbar / input accessory view (iOS) vs. IME action (Android)** — iOS `inputAccessoryView` is a bar pinned above the keyboard for Prev/Next field arrows, a Done button, autofill chips, or formatting controls. SwiftUI uses `.toolbar { ToolbarItemGroup(placement: .keyboard) }`. A **Done** button is mandatory for number-pad keyboards (they have no return key to dismiss). Android surfaces a single IME action via `imeOptions`; multi-field navigation relies on the form layout, not a toolbar.
- **Inline predictions (iOS 17+)** — the English keyboard shows on-device next-word predictions inline in the field. Don't fight it with aggressive custom autocomplete on the same field.
- **Number pad has no dismiss key** — on iOS `.numberPad`/`.decimalPad`/`.phonePad` there is no return key. Provide a toolbar Done, a tap-outside-to-dismiss gesture, or `keyboardDismissMode = .interactive`. Forgetting this traps the user.
- **Custom keyboards / third-party IMEs** — users run Gboard, SwiftKey, swipe input, and emoji/sticker keyboards; never assume key positions, a fixed keyboard height, or that a given key exists. Read committed text, not keystrokes.
- **Hardware keyboard (iPad, desktop, web)** — support Tab to move fields, Shift+Tab back, Enter to submit, Esc to cancel/dismiss. Tab order follows visual order (→ §19). On macOS, Full Keyboard Access; on Windows, access keys + tab order — every control reachable without a pointer.

## 9.3 Autofill, autocomplete & credentials

- **`autocomplete` tokens (web)** — name the semantic, not the field: `email`, `tel`, `given-name`, `family-name`, `street-address`, `postal-code`, `cc-number`, `current-password`, `new-password`, `one-time-code`. This drives browser autofill *and* password-manager recognition, and satisfies **WCAG 2.1 SC 1.3.5 Identify Input Purpose (AA)**. Trap: `autocomplete="off"` rarely stops modern browsers and actively harms users on address/payment fields — only suppress on genuinely one-off secrets.
- **`textContentType` (iOS) / AutofillFramework hints (Android)** — the native analog. iOS `UITextContentType` (`.emailAddress`, `.telephoneNumber`, `.fullStreetAddress`, `.password`, `.newPassword`, `.oneTimeCode`); Android `setAutofillHints(AUTOFILL_HINT_EMAIL_ADDRESS, …)` consumed by the **Autofill Framework** (Android 8.0+/API 26+). Wrong/missing hints = autofill silently doesn't offer.
- **One-time-code (SMS OTP) autofill** — the divergence is sharp. **iOS**: set `textContentType = .oneTimeCode`; iOS surfaces the code as a QuickType keyboard suggestion when the SMS contains "code" (or its localization). No programmatic SMS read — privacy by design. iOS 16+ adds *domain-bound* codes (`@`-suffix in the SMS tied to the site) for anti-phishing. **Android**: full automation via the **SMS Retriever API** (no SMS permission; requires an 11-char app hash appended to the message) or **SMS User Consent API** (one-tap user approval, no hash). **Web**: `autocomplete="one-time-code"` + the **WebOTP API** (`navigator.credentials.get({ otp: { transport: ['sms'] } })`) on Chromium/Android; the SMS's last line must read `@host #code` (e.g. `@example.com #123456`). Trap: get the SMS format wrong and autofill silently never fires — there's no error.
- **Passkeys / WebAuthn (load-bearing, not hype — 2026)** — passwordless credentials backed by platform authenticators (Touch/Face ID, Windows Hello, Google Password Manager, Samsung Pass, iCloud Keychain). **Conditional UI / autofill**: add `webauthn` to the `autocomplete` token list (must be *last* — the grammar parses right-to-left, e.g. `autocomplete="username webauthn"`) and call `navigator.credentials.get({ mediation: 'conditional' })` so passkeys appear inline in the username field's autofill dropdown alongside saved passwords. Firefox recognized the `webauthn` token in v122; Chrome/Edge/Safari support conditional UI. Android routes through **Credential Manager** (unifies passkeys, passwords, federated sign-in); iOS uses `ASAuthorizationController` + Associated Domains. 2025–26 additions: **conditional create** (silently upgrade a password login to a passkey post-sign-in) and `getClientCapabilities()` to feature-detect before the ceremony. Trap: always keep a non-passkey fallback — cross-device/older-OS gaps mean passkey-only locks users out; passkeys also directly satisfy WCAG 3.3.8 (no memorized secret).
- **Don't break the password manager** — keep username + password in one `<form>`, label them with correct `autocomplete` tokens, and don't inject fields via JS after autofill runs. Identifier-first flows (username and password on separate screens) need the username field to persist (hidden, with `autocomplete="username"`) on the password screen so managers fill both.

## 9.4 Validation & inline errors

- **Validation timing** — validate on **blur** for format (email, phone), on **submit** for cross-field/server rules, and **as-you-type only to relax an existing error** (once a field is invalid, re-check on each keystroke so the error clears the instant it's fixed). Trap: validating on every keystroke *before first blur* yells "invalid email" while the user is still typing the first character — the most hated form pattern.
- **Inline error placement** — error text directly below/adjacent to the field, tied programmatically (`aria-describedby` + `aria-invalid` on web; `accessibilityValue`/error trait native), field border recolored. Color alone fails colorblind users — pair red with an icon + text (→ §19). Material swaps `supportingText` to the error and sets the error state; iOS shows a caption + tints the field; Fluent shows an inline error message with an icon.
- **Error summary (multi-field submit)** — on submit-time failure, move focus to the **first** invalid field and (for long forms / web) render a summary list at the top linking to each error. WCAG-aligned and the GOV.UK pattern; announce via a live region (`role="alert"`). Don't just scroll silently.
- **Success / positive validation** — a green check on a valid field reassures on high-stakes input (password strength, username availability) but is clutter on trivial fields. Use sparingly; never convey state by color alone (→ §19).
- **WCAG 2.2 form criteria (AA, legally load-bearing under ADA/EAA 2025)** — **3.3.7 Redundant Entry (A)**: don't make users re-enter info already given in the same process — prefill or offer it (except passwords/security). **3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (Minimum, AA)**: no cognitive-function test (memorized password typed from memory, transcription puzzles, image CAPTCHA) without an alternative — exactly why passkeys, email-link, and copy-paste-friendly OTP matter. Never block paste into password/OTP fields (→ §19).

## 9.5 Structured input: pickers, steppers, OTP, chips

- **Date picker (iOS `.compact`/`.wheels`/`.graphical` vs. Material date picker vs. native `<input type="date">`)** — iOS: `UIDatePicker`/SwiftUI `DatePicker` — `.compact` (tap-to-popover calendar, iOS 14+), `.wheels`, or `.graphical`. Material: modal calendar dialog **plus** a keyboard text-input toggle (offer both — accessibility requires typed entry). Web: `type="date"` renders the OS-native picker (great on mobile, inconsistent chrome on desktop). For known, far-past dates (birthdays), a **typed/segmented** field beats spinning a wheel decades back. Trap: never force a wheel picker for a 1950 birth year.
- **Time / date-time** — same families with `.time`/`datetime-local`. Respect 12h/24h from device locale, not hardcoded. Default to a sensible value (now, or the most likely date) so the picker opens near the answer.
- **Stepper (`+`/`−`)** — iOS `UIStepper`, Material has no core stepper (compose `IconButton`s), Fluent `NumberBox` has inline spin buttons, web `<input type="number">` spinners. Good for small bounded integers (1–10 quantity); useless for large/arbitrary numbers — give a keyboard. Always allow direct text entry alongside, and ensure the buttons have ≥44pt/48dp targets and accessible labels (→ §19).
- **Picker / spinner / `<select>` for enumerations** — iOS wheel `Picker` or menu-style; Material exposed dropdown menu (a text field that opens a menu) or modal; Fluent `ComboBox`; web `<select>` (native control, free a11y + mobile wheel). Use a native select for ≤~15 options; switch to a searchable list above that.
- **Segmented control / single-choice chips** — for 2–5 mutually-exclusive options visible at once (iOS `UISegmentedControl`, Material segmented buttons / single-select filter chips, Fluent `SegmentedControl`, web radio group styled as a toggle). Beats a dropdown when the choices are few and worth showing. Expose as a radio group to assistive tech, not a set of unrelated buttons (→ §19).
- **OTP / PIN entry** — one logical field rendered as N boxes; auto-advance on digit, backspace returns to the prior box, **paste fills all boxes at once** (the #1 OTP bug is paste landing only in box 1). Set `inputmode="numeric"`/`.numberPad` and `autocomplete="one-time-code"`. For sensitive PINs use `secureTextEntry`/`type="password"`. Don't disable paste (→ §9.4, WCAG 3.3.8). The multi-box visual must still read as a single labeled field to screen readers.
- **Token / chip input (tags, recipients)** — free text commits to a removable chip on Enter/comma/space; chips are deletable via an `×` and via Backspace from the empty input. Material input chips, iOS `UISearchTextField` tokens / custom, Fluent custom, web custom (no native primitive). Trap: must stay keyboard-navigable — arrow into chips, Delete to remove, and each chip's remove control needs an accessible name (→ §19).

## 9.6 Search, media & document input

- **Search-as-input** — a search field is an input with its own conventions: `type="search"` (web, adds a clear `×`, `role="searchbox"`), iOS `UISearchBar`/`.searchable`, Material `SearchBar` (the expanding search view), Fluent `AutoSuggestBox`. Set the return/IME action to "search", debounce as-you-type queries (~250–300ms), and show recent/suggested queries before the first keystroke. Place per platform navigation convention (top bar on iOS/web; magnifier-to-expand on Android).
- **Photo picker (privacy-scoped)** — **iOS** `PHPickerViewController` runs out-of-process: the user picks specific photos and the app gets *only those* — no library-access permission prompt. **Android** `Photo Picker` (`ActivityResultContracts.PickVisualMedia` / `ACTION_PICK_IMAGES`, system component, no storage permission; back-ported to Android 4.4+ via Google Play services). **Web** `<input type="file" accept="image/*" capture="environment">` (`capture` hints camera). Trap: requesting full gallery/storage permission when the scoped picker would do is now an App Store / Play review flag and a privacy regression — reach for the scoped picker first.
- **Document picker** — iOS `UIDocumentPickerViewController` (Files), Android Storage Access Framework (`ACTION_OPEN_DOCUMENT` / `ActivityResultContracts.OpenDocument`), macOS `NSOpenPanel`, Windows `FileOpenPicker`, web `<input type="file">` / File System Access API (Chromium). All return scoped handles/security-scoped URLs, not raw paths.
- **Camera / scan-to-fill** — **Scan card** for credit cards (iOS surfaces "Scan Credit Card" above the keyboard automatically when a field has `textContentType = .creditCardNumber`); document/ID scanning via `VNDocumentCameraViewController` (iOS VisionKit) / ML Kit (Android). Live Text / OCR can fill a field from the camera. Always pair with manual entry.
- **Dictation / voice input** — the keyboard mic (iOS dictation, Gboard voice) fills any text field for free; don't build a custom voice button that duplicates it. For long-form, on-device dictation respects punctuation commands. On watchOS this is the *primary* text path (scribble/dictation/canned replies), not an add-on (→ §9.9).
- **Paste affordances** — honor the system Paste menu; for OTP/2FA/cards, iOS may surface the value as a keyboard suggestion (don't block it). Never strip whitespace/formatting on paste *before* the user sees it land (breaks card numbers, IBANs). iOS 16+ prompts on programmatic clipboard reads — read pasteboard only on explicit user action.

## 9.7 Focus, keyboard avoidance & flow

- **Keyboard avoidance** — the keyboard must never cover the focused field or its submit button. **iOS**: SwiftUI handles it automatically inside `ScrollView`/`Form`; UIKit uses `keyboardLayoutGuide` (iOS 15+) or keyboard-frame notifications. **Android**: `windowSoftInputMode="adjustResize"` is no longer sufficient once you target SDK 35 (Android 15 enforces edge-to-edge) — consume `WindowInsetsCompat.Type.ime()` / use `Modifier.imePadding()` in Compose. **Web**: the visual viewport shrinks — use `interactive-widget=resizes-content` in the viewport meta and `env(keyboard-inset-*)` / the VirtualKeyboard API where supported. Trap: a fixed-position submit bar the keyboard overlaps — test on a small device.
- **Focus management** — autofocus the first (or only) field on a single-purpose screen to pop the keyboard immediately; do **not** autofocus on screens with content to read first (it hijacks the screen-reader cursor — → §19). "Next" advances; the last field's action submits. On web, move focus to the first error on validation failure and to the heading/first field on step transitions.
- **Password reveal toggle** — an eye icon to unmask `secureTextEntry`/`type="password"`. iOS offers a built-in reveal on password fields; Material has a trailing-icon toggle; Fluent `PasswordBox` has a built-in reveal button; web is custom (toggle `type` between `password`/`text`). Default to masked; the toggle is what lets users verify without forcing memorized transcription. Trap: a toggle that lacks a state label, or that lets a screen reader announce the still-masked password.
- **Autocapitalization & autocorrect per field** — disable both on email, username, password, URL, and codes (`autocapitalize="none"` + `autocorrect="off"` + `spellcheck="false"` / `.never` + `.no`). Leave them on for name/prose fields. An autocorrected email is a support ticket.

## 9.8 Multi-step forms & wizards

- **Wizard / multi-step form** — break long forms into steps with a visible progress indicator and back navigation that preserves entered data. One logical group per step. Material has a Stepper pattern; Fluent has no native wizard (compose); iOS/web are custom (navigation stack + progress view). Trap: losing data on Back or on accidental app-background — persist draft state.
- **One-thing-per-screen (mobile pattern)** — popularized by GOV.UK; a single question per screen on small viewports reduces error and keyboard thrash. Costs more taps but raises completion on complex flows. Pair with a final review step.
- **Inline save & resume** — long/high-value forms (applications, KYC) should autosave per step and let the user resume. Never make the user re-enter prior steps (WCAG 3.3.7, → §9.4).
- **Review-before-submit** — a final summary screen listing all entered values with edit links. Standard for checkout and applications; satisfies redundant-entry expectations and catches errors before an irreversible submit.

## 9.9 Constrained surfaces: watch, TV, spatial

- **watchOS / Wear OS text** — typing is hostile on a watch; the platform substitutes **dictation, Scribble (handwriting), emoji, and canned/Smart Replies**. SwiftUI `TextField` on watchOS opens the system input sheet; Wear OS uses `RemoteInput` (often delegating to the phone). Design for selection over entry — offer presets, never a free-text field where a list would do.
- **tvOS / Android TV text** — there is no keyboard, only a D-pad over an on-screen grid; every character is a 5-way navigation. Minimize text fields to the absolute minimum (sign-in via on-phone pairing, QR, or code-on-phone beats grid-typing a password). Search defaults to voice.
- **visionOS text input (2025–26, emerging)** — eye-gaze targets a field, pinch focuses it, and entry comes from the floating virtual keyboard, **dictation**, or a paired Bluetooth/Mac keyboard. Look-to-focus needs generous targets and clear focus highlights; prefer dictation and selection for anything long. Volatile — APIs and ergonomics still shifting.

*Sources: [Material 3 — Text fields](https://m3.material.io/components/text-fields/guidelines); [Apple HIG — Keyboards](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/keyboards); [MDN — autocomplete attribute](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTML/Reference/Attributes/autocomplete); [web.dev — Sign in with a passkey through form autofill](https://web.dev/articles/passkey-form-autofill); [Chrome for Developers — WebOTP API](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/identity/web-apis/web-otp); [Android — Handle edge-to-edge enforcements in Android 15](https://developer.android.com/codelabs/edge-to-edge); [WCAG 2.2 — Understanding 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (Minimum)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/accessible-authentication-minimum.html).*

Both confirmed. Squeeze defaults to a tool/undo-redo modal (not just a palette "at hover location" — I'll correct that). Predictive back is on by default for API 34+ (Android 14), opt-in per-app via the manifest flag — I'll correct the "13+ enabled per-app" framing. Now the section.

# 10. Gestures & input modalities

Every way a user drives an app — touch, pointer, keyboard, stylus, voice, gaze, crown, controller. The governing principle: **a gesture is an accelerator, never the only path.** Any hidden gesture needs a visible, single-pointer fallback, because gestures are undiscoverable, unlabeled, and routinely inaccessible (→ §19). Match the input to the platform; the same intent ("go back", "delete") wears a different gesture on each OS, and the same screen now ships under a finger, a cursor, a Pencil, and an eye.

## 10.1 Core touch gestures

- **Tap (touch / click / activate)** — single discrete activation. The atomic gesture; the only one users reliably discover. iOS wants a **44×44pt** minimum hit target, Material a **48×48dp** target with **8dp** minimum spacing, regardless of the visual size of the control. Trap: shrinking the visible glyph and the tappable region together — pad the hit area beyond the icon, and keep neighbors far enough apart that fat fingers don't mis-hit (→ §19, WCAG 2.5.8 Target Size).
- **Double-tap** — two quick taps. iOS uses it sparingly (zoom-to-fit in Photos/Maps, the like-on-Instagram convention). Material avoids it for primary actions; web inherits a ~300ms tap-delay legacy unless `touch-action` opts out. Trap: VoiceOver/TalkBack reserve double-tap as "activate", so a custom double-tap is unreachable while a screen reader is on — ship an accessibility custom action (→ §19).
- **Long-press / touch-and-hold (Android: long press; iOS: touch and hold; web: `contextmenu`)** — press and hold past a delay (~**500ms** on both Android and iOS) to reveal a context menu, preview, or drag handle. iOS fires a haptic "tick" on trigger and shows a `UIContextMenuInteraction` preview; Material shows a ripple then the menu. The single most-overloaded hidden gesture. Trap: nothing on screen says it exists — pair it with a visible affordance (an overflow "⋮", a disclosure chevron) or users never find it.
- **Swipe (Android: swipe-to-dismiss / swipe actions; iOS: swipe actions; web: custom)** — short directional flick on a list row or card. iOS: leading/trailing `UISwipeActionsConfiguration` reveal colored action buttons (swipe-to-delete is canonical; full-swipe triggers the destructive default). Material: `SwipeToDismiss` with a background icon + color. Web has no native row-swipe — you build it, and it fights the browser's overscroll/back. Trap: hidden swipe actions fail discoverability and WCAG 2.5.1 — always duplicate the action in the row's overflow menu.
- **Pan / drag** — continuous one-finger move that tracks the finger (scrubbing a slider, repositioning a pin, panning a map). Distinct from swipe (path and position matter, not just direction). Trap: WCAG 2.2 **2.5.7 Dragging Movements (AA)** requires a single-pointer alternative for anything draggable unless dragging is *essential* — give sliders tap-to-position or +/− steppers (→ §19).
- **Pinch / spread (pinch-to-zoom)** — two-finger scale. Universal for maps, photos, PDFs. Both platforms treat it as standard for zoomable content only; web routes it through `touch-action`/Pointer Events and must not block browser page-zoom (WCAG 1.4.4). Trap: it's a multipoint gesture → WCAG **2.5.1 Pointer Gestures (A)** demands a single-finger alternative (double-tap-zoom, +/− buttons).
- **Rotate** — two-finger twist, almost exclusively maps and image editors. Niche; never the sole way to set an angle — pair with a numeric field or rotation handle.
- **Fling (Android) / momentum scroll (iOS / web inertial scroll)** — fast swipe that imparts velocity to a scroll surface. Material names `fling` explicitly; iOS bakes deceleration into `UIScrollView`; web exposes `-webkit-overflow-scrolling`/`overscroll-behavior`. Trap: don't gate content behind a fling that a slow, deliberate drag can't reach, and never hijack the scroll wheel/inertia for parallax that traps the user.
- **Pull-to-refresh (iOS: `UIRefreshControl`; Android: `SwipeRefreshLayout` / Compose `PullToRefresh`; web: custom or browser-native on Chrome Android)** — overscroll a list past its top to reload. Spinner under the nav bar (iOS) or a circular indicator that descends from the top (Material). Trap: invisible until performed, and it collides with the browser's own pull-to-refresh on mobile web — keep a manual refresh affordance and `overscroll-behavior: contain` where it conflicts.
- **Edge-swipe back (iOS: interactive pop / "swipe to go back"; Android: predictive back gesture; web: browser back-swipe)** — swipe inward from the screen edge to pop the nav stack. iOS owns the **left** edge (default on every `UINavigationController` push). Android 10+ uses **both** edges for system Back; **predictive back** previews the destination as you drag and is **on by default for apps targeting API 34+ (Android 14)** — opt in via `android:enableOnBackInvokedCallback="true"`, and as of Android 15 the back-to-home/cross-activity animations no longer hide behind a developer option. Web back-swipe is the OS browser's, not yours. Trap: full-bleed horizontal content (carousels, drawing canvases) collides with the system edge zone — Android's `setSystemGestureExclusionRects` carves out a band (capped, ~200dp); abuse it and you break the user's escape hatch.

## 10.2 Multi-finger, force, and preview gestures

- **Two-finger / multi-finger gestures** — two-finger pan (scroll inside a zoomed surface, sheet music), three-finger swipe (iPad since iOS 13: swipe-left = undo, swipe-right = redo; three-finger pinch-in = copy, pinch-out = paste), four/five-finger pinch-to-home on iPad. Power-user territory. Trap: undiscoverable by definition and impossible for single-finger AT users — always ship menu/keyboard equivalents (→ §19).
- **Force Touch / 3D Touch (deprecated)** — pressure-sensitive press on iPhone 6s–XS. **Removed from iPhone hardware after iPhone XS (2018); the pressure API is effectively dead.** Apple Watch Force Touch was also retired (watchOS 7, 2020). Do not design new pressure-tiered interactions.
- **Haptic Touch (iOS)** — the *time-based* successor to 3D Touch: press-and-hold a duration to open context menus and previews, confirmed by a haptic tap (→ §11 haptics). This is what "long-press a link/icon" runs on today. It's touch-and-hold with feedback — design for duration, never pressure.
- **Peek and Pop (legacy term)** — the 3D-Touch-era "light press to peek, press harder to pop." Survives conceptually as the **context-menu preview** (`UIContextMenuInteraction`, iOS 13+): long-press shows a large preview + action list; tap the preview to commit. Trap: don't call it "Peek and Pop" in new specs — it's a context menu now, and the "harder press" tier no longer exists.
- **Drag and drop (`UIDragInteraction`/`UIDropInteraction` + `NSItemProvider`; macOS `NSDragging`; web HTML Drag-and-Drop / Pointer Events)** — touch-and-hold to lift an item, then drag to a drop target; on iPadOS and macOS this crosses **app boundaries** (drag a photo from Photos into Mail). **Spring-loading**: hover a dragged item over a folder/tab to auto-open it. Web's native DnD API is notoriously inconsistent and keyboard-inaccessible — many teams reimplement with Pointer Events. Trap: cross-app drag is a pro accelerator with zero affordance — never the only way to move data, and honor 2.5.7's single-pointer alternative (→ §19).

## 10.3 Pointer, hover, and the desktop/iPad lane

iPadOS (since 13.4) and every desktop OS add a **pointer** — so apps must handle hover, right-click, and precise cursors, not just touch. Detect capability, not device: `@media (hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)` on web; `UITraitCollection`/`UIPointerInteraction` on iPad.

- **Hover (pointer hover)** — cursor-over reveals state without committing: tooltips, button highlights, row affordances. iPadOS morphs the cursor into the control it's over via `UIPointerInteraction` "pointer effects" (highlight, lift, hover); web uses `:hover`. Trap: **hover-only content is invisible to touch and violates WCAG 1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus** — anything revealed on hover must also be reachable by tap/focus and be dismissable/persistent (→ §19).
- **Right-click / secondary click (context menu)** — desktop + iPad-with-trackpad summon the same `UIContextMenuInteraction` / `NSMenu` / web `contextmenu` that long-press does on touch. Good pattern: **one menu definition, three triggers** (long-press, right-click, keyboard Menu key / Shift-F10). Trap: putting unique actions *only* in the right-click menu — touch-only users can't reach them.
- **Trackpad / mouse precision & momentum** — two-finger scroll, pinch-zoom and rotate on trackpad, natural-scroll inertia. macOS/iPadOS expect inertial deceleration; Windows precision-touchpad gestures (three-finger swipe = task view, four-finger = desktop switch) are **OS-owned** — don't remap them. Trap: assuming a click is a tap — trackpad click has no touch-down "press" state, so press-and-hold timers behave differently.
- **Pointer vs touch target sizing** — Material specs a smaller minimum for precise pointers (**~24×24dp** mouse) vs **48×48dp** touch; **macOS HIG** wants ~**28×28pt** clickable controls; WCAG **2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum), AA** sets **24×24 CSS px** as the web floor (with spacing exceptions). Trap: designing only for the mouse on a convertible/2-in-1 or touchscreen laptop — the same build runs under a finger; size for the *coarsest* pointer the surface can receive.

## 10.4 Hardware & alternative input

- **Hardware keyboard & shortcuts** — iPad and desktop apps must support physical keyboards: arrow-key navigation, ⌘-shortcuts (`UIKeyCommand` / `NSMenu` key-equivalents / web `keydown`), and a logical Tab focus order. iPadOS surfaces shortcuts via the **hold-⌘ discoverability HUD**; macOS lists them in the menu bar; web should expose them in a help overlay. Trap: trapping focus, omitting a visible focus ring, or stealing single-key shortcuts from AT — breaks keyboard-only users (WCAG 2.1.1 Keyboard, 2.1.2 No Keyboard Trap, 2.4.7 Focus Visible, 2.1.4 Character Key Shortcuts → §19).
- **Stylus / Apple Pencil (`UIPencilInteraction`; Android `MotionEvent` TOOL_TYPE_STYLUS / `MotionEventCompat`)** — pressure, tilt (azimuth/altitude), and palm rejection across platforms. **Apple Pencil Pro (2024)** adds **squeeze** (default = a tool palette / undo-redo modal, remappable), **barrel roll** (gyroscope — rotates shaped/calligraphy brushes), **haptic feedback**, and Find My; **double-tap** (Pencil 2nd-gen+ — switch tool/eraser) and **hover** (Pencil Pro and M2-class iPad — preview the stroke before it lands) round it out. Trap: making double-tap/squeeze the *only* way to switch tools — keep an on-screen tool picker; both gestures are user-configurable and can be disabled in Settings.
- **Game controller (`GCController`; Android `InputDevice` SOURCE_GAMEPAD; web Gamepad API)** — drive a **focus engine**, not a cursor: D-pad/stick moves focus, A/Cross confirms, B/Circle backs out. Mandatory on tvOS and Android TV. Trap: assuming touch coordinates or hover — there is no pointer; every focusable element needs a visible focused state and a sane focus order (→ §19).
- **Voice Control / voice access (iOS & macOS Voice Control; Android Voice Access)** — OS-level input that drives the *whole* UI by speaking control labels and numbered overlays. This is why **every interactive element needs an accessible name** — unlabeled icon buttons are unspeakable and unreachable (→ §19). Distinct from in-app dictation/speech-to-text, which is a text-entry method, not a navigation one.
- **Digital Crown (watchOS; `WKCrownSequencer` / SwiftUI `.digitalCrownRotation`)** — the rotary input on Apple Watch: precise scrolling, value adjustment (volume, zoom, picker wheels), with detented haptic feedback. Wear OS mirrors this with the **rotating bezel/crown** (`RotaryScrollEvent`). Pair crown-scroll with the touch screen; it's the primary fine-control input on a display too small for accurate drag. Trap: a watch list that *only* responds to touch flicks — the Crown is what users actually reach for, and it's the accessible scroll path.
- **Gaze + pinch (visionOS)** *(2024–2026; load-bearing on Apple Vision Pro, not hype)* — **eyes are the pointer, hands are the trigger.** Look at a target (the system highlights it on hover), then **pinch** thumb-to-index to activate; pinch-and-drag to scroll or move; two-handed pinch-spread to zoom. Hands rest comfortably in the lap (indirect input) — Apple prefers this over reaching out to "directly" touch windows, for ergonomics. visionOS demands large, well-spaced targets (eye-tracking is coarser than a fingertip) and an unambiguous hover/highlight state. Trap: porting phone-density tap targets — gaze can't reliably select small, dense controls; eye-tracking data is also privacy-sensitive (never exposed to the app) and sustained targeting causes fatigue, so don't require precise dwell.

## 10.5 Discoverability & accessibility (the cross-cutting veto)

The recurring failure of app design: **shipping a critical action behind an invisible gesture.** The gesture is the accelerator; a visible control is the contract.

- **Always pair hidden gestures with a visible control.** Swipe-to-delete → also an overflow "Delete". Long-press menu → also a "⋮" button. Pinch-zoom → also +/− controls. Shake-to-undo → also an Undo button.
- **Gestures vs assistive tech.** VoiceOver (iOS) and TalkBack (Android) **capture single-finger gestures** and remap them — swipe becomes "next element", double-tap becomes "activate". Your custom one-finger swipes and double-taps don't fire while a screen reader is on; provide an **accessibility custom action** (`accessibilityCustomActions` / `ViewCompat.addAccessibilityAction` / web `role`+keyboard handler) so the same intent is reachable from the rotor/menu (→ §19).
- **WCAG floor for gestures:** **2.5.1 Pointer Gestures (A)** — nothing may *require* a multipoint or path-based gesture; **2.5.2 Pointer Cancellation (A)** — fire on the up-event so users can drag off to abort; **2.5.4 Motion Actuation (A)** — don't require shake/tilt without a UI equivalent, and let users disable it; **2.5.7 Dragging Movements (AA)** — anything draggable needs a single-pointer alternative; **2.5.8 Target Size (AA)** — 24×24 CSS px minimum.
- **Respect system gesture zones.** iOS Home-indicator swipe, Control/Notification Center edge pulls, and Android's back/home/recents gesture bands are OS-owned. Don't place app gestures in them; use exclusion APIs surgically. Trap: an edge-anchored drawer that fights the system back gesture leaves users unable to leave the screen.
- **Honor reduce-motion and interaction settings.** `UIAccessibility.isReduceMotionEnabled`, Android "Remove animations", web `prefers-reduced-motion`, plus AssistiveTouch / Switch Control / Full Keyboard Access change how gestures arrive — test with them on, not as an afterthought (→ §19).

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Gestures](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/gestures); [Material Design 3 — Applying interaction / gestures](https://m3.material.io/foundations/interaction/gestures); [Apple Developer — UIPencilInteraction](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/uikit/uipencilinteraction); [Android Developers — Add support for the predictive back gesture](https://developer.android.com/guide/navigation/custom-back/predictive-back-gesture); [Apple Support — Vision Pro basic gestures & controls](https://support.apple.com/guide/apple-vision-pro/basic-gestures-and-controls-tan1e2a29e00/visionos); [W3C — Understanding WCAG 2.5.1 Pointer Gestures](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Understanding/pointer-gestures.html); [W3C — Understanding WCAG 2.5.7 Dragging Movements](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/dragging-movements.html).*

The draft says GESTURE_START is API 34, which is wrong — it's API 30. I'll correct that. Apple soft/rigid = iOS 13 confirmed.

# 11. Haptics & sensory feedback

Touch feedback is the dimension that most separates app design from web design: a phone in a hand can speak back through the skin. This section governs the tactile, sonic, and combined-modality feedback an app emits. Governing principle: **haptics are punctuation, not prose** — they confirm a meaningful state change, ride alongside a visual and/or audio cue, and stay silent the other 99% of the time. A haptic is never the only channel carrying information (→ §19 accessibility).

## 11.1 The hardware

- **Taptic Engine (Apple)** — the linear resonant actuator (LRA) in every iPhone since iPhone 6s/7 and Apple Watch. A precise, fast-settling mass that produces sharp, localized "taps" rather than the smeary buzz of an eccentric-rotating-mass (ERM) motor. Its precision is *why* iOS haptics feel crisp and Android's historically felt mushy — the OS can address it down to per-event transients. Apple Watch reuses the Taptic Engine for its wrist-tap notification system.
- **Vibration motors / actuators (Android)** — fragmented by design. Cheap devices ship an **ERM** (rotating offset weight: slow spin-up, only on/off, buzzy). Flagships (Pixel, Samsung S/Z) ship an **LRA** comparable to the Taptic Engine, addressable with amplitude control and composition primitives. The trap: the *same* Android code runs on both, so a rich effect authored on a Pixel degrades to a coarse buzz on a budget phone. Always query capability (`Vibrator.hasAmplitudeControl()`, `areEffectsSupported()`, `areAllPrimitivesSupported()`) and let the OS fall back, rather than hardcoding waveforms.
- **Amplitude vs. frequency/sharpness control** — LRAs vary intensity (amplitude) and, on better hardware, perceived sharpness; ERMs do neither well. This is the hardware root of why "design once, ship everywhere" fails for custom haptics on Android but mostly holds on iOS (one actuator family across the lineup).
- **Permissions divergence** — Android `Vibrator` calls (raw `vibrate()`, `VibrationEffect`) require the `VIBRATE` manifest permission; `View.performHapticFeedback()` does **not** (it routes through the window). iOS generators require no permission. Trap: dropping to `Vibrator` for a tap you could have done with `performHapticFeedback` adds a permission for nothing and bypasses the user's haptic theme.
- **Desktop has no haptics** — macOS/Windows laptops vibrate nothing. The one exception is Apple's **Force Touch trackpad**, which fakes a physical click via a Taptic Engine under the glass (`NSHapticFeedbackManager`, patterns `.alignment` / `.levelChange` / `.generic`). Windows precision trackpads and a few premium mice expose no app-controllable haptic API. Don't design app feedback assuming a desktop user can feel anything; treat haptics as a mobile/wearable channel.

## 11.2 iOS — UIFeedbackGenerator & Core Haptics

iOS exposes two tiers: a small set of **semantic generators** (use these for ~95% of cases) and **Core Haptics** for authored, synchronized patterns. Reaching for Core Haptics when a generator would do is the classic over-engineering trap.

The semantic generators (`UIFeedbackGenerator` subclasses):

| Generator | Variants / call | Meaning | Reach for it when |
|---|---|---|---|
| `UIImpactFeedbackGenerator` | `.light` `.medium` `.heavy` `.soft` `.rigid` | A physical collision/landing | An object snaps into place, a toggle flips, a drag hits a boundary |
| `UINotificationFeedbackGenerator` | `.success` `.warning` `.error` | Outcome of an async operation | Payment cleared (success), form invalid (warning), action failed (error) |
| `UISelectionFeedbackGenerator` | `.selectionChanged()` | Discrete value tick | Scrolling a picker, stepping a slider, moving between segmented options |

- **`.soft` / `.rigid`** — added iOS 13; they modulate the *attack* of an impact (squishy vs. crisp) independent of strength. `.soft` for organic/cushioned motion, `.rigid` for hard mechanical snaps. Trap: most teams ship only `.light/.medium/.heavy` and miss that texture axis.
- **`prepare()`** — call before the moment you'll fire, to wake the Taptic Engine out of idle (~saves the few-hundred-ms warmup so the tap lands *with* the visual, not after). The single most common iOS haptic bug is a late buzz because `prepare()` was skipped. The engine re-idles after a short window, so prepare close to use.
- **Core Haptics (`CHHapticEngine`, iOS 13+)** — author custom patterns from two event types: **transient** (`hapticTransient`, a single sharp tap) and **continuous** (`hapticContinuous`, a sustained vibration you shape over time), each parameterized by **intensity** and **sharpness** (0–1). Sync to audio via `CHHapticPattern` and **AHAP** files (Apple Haptic Audio Pattern, a JSON timeline of haptic + audio events). Reach for it for game feel, scrubbing textures, or branded "signature" haptics — not for a button tap.
- **`CHHapticEngine.capabilitiesForHardware().supportsHaptics`** — gate every Core Haptics path on this; most iPads, older devices, and the Simulator return `false`. The Simulator never plays haptics — device-test or you're flying blind.
- **SwiftUI** — `.sensoryFeedback(_:trigger:)` (iOS 17+) is the modern, declarative wrapper over the generators (`.success`, `.impact`, `.selection`, etc.); prefer it in new SwiftUI code over manually instantiating generators.
- **iOS 26 (Liquid Glass, 2025)** *[volatile]* — the redesign leans harder on coupled haptic+visual feedback as glass elements morph, lift, and settle, but the generator/Core Haptics APIs are unchanged. No new public haptic primitive shipped — the change is *editorial* (more deliberate use), not API. Treat "Liquid Glass haptics" as styling guidance, not a new capability.

## 11.3 Android — HapticFeedbackConstants, VibrationEffect, Composition

Android's guidance (and Material 3) is emphatic: **prefer action-oriented constants over hand-rolled waveforms**, so effects stay consistent device-wide and the OS falls back gracefully on weaker hardware.

- **`View.performHapticFeedback(HapticFeedbackConstants.*)`** — the top-of-funnel API and the rough analogue of iOS's semantic generators. Action-semantic constants: `CONFIRM`, `REJECT`, `TOGGLE_ON`/`TOGGLE_OFF`, `GESTURE_START`/`GESTURE_END`, `LONG_PRESS`, `KEYBOARD_PRESS`/`KEYBOARD_RELEASE`, `CLOCK_TICK`, `TEXT_HANDLE_MOVE`, `VIRTUAL_KEY`/`VIRTUAL_KEY_RELEASE` — most of this abstract set landed in **API 30 (Android 11)**; `SEGMENT_TICK`/`SEGMENT_FREQUENT_TICK` and `DRAG_START`/`GESTURE_THRESHOLD_ACTIVATE`/`GESTURE_THRESHOLD_DEACTIVATE` in **API 34 (Android 14)**. Use these first — they map to the user's chosen system haptic theme, degrade automatically, and need no `VIBRATE` permission.
- **`VibrationEffect.createPredefined(EFFECT_*)`** — `EFFECT_TICK` (light) · `EFFECT_CLICK` (the median — *start here* and tune up/down) · `EFFECT_HEAVY_CLICK` (strong) · `EFFECT_DOUBLE_CLICK`. Mental model: `CLICK` is your reference unit; move toward `TICK` for frequent/subtle, toward `HEAVY_CLICK` for important/rare.
- **`VibrationEffect.Composition`** — chain **primitives** with per-step scale (0–1) and delay to build rich, custom effects. `PRIMITIVE_CLICK` and `PRIMITIVE_TICK` arrived in **Android 11 (API 30)**; the expressive set — `PRIMITIVE_LOW_TICK`, `PRIMITIVE_QUICK_RISE`/`SLOW_RISE`/`QUICK_FALL`, `PRIMITIVE_SPIN`, `PRIMITIVE_THUD` — in **Android 12 (API 31)**, which also brought the modern haptics framework and the amplitude-control story. Gate with `areAllPrimitivesSupported()` — unsupported primitives are silently dropped.
- **`VibrationEffect.createWaveform(long[] timings, int[] amplitudes, repeat)`** — low-level on/off (or amplitude-stepped) patterns. The trap: this is the *legacy* path; one-shot `vibrate(ms)` and bare waveforms read as "buzzy pager" on ERM devices and ignore the user's haptic theme. Reserve for notifications where a literal buzz is the point.
- **`VibrationAttributes`** — tag every effect with a usage so the OS respects the right user toggle and Do-Not-Disturb: `USAGE_TOUCH` (UI feedback), `USAGE_NOTIFICATION`, `USAGE_RINGTONE`, `USAGE_ALARM`, `USAGE_ACCESSIBILITY`. Mis-tagging a UI tap as a notification fires it even when the user muted touch feedback — a respect-the-settings violation (→ §19).
- **Android 12 (API 31)** also lets the system **derive haptics from an audio/ringtone track** (haptic-audio coupling at the OS level via `HapticGenerator`); **Android 13 (API 33)** lets users opt notification vibrations to follow the haptic-strength setting. Don't reimplement what the platform now does.
- **Jetpack Compose** — `LocalHapticFeedback.current.performHapticFeedback(HapticFeedbackType.*)`; the type set expanded in 2024–2025 (`Confirm`, `Reject`, `ToggleOn`/`ToggleOff`, `GestureEnd`, `SegmentTick`, `ContextClick`, etc.) to mirror the View constants. Prefer this in Compose over dropping to `Vibrator`.
- **Material 3** treats haptics as a first-class feedback channel: correlate **strength with importance and inverse frequency** (subtle for scroll ticks, stronger for a submit), keep the *same interaction → same haptic* across the app, and co-design with motion and sound. Material's own components (sliders, switches, pull-to-refresh, time/date pickers) ship default haptics — don't double-fire your own on top.

## 11.4 Web / PWA — the Vibration API

- **`navigator.vibrate(pattern)` (Web Vibration API)** — the *only* web haptic primitive, and a weak one. Takes a duration in ms or an alternating vibrate/pause array (`navigator.vibrate([200,100,200])`); `vibrate(0)` or `[]` cancels. It is **on/off only** — no amplitude, no sharpness, no Taptic-grade transients. It is a buzz, not a haptic.
- **Support is the catch** — Android Chrome/Edge/Firefox support it; **iOS Safari does not** (and never has). So a PWA's vibration silently no-ops for every iPhone user. It requires a prior user gesture and a visible/active document to fire, and is ignored when the device's system vibration is off. Never make vibration load-bearing on the web; treat it as a bonus that exists on roughly half your mobile traffic. (→ §19 — a web feature absent on a whole platform can't be the sole signal.)
- **No notification/selection semantics** — there is no web equivalent of `.success` / `.selectionChanged`. The **iOS-Safari workaround** teams reach for — a hidden `<input switch>`/`<label>` whose toggle triggers the system's own control haptic — is a fragile hack, not an API; don't ship it as if it were one. If you need real haptic richness on mobile web, that's a signal to consider a native/wrapped app, not to fight the Vibration API.

## 11.5 Wearables & spatial

- **Apple Watch haptics (`WKInterfaceDevice.current().play(_:)`)** — a *named* vocabulary, not raw impacts: `.notification`, `.directionUp`/`.directionDown` (turn-by-turn), `.success`, `.failure`, `.retry`, `.start`, `.stop`, `.click`. The wrist is the highest-stakes haptic surface — it's often the *primary* output (screen dark, user not looking), so watch haptics must be unambiguous and rare. The Digital Crown adds detent haptics for scrolling. **Taptic Time** (Settings → Clock) even encodes the hour/minute as a haptic pattern for non-visual readout — the purest "haptic as primary channel" case.
- **Wear OS** — uses the same Android `Vibrator`/`VibrationEffect` stack, but the watch's tiny actuator and on-wrist context demand even more restraint; lean on `CONFIRM`/`REJECT` constants, not custom waveforms. Compose-for-Wear emits standard component haptics; don't stack your own.
- **visionOS** *[volatile, 2024–]* — **no haptics**: Vision Pro is worn on the face with no actuator against skin. Feedback is purely **spatial audio + visual** (hover highlights, a pinch-confirmation sound). Designers coming from iOS must *replace* the haptic confirm with an audio/visual one — the tactile channel simply isn't there. Connected game controllers can carry their own haptics; the headset cannot.

## 11.6 Design principles — when haptics help vs. annoy

- **Confirm meaningful state changes only** — a haptic should mark a discrete event the user caused or needs to know about: a toggle committed, an item dropped, a threshold crossed, an async op resolved. Decorative haptics on every scroll frame or hover are the fastest way to get users to disable haptics globally — and then you've lost the channel for the moments that matter.
- **Strength ∝ importance, inverse to frequency** — frequent micro-events (picker ticks, slider steps) get the lightest tap (`EFFECT_TICK`, `.selectionChanged`, `.light`); rare consequential ones (purchase, delete, error) get a stronger, distinct signature (`.error`, `EFFECT_HEAVY_CLICK`). Uniform-strength haptics carry no information.
- **Pair, never solo** — every haptic rides with a visual change and usually a sound. Haptic-as-sole-feedback fails Deaf-blind users, fails any muted/pocketed device, and fails the ~half of web users with no vibration support. The haptic *reinforces*; it never *replaces* (→ §19). It also can't gate flow: never block progress on "user must feel the buzz."
- **Synchronize to the millisecond** — a haptic that lands even ~50–100 ms after its visual feels broken ("off-sync feels broken" is explicit Android guidance). On iOS this means `prepare()`; on both, fire the haptic on the same frame as the animation keyframe it punctuates.
- **Restraint as default** — "less is more." If you're unsure whether a moment warrants a haptic, it doesn't. Audit: count haptics per typical session; if routine flows hit the dozens, cut.
- **Respect system settings & battery** — honor the OS haptic toggle (iOS *Settings → Sounds & Haptics*; Android *Settings → Sound & vibration → Touch feedback*/haptic strength) and Do-Not-Disturb. On Android this is automatic *only if* you use `performHapticFeedback` / tagged `VibrationAttributes` — raw `vibrate()` bypasses the user's choice and is a respect violation. iOS additionally suppresses `UIFeedbackGenerator` haptics in Low Power Mode — don't treat a haptic as guaranteed-delivered. Continuous/high-amplitude haptics drain battery and warm the actuator; reserve them for deliberate moments, not idle ambiance.
- **Don't fight platform defaults** — iOS pickers, switches, and `UIRefreshControl`, plus Material sliders/switches/pickers, already emit correct haptics. Adding your own on top double-fires. Inherit the platform's haptic where a standard control exists.
- **Reduce Motion ≠ haptic-off, but check the spirit** — a user on Reduce Motion often wants *calmer* feedback overall; gratuitous haptics undercut that intent even though they're a separate toggle. Lean conservative when accessibility settings signal sensitivity (→ §19).

## 11.7 UI sound design & multimodal events

- **System sounds vs. custom** — iOS `AudioServicesPlaySystemSound` / `AVAudioSession` earcons; Android `SoundPool` / `RingtoneManager` and notification-channel sounds. Prefer the platform's vocabulary (the OS keyboard click, the camera shutter) — users have learned its meanings, and it respects silent-mode rules you'd otherwise reimplement. Note the camera-shutter sound is **legally mandatory and unmutable** in some regions (Japan, South Korea); don't assume you can silence system audio everywhere.
- **Earcon** — a short, abstract, learnable sound mapped to an event (sent-message *whoosh*, error *thunk*). Keep them under ~500 ms, distinct from each other, and culturally neutral. Trap: a custom earcon that collides with a familiar system sound (your "send" sounding like the OS "error") miscommunicates. Distinguish from a **spearcon** (time-compressed speech) and an **auditory icon** (a real-world sound, e.g. trash crumple) — pick the cheapest that's unambiguous.
- **Silent-mode & focus rules** — UI feedback sounds must obey the hardware mute switch / Do-Not-Disturb; only alerting sounds (alarms, calls) may override, and only with the right audio category (`AVAudioSession.Category` / `.ambient` vs `.playback` on iOS; `AudioAttributes` usage on Android). Shipping a "tap" sound that ignores silent mode is an instant uninstall in a quiet room.
- **The unified feedback event** — model a single user-meaningful moment (e.g. "payment succeeded") as **one event with three coordinated tracks**: visual (checkmark animation), haptic (`.success` / `CONFIRM`), audio (success earcon). Fire them on the same frame; design them to feel like one perception, not three. Apple's AHAP and Android's audio-coupled haptics exist precisely to author haptic+audio as a single timeline. Trap: tracks authored separately by different people drift out of sync and read as broken.
- **Graceful degradation per modality** — the same event must still communicate with any one track removed: muted (no audio) → haptic + visual carry it; haptics off/unsupported (web, visionOS, budget Android, Low Power Mode) → audio + visual; screen unseen (wrist, pocket) → haptic + audio; screen-reader active → the event must also surface as an accessibility announcement (`UIAccessibility.post` / `announceForAccessibility`), since a checkmark animation is invisible to VoiceOver/TalkBack. Designing the event as "must survive losing any single channel" is the multimodal expression of the accessibility veto (→ §19): no information lives in exactly one sense.

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Playing haptics](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/playing-haptics); [Apple — Core Haptics](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/corehaptics); [Android — Haptics design principles](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/haptics/haptics-principles); [Android — Add haptic feedback to events](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/haptics/haptic-feedback); [MDN — Vibration API](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Vibration_API).*
