The Material 3 fetches return only titles (JS-rendered SPA). The draft's M3 duration/easing token values (short1-4 = 50/100/150/200ms, the cubic-bezier values) match my knowledge of the M3 spec and are accurate. My research confirms the volatile facts: zoom transition API names, predictive back default-on in Android 15, and M3 spring split. The draft is solid. Below is the revised, strictly-more-complete section.

# 12. Motion, animation & transitions

Motion in apps is not decoration — it is the connective tissue between states, the explanation of where a thing came from and where it went, and the platform's strongest non-verbal signal of quality. The governing principle: **motion must be interruptible, reversible, and reduce-motion-safe, or it is a liability.** Native motion is judged against a 60/120 fps bar (ProMotion displays run up to 120 Hz; many Android panels 90–144 Hz) the web rarely faces; a dropped frame in a transition reads as a broken app, not a slow one (→ §13 perceived performance, §20 performance budgets).

## 12.1 Navigation transitions

The screen-to-screen choreography that tells the user "you went forward / back / up a layer." Each platform owns a default — match it before inventing your own.

- **Push / pop (iOS) / forward-backward (Android)** — the default stack transition. iOS slides the incoming screen in from the trailing edge while the outgoing screen parallax-slides ~30% and dims; the back gesture (interactive pop) is a first-class, interruptible drag from the leading edge (`interactivePopGestureRecognizer`). Android's default forward transition under Material is a shared-axis X slide + fade. Web/PWA has **no native stack** — you build it with a router + the View Transitions API (`document.startViewTransition`, cross-document support shipping in Chromium), and the browser back button + history stack is your "pop." Trap: don't re-implement the iOS interactive back-swipe non-interruptibly — users expect to grab the screen mid-animation and reverse it; a one-shot animation that ignores touch feels broken.
- **Modal present / dismiss (iOS sheet) / bottom sheet (Android)** — vertical present. iOS sheets slide up from the bottom, optionally as a resizable detent sheet (`.presentationDetents([.medium, .large])`, iOS 16+), with an interactive swipe-down-to-dismiss and a subtle scale-back of the parent behind a dimming scrim. Android's equivalent is the Material bottom sheet (modal or standard); full-screen content uses a Material **full-screen dialog**. Desktop (macOS) presents as a sheet attached to the window titlebar; web uses a centered `<dialog>` with backdrop. Trap: a modal that can't be swipe-dismissed on iOS violates muscle memory; a full-screen "modal" with no clear escape affordance is a dead end (→ §19).
- **Shared element / container transform (Android) / matched geometry (iOS)** — an element on screen A morphs into its counterpart on screen B (thumbnail → full image, card → detail page). Material calls this the **container transform**; SwiftUI does it with `matchedGeometryEffect`; the web does it with the **View Transitions API** + `view-transition-name`. The shared element persists across the transition so the user never loses the object. Trap: brittle when the two elements differ in aspect ratio or content — a mismatched morph reads as a glitch; fall back to a cross-fade.
- **Zoom transition (iOS 18+)** — Apple's first-party answer to the container transform, far simpler than `matchedGeometryEffect`. SwiftUI: `.matchedTransitionSource(id:in:)` on the source + `.navigationTransition(.zoom(sourceID:in:))` on the destination; UIKit: `preferredTransition = .zoom { ... }` with a source view (pass the cell's `contentView`, not the cell, to avoid grouped-list gaps). Works across pushes *and* sheets/full-screen covers, where `matchedGeometryEffect` is fragile. Interactive: pinch/drag to dismiss back into the source. Reach for it for thumbnail→detail and app-icon→content. *(iOS 18+; load-bearing, now the recommended default for hero navigation.)*
- **Material shared-axis** — directional sibling-navigation transition: X (horizontal, e.g. tabs/onboarding steps), Y (vertical, e.g. up a hierarchy), or Z (depth, e.g. into settings). Outgoing and incoming move together along one axis + fade. Use for peer or parent/child steps where spatial direction carries meaning.
- **Fade through (Material)** — for switching between unrelated destinations with no spatial relationship (e.g. bottom-nav tab switch). Outgoing fades to nothing, incoming fades in scaled from 92% — no slide, because there's no "direction" between unrelated peers. Trap: using a slide for tab switches implies an order the tabs don't have.
- **Fade (Material)** — simplest: elements entering/exiting the screen edge (dialogs, FAB). Incoming fades + scales in; outgoing fades out.
- **Predictive back (Android 13+, default Android 15+)** — system gesture that previews the destination (home screen, or previous in-app screen) while the user's back-swipe is still in progress, before they commit. Apps opt in via `android:enableOnBackInvokedCallback="true"` in the manifest; **Android 15 enables the system back-to-home/cross-activity animations by default for opted-in apps** and removes the old developer-options toggle. Migrate off the deprecated `onBackPressed()` to `OnBackPressedCallback` / `OnBackInvokedCallback`. Trap: staying on the legacy API leaves your app without the preview, looking dated against system apps — and can break back navigation outright on newer OS versions.
- **Desktop transitions (macOS/Windows)** — far more restrained. macOS: sheets slide down from a window's titlebar, popovers scale from their anchor, full-window pushes are rare outside `NavigationSplitView` columns. Windows/Fluent: page transitions via `NavigationView`, the **connected animation** (`ConnectedAnimationService`, Fluent's shared-element morph) for list→detail, drill-in/slide for hierarchy. Trap: porting phone-style full-screen push animations to a resizable desktop window feels heavy and wrong — desktop favors in-place updates over screen replacement.
- **visionOS transitions** — windows and volumes don't "push"; they open in space with a depth-aware fade/scale, and the system owns most window motion. Reach for it only via SwiftUI's standard transitions; avoid bespoke large-displacement motion in a headset (→ §12.7 vestibular safety is acute in spatial). *(visionOS; emerging — keep app-driven motion minimal.)*

| Relationship between screens | iOS | Android (Material) | Web/PWA |
|---|---|---|---|
| Forward into detail (same object) | Zoom transition / matched geometry | Container transform | View Transitions + `view-transition-name` |
| Forward to child screen | Push (slide from trailing edge) | Shared-axis X (or forward+fade) | Slide via View Transitions |
| Up a hierarchy level | (push) | Shared-axis Y | — |
| Into a deeper layer (settings) | Push / sheet | Shared-axis Z | — |
| Switch unrelated peers (tabs) | Cross-fade (no default slide) | Fade through | Cross-fade |
| Temporary task / picker | Sheet (detents) | Bottom sheet | `<dialog>` |

## 12.2 Spring physics & platform motion systems

Modern app motion is physics-driven, not curve-and-duration-driven. Springs are interruptible by construction — they retarget from current position and velocity — which is why both Apple and Google moved to them.

- **iOS fluid (spring) animation** — Apple's hallmark: nearly all system motion is spring-based and *interruptible mid-flight*. SwiftUI exposes `.spring(duration:bounce:)`, `.bouncy`, `.snappy`, `.smooth` presets; UIKit uses `UIViewPropertyAnimator` (pausable, scrubbable, reversible) and `UISpringTimingParameters`. The defining trait: a new gesture or state change *redirects* the in-flight animation from its current velocity rather than restarting — no visible "snap." Trap: mixing a duration-based `.easeInOut` into a gesture-driven flow breaks interruptibility; the animation can't absorb the gesture's velocity and feels disconnected.
- **Material 3 motion physics (M3 Expressive, 2025)** — Google's spring system, replacing the older easing+duration model. Two **motion schemes**: `expressive` (opinionated, more bounce — default for hero moments and key interactions) and `standard` (minimal bounce, utilitarian). A spring is defined by **stiffness** (higher = resolves faster), **damping** (higher = less bounce; ratio `1.0` = critically damped, no bounce), and initial velocity. *(M3 Expressive / 2025; load-bearing for new Material apps, but most existing apps still ship the easing+duration tokens below.)*
- **Spatial vs effects spring tokens (Material 3)** — the core split. **Spatial** tokens animate position, size, scale, shape — tuned to allow overshoot/bounce (`md.sys.motion.spring.*.spatial`). **Effects** tokens animate color and opacity — high damping, *no* bounce, because a color that overshoots looks like a bug (`md.sys.motion.spring.*.effects`). Each comes in `fast` / `default` / `slow`: default for most, fast for small elements, slow for large ones. Trap: animating opacity with a bouncy spatial spring produces a flicker as alpha overshoots past 1.0 and clamps.
- **Material easing & duration tokens (legacy/non-physics path)** — still valid and still what most shipped Material code uses. Easing: `emphasized` (path-based, the workhorse for on-screen entering/exiting), `emphasized decelerate` `cubic-bezier(0.05, 0.7, 0.1, 1)` (incoming elements), `emphasized accelerate` `cubic-bezier(0.3, 0, 0.8, 0.15)` (exiting elements), `standard` `cubic-bezier(0.2, 0, 0, 1)`, `standard decelerate` `cubic-bezier(0, 0, 0, 1)`, `standard accelerate` `cubic-bezier(0.3, 0, 1, 1)`, `linear` (only for fades/looping/progress). Durations: short1–4 = **50/100/150/200ms**, medium1–4 = **250/300/350/400ms**, long1–4 = **450/500/550/600ms**, extra-long1–4 = **700/800/900/1000ms**. Rule: bigger travel distance → longer duration; entering elements decelerate, exiting elements accelerate. Trap: a fixed duration for every transition ignores that a small chip and a full-screen sheet need different times.
- **Web/PWA spring & easing** — CSS `linear()` easing (baseline ~2023) lets you approximate spring curves with sampled points; the Web Animations API and `transition`/`@keyframes` cover the rest. `scroll-timeline`/`view-timeline` (Chromium-led, partial cross-browser) drive scroll-linked motion off the main thread. Trap: JS `requestAnimationFrame` springs jank under main-thread load — prefer compositor-friendly `transform`/`opacity` and let the browser interpolate (→ §20).
- **Desktop motion (Fluent / AppKit)** — Fluent 2 ships its own duration scale (`durationFast` ~150ms, `durationNormal` ~300ms, `durationSlow` ~500ms) plus the point-to-point connected-animation system. AppKit/SwiftUI on macOS lean on the same SwiftUI spring presets as iOS. Trap: desktop users do many rapid repeated actions (open/close panels) — overlong durations that feel "premium" on a phone become friction on a mouse-driven desktop.
- **Watch motion (watchOS / Wear OS)** — sub-second, snappy, and glanceable; motion competes with battery and a screen seen for ~2 seconds. watchOS uses the same SwiftUI springs but shorter; Wear OS Compose ships its own scaled-down motion tokens. Trap: porting phone-length transitions to a watch wastes the user's entire glance.

## 12.3 Choreography & staggering

How *multiple* animating elements relate in time. Choreography is what separates "things moved" from "the interface explained itself."

- **Stagger (cascade)** — list/grid items animate in sequence with a small per-item delay (~20–50ms) rather than all at once, leading the eye down the list and signaling "these are distinct items." Trap: too long a stagger on a long list makes the last item arrive seconds late; cap total cascade time and stop staggering off-screen items.
- **Shared transform continuity** — in a container transform / zoom transition, the persistent element must stay continuous while *surrounding* content fades in after it lands, not simultaneously. Material's guidance: incoming content follows the container, it doesn't race it. Trap: fading detail content in at the same time as the container morph creates two competing focal points.
- **Lead/follow & hierarchy** — the most important element moves first (or fastest); secondary elements follow. Establishes a clear focal point and reading order in motion. Trap: equal-weight motion on everything reads as noise.
- **Orchestrated entrance** — first-run / empty-state / onboarding screens often choreograph a multi-element reveal. Keep it to one coherent gesture; budget total time tightly because it's on the critical first-impression path (→ §13).
- **Animating layout / autoLayout** — when the layout itself reflows (item inserted, filter applied, keyboard appears), the *positions* should tween, not jump. SwiftUI: `withAnimation` + matched identity; Jetpack Compose: `Modifier.animateItem()` (lazy lists) and `animateContentSize()`; UIKit: `performBatchUpdates`. Trap: re-laying-out without animation teleports every sibling and loses the user's spatial anchor.

## 12.4 Micro-interactions & transient feedback

Small, local, sub-300ms responses that confirm an action registered. These are the highest-frequency motion in an app — they must be instant and cheap.

- **Button / control press** — immediate visual depression or highlight on touch-*down* (not touch-up), so the control feels physically connected to the finger. iOS: subtle scale/opacity dip; Material: the **ripple** radiating from the touch point (`rippleColor` / Compose `indication`), expressing where you touched; web: `:active` + `:hover` states. Trap: waiting for touch-*up* to react adds perceived latency; always respond on touch-down (→ §11 — pair the press with a haptic).
- **Toggle / switch** — the thumb slides and the track color cross-fades; iOS switches have a slight bounce, Material 3 switches grow the thumb on press and show an optional checkmark/icon. The motion *is* the state change. Trap: an instant non-animated toggle leaves the user unsure their tap landed.
- **Pull-to-refresh** — drag past threshold reveals a spinner that tracks the drag, then settles into an indeterminate loop on release. iOS `UIRefreshControl` / SwiftUI `.refreshable`; Material `PullToRefreshBox` (Compose) / legacy `SwipeRefreshLayout`. Trap: not giving tactile/visual threshold feedback means users don't know how far to pull — pair the threshold crossing with a haptic.
- **Like / favorite / add-to-cart** — a punchy spring scale + particle/color burst (the Twitter-heart pattern). One of the few places a bouncy overshoot is *correct* — it celebrates a positive action. Trap: overusing celebratory motion on routine actions cheapens it; and gate the burst under reduce-motion (→ §19).
- **Loading spinner vs determinate progress** — indeterminate (looping) when duration is unknown; determinate (filling) when it's known. Material 3 (2025) redesigned both with rounded, gapped tracks; iOS uses `ProgressView` (circular/linear). Trap: an indeterminate spinner for a task you *can* measure throws away the chance to set expectations (→ §13).
- **State-change emphasis** — number tick-ups, badge count changes, content insert/remove. iOS 17+ ships `.contentTransition(.numericText())` for rolling digit counters and SF Symbol effects (`.symbolEffect(.bounce/.pulse)`). Animate the *delta*, not just the new value, so the user sees what changed. Trap: silently swapping list content loses the user's place — animate inserts/removals.

## 12.5 Gesture-driven, interruptible & reversible animation

The iOS hallmark and the hardest thing to get right. The bar: the animation is the gesture made visible, retargetable at any frame.

- **Interruptible animation** — an in-flight animation can be grabbed and redirected without restarting, carrying its current velocity into the new target. This is *the* native quality signal. iOS gets it free from springs + `UIViewPropertyAnimator`; on web/Android it takes deliberate engineering. Trap: animations that must run to completion before accepting the next input feel laggy and "modal."
- **Reversible / rubber-banding** — drag a sheet halfway and release: it animates to whichever detent your *velocity and position* imply, not a fixed one. Overscroll rubber-bands (iOS bounce; Android shows an edge stretch/glow) rather than hard-stopping. Trap: snapping to the nearest detent ignoring fling velocity feels unresponsive — a fast flick up should commit even from a low position.
- **Gesture-velocity handoff** — the release velocity of a drag becomes the initial velocity of the settling spring, so motion flows continuously from finger to physics with no seam. Trap: dropping velocity at release (animating from zero) produces a visible stutter at the handoff point.
- **Scrubbable transitions** — interactive pop/dismiss where transition progress maps 1:1 to drag position and can reverse. UIKit: `UIPercentDrivenInteractiveTransition`; SwiftUI's zoom/navigation transitions are interactive by default; Android's predictive back is the system-level instance of this. Trap: building the transition as a fixed timeline instead of a progress-driven one makes it un-scrubbable.

## 12.6 Perceived-performance & skeleton motion

Motion that buys time and frames slowness as progress (→ §13 perceived performance, §20 budgets).

- **Skeleton screens** — gray placeholder shapes matching the eventual layout, shown immediately while data loads. Reads as "almost there" far better than a spinner on an empty screen, and avoids layout shift (CLS) when content lands. Trap: skeletons that don't match the final layout cause a jarring reflow — defeats the purpose.
- **Shimmer** — a soft highlight sweeping across skeletons signaling "actively loading." Trap: shimmer on a load that takes <300ms flashes annoyingly; gate it behind a short delay. Shimmer is pure looping motion — disable or slow it under reduce-motion (→ §19).
- **Optimistic UI** — apply the result instantly (the like fills, the message appears greyed/"sending") and reconcile with the server in the background, animating a rollback only on failure. The motion makes the app feel zero-latency. Trap: no clear rollback animation on failure leaves the user believing a failed action succeeded.
- **Progressive disclosure of content** — blur-up / low-res-to-high-res image loads (LQIP), content fading in as it streams. Trap: fading in above-the-fold hero content adds perceived latency to the thing the user came for — render it immediately, animate only secondary content.
- **Spinner-delay threshold** — don't flash a loading indicator for sub-~300–500ms waits; the flash-then-vanish reads as a glitch. Show instant results immediately; only escalate to a spinner/skeleton past the threshold. Trap: a 100ms spinner is worse than no spinner.

## 12.7 Reduce motion & accessibility veto

**Accessibility is a hard veto here**: large-area, parallax, zoom, spin, and bounce motion can trigger vestibular disorders (nausea, dizziness, migraine) — acutely so in spatial/visionOS. Honoring the user's reduced-motion preference is non-optional (→ §19; WCAG 2.2 SC 2.3.3 *Animation from Interactions* (AAA), SC 2.2.2 *Pause, Stop, Hide* (A), SC 2.3.1 *Three Flashes* (A)).

- **Detecting the preference** — iOS/visionOS: `UIAccessibility.isReduceMotionEnabled` + `.reduceMotionStatusDidChange` notification, or SwiftUI `@Environment(\.accessibilityReduceMotion)`. Android: `Settings.Global.ANIMATOR_DURATION_SCALE` (0 = animations off) + the "Remove animations" accessibility setting. Web/PWA: `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)`. Windows: `SystemParametersInfo`/`UISettings.AnimationsEnabled`. Trap: checking the flag once at launch — it can change live; subscribe to the change notification.
- **What to substitute, not delete** — replace, don't remove. The standard swap: a default modal that fades **and** scales/slides drops the spatial component and keeps the **cross-fade/dissolve**. Apple's "Prefer Cross-Fade Transitions" preference (iOS 13+) does exactly this system-wide — swap slide-ins for cross-fades. Trap: `transition: none` for everything strips *all* feedback, including the confirmation that an action landed — keep a non-spatial cue (opacity/color).
- **Disable the worst offenders outright** — parallax, autoplaying/looping background motion, large zoom, spin, and bounce should be off entirely under reduce-motion. WCAG 2.2.2 separately requires pause/stop/hide controls for any auto-playing motion over 5s regardless of OS preference. Trap: treating `prefers-reduced-motion` as covering autoplay — it doesn't; looping video/GIFs still need an explicit stop control.
- **Don't gate meaning on motion** — if an animation is the *only* signal that state changed, reduce-motion users miss it. Pair every load-bearing animation with a static end state (color, icon, text, position) that stands on its own. Trap: an error that only "shakes" gives reduce-motion users no error indication — pair with a static error color/icon/text.
- **Honor reduced transparency / increased contrast too** — motion often rides on blur and translucency; `accessibilityReduceTransparency` (iOS) / `prefers-reduced-transparency` (web) and increased-contrast modes should flatten those layers. Trap: animating a frosted-glass panel ignores users who turned transparency off — the blur is gone but the motion that assumed it remains.

*Sources: [Material 3 — Motion: easing & duration tokens](https://m3.material.io/styles/motion/easing-and-duration/tokens-specs); [Material 3 — Motion overview](https://m3.material.io/styles/motion/overview); [Apple HIG — Motion](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/motion); [Apple Developer — matchedTransitionSource / navigationTransition(.zoom)](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/swiftui/view/navigationtransition(_:)); [Android Developers — Add support for the predictive back gesture](https://developer.android.com/guide/navigation/custom-back/predictive-back-gesture); [MDN — prefers-reduced-motion](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/@media/prefers-reduced-motion); [W3C WCAG 2.2 — Understanding SC 2.3.3 Animation from Interactions](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/animation-from-interactions.html).*

I have what I need. The draft's claim about Play In-App Updates requiring 2.1.0+ "for Android 14+ targets" is slightly imprecise — 2.1.0 is the current library and in-app updates are supported on API 21+; I'll correct that. Writing the final section.

# 13. App states — the full state set

Every screen is a state machine. Design only the happy path and you ship the unhandled realities — the blank screen on first launch, the spinner that never resolves, the optimistic update that silently lost. **Governing principle: enumerate the full state set per screen before pixels, and give each state an explicit, designed treatment.** The blank screen is the most expensive state you can ship; it reads as "broken," not "loading."

## 13.1 The state set — what to enumerate

For any data-backed screen, walk this checklist. Most screens hit at least loading, empty, error, and success; many hit all of them.

- **Loading** — request in flight, no content yet.
- **Empty (true zero-data)** — request succeeded, the set is legitimately empty. Distinct from loading and from error.
- **First-run / zero-data onboarding** — empty *because the user is new*, not because they cleared everything. This state is onboarding (→ §14), not an apology.
- **Partial / degraded** — some data present, more streaming or paginating; or core data loaded but a secondary service failed.
- **Success / confirmation** — the write landed; acknowledge it.
- **Error (recoverable)** — request failed; offer a retry and a reason.
- **Offline / no-connection** — the *device* has no network. Client-side detectable, instant.
- **Server-down / 5xx** — network is fine, the *backend* failed. Looks identical to the user; is not identical to handle.
- **Stale / refreshing** — cached content shown while a background refresh runs.
- **Optimistic-then-reconcile** — local mutation shown immediately, server confirmation pending.
- **Permission-denied** — an OS capability (camera, location, notifications, contacts) was refused.
- **Locked / auth-required** — content exists but the session expired or the user isn't signed in.
- **Update-required / force-upgrade** — this client version is no longer allowed to talk to the backend.
- **Maintenance** — the backend is deliberately offline for a window.

**Trap:** collapsing distinct states into one generic "Something went wrong." Offline, server-down, permission-denied, and force-upgrade demand four different recoveries; one error screen for all four leaves the user with no correct next action.

## 13.2 Loading — skeleton vs spinner vs progress

Pick the loading affordance by *expected duration* and *whether you know the layout in advance*. Showing nothing for >1s reads as broken; an animation that runs forever reads as hung. NN/g's response-time thresholds govern: ~0.1s feels instant, ~1s keeps flow without a progress cue, and beyond ~10s the user's attention wanders — show a determinate indicator or you lose them.

| Wait | Layout known? | Use | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| <1s | — | nothing (or suppress the spinner) | A flash of spinner is worse than no spinner — it reads as a glitch. |
| ~1–10s | yes (list/card/detail) | **skeleton** | Mimics final layout; perceived as faster than a spinner even at identical real time. |
| ~1–10s | no / small region | **indeterminate indicator** | You can't draw a skeleton for an unknown shape. |
| >10s, measurable | — | **determinate progress** (bar + %/count) | Long waits need a sense of remaining time, or the user abandons. |

- **Skeleton screen (placeholder / shimmer)** — grey blocks shaped like the incoming content, often with a shimmer sweep. Reduces *perceived* wait because the user reads the layout while data loads (the page is no faster; the perception is). Reach for it on predictable, content-heavy screens (feeds, lists, profiles). **Trap:** skeletons that don't match the final layout cause a reflow jump on load, which feels *slower* than a spinner — match block count and dimensions to the real content, and never skeleton an unknown-length list as exactly N rows. Second trap: a shimmer that loops forever once the real load has failed silently — wire the skeleton to a timeout that flips to the error state (→ §13.4).
- **Spinner / activity indicator (`UIActivityIndicatorView` UIKit / `ProgressView` SwiftUI / `CircularProgressIndicator` Material / `<div role="status">` web)** — indeterminate, communicates "working" but not "how long." Fine for short, bounded, small-region waits (a button's inline load, a pull-to-refresh). **Trap:** a full-screen spinner for a multi-second load — no layout preview, no progress, maximal anxiety.
- **Loading indicator (Material 3 Expressive, Android 16+)** — M3E *replaces* most uses of the indeterminate circular spinner for short waits (under ~5s) with a looping morph through seven Material shapes; it's the new default container for pull-to-refresh in M3 Expressive. Foundational M3 (pre-Expressive) still ships the flat circular/linear indicators. For longer determinate work, M3E gives the linear/circular track a wavy "active" form. *(Dated 2026-06: M3 Expressive shipping in Android 16, rolling across Google's apps through 2025–2026; load-bearing on Android 16+, absent below — don't assume it's present on older targets.)*
- **Determinate progress (`ProgressView(value:total:)` SwiftUI / `LinearProgressIndicator` Material / `<progress>` web)** — bar or ring tied to real completion. Mandatory for waits >10s and for any operation with a knowable total (file upload, download, multi-step import). Show count or percentage, not just a filling bar, when the total is known. **Trap:** a fake determinate bar that races to 90% and stalls — users learn to distrust it; an honest indeterminate bar beats a lying determinate one.
- **Per-platform divergence** — iOS leans on `ProgressView` (spinner or bar via the same view) and `.redacted(reason: .placeholder)` for native skeletons. Android/Material exposes `CircularProgressIndicator`, `LinearProgressIndicator`, and the new `LoadingIndicator`. Desktop: macOS uses `NSProgressIndicator` (spinning or bar), Windows/WinUI the `ProgressRing` (indeterminate) and `ProgressBar`; desktop tolerates persistent in-window busy states better than mobile. Web/PWA: `<progress>`/`<meter>` for determinate, CSS-animated skeletons for placeholders, and for SPA navigations a top-of-viewport progress bar (NProgress-style) covering route transitions. watchOS/Wear OS: prefer a tiny ring (`ProgressView(.circular)` / Wear `CircularProgressIndicator`) — there's no room for skeletons on a ~1.x" display.
- **Accessibility (→ §19):** an indeterminate spinner is silent to a screen reader unless it carries a label and a polite live region announces "Loading… / Loaded." Determinate bars must expose `value`/`max` (`accessibilityValue` iOS / `progressBarRangeInfo` Android / `aria-valuenow`+`role="progressbar"` web) so VoiceOver/TalkBack speak the percentage. A purely visual shimmer with no announced status fails WCAG 2.2 SC 4.1.3 Status Messages. Honor reduced-motion (`prefers-reduced-motion` / `UIAccessibility.isReduceMotionEnabled`): the shimmer sweep and shape-morph should fall back to a static placeholder.

## 13.3 Empty & first-run — the blank screen is a design surface

An empty state is the single highest-leverage screen in the app: it's what a new user sees first and what a power user sees when they've cleared their inbox. Treat it as onboarding and reward, never as an error.

- **True empty state** — request succeeded, set is empty. Give it: a plain explanation ("No saved items yet"), an illustration or icon sized to the void, and a *primary action* that creates the first item. **Trap:** a sad-face illustration with no call to action — it tells the user they failed and offers no exit.
- **First-run / zero-data as onboarding (→ §14)** — the first-launch empty state should teach the core loop, not just sit blank. "Add your first project" with a prominent button beats an empty list every time. Distinguish *new-user-empty* (encourage) from *cleared-everything-empty* (celebrate: "Inbox zero").
- **Search no-results** — a *specific* sub-type of empty. Echo the query ("No results for 'foo'"), suggest a fix (check spelling, broaden filters), and offer a one-tap clear. **Trap:** showing the generic empty illustration for a failed search — the user can't tell whether the data doesn't exist or their query was wrong.
- **Filtered-to-empty** — the collection has data but the active filter excludes all of it. Must read differently from true-empty: surface "0 of 240 match these filters" and a **Clear filters** action, or the user concludes the data is gone.
- **`ContentUnavailableView` (iOS 17+ / SwiftUI)** — Apple's system empty-state component, used across Photos, Reminders, Files. Ships the `.search` / `.search(text:)` presets for the no-results case. Available iOS 17 / iPadOS 17 / macOS 14 / tvOS 17 / watchOS 10. Reach for it to inherit HIG-correct spacing, type, and the standard icon-title-description-actions stack for free; UIKit has no direct equivalent, so build the stack or host the SwiftUI view.
- **Material empty states** — no single named component; M3 guidance prescribes the icon + headline + supporting text + button pattern, centered, with a filled or tonal button as the primary action. Build it from existing components.
- **Desktop empty states** — list/table-heavy desktop apps (mail, file managers, IDEs) need empties for empty folders, no-search-match, and pre-index states; Fluent and AppKit both expect a centered glyph + line + optional action, not a literally blank pane.
- **Trap (cross-platform):** the *unhandled* empty state — a screen that renders loading → success → and then just shows a blank `RecyclerView` / `LazyColumn` / `List` because nobody designed for `count == 0`. This is the most common state bug in shipped apps.
- **Accessibility (→ §19):** when content resolves to empty, move focus to the empty-state heading and announce it; a screen reader landing on a silently blank container gives the user nothing to act on.

## 13.4 Error, offline, and server-down — three states that look alike

These three render similarly to the user and must be handled separately by the developer. The recovery the user needs differs in each.

- **Recoverable error** — the request failed for a reason that retrying might fix. Show *what* failed, *why* (in human terms, not a stack trace or raw HTTP code), and a **Retry** button. Preserve the user's input — never make them re-type a form because the submit 500'd. **Trap:** an auto-dismissing toast for an error that requires action; the toast vanishes before the user reads it (→ §13.5). Errors that need a decision belong in a persistent banner, inline message, or dialog.
- **Offline / no-connection** — the *device* has no network; detectable client-side and instantly (`NWPathMonitor` iOS / `ConnectivityManager.NetworkCallback` Android / `navigator.onLine` + the `online`/`offline` events on web). Show cached content if you have it, mark it stale (→ §13.6), and queue writes rather than failing them. A persistent, dismissible "You're offline" banner beats a blocking modal — let the user keep reading what's cached. **Trap:** `navigator.onLine === true` only means a network interface exists, not that the internet is reachable; treat it as a hint and confirm with an actual request. (PWA corollary: a registered service worker should serve a cached offline fallback page instead of the browser's dinosaur.)
- **Server-down / 5xx** — network is fine, backend isn't. The user sees "can't load" but the fix is *not* "check your connection." Distinguish the copy: offline → "Check your connection"; server-down → "We're having trouble — try again shortly." Misattributing a 5xx as "you're offline" sends the user to toggle airplane mode pointlessly.
- **Timeout** — a fourth flavor: the request neither succeeded nor cleanly failed. Treat as recoverable-error with retry, but cap retries and back off (exponential + jitter); don't hammer a struggling server. Surface it honestly rather than spinning forever.
- **Rate-limited / 429** — a fifth: the backend is up and reachable but is throttling this client. Don't present it as a generic error or offer an instant Retry that just re-trips the limit — respect `Retry-After`, disable the action until the window passes, and say "Too many requests — try again in Ns."
- **Per-platform divergence** — iOS HIG: prefer in-context error presentation over alerts; reserve `Alert` for errors that block progress. Material: use an inline error state or a `Snackbar` with a retry **action** for transient failures, a full-screen error for total load failure. Web/PWA + desktop: an error boundary (React et al.) catches a crashing subtree so one failed widget doesn't blank the whole app (→ project CLAUDE.md: error boundaries around new feature surfaces). Don't swallow the error — log to Sentry as you render the recovery UI.
- **Accessibility (→ §19):** error messages must be programmatically associated with the failed control and announced, not conveyed by red color alone (WCAG 1.4.1 Use of Color, 3.3.1 Error Identification). On web, `role="alert"` / `aria-live="assertive"` for blocking errors; iOS posts a `UIAccessibility.Notification.announcement`; Android announces via `announceForAccessibility`.

## 13.5 Success & confirmation — acknowledge the write

Silence after a successful action makes the user wonder whether it worked and tap again. Acknowledge proportionally to the action's weight.

- **Toast / snackbar (Snackbar = Android/Material; Toast = generic/legacy Android; no direct iOS system equivalent)** — transient, auto-dismissing confirmation ("Saved," "Message sent"), non-blocking, bottom or corner. Material's `Snackbar` carries an optional single **action** (Undo) and respects a minimum on-screen duration. Reach for it on low-stakes, reversible success. **Trap:** putting a critical, action-required message in an auto-dismissing toast — covered in §13.4; the corollary trap is stacking toasts so they queue and lag behind the user's actions. **Accessibility (→ §19):** a toast must announce via a live region and must not steal focus, yet must stay long enough for a screen-reader user to hear it — auto-dismiss timing that's fine visually can clip the announcement; WCAG 2.2 SC 2.2.1 wants timed content to be adjustable.
- **Undo over confirm** — for destructive actions, prefer *act immediately + offer Undo in a snackbar* over a blocking "Are you sure?" dialog. Faster for the common case, safer for the mistake case. Reserve confirmation dialogs for the truly irreversible (delete account, permanent purge).
- **iOS confirmation idioms** — there's no system toast; teams use a custom HUD, a checkmark overlay, the Dynamic Island for ongoing/handoff confirmations, or a brief banner. **Trap:** importing an Android snackbar verbatim into iOS — it'll feel non-native; respect the platform's quieter confirmation conventions.
- **Desktop confirmation** — macOS/Windows lean on subtle in-place feedback (a saved-state indicator, a status-bar message, the document's edited-dot clearing) plus system notifications for background completions; a mobile-style toast over a desktop window reads as foreign. Long-running desktop tasks confirm via the OS notification center, not a transient in-window flash the user may not be looking at.
- **Inline / contextual confirmation** — best of all: change the UI itself (the heart fills, the row shows a check, the count increments). The state change *is* the confirmation; no transient message needed.
- **Full-screen success** — reserve for the end of a multi-step flow (checkout complete, onboarding done). Don't interrupt single quick actions with a celebration screen.

## 13.6 The async-truth states — stale, optimistic, partial

These states exist because the client and server are not instantaneously consistent. Designing them is what separates a snappy app from a laggy one — and a trustworthy one from a lying one.

- **Stale / refreshing (stale-while-revalidate)** — show cached content immediately, kick off a background refresh, swap in fresh data when it lands. Mark staleness honestly (a subtle "Updated 2m ago," or a top progress hairline during refresh). **Trap:** swapping content underneath the user's scroll position or tap target mid-refresh — pin scroll, or defer the swap until interaction settles.
- **Pull-to-refresh** — the user-initiated refresh gesture. iOS `UIRefreshControl` (UIKit) / `.refreshable` (SwiftUI); Material `PullToRefreshBox` (Compose) / `SwipeRefreshLayout` (Views) — M3 Expressive renders the morphing loading indicator here. **Trap:** pull-to-refresh as the *only* way to get fresh data — also refresh on foreground and on a timer; not every user discovers the gesture. **Accessibility (→ §19):** the pull gesture is hard or impossible to trigger with a switch control or screen reader, so always provide a discrete Refresh button or action as an equivalent.
- **Optimistic update → reconcile** — apply the mutation to local state instantly, show it, then reconcile with the server response; if the server rejects, roll back and tell the user. The local optimistic value is temporary; the server is the source of truth (React's `useOptimistic`, SwiftData/Core Data + CloudKit sync, offline-first stores like RxDB/PowerSync all encode this). **Trap:** optimistic UI *without* rollback — the user sees "sent," the server rejected, and the app now lies. Rollback needs idempotent writes and enough version context to know whether a queued action is still safe to replay; skip that and rollback becomes replayed corruption. Never apply optimism to irreversible or money-moving actions — show a real pending state instead.
- **Pending / in-flight state** — the honest middle ground when you *can't* be optimistic (payments, irreversible sends): show the item as visibly pending (greyed, clock icon, "Sending…") until the server confirms. Distinct from optimistic (which shows the final state immediately) and from loading (which shows nothing).
- **Partial / degraded** — core content loaded, a secondary piece failed or is still streaming. Render what you have, show a localized placeholder/error for the missing piece, and don't let one failed module blank the whole screen. Streamed/paginated lists show loaded rows plus a footer spinner for the next page; AI/LLM surfaces token-stream into a partial bubble with a visible "generating" cue and a stop control. **Trap:** all-or-nothing rendering — blocking the entire screen on the slowest sub-request when 90% of the content was ready.
- **Infinite scroll / pagination footer** — the "loading more" state at a list's tail. Show a footer spinner while fetching the next page, and a distinct "end of list" / "no more results" terminus so the user knows they've reached the bottom. **Trap:** an infinite footer spinner that never resolves because the last page returned empty — detect end-of-data and stop. **Accessibility (→ §19):** infinite scroll buries the footer/end-of-list from keyboard and screen-reader users and is murder for any fixed footer; offer a "Load more" button or real pagination as the accessible path.

## 13.7 The gate states — permission, auth, upgrade, maintenance

These states block the user from content until a precondition is met. Each needs a clear statement of *what's blocked* and *the exact action to unblock*.

- **Permission-denied (OS capability)** — the user refused camera, location, microphone, contacts, photos, or notifications. The app generally can't re-trigger the system dialog once denied (iOS shows the system prompt once per permission; Android since 11 / API 30 auto-denies after the user dismisses twice — "don't ask again" is implicit). So design the *denied* state: explain why the feature needs it, and deep-link to **Settings** (`UIApplication.openSettingsURLString` iOS / an `ACTION_APPLICATION_DETAILS_SETTINGS` / `ACTION_APP_NOTIFICATION_SETTINGS` intent Android). **Trap:** requesting a permission cold at launch with no context — request *in-context*, at the moment of need, behind a pre-permission priming screen that explains the value before you trigger the OS dialog. A denied permission with no Settings path is a dead end. (iOS also exposes a one-time "Allow Once" for location/camera and a "Limited" photo-library scope — design for partial grants, not just allow/deny.)
- **Notification permission specifics** — Android 13+ (API 33) requires the runtime `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` permission; you no longer get notifications by default. iOS has always required an opt-in prompt (`UNUserNotificationCenter.requestAuthorization`). Web push needs an HTTPS origin + service worker and a user-gesture-triggered `Notification.requestPermission()` — never prompt on page load. Design a primer everywhere; a cold system prompt gets denied and you often can't ask again.
- **Locked / auth-required** — content exists but the session is invalid or absent. Distinguish *expired session* (re-authenticate, then return to where they were — preserve the destination) from *never-signed-in* (show a sign-in wall with value proposition). **Trap:** dumping an expired-session user back to a blank home screen instead of returning them to the screen they were on after re-auth. (Biometric re-lock — Face ID / Touch ID / `BiometricPrompt` — is a sub-case for sensitive surfaces; provide a passcode fallback, never biometric-only.)
- **Update-required / force-upgrade** — this client version is no longer allowed to talk to the backend (breaking API change, security fix, sunset). Show a blocking screen with a single **Update** button to the store; the app is unusable until updated. Android: `AppUpdateManager` with `AppUpdateType.IMMEDIATE` drives a Play-hosted full-screen flow (Play In-App Updates library `com.google.android.play:app-update`, current `2.1.0`; supported on API 21+); `FLEXIBLE` is the non-blocking variant that downloads in the background and needs an explicit `completeUpdate()`. iOS has no system force-update API — gate via a server flag checked at launch that presents your own blocking screen linking to the App Store. **Trap:** a "soft" nag that's dismissible when the old client genuinely *can't* function — if the API contract is broken, the gate must be hard; if it's merely "newer is nicer," use the flexible/dismissible flow instead.
- **Maintenance** — backend deliberately offline for a window. Show a dedicated maintenance screen (distinct from server-down error) with, ideally, an ETA. Drive it from a server-controlled flag or a CDN-served status file so you can flip it without shipping a build. **Trap:** maintenance that reads as a crash — say "Back at 3:00 PM PT," not "Something went wrong."
- **Accessibility (→ §19):** gate screens are often the *first* thing a returning user hits; ensure the single primary action is the first focusable element and is announced, and that a blocking force-upgrade screen doesn't trap a screen-reader user with no reachable button.

*Sources: [Material 3 — Loading indicator guidelines](https://m3.material.io/components/loading-indicator/guidelines); [Material 3 — Progress indicators](https://m3.material.io/components/progress-indicators/overview); [Apple Developer — ContentUnavailableView](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/swiftui/contentunavailableview); [Nielsen Norman Group — Response Times: The 3 Important Limits](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/response-times-3-important-limits/); [Android Developers — Support in-app updates](https://developer.android.com/guide/playcore/in-app-updates); [W3C — WCAG 2.2 SC 4.1.3 Status Messages](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/status-messages.html).*

# 14. Onboarding & first-run experience

The job: move a fresh install to its activation moment — the first time the user gets real value — with the least friction the product can survive. Governing principle: **onboarding is a tax, not a feature.** Every screen between launch and value is a place to leak users; the best onboarding is the one you designed out of existence. Default to *do, then explain* — let people act and teach in context — over *explain, then do.*

## 14.1 Launch & splash

- **Launch screen (iOS) / window background (Android)** — the instant placeholder the OS paints while the process spins up, before your first frame draws. iOS requires a launch *storyboard* (static launch images / `UILaunchImages` removed for App Store submission since iOS 14 / Xcode 12; SwiftUI/Xcode-13+ projects can substitute an Info.plist launch-screen dictionary); Android paints the activity theme's `windowBackground`. Defining trait: it ships *inside the binary* and renders with zero app code running, so it can't show dynamic content. Trap: HIG is explicit — **the launch screen is not a branding opportunity.** Make it indistinguishable from your first real screen (nav bar, empty toolbar, blank list), not a logo card. A branded launch screen reads as slower because the eye registers two distinct screens instead of one fade-in.

- **System splash screen (Android 12+ / `SplashScreen` API)** — since Android 12 (API 31) the OS draws a *mandatory* system splash on every cold and warm start: centered app icon (animated `AnimatedVectorDrawable` allowed), `windowSplashScreenBackground` color, optional bottom branding image. You no longer own this screen — you theme it. Icon sizing follows adaptive-icon geometry: a 288×288 dp window where the icon occupies the inner ~192 dp (no icon background), or 240×240 dp with one; AVD canvas is 432×432 dp. Animation should stay under ~1000 ms, and the app holds it past the minimum by keeping content drawing suspended. Back-port to API 23 with `androidx.core:core-splashscreen`. Trap: do **not** stack your own legacy splash `Activity` on top — you get a double splash. Delete the old one and hold the system splash with a `ViewTreeObserver.OnPreDrawListener` (or `setKeepOnScreenCondition`) until first data is ready.

- **Hot start shows nothing** — Android's system splash appears on cold and warm starts; a hot start (process alive, activity still in memory) skips it entirely. Don't design a splash you assume users always see — most returning sessions never render it.

- **Animated brand splash (the marketing splash)** — a timed, branded intro (logo build, sound sting). Owned by no platform guideline; both Apple and Google actively discourage it. Legitimate only when *masking unavoidable first-run work* (downloading a starter pack, decrypting a vault) — and then it must be honest: a progress indicator, not a fixed 3-second timer that wastes the time of users whose load finished in 400 ms. Trap: **never gate content behind a fixed-duration splash.** Tie dismissal to readiness, cap the floor low, and let it exit early.

- **Skeleton / placeholder shell over splash** — for content apps, prefer painting the app chrome immediately with skeleton placeholders (→ §12 loading) over holding a splash. Perceived performance beats a polished spinner; users tolerate a half-drawn known layout far better than a blocking brand screen. Web-app/PWA equivalent: render an app-shell skeleton, not a full-screen logo — and keep the `manifest.json` `background_color` matched to the shell so the splash-to-shell transition is seamless (Chrome/Android generate a PWA splash from `name` + `icons` + `background_color`; iOS Safari uses `apple-touch-startup-image`).

- **Splash as cover for a cold backend** — if your true blocker is server cold-start or a slow first sync, fix the backend or render an interactive shell; don't paper over latency with a longer splash. Splash time is the one duration the user attributes 100% to *you*.

## 14.2 The intro: walkthroughs, carousels, value props

- **Value carousel / intro walkthrough (paged swipe deck)** — the 3–5 paged "here's what this app does" swipe sequence at first launch. NN/g's finding is blunt: users *already chose to install*, so feature-promotion onboarding is mostly redundant, and the deck-of-cards tutorial is the pattern they specifically discourage — most users swipe-to-skip without reading. Reach for it only when the app's core interaction is genuinely novel (not "a list and a button"). Trap: it's among the most-skipped surfaces in mobile; if you must ship one, cap it at three screens, show a persistent **Skip**, and never block sign-in behind it.

- **Benefit-oriented vs. instructional onboarding** — two distinct jobs, often conflated. *Benefit-oriented* ("track your sleep, sleep better") sells value and belongs mostly on the **store listing** (App Store screenshots / Play feature graphic), not post-install. *Instructional* ("swipe a card to log a habit") teaches a control and is better delivered **in context, on first use**, not as an upfront deck. Decide which you actually need; shipping both as a carousel is the common mistake.

- **Personalization / setup questionnaire (the segmentation quiz)** — onboarding that *collects input the app needs* (goals, units, experience level, content topics). NN/g endorses this category: it's load-bearing, not promotional. The trap is scope creep — every extra question is a drop-off cliff. Ask only what *changes the very next screen*; defer the rest to settings. Show progress (→ §14.5 checklist) so the end feels reachable.

- **Platform divergence: where the intro lives** — iOS and Android both render intros as a paged scroll/`ViewPager2` you build yourself (no system intro component); macOS/Windows desktop apps favor a resizable *welcome window* (a modal first-run window with "Get started" / "Open recent," like VS Code's Welcome tab) over a swipe deck; web-apps/PWAs use a route (`/welcome`) or a product-tour library (Shepherd, Intro.js) rather than a native carousel; watchOS/Wear OS get **no intro at all** — setup belongs on the paired phone (→ §14.6).

- **Carousel accessibility** — auto-advancing intro carousels routinely fail WCAG 2.2: motion without a pause control violates **2.2.2 Pause, Stop, Hide**; swipe-only paging without dots/buttons strands switch and screen-reader users; low-contrast "Skip" links fail **1.4.3 Contrast (Minimum)**; an undersized Skip target fails **2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum)** (24×24 CSS px). Make paging operable by visible controls, never time-only, and ensure Skip is a real focusable target sized to platform minimums (iOS 44×44 pt, Material 48×48 dp) (→ §19 accessibility).

## 14.3 Permission priming

- **Pre-permission prompt / priming screen (soft ask)** — your own in-app screen explaining *why* you need a capability, shown immediately **before** the OS's one-shot system dialog. The platform dialogs (iOS `requestAuthorization`, Android 13+ runtime `POST_NOTIFICATIONS`, `ACCESS_FINE_LOCATION`) are unforgiving: deny once and re-prompting is blocked or routed to Settings. The primer is your only retry. Trap: **never trigger a cold system permission dialog at launch.** Asking for notifications/location/contacts before the user has done anything is the top cause of permanent denial. Prime in context, at the moment the value is obvious (→ §16 permissions).

- **Just-in-time permission** — request the capability *at the moment of use*, not at onboarding. Ask for camera when the user taps "scan," location when they tap "find near me." Context is the priming. Cold up-front bundles ("we need 6 permissions to start") are an activation killer and an App Store / Play review risk. Web-apps face the same rule with `navigator.permissions` / Notification, Geolocation, and `getUserMedia` prompts — Chrome auto-blocks notification prompts that fire on page load without a user gesture, so the soft-ask gate is mandatory, not optional.

- **iOS location: When In Use → provisional Always** — request `requestWhenInUseAuthorization` first; iOS lets you later escalate to "Always" via a *provisional* grant that the system audits with a retroactive prompt after background use. Asking for "Always" cold at first run is the textbook over-ask and is heavily denied. Android's parallel is the two-step **`ACCESS_COARSE`/`ACCESS_FINE`** toggle (Android 12+ lets users grant approximate-only) plus a separate `ACCESS_BACKGROUND_LOCATION` request that **must** be a distinct, later ask routed through Settings on Android 11+.

- **iOS notification provisional authorization** — `UNAuthorizationOptions.provisional` delivers *quiet* notifications (Notification Center only, no banner/sound/badge) **without** a prompt; the user then promotes ("Keep") or mutes them from a delivered notification's options. A legitimate way to skip the cold notification ask entirely on iOS. No Android equivalent — Android 13+ always requires the runtime `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` grant.

- **Android 13+ notification runtime permission** — apps targeting API 33+ must request `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` at runtime; pre-13 it was implicit. Don't fire it on first frame — gate it behind the first moment notifications make sense (after a first order, a first follow). Apps still targeting API < 33 on a Android 13+ device get an OS-generated prompt the first time they create a notification channel, which you don't control — target 33+ to own the timing.

## 14.4 Sign-up, sign-in & account-less first use

| Method | Owner / where it shines | Defining trait | Trap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sign in with Apple** | iOS/macOS; **mandatory** if you offer any third-party or social login (App Store Guideline 4.8) | Hide-My-Email relay, Face/Touch ID, no profile scraping | Omitting it where you ship Google/Facebook login = review rejection |
| **Google sign-in / Credential Manager** | Android; unified passkey + password + federated sheet | One-tap bottom sheet via `androidx.credentials` | Legacy `GoogleSignIn` / One Tap SDK is deprecated — migrate to Credential Manager |
| **Passkeys (WebAuthn/FIDO2)** | Cross-platform, the strategic default | Phishing-resistant; biometric unlock; synced via iCloud Keychain / Google Password Manager | No recovery path = a lost-passkey user is locked out; always offer fallback |
| **Magic link / email OTP** | Web-apps, PWAs, no-password flows | No password to forget | Link opens in wrong browser/in-app webview → broken session; email latency stalls activation |
| **Social OAuth (Google/Facebook/X)** | Fast, familiar | One tap, no password | Account-linking hell when the same human returns via a different provider |
| **Guest / anonymous** | Lowest-friction entry | Try before identifying | Migrating guest state to a real account later is the hard part — design it up front |

- **Passkeys, platform reality (2025–2026)** — passkeys work on Android 9+ (API 28) via **Credential Manager** (`androidx.credentials`) and iOS/iPadOS 16+ via AutoFill / `ASAuthorizationPlatformPublicKeyCredentialProvider`; web via WebAuthn in all current evergreen browsers. **Conditional UI / autofill** surfaces a passkey directly in the username field's autofill; **cross-device auth (CDA)** lets a phone-stored passkey sign you in on a nearby device via QR + Bluetooth proximity. `getClientCapabilities()` (Credential Manager) and `PublicKeyCredential.isConditionalMediationAvailable()` (web) let you probe support before starting the ceremony. Load-bearing, not hype — but recovery and a password/OTP fallback are still non-negotiable. (Dated 2026: adoption climbing, recovery UX still immature.)

- **Guest mode (account-less first use)** — let people reach the core loop with no account; defer sign-up to the first moment identity is *actually* required (save, sync, pay). Cuts the steepest drop-off in the funnel. Trap: the **conversion bridge** — capturing guest-created state into a real account — must be built from day one, or you train users to never sign up and lose their work on reinstall. (iOS implements this natively with **Sign in with Apple's anonymous credential**; web/PWA use an anonymous session token promoted on sign-up.)

- **Don't wall the value** — the cardinal sin: a mandatory account / paywall / permission gate *before* the user has seen anything worth signing up for. Show value first, ask for commitment second. Sign-up before the aha-moment inverts the trade the user is willing to make. (Note the platform exception: some categories — banking, health records — legitimately *must* gate on identity; "value first" yields to compliance there.)

- **Biometric re-auth (Face ID / Touch ID on iOS via `LAContext`; `BiometricPrompt` / `androidx.biometric` on Android; Windows Hello on desktop)** — for returning users, biometric unlock replaces the login screen. Defining trait: it's *re-auth*, not first-auth — offer to enable it right after the first successful sign-in, while trust is fresh. Always keep a passcode/password fallback (biometrics fail wet, gloved, masked, or after a reboot/`BIOMETRIC_ERROR_LOCKOUT`), and never store the credential itself behind biometrics without OS Keychain/Keystore backing (→ §16).

## 14.5 Progressive onboarding & the activation moment

- **Aha moment / activation event** — the specific first action that makes the value click: Slack's first message sent, Spotify's first play, Dropbox's first synced file, Facebook's "7 friends in 10 days." Defining trait: it's a *measurable event*, not a vibe — instrument it and treat **time-to-value (TTV)** as the north-star onboarding metric. Reach for everything in this section in service of shrinking TTV. Trap: optimizing "completed onboarding" instead of "reached aha" — a 100%-completion tutorial that nobody activates after is a failure dressed as success.

- **Empty state as onboarding** — the zero-data screen (no projects, no messages) doing double duty as the teacher: one-line explanation + a single primary CTA seeding the first real action. The most effective onboarding surface because it's contextual and unskippable-by-being-the-content. Trap: a blank screen with no guidance, or three competing CTAs — give exactly one obvious next step (→ §12 empty states).

- **Progressive / contextual onboarding** — teach features at the moment they become relevant, spread across the first sessions, instead of front-loading everything. NN/g's preferred model: "learn by doing." Reach for it for anything beyond the single core action. Trap: death by a thousand tooltips — sequence and rate-limit, or you've rebuilt the carousel one popover at a time.

- **Coach marks / spotlight overlay (feature discovery hint)** — a dimming scrim that highlights one control with a caption ("tap here to add"). Material 3 calls the spotlight-on-one-feature pattern **feature discovery** (a scrim + circular reveal centered on one target); iOS apps roll their own (no system component). Good for *one* genuinely non-obvious gesture. Trap: a full-screen coach-mark map labeling every button at once — users dismiss it reflexively and learn nothing. Accessibility veto: the scrim must be dismissible, focus must move to the callout, it must not trap VoiceOver/TalkBack, and the highlight must not rely on color alone (→ §19).

- **Tooltip / contextual tip (Android `Tooltip` via `tooltipText` / iOS `TipKit` / desktop hover tooltip)** — a small inline hint anchored to a control. iOS 17+ ships **TipKit** as the system framework for exactly this: rule-based, frequency-capped, dismissible tips with `@Parameter`/event triggers that respect "show once, then never nag." Prefer it on iOS over a hand-rolled popover. Android exposes basic tooltips via `View.setTooltipText`/`app:tooltipText`; desktop relies on hover (useless on touch — never hide first-run guidance behind hover-only tooltips). Trap: tips that reappear every launch — cap frequency and honor dismissal.

- **Sample / seed data (starter content, demo workspace)** — pre-populate the empty app with example projects/boards so the user sees a *populated* product, not a void, and can poke before committing real data. Strong for productivity/canvas tools (Notion's template gallery, Trello's welcome board). Trap: sample data the user can't tell from their own, or can't cleanly delete — label it and make teardown one tap.

- **Setup checklist (getting-started list, progress tracker)** — a short list of first tasks ("add a teammate ✓, connect calendar ☐") with a completion bar. Leans on the **Zeigarnik effect** — unfinished tasks nag pleasantly toward completion — and the **endowed-progress effect** (start the bar above zero and people push to finish). Excellent for multi-step B2B/SaaS activation (Slack, Asana). Trap: a checklist of busywork that doesn't map to real value, or one with no visible end; cap it at ~3–5 items and let users dismiss it once activated. Accessibility: the progress bar needs a text equivalent (`aria-valuenow` / accessibilityValue), not color-fill alone.

- **Progressive profiling** — collect profile/account detail incrementally across sessions instead of one long first-run form. Each field asked only when it unlocks something. Keeps the initial funnel short while still gathering what you need over time.

- **Gamified onboarding streak / first-win reward** — Duolingo's day-1 lesson + streak start, a confetti micro-celebration on first completion. Legitimate when the product's value *is* habit (language, fitness, finance). Trap: rewarding completion of onboarding rather than the real behavior — and respect `prefers-reduced-motion` / Reduce Motion for the celebration animation (→ §11 haptics, §19).

## 14.6 Re-onboarding & lifecycle

- **What's-new / release-notes screen (post-update onboarding)** — a one-screen "here's what changed" after a significant update. iOS apps often use the **standardized three-row "What's New" sheet** convention (icon + title + blurb per feature); both stores also surface release notes on the product page. Justified only for *behavior-changing* releases (moved navigation, new core capability); routine updates don't earn an interstitial. Trap: a what's-new modal on every patch trains users to dismiss without reading — reserve it for changes that alter muscle memory.

- **Change-blocking interstitials** — when an update relocates a primary action, a one-time coach mark on the new location beats a wall of text. Point, don't lecture.

- **Re-activation onboarding (win-back / returning-after-lapse)** — a returning lapsed user is *not* a new user: skip the intro, acknowledge the gap, surface what changed and what's waiting (new messages, unfinished setup). Trap: forcing a returning user back through first-run onboarding — fastest way to lose them a second time.

- **Re-permission priming** — capabilities the user previously denied can't be re-prompted by the OS; a contextual primer that deep-links to **Settings** (iOS `UIApplication.openSettingsURLString`; Android `Settings.ACTION_APP_NOTIFICATION_SETTINGS` / `ACTION_APPLICATION_DETAILS_SETTINGS`) is the only recovery. Use it sparingly and only at a moment the capability's value is concrete.

- **Cross-device / handoff onboarding** — setup begun on one device finished on another: iOS **Handoff** + iCloud account state, Android **Credential Manager** + Google account sync, web sessions resumed by sign-in. For an app that ships phone + tablet + watch + desktop, design which steps happen where — don't make the user re-onboard per device.

- **Onboarding on wearables / spatial (volatile, 2026)** — watchOS and Wear OS push setup onto the **paired phone** (the Apple Watch / Wear OS companion app); the watch first-run should be glanceable, not a multi-screen flow on a ~1.9″ display. visionOS onboarding teaches spatial gestures (look-and-pinch), runs a one-time **hand-tracking + space/room setup**, and needs ergonomic placement guidance — genuinely novel interactions where instructional onboarding *is* warranted (the rare exception to "design it out"). Flagged volatile: spatial onboarding conventions are still forming as of 2026 and not yet load-bearing canon.

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Launching](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/launching); [Apple HIG — Onboarding](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/onboarding); [Android Developers — Splash screens](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/launch/splash-screen); [Android Developers — Sign in users with Credential Manager](https://developer.android.com/identity/sign-in/credential-manager); [Nielsen Norman Group — Mobile-App Onboarding](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/mobile-app-onboarding/); [WCAG 2.2 — Pause, Stop, Hide (2.2.2)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/pause-stop-hide.html); [web.dev — Add a splash screen for installed web apps](https://web.dev/learn/pwa/enhancements).*
