Correction noted: APNs broadcast/channel push for Live Activities actually shipped in **iOS 18** (WWDC24), not iOS 26. I'll fix that in the draft. All other facts hold. Here is the revised section.

# 15. Notifications & messaging

Reaching the user outside the app and inside it. Governing principle: notification permission is a one-shot trust gift the user can revoke forever — spend it on signal, never on marketing dressed as urgency. Every platform now gates push behind explicit consent; the design problem is earning the yes, then never abusing it.

## 15.1 Channels & permission

The pipe and the price of admission. Both platforms made notifications opt-in by default and gave users granular kill-switches; respect them or get muted wholesale.

- **APNs (Apple Push Notification service) / FCM (Firebase Cloud Messaging)** — the OS-level gateways that route a push from your server to the device. APNs covers iOS/iPadOS/watchOS/macOS/visionOS; FCM is the cross-platform front-end for Android (and proxies to APNs and web push). Both are best-effort, not guaranteed delivery — a push is a hint to fetch, not a transactional channel. iOS distinguishes **alert** push (token-authenticated, user-visible) from **background/silent** push (`content-available: 1`, throttled and droppable); Android mirrors this with **notification messages** (FCM renders them) vs **data messages** (your code handles them). Trap: treating push as a reliable message queue — the user who had notifications off never gets a payload-as-data push; reconcile on next app open.
- **Notification permission prompt** — the system consent dialog. iOS has always required `requestAuthorization`; Android required it only from **Android 13 (API 33, `POST_NOTIFICATIONS`)** onward — before 13, notifications were on by default. On 13+ a fresh install ships with notifications OFF; the runtime dialog fires when an app targeting API 33+ calls the request (you control timing), or auto-fires on first channel creation for apps still targeting 12L or lower. Trap: the **cold ask** — firing the prompt on first launch before the user knows what they'd be pinged about. A denied iOS prompt is near-permanent (only re-grantable in Settings); Android lets you re-ask but `shouldShowRequestPermissionRationale` gates the politeness, and two ignored prompts auto-denies.
- **Permission priming (pre-permission prompt / soft ask)** — your own in-app screen shown *before* the system dialog, explaining the value and offering a "Maybe later" that doesn't burn the real prompt. Standard NN/g-backed pattern because the OS dialog is unrecoverable on iOS once dismissed. Trap: a priming screen that's pure nag with no concrete benefit — "Enable notifications to stay updated" converts worse than "Get notified the moment your order ships." (→ §19 — priming and the system sheet must be screen-reader-announced and keyboard/switch-reachable.)
- **Provisional authorization (iOS quiet notifications)** — request with `.provisional` and iOS grants *silent* delivery with no prompt: messages land in Notification Center only — no sound, no lock-screen banner. After the user sees one, an inline prompt lets them promote to prominent, keep quiet, or turn off. Lets you prove value before asking for the loud channel. Trap: provisional traffic still spends goodwill — bury someone in quiet notifications and they pick "Turn Off" the first time asked.
- **Notification channels (Android 8+, API 26)** — mandatory per-category buckets (`NotificationChannel`) the user controls individually: mute "Promotions" while keeping "Order updates." Each channel carries an **importance** set at creation and immutable from code thereafter (only the user can lower it): `IMPORTANCE_HIGH` (heads-up + sound), `IMPORTANCE_DEFAULT` (sound, no heads-up), `IMPORTANCE_LOW` (no sound), `IMPORTANCE_MIN` (no badge, collapsed in the shade). Channel **groups** bundle related channels. Trap: dumping everything into one channel — the user's only recourse is to nuke your whole app, so one annoying marketing push kills your transactional pipe too.
- **Notification categories (iOS, `UNNotificationCategory`)** — iOS's analog, but they define **interactive actions** per type, not user-facing on/off switches. iOS exposes user control through *interruption levels* and per-app Settings, not channels — there's no built-in per-topic mute the way Android channels give. Trap: assuming iOS users can mute one notification type while keeping another; they can't without you shipping in-app preference toggles backed by server-side suppression.

| Concept | iOS | Android (M3) |
|---|---|---|
| Consent gate | `requestAuthorization` (always) | `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` runtime (13+) |
| Per-topic user control | App-built prefs + Settings | Notification channels (8+) |
| Urgency tiers | Interruption levels (4) | Channel importance (5) |
| Silent trial | Provisional auth | (none — channel `LOW`/`MIN`) |
| Interactive actions | Categories + actions | Actions on the builder |
| Re-ask after deny | Settings only | Allowed (rationale-gated) |

## 15.2 Anatomy & surfaces

Where a notification lands and what it looks like.

- **Banner / alert / lock-screen / Notification Center** — iOS presentation styles. **Banner** = temporary (slides in, auto-dismisses) or persistent (stays until acted on); **lock-screen** and **Notification Center** are the standing list. The user picks per-app in Settings. The **heads-up notification** on Android (a floating banner over the current app) is the rough equivalent, triggered by `IMPORTANCE_HIGH` + sound. Desktop equivalents: macOS **banner vs alert** styles (alert persists until dismissed), Windows transient **toast** that falls back to the **Notification Center / Action Center** list. Trap: designing the push assuming it shows as a banner — if the user set it to Notification Center only, your time-critical alert sits silent in a list.
- **App icon badge (badge / notification dot)** — the count or dot on the home-screen icon. iOS badges carry an explicit integer you set (and must clear yourself — stale counts are a classic bug); the app needs the **badge** authorization option. Android shows a **dot** by default (long-press reveals the count), ties badging to channels, and lets users and launchers disable it. M3 also defines in-app **badges** on nav items (small dot, or a number capped at "999+"). Windows uses taskbar **badges + overlay icons**; macOS Dock badges. Trap: an iOS badge that never decrements because you only clear it on one screen — the "1" that won't die trains users to ignore the badge entirely. (→ §19 — badge state must be conveyed to assistive tech, not by color/dot alone.)
- **Snackbar (Android / Material) / Toast** — transient in-app message at the bottom, optional single action, auto-dismiss. M3 snackbars sit above the bottom nav, hold one action max, shouldn't stack, and default to ~4–10s. **Toast** (Android legacy) is text-only, no action, no theming, system-positioned. iOS has **no system toast** — you build a transient overlay or use a banner. Trap: putting a critical, must-act error in an auto-dismissing snackbar — if it vanishes before the user reads it, the action is gone (→ §19 — also fails screen-reader timing and WCAG 2.2.1 unless the visible duration is extended or the message persists).
- **In-app message vs push** — push reaches users *outside* the app (server-initiated, permission-gated, OS-rendered); in-app messages render *inside* your UI while the user is already engaged (no permission needed, full design control, can't reach a closed app). Use in-app for onboarding nudges, feature discovery, contextual upsell; reserve push for things worth interrupting a closed app. Trap: shipping a marketing push for something that only matters while the app is open — an in-app message wearing push's costume, and it bleeds permission goodwill.
- **In-app inbox / notification center (message center)** — a persistent in-app list of past notifications so a missed or dismissed push isn't lost forever. The durable record that push (best-effort, ephemeral) can't be. Trap: an inbox that only mirrors what was pushed — users who denied push see an empty inbox and assume nothing happened; populate it server-side regardless of push state.

## 15.3 Urgency & interruption

The lever that decides whether you break Focus — and the one most abused.

- **Interruption levels (iOS, `UNNotificationInterruptionLevel`)** — four tiers: **`.passive`** (no sound, won't light the screen, lands in Notification Center — low-value updates), **`.active`** (default: sound + screen, obeys Focus and the scheduled summary), **`.timeSensitive`** (breaks through Focus and the summary; needs the *Time Sensitive Notifications* entitlement; for delivery alerts, security codes, ride arrival), **`.critical`** (bypasses Focus *and* the ringer switch, plays even on silent; requires a special Apple-granted entitlement; reserved for safety — medical, security, public alerts). Trap: shipping marketing as `.timeSensitive` to dodge Focus — App Review rejects it, and Apple Intelligence notification ranking (iOS 18.1+) demotes generic blasts regardless of the level you claim.
- **Channel importance (Android)** — the Android equivalent, fixed per channel: `HIGH` peeks as a heads-up over the current app, `DEFAULT` sounds without peeking, `LOW`/`MIN` stay silent and (for `MIN`) badge-less. Users can only lower it. Trap: setting every channel to `HIGH` so everything heads-up — the user's defense is to mute the channel, and you've taught them your app is noise.
- **Time-sensitive (Android `setCategory` + importance)** — Android has no single time-sensitive flag like iOS; urgency is the channel's importance plus `CATEGORY_*` hints (`CATEGORY_ALARM`, `CATEGORY_CALL`, `CATEGORY_MESSAGE`, `CATEGORY_REMINDER`) that interact with Do Not Disturb and the user's DND exceptions. Trap: assuming a `HIGH` channel bypasses DND — it doesn't unless the user granted that category a DND exception.
- **Notification fatigue / opt-out respect** — over-notifying ends in a system-level mute, irreversible without the user going back into Settings. Cap frequency, batch low-value updates into digests, honor unsubscribes server-side, and watch the *opt-out / mute rate* as a primary health metric, not just open rate. Trap: treating a high open rate as license to send more — the users who'll mute you don't open first, they just leave.

## 15.4 Rich & interactive

Notifications that do more than display text.

- **Actionable / interactive notifications (notification actions)** — buttons on the notification itself: reply, snooze, accept/decline, mark-done — handled without opening the app. iOS via `UNNotificationAction` on a category (mark *destructive* or *authentication-required* as needed); Android via `Notification.Action` on the builder. **Text-input actions** (`UNTextInputNotificationAction` / `RemoteInput`) let the user type a reply inline. Trap: an action that silently opens the app to finish the job — if "Reply" just launches a blank compose screen, it's a button lying about being an action. (→ §19 — every action needs an accessible label; icon-only actions read as "button" to VoiceOver/TalkBack.)
- **Rich notifications (attachments / media / big-picture)** — images, GIFs, audio, video thumbnails. iOS uses a **Notification Service Extension** to download/decrypt media before display and a **Notification Content Extension** for fully custom UI; Android uses `BigPictureStyle`, `BigTextStyle`, `InboxStyle`, `MediaStyle`. Trap: a multi-MB hero image on a metered connection — iOS gives the service extension only ~30s and a tight memory budget, so the attachment fails silently and the user gets a bare notification; keep media small and degrade gracefully.
- **Communication notifications (iOS, `INSendMessageIntent` / Communication Notifications API)** — messaging/call notifications that show the sender's **avatar**, honor Focus "allowed people" lists, surface in the Dynamic Island, and feed Apple's contact-based prioritization. Donate a communication intent so a message from a real person ranks above app chatter. Android's parallel is **`MessagingStyle`** (sender avatars, threaded conversation lines) plus the **Conversations** section (Android 11+) where conversation notifications cluster above everything else, can be marked **Priority**, or floated as a **Bubble**. Trap: rendering person-to-person messages as generic notifications — they sink in the stack and lose the avatar/Focus integration users now expect.
- **Grouping / threading / summary** — collapsing many notifications into one stack. iOS auto-groups by app and by **`threadIdentifier`** (set it so one conversation threads together), with a **summary** line; Android groups via `setGroup` plus a **group summary** notification. Trap: omitting the thread/group id so 20 messages from one chat render as 20 separate lock-screen rows — the fastest route to a muted channel.
- **Notification Summary / Scheduled Summary (iOS)** — the user-configured batch that holds non-urgent notifications for a chosen delivery time. `.active` and `.passive` levels are eligible to be held; `.timeSensitive`/`.critical` break through. Trap: designing as if every push arrives instantly — for summary users an `.active` notification may surface hours later, so don't put a 10-minute-window offer in one.
- **Local notifications (`UNNotificationRequest` / `AlarmManager` + `NotificationManagerCompat`)** — scheduled on-device, no server or network (timers, reminders, calendar alerts). iOS uses calendar/interval/location triggers; Android schedules the post via `AlarmManager`. **Exact alarms** need `SCHEDULE_EXACT_ALARM` — **denied by default on Android 14 (API 34)** for apps targeting API 33+ that aren't clock/calendar apps; clock/calendar apps use the auto-granted `USE_EXACT_ALARM`. Check `canScheduleExactAlarms()` first; users can revoke the permission. Trap: relying on exact alarms for non-critical reminders — Doze/battery optimization defers inexact ones, so design for "around then," not "to the second."

## 15.5 Cross-platform & emerging

- **Web push (Push API + Notifications API + Service Worker)** — browser-delivered notifications via a service worker and a `PushSubscription`. Works on Android Chrome/Firefox and, since **iOS/iPadOS 16.4 (2023)**, on Safari **only for Home-Screen-installed PWAs** — not in the Safari tab — and the prompt must fire from a user gesture. Requires explicit `Notification.requestPermission()`. Trap: prompting for web-push permission on page load — browsers (and NN/g) treat the un-primed page-load prompt as the canonical dark pattern; Chrome can quiet-block or auto-block sites with low accept rates. (→ §19 — permission prompts must be keyboard-reachable and not trap focus.)
- **Desktop notifications (macOS Notification Center / Windows toast + Action Center)** — OS-level banners with their own permission and Focus / Do-Not-Disturb (macOS) or Focus Assist / Do Not Disturb (Windows) gates. Windows toasts support adaptive templates, inputs, and images via the **Fluent toast (XML) schema**; macOS native apps use `UNUserNotificationCenter`, same framework as iOS. Web apps installed as PWAs inherit the OS notification surface. Trap: spamming desktop notifications for an app the user keeps open all day — they'll silence the whole app in OS settings.
- **watchOS / Wear OS notifications** — by default phone notifications **mirror** to the watch when the phone is locked or away; tune the wrist experience with **short-look / long-look** layouts (watchOS) or notification **bridging/dismissal sync** (Wear OS). Built for a 1–2 second glance, with notification actions and inline reply (voice, scribble, canned). Trap: mirroring a verbose phone notification verbatim — a wrist banner has room for a sender and a verb, not a paragraph.
- **Live Activities (iOS, ActivityKit) — *as of iOS 16.1+, expanded through iOS 26 (2025) to CarPlay/iPad/Mac; load-bearing, not hype*** — a persistent, updating glanceable surface on the Lock Screen, in the **Dynamic Island** (compact/minimal/expanded), and (iOS 26) on CarPlay, iPad (iPadOS 26), and Mac (macOS Tahoe) — for live-tracked events: ride ETA, sports score, delivery, timer. Updated via ActivityKit pushes or local updates; **active up to 8 hours, then up to 4 more on the Lock Screen (12h max)**, with a combined static+dynamic payload that **cannot exceed 4 KB**; set a **stale date** so outdated content self-flags. **Broadcast/channel APNs push** (one push → many subscribers via `pushType: .channel`) shipped in **iOS 18 (WWDC24)**. The right answer for "what's happening *right now*," replacing a stream of repetitive status pushes with one living surface. (→ §22 Live Activities & widgets.) Trap: using a Live Activity for a non-live event or pushing past its window — it's for bounded, in-progress activity, and not a substitute for push to a fully closed app.
- **AI notification ranking (Apple Intelligence prioritized notifications + summaries / Android adaptive notifications) — *iOS 18.1+ / 2024–2026; partially load-bearing, partly hype*** — the OS reorders and summarizes notifications by inferred importance, promoting person-to-person and context-tied messages and demoting generic marketing. You don't control the ranking, but correct signals — communication intents, accurate interruption levels, real thread ids — keep you near the top. Trap: gaming urgency flags to climb the rank; mislabeled marketing gets demoted and, on iOS, risks App Review. Also: Apple's notification **summaries** garbled headlines badly enough that Apple paused them for news apps (Jan 2025) — don't rely on the OS to summarize anything where wording is load-bearing. Date-sensitive: ranking/summary heuristics shift release-to-release; don't hard-design around today's exact ordering.

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Managing notifications](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/managing-notifications); [Apple — UNNotificationInterruptionLevel](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/usernotifications/unnotificationinterruptionlevel); [Apple — Displaying live data with Live Activities (ActivityKit)](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/activitykit/displaying-live-data-with-live-activities); [Android — Notification runtime permission](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/notifications/notification-permission); [Android 14 — Exact alarms denied by default](https://developer.android.com/about/versions/14/changes/schedule-exact-alarms); [Material 3 — Snackbar](https://m3.material.io/components/snackbar/guidelines); [MDN — Push API](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Push_API).*

---

My facts check out. The draft's ATT figure (~35%, Adjust mid-2025), Android 14 / API 34 partial photo access, and the API names are all accurate. The draft is already strong; I'll tighten it, remove the leading meta-line, add missing patterns (screen-recording/FLAG_SECURE nuance, ATT in B2B, notification permission API 33, location background/`ACCESS_BACKGROUND_LOCATION`, permission auto-reset/hibernation, secure paste/Universal Clipboard, visionOS/wearable divergence, consent-string persistence, privacy indicators on macOS/desktop), and strengthen platform divergence and cross-refs.

# 16. Permissions, privacy & trust UX

Access is borrowed, not owned. This section governs how an app asks for sensitive capabilities and data without spending the trust it needs to keep a user. Governing principle: **least privilege, asked at the moment of value, in your own honest words before the OS asks in its non-negotiable ones.** The system dialog is a one-way door — spend your priming budget *before* it, never after.

## 16.1 Timing & the priming pattern

The single highest-leverage decision is *when* you ask. Cold-asking at launch is the canonical failure: it trades a permanent grant for a user who hasn't yet seen why.

- **Just-in-time request (contextual / in-context request)** — ask at the point of value, when the user taps a feature that needs the capability, not at first launch. Owned by every platform's guidance (Apple HIG "Privacy"; Android "minimize permission requests"). Grant rates rise sharply: deferred prompts measurably out-convert launch-time prompts. Reach for it always. Trap: a launch-time gauntlet of camera + mic + contacts + location — users deny reflexively and you've burned every one-shot at once.
- **Pre-permission dialog (priming prompt / soft ask / pre-prompt)** — your own in-app screen shown *before* the system dialog, explaining the benefit and letting the user say "not now" cheaply. Because the iOS system prompt is one-shot, the pre-prompt is where you persuade; the OS dialog is just the hard yes/no. Pattern: soft-ask ("Turn on notifications for shipping updates?") → if yes, fire the system prompt; if no, defer and re-ask later from a different entry point. Trap: Apple App Review rejects pre-prompts that **mimic the system UI**, offer incentives to opt in, or shame the user for declining — keep it visibly *your* UI, with a real decline path (→ §16.7 dark patterns).
- **Rationale / educational UI (Android `shouldShowRequestPermissionRationale()`)** — Android exposes whether to show a rationale; `true` means the user denied once and you get one explanation before re-asking. Show in-context UI with a clear "no thanks" escape. iOS has no equivalent API — you never learn *why* a user denied, only the resulting `authorizationStatus`. Trap: looping the rationale on every launch reads as nagging.
- **Sequencing** — order requests by value-proximity; never batch unrelated ones. A maps app earns location at "find near me," not on the splash.
- **Notification permission is its own ask** — Android 13+ (API 33) made `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` a *runtime* permission (pre-33 it was granted by default); iOS has always gated it via `UNUserNotificationCenter.requestAuthorization`. It is the **most-denied permission** — never ask on first launch; ask after the user has a reason to want a ping. Web push (PWA) is gated by the Notifications API behind a transient gesture, and Safari/iOS only grants it to **home-screen-installed** PWAs (iOS 16.4+).
- **The deny-twice cliff (Android)** — two denials = permanent denial; the system dialog never shows again and your only recourse is deep-linking to Settings. iOS is stricter: the prompt fires exactly once, ever (→ §16.2). Treat the first ask as precious.

## 16.2 System permission dialogs — one-shot vs. runtime

The two platforms diverge fundamentally here, and that divergence drives your whole strategy.

| Trait | iOS | Android (Material 3) |
|---|---|---|
| When dialog can fire | Once, ever, per permission | Repeatable until two denials |
| Recovery after deny | Settings deep-link only | Rationale + one re-ask, then Settings |
| Partial grants | Limited Photos, Precise-off location, Allow Once | Selected Photos, Approximate location, "Only this time" |
| Mandatory copy | Purpose string (Info.plist) | System-templated; app supplies rationale separately |
| Auto-reset when unused | iOS prompts to offload; permissions persist | Permissions auto-revoked after months idle (API 30+) |

- **One-shot system alert (iOS)** — `requestWhenInUseAuthorization` / `AVCaptureDevice.requestAccess(for:)` and friends present the OS alert exactly **once**. Deny, and it's dead forever; the user must dig into Settings → Privacy & Security. This is *the* reason iOS apps lean on pre-prompts. Trap: triggering the real prompt to "see what it looks like" during dev on a fresh capability — you've spent the user's one shot (reset via Settings → Developer or a reinstall).
- **Purpose string (usage description / `NS…UsageDescription`)** — the required Info.plist line rendered inside the iOS alert ("This app uses your location to show nearby stores"). Missing string = guaranteed crash on request; vague string = App Review rejection. Write it specific and benefit-led. Owned by iOS.
- **Android runtime permissions** — since Android 6 (API 23), dangerous permissions are granted at runtime via `ActivityResultContracts.RequestPermission` / `RequestMultiplePermissions`, checked with `ContextCompat.checkSelfPermission()`. Repeatable until the deny-twice cliff. Group related requests (e.g. fine + coarse location) into one dialog.
- **"Only this time" / one-time grant (Android 11+, API 30)** — location, mic, and camera dialogs offer a session-scoped grant; access ends when the app backgrounds, and on next launch you ask again. iOS's **Allow Once** for location is the analogue. Design for re-asking gracefully — never assume a grant persists.
- **Background location (`ACCESS_BACKGROUND_LOCATION` / iOS "Always")** — both platforms split foreground from background location and make background the harder, separately-justified ask. Android 11+ forces a **two-step** flow: grant foreground first, then the OS sends the user to Settings to upgrade to "Allow all the time" — you cannot prompt for it inline. iOS surfaces a delayed "keep allowing Always?" re-confirmation. Trap: requesting Always/background when foreground-only does the job — a guaranteed review flag and trust hit.
- **Approximate location (Android `ACCESS_COARSE_LOCATION` / iOS Precise toggle)** — Android 12+ shows Precise/Approximate toggles when you request `ACCESS_FINE_LOCATION` *and* `ACCESS_COARSE_LOCATION` together; iOS 14+ shows a "Precise: On/Off" switch, with a one-shot temporary upgrade via `NSLocationTemporaryUsageDescriptionDictionary`. Request the coarsest tier that works. Trap: demanding precise location for a weather app — users notice and downgrade you.
- **Limited Photo Library (iOS 14+) / Selected Photos Access (Android 14+, API 34)** — both let the user grant a *subset* of the library. Android's `READ_MEDIA_VISUAL_USER_SELECTED` (request alongside `READ_MEDIA_IMAGES`/`READ_MEDIA_VIDEO`) and iOS's limited-library mode give access to chosen items only; never assume the library is "empty" when partial. Offer a reselection entry point.
- **Photo Picker (Android `PickVisualMedia` / iOS `PHPickerViewController`)** — the right answer for "let me pick a photo": a system picker that needs **no permission at all** and grants scoped, temporary read access to the selected items. Reach for the picker before reaching for a storage permission. Trap: requesting full media access when a picker would do — least-privilege says don't.
- **Permission auto-reset & hibernation (Android 11+/12+)** — the OS revokes runtime permissions for apps unused for months and can hibernate them entirely; design for a grant silently disappearing and re-ask in context. iOS has no auto-reset but offloads unused apps. Don't cache a grant forever in app state — re-check at use.
- **Cross-platform tier (web-app/PWA + desktop)** — the Permissions API + Permissions Policy gate `geolocation`, `camera`, `microphone`, `notifications`; browsers require a **transient user gesture** and (Chrome) auto-block prompts that fire without interaction or on repeated dismissal. `navigator.permissions.query()` reads state without prompting. A PWA cannot render a native-fidelity soft-ask over the browser's own dialog — the chrome owns it. Desktop: **TCC** (macOS) and the Windows privacy settings model gate camera/mic/location/screen-recording at the OS level, surfaced as system prompts the app can't restyle; macOS additionally gates screen recording, accessibility, and full-disk access behind System Settings → Privacy & Security toggles the user must flip manually.

## 16.3 Tracking & cross-app identity (ATT)

- **App Tracking Transparency (ATT)** — iOS framework (`ATTrackingManager.requestTrackingAuthorization`) gating access to the **IDFA** and any cross-app/cross-site tracking, required since iOS 14.5. Denied = IDFA returns all-zeros. Mandatory purpose string `NSUserTrackingUsageDescription`. The system copy is fixed — you cannot change "Ask App Not to Track." Use a pre-prompt to set context first. Trap: Apple forbids incentivizing, gating content behind, or pressuring the ATT opt-in — and forbids fingerprinting to track when denied. Android has **no ATT equivalent** today.
- **ATT opt-in reality (2025–2026 — load-bearing, volatile)** — industry panels put the average opt-in (of users shown the prompt) around **~35%** (Adjust, Q2 2025), trending slowly up from ~34% in 2023; pre-prompted onboarding flows report materially higher rates than cold asks. Numbers swing hard by vertical (sports/games far higher than education) and country. Treat any single figure as directional. Date this entry.
- **Android Privacy Sandbox / Topics (2025–2026 — emerging, hype-watch)** — Google's on-device interest-cohort replacement for the cross-app advertising ID, rolling out gradually. The legacy advertising ID still exists and requires the `AD_ID` permission for apps targeting Android 13+ (API 33). Not yet a hard ATT-style gate; watch but don't over-invest. Date this entry.
- **IDFV / app-set ID** — `identifierForVendor` (iOS) and the Android **app-set ID** are per-vendor identifiers that do *not* require ATT/consent because they don't track across vendors. Use these for first-party analytics; reserve IDFA for genuine cross-app advertising. Trap: conflating IDFV with IDFA in your privacy label — they collect different things.

## 16.4 Privacy disclosure & nutrition labels

- **Privacy Nutrition Label (App Privacy / iOS)** — the App Store Connect questionnaire that renders the at-a-glance label: **Data Used to Track You**, **Data Linked to You**, **Data Not Linked to You**. User-facing pre-download. Must match your actual SDK behavior and privacy policy. Trap: an embedded analytics or ad SDK silently collects data you didn't declare — the label is *your* liability, not the SDK's.
- **Data Safety section (Google Play)** — Play's parallel disclosure form: what's collected, what's shared, security practices, deletion options. User-facing, must match the privacy policy exactly, and uses a *different* taxonomy than Apple's — you fill out both separately, not once.
- **Privacy Manifest & required-reason APIs (iOS 17+)** — `PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` declares data collection and justifies use of "required-reason" APIs (file timestamps, system boot time, disk space, `UserDefaults`); third-party SDKs on Apple's list must ship signed manifests, and App Store submission is **blocked** without them. Now foundational for new submissions, not optional.

## 16.5 Biometric auth & passwordless

- **Face ID / Touch ID (iOS, `LocalAuthentication` / `LAContext`)** — `evaluatePolicy(.deviceOwnerAuthenticationWithBiometrics)` for biometric-only, `.deviceOwnerAuthentication` to fall back to passcode. The match never leaves the **Secure Enclave**; your app gets a boolean, never the biometric. Requires `NSFaceIDUsageDescription` for Face ID. Gate sensitive actions with it — not as the *only* lock.
- **Android `BiometricPrompt` (Class 3 / `BIOMETRIC_STRONG` vs Class 2 / `BIOMETRIC_WEAK`)** — the unified system sheet for fingerprint/face/iris. Only **Class 3 (`BIOMETRIC_STRONG`)** is trusted for payments and `CryptoObject`-backed Keystore operations; Class 2 isn't. `setAllowedAuthenticators(BIOMETRIC_STRONG or DEVICE_CREDENTIAL)` adds PIN/pattern fallback. Trap: assuming all face unlock is strong — many devices ship Class 2 face auth that can't gate a Keystore key.
- **Passkeys (FIDO2 / WebAuthn)** — phishing-resistant public-key credentials replacing passwords; the private key stays on device, synced by the provider (iCloud Keychain, Google Password Manager). iOS via `ASAuthorizationController` / AutoFill; Android via **Credential Manager** (`CredentialManager`, unifying passkeys + passwords + federated sign-in), single-tap creation behind a biometric prompt. The 2025–2026 default for new auth flows; web uses the same WebAuthn API. Trap: shipping passkeys with no password/recovery fallback strands users on devices without a synced provider — always offer account recovery.
- **Always offer a non-biometric path** — biometrics fail (wet fingers, masks, disabled sensors) and exclude users who can't or won't enroll. A biometric gate with no passcode/PIN/recovery fallback is an **accessibility veto** (→ §19). Biometrics authenticate *intent in the moment*, not identity for the account — back them with a real credential, and never store the only copy of account access behind a single sensor.

## 16.6 Runtime privacy signals & sensitive-data masking

- **Privacy indicators (camera/mic dot)** — **iOS (14+):** orange dot = mic active, green dot = camera active, in the status bar. **Android (12+):** a chip that collapses to a green dot; tapping it in Quick Settings reveals which app. **macOS:** an orange/green dot by the menu-bar Control Center; a hardware camera LED is wired to the sensor. The user always knows when a sensor is live — you cannot suppress it, and trying looks adversarial. Android 16 extends indicator surfacing toward more sensor types (emerging — date this).
- **Privacy Dashboard (Android 12+) / App Privacy Report (iOS 15+)** — a ~24-hour timeline of which apps touched location, mic, camera, and other permissions; iOS additionally logs network domains contacted. Assume every access is logged and visible — design so the dashboard matches what the user expects.
- **App-switcher snapshot masking** — the OS screenshots your app for the multitasking/recents thumbnail; sensitive screens (banking balances, health, messages) leak there. **iOS:** blur or swap the view in `sceneWillResignActive`, or cover with an overlay window. **Android:** `setFlags(FLAG_SECURE)` blanks the recents thumbnail *and* blocks screenshots/screen recording. Trap: forgetting the recents snapshot persists after backgrounding — a glance at the task switcher exposes the last screen.
- **Screen-capture & recording defense** — beyond `FLAG_SECURE`, iOS exposes `UIScreen.isCaptured` / `capturedDidChangeNotification` to detect active recording or mirroring and blank sensitive content; macOS gates screen recording behind TCC. Web has no reliable screenshot block. Trap: assuming `FLAG_SECURE` covers casts — it blocks most, but treat true secrecy as unachievable on a rooted/jailbroken device.
- **Autofill & secure-field hygiene** — mark password fields `textContentType = .password` (iOS) / autofill hints + `importantForAutofill` and `inputType=textPassword` (Android) so the keyboard disables learning and the field masks. Use `.oneTimeCode` / Android SMS Retriever for OTP autofill instead of reading the clipboard. Never echo OTPs or full card numbers into logs, analytics, or crash reports (→ §19 for autofill as an a11y affordance, not just convenience).
- **Clipboard transparency** — iOS shows a banner on programmatic `UIPasteboard` reads (and offers `UIPasteControl` for read-with-consent); Android 12+ toasts when an app reads the clipboard. Read only on explicit user paste. Trap: a "smart" auto-detect-coupon clipboard read reads as spyware to the OS notification — and on iOS, Universal Clipboard means you may be reading another device's data.

## 16.7 Consent, regulation & least privilege

- **GDPR / CCPA in-app consent** — lawful basis, granular opt-in for non-essential processing, and an equally easy opt-out. Consent must be **unbundled** — separate toggles for analytics, ads, personalization, not one "I agree." Pre-ticked boxes and a "reject" buried two taps deeper than "accept all" are unlawful dark patterns under GDPR. ATT (a platform control) and GDPR (a legal basis) are *different layers* — granting ATT is not legal consent, and vice versa.
- **Consent Management Platform (CMP) / IAB TCF** — the in-app consent SDK (often required for ad monetization, e.g. Google's UMP). Render it as a clear first-run choice, persist the signal (TC string), and honor it downstream across every SDK. Trap: a CMP that defaults all vendors on and hides the vendor list — non-compliant and trust-corrosive.
- **Least privilege (data minimization)** — request the narrowest scope, coarsest precision, and shortest retention that delivers the feature. Prefer the **picker over the permission** (photo picker, document picker, contact picker, `CNContactPickerViewController`) — these grant scoped access to user-chosen items with *no* prompt at all. Every permission you don't request is a trust cost you don't pay.
- **Graceful degradation on denial** — denial is a first-class state, not an error. The feature should still do *something* useful (manual entry instead of location, a picker instead of full library) and offer a calm path to Settings (`UIApplication.openSettingsURLString` / Android `Settings.ACTION_APP_NOTIFICATION_SETTINGS`) for users who change their mind. Never trap the user on a "you must grant X to continue" wall for a capability that isn't load-bearing — that's both a dark pattern and an accessibility veto (→ §19).
- **Dark-pattern consent (the anti-patterns)** — confirmshaming ("No, I don't want to be safe"), asymmetric button weighting, nagging re-prompts, mimicking the system dialog, and pre-checked consent. All are App Review / Play Policy violations *and* GDPR violations, and all are accessibility-hostile to users relying on assistive tech (→ §19). The trap that voids the whole section: treating consent as an obstacle to defeat rather than a decision to respect.

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Privacy](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/privacy); [Android Developers — Request runtime permissions](https://developer.android.com/training/permissions/requesting); [Android Developers — Grant partial access to photos and videos (Android 14)](https://developer.android.com/about/versions/14/changes/partial-photo-video-access); [Android Developers — BiometricPrompt / biometric auth](https://developer.android.com/identity/sign-in/biometric-auth); [Apple Developer — Describe your use of required-reason APIs / privacy manifests](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/bundleresources/describing-use-of-required-reason-api); [Adjust — ATT opt-in rates: 2025 data & benchmarks](https://www.adjust.com/blog/att-opt-in-rates-2025/).*

---

Facts confirmed: `glassEffect(_:in:)`, `GlassEffectContainer`, `glassEffectID`; iOS 18 themed icons use a single transparent foreground layer (system supplies the background); Material's "On" colors target 3:1. Here is the revised section.

# 17. Color, theming & materials

App color is a token system, not a swatch list: every surface, label, fill, and stroke resolves through a *semantic role* the OS owns, so it adapts to dark mode, contrast settings, accent choice, and wallpaper without per-screen rework. Governing principle: **name colors by job, not by hue.** A button is `accentColor`/`primary`, not "blue"; text is `label`/`onSurface`, not "#1c1c1e". Hardcode a hex and you opt out of every system adaptation below.

## 17.1 Semantic & system color systems

Both major platforms ship a closed set of role tokens. Map your brand into them once; never reach past them to raw values in UI code.

- **System colors (iOS) / palette tones (Android)** — iOS exposes a fixed palette of vivid, dark-mode-aware accents: `systemBlue`, `systemRed`, `systemGreen`, `systemOrange`, `systemYellow`, `systemPink`, `systemPurple`, `systemTeal`, `systemIndigo`, `systemBrown`, `systemMint`, `systemCyan`, plus `systemGray` through `systemGray6`. Each shifts automatically between light and dark. Trap: these are *tuned* per appearance — picking your own fixed RGB to "match systemBlue" breaks in dark mode where Apple lightens it.
- **Label colors (iOS) / on-colors (Material 3)** — text/foreground roles with built-in hierarchy. iOS: `label`, `secondaryLabel`, `tertiaryLabel`, `quaternaryLabel` (decreasing opacity, dark-mode aware). Material 3: `onSurface`, `onSurfaceVariant`, `onPrimary`, `onPrimaryContainer` — each *paired* to a background role and algorithmically held to a contrast minimum against it (the tonal generator targets ~3:1 for the standard scheme, *not* the 4.5:1 body-text bar). Trap: don't use `secondaryLabel`/`onSurfaceVariant` for primary reading copy; the reduced contrast is for de-emphasis only and routinely dips below 4.5:1 (→ §19).
- **Background colors (iOS)** — two hierarchies: `systemBackground`/`secondarySystemBackground`/`tertiarySystemBackground` (default, for non-grouped UI) and `systemGroupedBackground`/`secondarySystemGroupedBackground`/`tertiarySystemGroupedBackground` (for grouped table/inset-list layouts where the base is darker and cells sit *lighter* on top). Trap: mixing the two hierarchies — a grouped list on plain `systemBackground` looks flat and wrong.
- **Fill colors (iOS)** — `systemFill` → `quaternarySystemFill`, translucent grays for filling shapes *behind* content (toggles, search-field backgrounds, slider tracks). They're semi-transparent so the layer below tints through. Use for component fills, never for large opaque regions.
- **Separator colors (iOS)** — `separator` (translucent, lets background bleed) vs `opaqueSeparator` (solid, for when you can't have bleed-through, e.g. over an image). Hairlines, not borders.
- **Material 3 accent roles** — `primary` (main brand action), `secondary` (less-prominent components, filters/chips), `tertiary` (contrasting accents, balancing), each with a `*Container` variant (lower-emphasis tinted fill) and `on*` / `on*Container` foregrounds. `error`/`onError`/`errorContainer` is the semantic danger role. Trap: treating `secondary`/`tertiary` as "two more brand colors" — they exist to give the tonal system room, not to triple your palette.
- **Material 3 surface roles** — `surface`, `surfaceVariant`, `surfaceDim`, `surfaceBright`, and the five-step container ramp `surfaceContainerLowest` → `surfaceContainerLow` → `surfaceContainer` → `surfaceContainerHigh` → `surfaceContainerHighest`. `surfaceContainer` is the recommended default for a contained region sitting on `surface`. This ramp **replaced** the old elevation-overlay model (see §17.3).
- **Outline roles (Material 3)** — `outline` (decorative borders, dividers, text-field outlines) and `outlineVariant` (lower-emphasis dividers). The 3:1 non-text-contrast target applies to `outline` when it's load-bearing for a control's boundary; `outlineVariant` is decorative-only and may fall below it (→ §19).
- **Web/PWA — the CSS `system-color` keywords + named surfaces** — web apps have no role catalog; the closest analogues are CSS *system colors* (`Canvas`, `CanvasText`, `LinkText`, `ButtonFace`, `Field`, `AccentColor`) which the browser maps to the OS theme, and which **become mandatory under `forced-colors`** (→ §19). Trap: most web apps invent a private token layer (CSS custom properties) and forget to honor the system keywords, so Windows High Contrast renders them as un-themed garbage.

| Job | iOS token | Material 3 role | Web (CSS system color) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Primary text | `label` | `onSurface` | `CanvasText` |
| Secondary text | `secondaryLabel` | `onSurfaceVariant` | (no system token; derive) |
| Screen base | `systemBackground` | `surface` | `Canvas` |
| Card/sheet on base | `secondarySystemBackground` | `surfaceContainer` | (derive) |
| Brand action | `tintColor`/`accentColor` | `primary` | `AccentColor` |
| On brand action | white/`label` (auto) | `onPrimary` | `AccentColorText` |
| Low-emphasis fill | `secondarySystemFill` | `secondaryContainer` | `ButtonFace` |
| Danger | `systemRed` | `error` | (no system token) |
| Divider | `separator` | `outlineVariant` | `GrayText` |

## 17.2 Dark mode as first-class

Dark mode is a parallel color set, not a runtime invert. Design it deliberately or it looks broken.

- **Adaptive (dynamic) colors** — the unit of dark-mode support. iOS: `UIColor`/`Color` resolve per `userInterfaceStyle` automatically; supply both appearances in your asset catalog or use `UIColor(dynamicProvider:)`. Material/Compose: define `lightColorScheme()` and `darkColorScheme()` (or let dynamic color derive both). macOS: `NSColor` semantic colors + `aqua`/`darkAqua` appearance variants. Web/PWA: CSS `light-dark()` plus `color-scheme: light dark` and the `prefers-color-scheme` media query. Trap: theming only half your tokens — a single hardcoded white surface is the giveaway.
- **Elevation by lightness, not shadow (Material 3 / Android)** — in dark themes shadows nearly vanish, so a raised surface reads as raised by being a **lighter** tone: `surfaceContainerHigh` sits visually above `surfaceContainer`. The same role ramp serves both modes. Trap: porting a light-mode shadow stack into dark and wondering why nothing looks layered.
- **Dark mode ≠ pure black** — iOS dark base is a near-black gray and grouped/elevated surfaces are progressively lighter dark grays, giving room for elevation steps. Material's dark surfaces are dark gray for the same reason. True black everywhere flattens the depth hierarchy (and is a separate AMOLED theme — §17.6, not the default dark mode).
- **Desaturate and dim accents** — saturated brand colors vibrate and bloom on dark backgrounds. iOS auto-lightens system colors in dark mode; for custom brand colors you must supply a lighter, slightly desaturated dark-mode variant. Material's tonal system handles this when you go through roles (`primary` pulls a higher tone — ~tone 80 — in dark). Trap: reusing the exact light-mode accent in dark — it'll fail contrast *and* halo.
- **Don't invert images/illustrations** — `accessibilityIgnoresInvertColors` / Smart Invert (iOS) and forced-colors modes deliberately skip photos; your manual dark theme should too. Provide dark-specific illustration assets, not filtered ones (→ §19).

## 17.3 Tonal elevation & surface tints

Material's depth model changed; know which era you're in.

- **Tonal elevation (Material 3, current)** — elevation is expressed by *swapping to a higher surface-container role*, not by overlaying a translucent tint. Pick `surfaceContainerLow/Container/High/Highest` to signal layering. This is the post-2023 tone-based model and is the default in Compose Material3 1.2+.
- **Surface tint overlay (Material You 2021–2023, legacy)** — the older model tinted `surface` with a `primary`-derived overlay whose opacity grew with `dp` elevation (+1 to +5). Deprecated; `surface` at +4/+5 maps to `surfaceContainerHighest` now. Trap: setting both a `surfaceTint` and a tonal container role double-tints. Migrating an old app: rip out `tonalElevation`/`surfaceTint` and assign container roles instead.
- **Shadow vs tonal elevation** — Material 3 still uses *shadows* (key + ambient) for components that must read as floating (FAB, menus, dialogs) on top of tonal elevation; the two are orthogonal signals. iOS leans on shadows + materials, not tonal surface swaps. macOS/Windows desktop lean on hairline borders + shadow, not tone.
- **Concentricity (iOS 26 / macOS 26, dated 2025–2026)** — Liquid Glass formalizes nested corner radii: a control's radius should be *concentric* with its container's (inner radius = outer radius − padding). A theming/material concern because mismatched radii break the "carved from one pane" read. Foundational once iOS 26 is the floor; treat as load-bearing for iOS 26 polish.

## 17.4 Vibrancy, materials & blur

Translucent "materials" sample what's behind them. They are a depth + context tool, never a place to drop unscrimmed text.

- **Materials (iOS) — `.ultraThinMaterial` → `.thickMaterial`** — five system blurs (`ultraThin`, `thin`, `regular`, `thick`, plus `bar`/`chrome` for bars) that blur + tint the backdrop and adapt to light/dark. Use for sheets, sidebars, nav/tab bar backgrounds. Pair with **vibrancy** (`.foregroundStyle(.secondary)`/`Material`-aware hierarchical styles) so labels blend correctly. Trap: text contrast over a material is fragile when the backdrop is busy — the system vibrancy levels are calibrated, custom opacity over blur is not (→ §19).
- **Liquid Glass (iOS 26 / iPadOS 26 / macOS 26 "Tahoe", dated 2025–2026)** — Apple's translucent material that *refracts and reflects* its surroundings and dynamically reshapes around content; the new substance for controls, nav, tab bars, and widgets. API: `.glassEffect(_:in:)` (variants `.regular`/`.clear`/`.identity`, chainable `.tint()`/`.interactive()`), `GlassEffectContainer` (groups elements so they blend/morph — glass can't sample other glass), `glassEffectID` for matched transitions. Principle: "hierarchy through depth." Hype-vs-load-bearing: the **material API** is load-bearing and shipping; the "redesign everything in glass" impulse is the hype — Apple reserves glass for *floating navigation/control layers*, not content backgrounds. Specular highlights only render on device, not Simulator. Accessibility veto: Reduce Transparency and Increase Contrast fall back to opaque fills, so glass must never be the *only* thing conveying a boundary or grouping (→ §19).
- **Acrylic (Windows 11 / Fluent)** — semi-transparent frosted-glass material for **transient, dismissable** surfaces: flyouts, context menus, command bars, search suggestions. Real-time backdrop blur. In-app acrylic blurs app content; background acrylic blurs the desktop. Trap: using acrylic on a primary persistent surface — Windows 11 moved those to Mica (acrylic's per-frame blur is also the expensive option, throttled on battery/low-power).
- **Mica / Mica Alt (Windows 11 / Fluent)** — *opaque* material tinted once by the desktop wallpaper's dominant color, sampled a single time (not per-frame) so it's far cheaper than acrylic. The default base layer for app/window backgrounds; Mica Alt is a stronger-tinted variant for tab bands/title areas. Trap: expecting see-through — Mica is opaque, only the *tint* comes from the wallpaper; and it only tints the **active** window (inactive windows fall back to a neutral solid).
- **Backdrop materials don't paint the element** — Fluent gotcha: a window backdrop (Mica) shows through a control only if every layer between the control and the window is transparent. Opaque intermediate surfaces block it.
- **Web/PWA equivalent — `backdrop-filter: blur()`** — the CSS primitive behind glass effects (now broadly supported, including Firefox). Trap: no automatic vibrancy, no automatic contrast fallback — you must add a scrim *and* honor `prefers-reduced-transparency` / `forced-colors` yourself, and heavy backdrop blur on a scrolling list tanks compositing performance (→ §19).

## 17.5 Dynamic & wallpaper-extracted color

System-personalized palettes the user controls. Opt in through roles or you get nothing.

- **Material You / dynamic color (Android 12+, foundational)** — the OS extracts a source color from the wallpaper (HCT-based), generates tonal palettes, and exposes them as the standard color roles. Apps call `dynamicLightColorScheme(context)` / `dynamicDarkColorScheme(context)` (Compose) to adopt them. Only meaningful if your UI reads from roles, not hardcoded brand hex. Trap: a strong brand identity *fights* dynamic color — gate it behind a "use system colors" preference rather than forcing wallpaper tints onto a branded surface.
- **Contrast levels (Material 3, Android 14+)** — users pick Standard / Medium / High contrast in system Display settings; the tonal system regenerates roles to hit higher contrast deltas. Free if you use roles; impossible if you hardcoded. Not the same lever as font scaling or bold text (→ §19).
- **System accent color (macOS / Windows / Android desktop)** — macOS and Windows let the user set a global accent; `accentColor`/`tintColor` (iOS, app-level), the `AccentColor` asset (macOS), and Fluent's `SystemAccentColor` brushes (+ light/dark 1/2/3 shades) pick it up. iOS ships a fixed app tint rather than a user-global accent. Trap: assuming the accent contrasts with your surfaces — a user's lime-green accent on a white sheet can fail 3:1 for controls; never rely on accent *alone* to indicate state (→ §19).
- **Themed app icons** — Android 13+: ship a `<monochrome>` layer in the adaptive icon; supporting launchers tint it to the wallpaper palette (developer must opt in per-icon). iOS 18+: provide *dark* and *tinted* icon variants in the asset catalog — the tinted variant is a single **transparent foreground** layer over a system-supplied gradient background (system derives luminance), toggled home-screen-wide by the user. Trap (Android): omitting the monochrome layer leaves your icon full-color in a themed grid, looking broken; (iOS): a tinted icon is a flat luminance map — detail-heavy logos turn to mush, so design a simplified high-contrast silhouette and don't bake in your own background.

## 17.6 Gamut, true-black & per-app overrides

The edges: color space, OLED, and letting users override your theme.

- **Display P3 / wide gamut** — iOS, modern Macs, and many Android flagships render Display-P3 (~25% wider than sRGB, vivid reds/greens). Author brand colors in P3 (`Color(.displayP3, …)`, Android wide-gamut `ColorSpace`, CSS `color(display-p3 …)`) with an sRGB fallback. Trap: a P3-only accent silently clamps to sRGB on older/cheaper screens — verify the fallback still reads as your brand *and* still passes contrast (clamping shifts luminance).
- **AMOLED / true-black theme** — an optional pure-`#000000` theme that powers off OLED pixels for battery + infinite contrast (common in Android power-user apps, some iOS apps). Offer as a *separate* theme, not your default dark mode, because true black kills tonal-elevation cues — reintroduce depth with visible `outline`/hairline borders instead of lighter surfaces. Trap: smearing/ghosting on scroll (OLED black-to-gray transition lag) and unreadable elevation.
- **Increase Contrast / High Contrast / Smart Invert (→ §19)** — iOS `Increase Contrast` strengthens colors and separators (read `accessibilityContrast`); Windows **High Contrast / `forced-colors`** *replaces* your palette with the user's system colors entirely (honor the CSS system-color keywords, don't fight it with `!important`); Smart Invert flips UI but spares media (mark images with `accessibilityIgnoresInvertColors`). These are accessibility vetoes: if a pattern only works at your hand-tuned contrast, it's broken.
- **Per-app appearance override** — iOS pins an app to light or dark via `overrideUserInterfaceStyle` (and iOS 15+ Settings → app → Appearance lets the *user* force per-app light/dark); Android apps expose an in-app theme picker (Light/Dark/System, often via `AppCompatDelegate.setDefaultNightMode`); web apps offer a Light/Dark/System toggle that writes `color-scheme` and persists the choice. Always offer "System" and default to it. Trap: shipping only a forced theme — forcing light mode on a dark-mode user is a top complaint.

## 17.7 Token mapping (brand hue → accessible role)

The bridge between a brand palette and the platform role systems (→ §26 design tokens).

- **Map, don't paint.** A brand color enters the system as the *source/seed* (`primary` seed in Material, `accentColor`/`AccentColor` asset in Apple, `AccentColor` brush in Fluent), and the platform derives the on-color, container, and pressed/disabled states. You assign roles; the tonal system or your light/dark asset pair fills the rest.
- **Generate tonal ramps, hand-check the anchors.** Material's HCT tonal palette yields 13 tones (0–100) per key color; roles pull specific tones (`primary` = tone 40 light / tone 80 dark; `surface` ≈ tone 98/6). The generator targets ~3:1 contrast for the standard scheme — so verify the two pairs you ship most, body-text-on-surface and on-primary, hit **4.5:1 (body) / 3:1 (large + UI components)** manually; the auto-pick doesn't guarantee 4.5:1 (→ §19).
- **Three-tier token structure.** Primitive (`brand-blue-600` = raw hex) → semantic/alias (`color-action` → `brand-blue-600`) → component (`button-bg` → `color-action`). UI code references only component/semantic tokens; swapping the brand hue or theme touches one alias layer. Trap: components pointing at primitives — you lose the single swap point and every theme/contrast variant becomes a manual edit.
- **One token, multiple values, resolved by mode.** Each semantic token carries light, dark, and optionally high-contrast / AMOLED values; the build (Style Dictionary, etc.) or runtime picks per appearance. This is the same dynamic-color contract §17.2 enforces, expressed as tokens — and it must include the high-contrast value, or §17.6's contrast settings have nothing to resolve to.
- **Don't encode state in hue alone.** Map success/warning/error to *semantic* tokens (`color-feedback-danger`), and always pair the color with an icon or label — color-only state fails for color-blind users and disappears entirely under `forced-colors` / Smart Invert (→ §19).

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Materials](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/materials); [Apple HIG — Dark Mode](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/dark-mode); [SwiftUI — GlassEffectContainer / glassEffect](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/swiftui/glasseffectcontainer); [Material 3 — Color roles](https://m3.material.io/styles/color/roles); [Material 3 — Tone-based surface color](https://m3.material.io/blog/tone-based-surface-color-m3); [Microsoft Learn — Materials (Mica & Acrylic)](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/signature-experiences/materials); [MDN — `<system-color>` & forced-colors](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/system-color); [W3C — WCAG 2.2](https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/).*

Material Symbols default opsz is 48 (not 24). My draft says "24 (M3)" — the canonical default is 48 per Google's docs, though M3 components commonly request 24. Let me adjust to be accurate. The axes ranges confirm: opsz 20-48, wght 100-700, GRAD -50 to 200, FILL 0-1. Icon count ~3,800+. I have what I need.

# 18. Typography & iconography in apps

Type and icons are the highest-density surface in any app — every screen is mostly text and glyphs. The governing principle: **the OS owns the type ramp and the icon system; you adopt it, you don't reinvent it.** Use the platform's named text styles and symbol library so your app inherits Dynamic Type, font scaling, weight-matching, dark mode, and themed icons for free — and so it looks native. Divergence between iOS, Material 3, desktop, and web is sharp here and load-bearing: the same "heading" is `title1` on iOS, `headlineLarge` on Android, and a CSS `clamp()` on the web.

## 18.1 System type families

The face is the OS's; ship a custom one only with a reason and a fallback.

- **San Francisco / SF Pro (iOS, iPadOS, macOS)** — Apple's system sans since 2015, an optically-corrected neo-grotesque. SF Pro is the default UI face; it has **two optical sizes that swap automatically at 20pt** — *SF Pro Text* below, *SF Pro Display* above (tighter tracking, refined detailing). You get this free via the system font; don't hardcode a single optical cut. Nine weights (Ultralight→Black) that align 1:1 with SF Symbols weights (→§18.4).
- **SF Compact** — narrower variant with straighter sides, the **default on watchOS** for legibility in tight rectangles. Distinct from SF Pro; the OS picks it per platform.
- **SF Rounded** — rounded-terminal variant for soft, friendly, or large-numeral contexts (timers, Fitness rings, kids/health apps). Opt-in via `design: .rounded`. Trap: not a body face — readability drops at small sizes and long measures.
- **SF Mono** — fixed-width face for code, terminals, and monospaced digits (→§18.3). Opt-in.
- **New York** — Apple's system **serif** (`design: .serif`), for reading/editorial contexts. Scales with Dynamic Type like the sans.
- **Roboto (Android, Material)** — Google's system sans since 2014; the Material default for Brand and Plain roles. Refreshed 2024 with a wider, more open default.
- **Roboto Flex** — the **variable-font** successor (2022), one file exposing axes `wght` 100–1000, `wdth` 25–151, `opsz` 8–144, plus grade and a dozen fine axes. Lets one font cover the whole ramp + optical sizing. Material 3 still ships static Roboto as the safe default; reach for Flex when you want true optical sizing or fluid weight.
- **Segoe UI Variable (Windows 11) / Segoe UI (Win10)** — Microsoft's system face; the Variable cut (Fluent 2) carries optical small/text/display sizes with built-in legibility tuning for small UI text. Small optical size is hinted for crisp rendering at caption sizes; display is loosened for headlines.
- **System UI stack (web app / PWA)** — `font-family: system-ui` resolves to SF on Apple, Roboto on Android, Segoe on Windows — i.e. the app inherits each platform's native face with one keyword. `ui-sans-serif`, `ui-monospace`, `ui-rounded`, `ui-serif` map to the SF design variants on Apple. Trap: `system-ui` renders differently per OS by design — don't pixel-match a mock to one platform, and don't rely on `ui-rounded`/`ui-serif` resolving anywhere but Apple.
- **Bundling a custom face** — if brand demands a non-system font: subset it, supply `woff2` on web, declare a system fallback in the stack to avoid FOIT/FOUT, and on iOS/Android wire it through `UIFontMetrics` / `sp` so it still scales (→§18.2). Trap: a custom font that ignores Dynamic Type / font-scale is an accessibility regression, not a brand win (→§19).

## 18.2 Type scales & scalable text

Each OS ships a **named, semantic** type ramp. Use the role, not a magic number — the role is what scales, themes, and translates.

**iOS Dynamic Type text styles** (default point size at the `.large` content-size category, the system default):

| Style | Default pt | Typical weight |
|---|---|---|
| `largeTitle` | 34 | Regular |
| `title1` | 28 | Regular |
| `title2` | 22 | Regular |
| `title3` | 20 | Regular |
| `headline` | 17 | Semibold |
| `body` | 17 | Regular |
| `callout` | 16 | Regular |
| `subheadline` | 15 | Regular |
| `footnote` | 13 | Regular |
| `caption1` | 12 | Regular |
| `caption2` | 11 | Regular |

**Material 3 type scale** (default size / line-height in sp; Roboto):

| Role | Size | Line | Weight |
|---|---|---|---|
| Display L / M / S | 57 / 45 / 36 | 64 / 52 / 44 | Regular |
| Headline L / M / S | 32 / 28 / 24 | 40 / 36 / 32 | Regular |
| Title L / M / S | 22 / 16 / 14 | 28 / 24 / 20 | Reg / Medium / Medium |
| Body L / M / S | 16 / 14 / 12 | 24 / 20 / 16 | Regular |
| Label L / M / S | 14 / 12 / 11 | 20 / 16 / 16 | Medium |

M3 Expressive (2025) adds **emphasized** variants of every role (heavier weight, tighter tracking) for moments that need punch. *(Dated: 2025; load-bearing for new M3 work, optional polish otherwise.)*

- **Dynamic Type (iOS)** — user-controllable text-size ramp: 7 standard categories (xSmall→xxxLarge) plus **5 accessibility categories** (AX1→AX5), reachable via Settings or Control Center. Adopt by tagging text with a style (`.font(.body)`); the OS scales it. Custom fonts scale via `UIFontMetrics` / SwiftUI `relativeTo:`. The largest AX5 size roughly **doubles** body text — your layout must reflow, not clip (→§18.3, →§19).
- **Android font scaling / `sp`** — type sizes use **scale-independent pixels**: `sp` = `dp` × the user's font-scale setting. Always size text in `sp`, never `dp`/`px`. **Android 14+ uses non-linear font scaling** above 100% — large text scales less aggressively than small text so display sizes don't explode; the system caps at 200%. Compose: use `MaterialTheme.typography` roles and `.sp` units. Trap: hardcoding `dp` for text silently breaks accessibility scaling and ships a lawsuit-grade bug (→§19).
- **Windows / Fluent type ramp** — Caption 12 / Body 14 / Body Strong 14-semibold / Subtitle 20 / Title 28 / Title Large 40 / Display 68. Respects the OS display-scale + text-size sliders. **macOS** ships its own named styles (Large Title, Title 1–3, Headline, Body, Callout, Subheadline, Footnote, Caption 1–2) mirroring iOS but tuned for pointer distance; it scales with the system text-size setting, not a per-app ramp.
- **Web app type scale** — no OS ramp; you define one. Use `rem` (respects user's root font size — never `px` for body) and fluid `clamp(min, vw-based, max)` for headings. Browser zoom and user font-size prefs **must not clip** (→§19, WCAG 2.2 1.4.4 resize-text + 1.4.10 reflow). Trap: `clamp()` with a `vw`-only middle term can fail 1.4.4 because it doesn't respond to the user's font-size — always include a `rem` term.
- **watchOS / Wear OS** — both ship condensed ramps tuned for glanceability; watchOS uses SF Compact text styles, Wear OS a Material ramp with larger minimums. Body minimums are higher than phone — small caption text is unreadable on-wrist. **visionOS** scales type by *distance*, not pixels: use the semantic styles and points; the system enlarges text for windows pushed farther back, so never hardcode pixel sizes for spatial UI.

## 18.3 Type details that bite

- **Largest accessibility size must not clip (→§19)** — at AX5 (iOS) or 200% font scale (Android), test every screen. Truncated labels, clipped buttons, and overlapping rows are the #1 Dynamic Type failure. Prefer vertical stacks over fixed-height rows; let buttons wrap. iOS caps `caption2` at 11pt minimum — it won't shrink below that even at xSmall. SwiftUI: gate dense horizontal layouts on `@Environment(\.dynamicTypeSize)` and switch to a `VStack` past `.accessibility1`.
- **Variable fonts** — one file, continuous axes (`wght`, `wdth`, `opsz`, `GRAD`). Roboto Flex and SF (internally) are variable. Wins: optical sizing, fluid weight animation, smaller payload than many static cuts. Trap on web: animating `wght` can trigger layout reflow unless you reserve space (`font-variation-settings` changes metrics) — pin `width` or use a fixed line-box.
- **Optical sizing (`opsz`)** — auto-adjusts stroke contrast and spacing by size: tighter/finer for display, sturdier for captions. SF does it at the 20pt Text/Display boundary automatically; Roboto Flex and Material Symbols expose it as an axis. Disabling it (`font-optical-sizing: none`) makes small text spindly. On web `opsz` tracks `font-size` automatically unless you override.
- **Monospaced / tabular digits** — proportional digits make changing numbers (timers, counters, prices, scoreboards) jitter as glyph widths shift. Use **fixed-width digits without going full monospace**: SwiftUI `.monospacedDigit()`, CSS `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums`, Android via the `tnum` font feature. Trap: don't switch the whole label to SF Mono / Roboto Mono just for the numbers — it changes the letterforms too.
- **Other numeric variants** — old-style (lowercase) figures, fractions, ordinals, and slashed-zero are font features (`font-variant-numeric`, OpenType `onum`/`zero`/`frac`). Reach for slashed-zero in dense data/code UIs where `O` vs `0` matters. Don't fake them by substituting glyphs.
- **Line length / measure on phones** — a phone column is ~30–40 characters at body size; that's fine for UI, too short for long-form. Don't import a 66-char web measure as a hard width. On tablets/desktop/iPad multi-column, cap reading columns (~`60ch` / `SwiftUI .frame(maxWidth:)`) so full-width text doesn't sprawl.
- **Letter-spacing / tracking** — the system faces are pre-tracked per optical size; **don't add manual tracking to SF or Roboto at UI sizes** — you fight the optical tuning. Material's type scale already specifies tracking per role. All-caps labels are the one place a small positive tracking helps legibility.
- **Line height & language reflow** — set generous line-height (Material bakes it into the scale; iOS handles it) and never lock text to a single fixed-height line. German/Finnish run ~30% longer than English and CJK needs taller line-boxes; a fixed height clips on localization (→§19).
- **Don't ship raw numeric sizes** — `Text(...).font(.system(size: 17))` opts out of Dynamic Type. Use the named style or `relativeTo:` so it scales.

## 18.4 SF Symbols (iOS / Apple platforms)

**SF Symbols** — Apple's system icon library (over **6,900** in SF Symbols 7, 2025; iOS/iPadOS/macOS/watchOS/tvOS/visionOS 26+), distributed as a variable font and **designed to sit in a text run** — they inherit the adjacent text's weight, size, and baseline. This text-alignment is the whole point: an icon next to a label matches it automatically. Use them for any standard glyph; draw custom only for brand marks.

- **Weights (9: Ultralight→Black)** — each maps 1:1 to an SF font weight, so a symbol beside Semibold text gets Semibold weight. Set via the symbol's font.
- **Scales (3: small / medium / large)** — defined relative to the **cap height** of the surrounding text, not absolute pixels. Default medium.
- **Rendering modes (4)** — *Monochrome* (one tint, all layers); *Hierarchical* (one hue, opacity steps per layer for depth); *Palette* (you supply 2–3 explicit layer colors); *Multicolor* (symbol's intrinsic colors — green leaf, red `trash.slash`). Pick monochrome/hierarchical for neutral UI; multicolor only where color carries meaning. Trap: color alone isn't a label — multicolor state still needs a text/`accessibilityLabel` cue (→§19).
- **Variable Color** — feed a 0–1 value; layers light up progressively (Wi-Fi strength, volume, download progress). For **state/progress, not depth**.
- **SF Symbols 7 (2025)** adds **Draw** animations (Draw On/Off, calligraphic reveal — whole-symbol, by-layer, or individual), **Variable Draw** (per-layer progress with more resolution than Variable Color), single-source-color **gradients** across all rendering modes, and enhanced **Magic Replace** transitions between related symbols. *(Dated: iOS 26 era; Draw/gradients are genuinely useful for feedback, not just decoration — but motion respects Reduce Motion, →§19.)*
- **Custom symbols** — export an SF Symbol as a template and edit in a vector tool to make your own that inherits weight/scale/rendering behavior. Use this for brand glyphs instead of a flat SVG so they weight-match text.
- **Trap: licensing + substitution.** SF Symbols are licensed for Apple platforms only — **don't export them into Android, web, or print**. And a symbol can render differently across OS versions or go missing on older ones; check availability and provide a fallback for symbols newer than your deployment target.

## 18.5 Material Symbols (Android / Material / web)

**Material Symbols** — Google's system icon set (~3,800), shipped as a **variable font** with four axes. Three styles (Outlined, Rounded, Sharp). Replaced the legacy fixed "Material Icons" in 2022. Free under Apache 2.0 — usable cross-platform (unlike SF Symbols).

Axes:

| Axis | Range | Default | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| `FILL` | 0–1 | 0 | Unfilled ↔ filled — animate to signal selection/active |
| `wght` | 100–700 | 400 | Stroke weight; match adjacent text |
| `GRAD` | −50–200 | 0 | Fine thickness tweak (e.g. emphasis in dark mode) without changing weight |
| `opsz` | 20–48dp | 48 | Optical size — stroke thickens as icon shrinks so it reads at any size |

- **Fill for state** — Material's idiom is **outlined = inactive, filled = active** (e.g. bottom-nav selected item). Animate `FILL` 0→1 on selection.
- **Grade vs weight** — grade is the granular knob; bump `GRAD` up in dark mode so light-on-dark icons don't look thinner (optical illusion). Weight is the coarse knob and should track text weight.
- **Optical size, set it to the render size** — `opsz` defaults to 48 but should match the dp you draw at (24 is the common UI size); a 24dp icon rendered at the 48 instance reads too thin. Don't transform-scale a single instance across sizes.
- **Trap:** loading the full variable font is heavy on web — subset to the glyphs you use, or self-host static instances; the gstatic full font is hundreds of KB. On Android prefer the bundled vector drawables / Compose `Icons` over pulling the web font.

## 18.6 Icon consistency & cross-platform

- **One library per platform, one style within it** — don't mix Outlined and Sharp Material Symbols, or SF Symbols with a third-party set, on the same screen. Inconsistent stroke weight and metaphor reads as broken.
- **Match icon weight to text weight** — both systems are built to weight-match adjacent labels; a Black-weight icon next to Regular text looks like a bug.
- **Optical size, not transform-scale** — scale icons by changing `opsz`/SF scale, not by CSS `transform: scale()` or stretching a bitmap; the latter thins or thickens strokes wrongly.
- **Keep metaphors platform-conventional** — a share action is the square-with-up-arrow on iOS, the three-node share glyph on Android; an overflow menu is `•••` (horizontal) on iOS, `⋮` (vertical) on Android. Don't ship one platform's glyph on the other (→§3 navigation conventions).
- **Cross-platform apps need a neutral set** — Flutter/React Native shipping one codebase to both stores can't legally use SF Symbols on Android. Either use Material Symbols everywhere (consistent but un-iOS-native) or platform-branch the icon set (native but double the work). Flutter ships `Icons` (Material) + `CupertinoIcons`; decide deliberately.
- **Accessibility (→§19)** — icon-only controls need a programmatic label (SwiftUI `accessibilityLabel`, Android `contentDescription`, web `aria-label`); decorative icons get an empty/null label so screen readers skip them. A bare glyph is invisible to screen readers, and a 44×44pt (iOS) / 48×48dp (Material) minimum touch target applies regardless of glyph size.

## 18.7 App icon design

The home-screen icon is a fixed-format deliverable with platform-specific masking and layering rules — get the format wrong and the store rejects you.

- **iOS app icon** — a single **1024×1024 PNG, fully opaque, no alpha, square**, sRGB or Display P3. **Don't round the corners or add the squircle yourself** — the system masks it. Transparent pixels = automatic rejection. No PNG-alpha, no SVG/JPEG/WebP.
- **iOS 26 layered icons / Icon Composer** *(Dated: 2025–26)* — the Liquid Glass era replaces the flat PNG pipeline with a **`.icon` file** authored in **Icon Composer** (ships with Xcode 26). You supply **stacked layers** (background, mid, foreground as SVG/PNG); the system composites them with dynamic specular highlights, blur, and parallax that react to device tilt, and auto-generates **Default, Dark, Clear (Light/Dark), and Tinted (Light/Dark)** appearance modes. Inspired by the visionOS layered model. Foreground art must survive the rounded-rect mask and the tint/clear treatments — high-contrast flat shapes survive; fine photographic detail muddies. Provide the single 1024 PNG as fallback for pre-26.
- **visionOS layered app icon** — **3 layers** (background, middle, foreground) composited into a **circle** with real depth and parallax as the user's gaze/head moves. Foreground floats above background. Design for the circular mask and the gap between layers.
- **Android adaptive icon** — **two layers**, foreground + background, **each 108×108dp**, with only the **central 72×72dp** guaranteed visible — the OEM launcher applies its own mask (circle, squircle, teardrop, rounded square). Keep the logo inside the **66dp safe-zone circle**; anything outside may be cropped. Background should be solid/opaque; foreground carries the mark with padding. Android 13+ adds the monochrome layer (below); Android 12+ launchers apply swipe/press motion.
- **Android themed (monochrome) icon** — Android 13+ lets users tint home-screen icons to the wallpaper palette. Supply a **`<monochrome>` layer** (single-color, transparent background, logo as alpha) or the launcher falls back to a generic-looking treatment. Effectively required for a polished Android 13+ presence.
- **macOS / Windows app icons** — macOS uses a rounded-square within a fixed safe area (and adopts Icon Composer / Liquid Glass in macOS 26); supply the full size set. Windows ships multiple PNG/`.ico` sizes (16→256px) plus tile/Store assets; small sizes need a simplified mark — fine detail vanishes at 16px. Trap: don't ship one bitmap scaled down — author distinct small sizes.
- **PWA / web-app icons** — `manifest.json` `icons[]` at **192 and 512px** **plus a `"purpose": "maskable"`** variant with art inside the **safe zone (central ~80%, the minimum-safe circle)** so Android's adaptive mask doesn't clip it. Add `apple-touch-icon` (**180×180**, no transparency — iOS won't mask a PWA icon the same way) for iOS home-screen installs. Trap: a non-maskable icon gets letterboxed in a white circle on Android; ship both a `any` and a `maskable` purpose.
- **Universal traps** — design at the size it'll be seen (a logo legible at 1024 can be mud at 40px on the home screen); **no text** in the icon (illegible + un-localizable); don't bake in a notification badge or platform UI chrome; keep one recognizable silhouette across every platform's mask shape.

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Typography](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/typography); [Apple HIG — App Icons](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/app-icons); [SF Symbols (Apple Developer)](https://developer.apple.com/sf-symbols/); [Material 3 — Type scale tokens](https://m3.material.io/styles/typography/type-scale-tokens); [Material Symbols guide (Google Fonts)](https://developers.google.com/fonts/docs/material_symbols); [Android — adaptive icons](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/launch/icon_design_adaptive); [WCAG 2.2 — 1.4.4 Resize Text](https://www.w3.org/WAII/WCAG22/Understanding/resize-text.html).*

Specs confirmed: iOS 26 Copied Speech rotor (last ten items, three-finger quadruple-tap), Android 14 font scaling caps at 200% with non-linear curve. Both draft facts are accurate. Here's the revised, strictly-more-complete section.

# 19. Accessibility in apps — the veto

Accessibility is not a layer you add at the end; it is a veto over every other decision in this document. A pattern that looks right, tests well, and ships fast is still wrong if a VoiceOver user can't operate it. The governing principle: every interactive element must be perceivable, operable, and understandable through at least three channels — visual, auditory/spoken, and assistive-technology semantics — and no information may live in only one. Apps are harder than the web here because the assistive tech is OS-owned (you inherit VoiceOver/TalkBack, you don't ship your own) and because WCAG was written for the web and leaves native-specific gaps you fill from platform guidance.

## 19.1 The semantic tree — what every AT reads

Every assistive technology — screen reader, switch, voice — consumes one shared **accessibility tree** the OS builds from your views. Get the tree right and most ATs work; get it wrong and they all fail together. Platform owners: iOS/macOS **VoiceOver**, Android **TalkBack**, Windows **Narrator** (plus JAWS/NVDA reaching UWP/WinUI via **UI Automation**), web **AOM/ARIA**, watchOS/Wear OS inherit the phone's reader. Each turns the tree into a linear, swipe-navigated list of elements with a spoken **name, role, value, state**.

- **Accessibility label (iOS `accessibilityLabel` / SwiftUI `.accessibilityLabel`) / content description (Android View `contentDescription` / Compose `contentDescription`)** — the spoken name. Required on every icon-only control; a bare share glyph announces as nothing without it. Trap: never bake the role into the label ("Share button") — the role is a separate channel and you get "Share button button". Decorative images get an empty/`null` description (Compose `contentDescription = null`; iOS `.accessibilityHidden(true)`) so the reader skips them.
- **Role / trait (iOS `accessibilityTraits`: `.button`, `.header`, `.link`, `.selected`, `.adjustable`…; Android Compose `Role.Button`/`Role.Switch`/`Role.Tab`, View `AccessibilityNodeInfo`; web ARIA `role`)** — declares *what kind of thing* this is so the reader announces it and exposes the right gestures. `.adjustable` (iOS) / slider + `stateDescription` semantics (Android) tells the reader to swipe up/down to increment.
