Trap: a tappable `View`/`div` with no role announces as inert text — the user never learns it's actionable.
- **Value / state (iOS `accessibilityValue`; Android `stateDescription` + checked/selected semantics)** — the dynamic part: a toggle's on/off, a slider's "70 percent", a tab's selected state. Trap: a custom checkbox whose checked state lives only in a tint color — the reader says "checkbox" forever, never "checked".
- **Hint (iOS `accessibilityHint`) / Android has no first-class hint** — a trailing phrase describing the *result* ("Opens the editor"). Spoken after a pause, user-suppressible. Don't put essential info here; it's the lowest-priority channel and many users turn it off.

## 19.2 Screen-reader navigation — making the tree usable

A correct tree can still be miserable to navigate. Grouping, order, and structure are where "technically passes" and "actually works" diverge.

- **Grouping / merge (iOS `accessibilityElement(children: .combine)` / `shouldGroupAccessibilityChildren`; Compose `Modifier.semantics(mergeDescendants = true)` / `clearAndSetSemantics`; web grouping role)** — collapse a card's title + subtitle + thumbnail into one swipe stop with one combined announcement instead of three orphaned fragments. The default un-grouped tree is the most common reason an app passes an audit but is exhausting to use.
- **Reading / traversal order (iOS `accessibilityElements` array; Android `android:accessibilityTraversalBefore`/`After`; Compose `isTraversalGroup` + `traversalIndex`; web DOM order)** — the reader follows a linear order you override when visual order ≠ logical order (e.g. a FAB read last). Trap: absolute-positioned overlays and z-stacked content routinely produce a reading order that jumps around the screen; verify it, never assume it matches layout.
- **Rotor (VoiceOver: twist two fingers) / reading controls + local context menu (TalkBack: swipe up-then-right)** — a radial/gesture menu to jump by headings, links, form controls, or **custom rotor actions** you register. Surface multi-touch/drag behaviors as **custom actions** (iOS `accessibilityCustomActions`; Compose `customActions`; web buttons) so they're reachable without the gesture. iOS 26 (Sept 15, 2025) added a **Copied Speech** rotor remembering the last ten clipboard speech items (three-finger quadruple-tap to copy). Trap: a swipe-to-delete row with no custom action simply does not exist for switch or screen-reader users (→ §19.4, §19.5).
- **Headings & landmarks (iOS `.header` trait; Android `accessibilityHeading` / Compose `heading()`; web `<h1–h6>` + ARIA landmarks)** — mark section titles so rotor/heading-jump works. Long scroll views with no headings force linear swiping through everything.
- **Live regions (Android `accessibilityLiveRegion = polite|assertive`; iOS `UIAccessibility.post(notification: .announcement / .screenChanged / .layoutChanged)`; web `aria-live`)** — announce changes the user didn't trigger (result count updating, toast). Trap: a toast (→ §15 components) that's only a visual overlay is invisible to a screen reader unless you post an announcement.

## 19.3 Beyond the screen reader — motor & speech AT

These rely on the *same* tree, plus correct focus order and gesture-free reachability. Building only for VoiceOver/TalkBack silently locks these users out.

- **Switch Control (iOS) / Switch Access (Android)** — drives the whole UI from one or two physical switches (or head-tracking / camera) by *scanning*: a cursor steps through focusable elements and the user fires on the highlight. Demands the semantic tree, correct focus order, and every action reachable without a gesture. Trap: gesture-only affordances (long-press, swipe, drag) with no equivalent custom action lock switch users out entirely.
- **Voice Control (iOS) / Voice Access (Android)** — operate by speaking control names ("Tap Share", or "Tap 7" via numbered overlays). It reads your **accessibility labels** as the spoken handles, so a mislabeled or unlabeled control has no name to say. Trap: two visible controls sharing one label become ambiguous ("which Send?") — labels must be unique within a screen.
- **Full Keyboard Access (iOS) / hardware keyboard nav (Android, macOS/Windows, web)** — every interactive element reachable and operable by Tab/arrow/Space/Enter, with a **visible focus indicator** (WCAG 2.4.7; 2.2's 2.4.11 *Focus Not Obscured* adds that focus must not be hidden behind sticky bars). Desktop and web-apps/PWAs treat this as table stakes; touch-first apps routinely ship custom controls that trap or skip focus. Trap: a custom modal that doesn't trap focus lets Tab wander to the page behind it (→ §19.4).
- **AssistiveTouch / Pointer Control / Dwell / head-tracking** — alternative pointers (eye-tracking, head-pointer, dwell-to-click). Rely on adequately sized, well-spaced targets — the same hit-area discipline as §19.5.

## 19.4 Focus management in custom controls

The hardest accessibility work is in components you build yourself; the OS can't infer focus intent.

- **Move focus on context change** — when a sheet, dialog, or new screen appears, programmatically move AT focus to it (iOS `UIAccessibility.post(.screenChanged, argument: firstElement)`; Compose `FocusRequester.requestFocus()`; web move DOM focus + `aria-modal="true"`). Trap: opening a modal while focus stays on the now-hidden trigger strands screen-reader and keyboard users behind the overlay.
- **Trap focus in modals** — keyboard/switch focus must cycle *within* the open dialog and not reach inert content behind it; restore focus to the trigger on close. Native iOS/Android sheets do this for you; hand-rolled overlays and most web-app modals do not unless you wire it.
- **Announce async results** — spinners, validation errors, and search-result counts must post an announcement or live-region update, not just repaint. A silent state change is invisible to AT.
- **Custom gestures need custom actions** — any swipe/long-press/drag affordance must register a custom action (iOS `accessibilityCustomAction`; Android `customActions`; web equivalent button) or it's unreachable by screen-reader, switch, and voice users (→ §19.2, §19.3).

## 19.5 Display & motion accommodations — what to substitute

Users set these system-wide; your job is to *respond* to the flag at runtime and re-render, not merely tolerate it.

| System setting | iOS API | Android / other | What you must substitute |
|---|---|---|---|
| Larger text | Dynamic Type (`UIFontMetrics`, `.dynamicTypeSize`) | scalable `sp` + `Configuration.fontScale`; Material type scale | Reflow; never clip or truncate; let rows grow |
| Reduce Motion | `UIAccessibility.isReduceMotionEnabled` / `.accessibilityReduceMotion` | `Settings.Global.TRANSITION_ANIMATION_SCALE` / `ANIMATOR_DURATION_SCALE`; web `prefers-reduced-motion` | Cross-fade (~0.3s) or instant cut; kill parallax & autoplay |
| Reduce Transparency | `isReduceTransparencyEnabled` | (vendor; web `prefers-reduced-transparency`) | Opaque fill — critical for iOS 26 Liquid Glass surfaces |
| Increase Contrast | `.accessibilityContrast` / `isDarkerSystemColorsEnabled` | High-contrast text; web `prefers-contrast` | Stronger borders, higher-contrast text/control colors |
| Bold Text | `isBoldTextEnabled` | Bold-text accessibility setting | Heavier weights app-wide |
| Differentiate Without Color | `shouldDifferentiateWithoutColor` | — (design-level) | Add icon/shape/text to anything coded by color alone |
| Smart Invert / color filters | `accessibilityIgnoresInvertColors` | Color correction / inversion | Exempt true photos/logos from inversion |
| Button Shapes / On-Off Labels | (system-applied) | — | Don't rely on color-only "this is tappable"; toggles show I/O glyphs |

- **Dynamic Type / large text** — iOS scales across content sizes up to the **Accessibility (AX) sizes** (to AX5, ~310% of the base body size); Android scales `sp` text by the user's font-scale, capped at **200% in Android 14+** with a **non-linear curve** so already-large text grows less (`scaledDensity` is now unreliable — read `fontScale`). Build with `UIFontMetrics`/`sp`, test at the largest size, and let layouts **reflow** — multi-line, vertical stacking, scrollable. Trap: fixed-height rows, single-line truncation, and `dp`/`pt`-pinned text clip the instant a user bumps the size; this is the #1 large-text failure. watchOS/Wear OS inherit the system text size — verify on the smallest display, not just the phone.
- **Reduce Motion** — Apple's App Store "Reduced Motion" criterion expects every non-essential animation to have a calmer alternative; large parallax/zoom transitions can trigger vestibular disorders. Substitute a fade or no transition. Trap: gating *only* decorative motion while leaving a full-screen zoom-push navigation transition intact — that transition is exactly what makes people sick.
- **Reduce Transparency** — most urgent on **iOS 26 Liquid Glass** and Windows **Acrylic/Mica**: frosted layers wreck text contrast. When the flag is on, render solid. Trap: text-over-blur that passes contrast on a dark photo and fails on a light one — blur contrast is content-dependent and unverifiable, so don't put load-bearing text on it at all (→ §19.6 contrast).
- **Color filters / Smart Invert (iOS) / color correction (Android)** — the system inverts or remaps colors for color-blind/light-sensitive users. Mark true photos/logos `accessibilityIgnoresInvertColors` so Smart Invert doesn't negate them. Never encode meaning in hue alone (→ Differentiate Without Color).

## 19.6 Touch targets, captions & non-visual channels

- **Touch target minimum — 44×44 pt (iOS HIG) / 48×48 dp (Material 3 / Android) / 24×24 CSS px (WCAG 2.2 SC 2.5.8, AA, web-apps/PWAs)** — the *hit area*, not the glyph; a 24-dp icon still needs ~48-dp padding around it. The three numbers differ deliberately: ship the platform's native minimum (44pt/48dp), not the lower WCAG web floor. visionOS leans larger and depends on gaze + pinch, so generous spacing matters more, not less. Trap: tightly packed icon rows and small "×" close buttons that meet the visible size but overlap neighboring hit areas — 2.5.8's spacing exception still requires undersized targets to not collide.
- **Captions & audio descriptions** — all spoken video/audio needs captions; meaningful visual-only video needs audio description (WCAG 1.2.x). Use the platform caption stack honoring the user's system caption appearance settings (iOS `AVPlayer` + Media Accessibility; Android `ExoPlayer` / `CaptioningManager`; web `<track kind="captions">`). Trap: burning captions into the pixels ignores the user's caption size/contrast preferences and can't be turned off.
- **Haptic & audio as supplementary, never sole, channels (→ §11)** — a haptic tap or success chime *reinforces* a state change, but a Deaf user misses audio and a user with reduced haptics or a muted/sleeved device misses haptics. Every meaningful signal needs a visual + AT-announced equivalent. On watchOS the Taptic Engine is a primary channel — still never the only one. Trap: "error" conveyed only by a buzz.
- **Sufficient contrast** — text 4.5:1 (normal) / 3:1 (large: ≥18pt, or ≥14pt bold); non-text UI components and states 3:1 (WCAG 1.4.3 / 1.4.11). Applies to placeholder text, meaningful disabled states, and text over imagery alike.

## 19.7 Testing & the standards gap

- **Accessibility Inspector (Xcode) + the built-in Audit** — inspect the live AT tree on iOS/macOS and audit for missing labels, contrast, hit-area, clipped text. **Accessibility Scanner (Android)** + **Espresso `AccessibilityChecks`** + Compose UI-test semantics assertions catch the same on Android; **Android lint** flags missing `contentDescription`. Web-apps/PWAs add **axe** / Lighthouse. Trap: automated tools catch only ~30–40% of issues — missing labels, contrast, target size — and cannot judge whether your reading order, grouping, or announcements *make sense*.
- **Test with the AT on, not just the inspector** — turn VoiceOver/TalkBack on and run the core flow eyes-closed; run Switch Control; bump Dynamic Type to AX5 / Android 200%; toggle Reduce Motion and Reduce Transparency. The inspector proves the tree *exists*; only real AT use proves it's *usable*. This manual pass is mandatory, not optional.
- **WCAG 2.2 applicability + the native gap** — WCAG 2.2 (Oct 2023) is web-normative; W3C's **WCAG2Mobile** (Guidance on Applying WCAG 2.2 to Mobile Applications) and **WCAG2ICT** map it onto native and hybrid apps. New 2.2 criteria that bite apps: **2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum)**; **2.5.7 Dragging Movements** (every drag has a single-pointer alternative — your swipe-to-delete must have a tap path); **2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured**; **3.2.6 Consistent Help**; **3.3.7 Redundant Entry**; **3.3.8 Accessible Authentication** (no cognitive-test CAPTCHA, no memorized-password gate without paste/biometric/passkey). What WCAG *doesn't* cover for native: OS-specific AT behavior — rotor/custom actions, Dynamic Type reflow, Switch scanning order, Reduce Motion/Transparency response, haptic-as-sole-channel — which comes from Apple HIG and Material 3, not WCAG. Treat the union of WCAG2Mobile + the platform HIG as the real bar; conformance to web WCAG alone is necessary but not sufficient for an app.

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Accessibility](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/accessibility); [Material 3 — Accessibility](https://m3.material.io/foundations/overview/accessibility); [Android Developers — Make apps more accessible](https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/ui/accessibility/apps); [Android 14 — non-linear font scaling](https://developer.android.com/about/versions/14/features#non-linear-font-scaling); [W3C WAI — Mobile Accessibility (WCAG2Mobile / WCAG2ICT)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/standards-guidelines/mobile/); [W3C — Understanding SC 2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/target-size-minimum.html).*

---

INP/March 12, 2024 confirmed. The Apple page doesn't state 400ms inline — that figure comes from WWDC guidance ("Optimizing App Launch", WWDC19), which is the standard citation. I'll soften the attribution to WWDC guidance and keep it accurate. I have enough verified facts. The draft's specs are sound; I'll tighten the "raised from 150MB" framing (the 200MB OTA limit is real, and the *uncompressed binary executable cap* is a separate ~500MB/4GB figure I won't overclaim), add missing patterns and platform halves, and strengthen traps/cross-refs.

Here is the complete revised section.

# 20. Performance & perceived performance

Speed is a feature; perceived speed is often the more valuable one. This section's job: name the latency budgets each platform enforces, the techniques that buy real speed, and the perception tricks that buy *felt* speed when real speed runs out. Governing principle: optimize the user's sense of time, not just the profiler's — but never use perception tricks to paper over a fixable stall.

## 20.1 Launch & startup

App launch is the first and harshest performance judgment; every platform meters it and the store consoles rank you on it.

- **Cold start / cold launch** — process not in memory: the OS forks/execs, loads the binary + frameworks, runs static initializers, then your first frame. Slowest path. iOS aims for **first frame within ~400ms** (WWDC "Optimizing App Launch" guidance; pre-main + first-frame budget); Android Vitals flags cold start ≥ **5s** as excessive. The trap: front-loading SDK init, network, or disk I/O on the main thread before first frame — defer everything not needed to draw screen one (→ §20.4 lazy init).
- **Warm start (Android) / resume-from-suspended (iOS)** — process alive, activity/scene torn down or backgrounded; cheaper than cold. Android Vitals threshold: warm ≥ **2s** excessive. iOS suspended apps resume near-instantly unless the OS jetsammed (memory-reclaimed) them, forcing a cold path.
- **Hot start** — process and UI both resident, app just foregrounded. Should feel instant. Android Vitals threshold: hot ≥ **1.5s** excessive. Trap: doing a full data refresh synchronously on every foreground so a "hot" start stutters like a cold one — render cached state first, refresh in background.
- **Launch screen (iOS `LaunchScreen.storyboard`) / splash screen (Android 12+ `SplashScreen` API) / web app `splash_screen` manifest icons** — static frame the OS shows *instantly*, before your code runs, to hide load latency. iOS **requires** a launch storyboard (static launch *images* are deprecated since iOS 14; the storyboard sizes to every device). Android 12+ mandates the system splash via `androidx.core:core-splashscreen`; rolling your own splash Activity on top fights the OS one and double-flashes. PWAs get an auto-generated splash from `name` + `background_color` + the maskable icon. Make it visually identical to your first real screen so the handoff is seamless; **never** put a logo-reveal animation or text (unlocalizable) on it. The trap: treating the splash as branding airtime — every extra second you hold it is latency you're advertising.
- **The launch-screen trick** — design the launch frame to match the first content frame's chrome (nav bar, background, empty list scaffold) so the transition reads as "already loaded" rather than "still loading." Pairs with a skeleton (→ §20.3) that the launch screen dissolves into.
- **Baseline Profiles (Android, AGP 7.1+ / `androidx.benchmark`)** — ship an AOT-compilation hint listing hot code paths (startup, scroll) so ART pre-compiles them at install instead of JIT-ing at runtime. ~20–30% faster cold start typical. Compose apps benefit most (Compose ships its own library profile). Cheap, high-leverage; no excuse to skip on a shipping Android app. iOS analog: the OS handles AOT itself (compiled Swift/ObjC), so there's no equivalent knob — you optimize static-init order instead.
- **Prewarming / `dyld` prewarm (iOS) / process pre-fork (Android Zygote)** — the OS may speculatively start your process before the user taps. You don't control it; don't *rely* on it (cold-start budget assumes no prewarm), but don't break it with heavy ObjC `+load`/static-init work either.
- **Time-to-interactive vs time-to-first-frame** — first frame is when pixels appear; interactive is when taps do something. The launch screen buys first-frame perception but a frozen-but-drawn screen still fails the user. Measure both; iOS `MetricKit` `MXAppLaunchMetric` and Android `Activity.reportFullyDrawn()` mark the *true* interactive moment, not just first draw. Web analog: TTI / INP-readiness vs LCP (→ §20.7).

## 20.2 Frame budget & jank

A dropped frame is the most visible performance failure — the eye catches a single stutter in a scroll. The budget is set by the display refresh rate, and it is unforgiving.

- **Frame budget** — total time to produce one frame: **16.67ms at 60Hz**, **8.33ms at 120Hz** (ProMotion / Android high-refresh), **6.94ms at 144Hz** (some gaming Androids). Everything — layout, paint, composite, your main-thread work — must fit. ProMotion (iPhone Pro since iPhone 13 Pro; all iPad Pro since 2017) and most flagship Android run adaptive refresh up to 120Hz, so the real ceiling for premium-feeling motion is 8ms, not 16ms. Adaptive panels also *drop* to 10–24Hz when static to save battery (→ §20.6).
- **Jank (Android) / hitch (iOS) / dropped frame** — a frame that misses its deadline, so the display repeats the previous frame and motion stutters. Android Vitals: a frame ≥ **16ms** is a *slow frame*; Play Console flags apps with slow frames in >25% of sessions. iOS Instruments calls these **hitches** and reports a "hitch time ratio" (ms of hitch per second of scroll).
- **Frozen frame (Android)** — a frame taking ≥ **700ms**. Play Console flags frozen frames in >0.1% of sessions. Almost always main-thread blocking (sync I/O, giant layout pass, decoding a bitmap inline). Treat as a bug, not a tuning target.
- **ANR — Application Not Responding (Android)** — main thread blocked ≥ **5s** on input, or a broadcast/service deadline missed. Triggers the system "App isn't responding" dialog and is the single worst Vitals signal — Google's current guidance is to **prioritize ANRs above all other latency**, because they're an outright failure, not a slowdown. iOS equivalent: the **watchdog** kills an app that blocks the main thread too long at launch/resume — the `0x8badf00d` ("ate bad food") termination code. Web equivalent: a long task that hangs the event loop → the browser's "page unresponsive" dialog (→ §20.7 INP).
- **Main-thread / UI-thread discipline** — the single rule under all of the above: never block the UI thread. iOS = main `DispatchQueue` / `@MainActor`; Android = main (UI) thread, offload via coroutines on `Dispatchers.Default`/`IO`; web = the single JS main thread, offload to Web Workers. Disk, network, JSON parse, image decode, crypto → background. The trap: a `@MainActor`-annotated function quietly `await`ing a slow call, or a `runBlocking` on the UI thread.
- **Choreographer (Android) / `CADisplayLink` (iOS) / `requestAnimationFrame` (web)** — the vsync-aligned callback to drive animation in lockstep with the display. Animate against these, not a fixed timer, so frames land on the refresh boundary and you inherit the right cadence on 60Hz vs 120Hz panels automatically. Trap: a `setTimeout`/`Timer`-driven animation that beats against vsync and visibly judders.
- **Overdraw** — painting the same pixel multiple times per frame (stacked opaque backgrounds, full-screen blurs). Wastes GPU fill rate, janks low-end devices. Android: "Debug GPU Overdraw" overlay; flatten view hierarchies, drop redundant backgrounds. iOS: Instruments "Color Blended Layers" / off-screen-rendering flags. Web: layer-count and paint-flashing in DevTools.

## 20.3 Perceived performance (the felt-speed toolkit)

When you can't make it faster, make it *feel* faster. These are perception techniques — they shift the user's experience of waiting, and they're as load-bearing as the real optimizations (→ §13 loading & feedback states).

- **Skeleton screen (shimmer / placeholder UI)** — render the page's structural shape (gray blocks where content will land) immediately, fill with real data when it arrives. Feels faster than a spinner because the eye perceives progress and layout stability. iOS: `redacted(reason: .placeholder)` in SwiftUI. Android: Compose placeholder modifiers / shimmer libs. Web: CSS skeletons. Trap: a skeleton that doesn't match the final layout causes a jarring reflow (and a CLS hit on web, → §20.7). Accessibility: mark skeletons `aria-hidden` / not-accessible and announce the real content on arrival, or screen readers read meaningless placeholder noise (→ §19).
- **Optimistic UI** — apply the user's action to the UI *immediately*, assuming success, then reconcile when the server responds (like/favorite, send message, reorder list). Collapses perceived latency to zero. Mandatory: a rollback path with a clear error if the write fails (→ §13 error states). Trap: optimistic UI on a non-idempotent or money-moving action — don't optimistically "transfer funds."
- **Doherty threshold (< 400ms)** — below ~400ms of system response, users stay in flow and productivity rises super-linearly; above it, attention drifts and the interaction feels like a transaction with a machine. The single most useful budget for interactive response. Pair with the RAIL **100ms** response goal for taps (→ §20.7).
- **Progressive rendering / streaming (progressive disclosure of content)** — show what you have the instant you have it (text before images, above-the-fold before below). Web: SSR streaming / React Suspense boundaries. Native: render the cached list, stream rows as they decode. Beats waiting for the complete payload.
- **Perceived-progress shaping** — a progress bar that accelerates toward the end *feels* faster than a linear or decelerating one; an indeterminate spinner past ~1s feels slower than a determinate bar. Use determinate progress whenever you can estimate duration. Trap: a determinate bar that stalls at 99% is worse than an honest indeterminate one — never fake precision you don't have.
- **Response-feedback latency tiers (Nielsen)** — **0.1s** = feels instantaneous (no indicator needed); **1s** = flow of thought stays uninterrupted (no indicator, but the user notices); **10s** = limit of attention, must show a determinate progress indicator or you lose them. Calibrate which feedback affordance you show to which tier.
- **Haptic + visual confirmation as latency mask** — a tap that fires a haptic and a state change *now* feels resolved even while the network call is in flight (→ §11 haptics). The micro-feedback occupies the perception gap. Accessibility: never make haptic the *only* confirmation — pair with a visual/aural state change (→ §19).
- **Optimistic navigation / instant transitions** — push the destination screen immediately with its skeleton rather than holding the current screen behind a spinner until data loads. The user perceives the app as responsive to their intent. Web: the View Transitions API (cross-document, Chromium 2024+) gives native-app-style transitions without a JS framework — but degrade gracefully where unsupported (Safari/Firefox lag).

## 20.4 Loading strategy: prefetch, lazy, virtualize

The fastest work is work you did before the user asked, or never did at all.

- **Prefetch / preload / predictive loading** — fetch the resource before the user requests it, based on likely next action (next list page on scroll-near-end, the article behind a hovered/focused link, the next onboarding step). Web: `<link rel="preload">` (this navigation), `rel="prefetch"` (next navigation), the **Speculation Rules API** for predictive prerender (Chromium). iOS: `UITableViewDataSourcePrefetching` / `UICollectionViewDataSourcePrefetching`. Android: Paging 3 `prefetchDistance`; `WorkManager` for predicted background fetch. Trap: aggressive prefetch on cellular burns the user's data and battery — gate on Wi-Fi / unmetered and respect Data Saver / Low Data Mode (→ §20.6).
- **Lazy loading / deferred init** — don't construct, fetch, or decode anything off-screen or not-yet-needed. Defer SDK init past first frame; instantiate view controllers/fragments on demand. iOS: `lazy var`, deferred `@StateObject`. Android: `by lazy`, `viewModels()` lazy delegate. The launch-time corollary of §20.1.
- **List virtualization / cell recycling (windowing)** — render only the rows in (and just beyond) the viewport, recycle off-screen views. The non-negotiable pattern for long lists. iOS: `UITableView`/`UICollectionView` dequeue, SwiftUI `LazyVStack`/`List`. Android: `RecyclerView`, Compose `LazyColumn`/`LazyRow`. Web: windowing (`react-window` / TanStack Virtual) or CSS `content-visibility: auto`. Trap: a SwiftUI `VStack` inside a `ScrollView`, or a Compose `Column` (eager), instead of the `Lazy` variant — renders every row up front and janks on long lists. Accessibility: virtualized rows must still expose correct collection semantics + total count to assistive tech, or the screen reader reports the wrong list length (→ §19).
- **Pagination / infinite scroll vs load-more** — cursor pagination keeps payloads small and TTI fast. Infinite scroll trades a clear endpoint for continuous flow; pair with prefetch-on-near-end so the next page is ready before the bottom. Accessibility veto: pure infinite scroll strands keyboard/screen-reader users and buries the footer — offer a "load more" button or a defined end, and manage focus on append (→ §19 accessibility).
- **Stale-while-revalidate** — render cached data instantly, fetch fresh in the background, swap when it lands. The user never waits on a spinner for content they've seen before. Underpins most "instant" feed apps (web: SWR/React Query; native: a cache layer in front of the network). Trap: swapping content under the user's eyes/scroll mid-read — reconcile without yanking scroll position.
- **Code splitting / on-demand modules** — ship less binary up front. Web: route-based dynamic `import()`. Android: Play Feature Delivery / dynamic feature modules. iOS: On-Demand Resources + app thinning. Smaller first download → faster first run (→ §20.6).

## 20.5 Assets, images & scroll

Media is where frame budgets go to die — decode and overdraw are the usual culprits behind scroll jank.

- **On-device image decode cost** — a JPEG/PNG is compressed bytes; decoding to a bitmap is CPU + memory and, done on the main thread mid-scroll, a guaranteed hitch. Decode **off the main thread**, downsample to the *display* size (never decode a 4000px image into a 100px thumbnail). iOS: `UIImage.preparingForDisplay()` (iOS 15+) / `ImageRenderer` / Nuke/Kingfisher. Android: Coil/Glide with explicit target sizing (Coil decodes off-thread by default). Web: `decoding="async"` + `createImageBitmap` in a worker.
- **Right-sized & modern formats** — serve the resolution the device actually shows at its scale factor (`@2x`/`@3x` iOS, `mdpi`→`xxxhdpi` Android, `srcset` density web). Prefer modern codecs: **HEIC** (iOS capture), **WebP** (universal baseline), **AVIF** (best ratio, web + Android). Web: `next/image` / responsive `srcset` + `sizes` + a resizing CDN beats shipping one giant asset. Trap: serving a 3x asset to a 1x display, or AVIF without a WebP/JPEG fallback for older clients.
- **`content-visibility` / off-screen skip (web)** — `content-visibility: auto` skips render + layout for off-screen subtrees, slashing initial render cost on long pages (pair with `contain-intrinsic-size` to keep the scrollbar honest and avoid CLS). The CSS analog of native virtualization.
- **Scroll performance** — the most scrutinized interaction; users feel a single dropped frame here. Keep cell binding cheap (no sync I/O, no inline decode, no layout thrash), pre-size cells to avoid re-measure, and prefetch the next page. Avoid blur/shadow/transparency-heavy cells that blow the GPU fill budget — *especially* under iOS 26 Liquid Glass, where layered real-time translucency is GPU-expensive and easy to overuse in scrolling content (→ §20.6 thermal). *[dated 2026: Liquid Glass scroll cost is real on older A-series silicon — profile glassy cells before shipping; Apple's own guidance is to use the system material sparingly, not on every row.]*
- **Asset preloading vs lazy media** — preload the hero/above-the-fold image (it's your LCP element on web, → §20.7) and never lazy-load it; lazy-load below-the-fold with `loading="lazy"` / native prefetching so off-screen media doesn't compete for bandwidth at launch. Trap: `loading="lazy"` on the LCP image — a measurable LCP regression.
- **Placeholder strategy (LQIP / blur-up)** — a low-quality placeholder (BlurHash, a tiny base64 thumbnail) or a dominant-color block holds layout and masks decode latency; the eye accepts a blurred-then-sharp transition far better than a pop-in, and a reserved box prevents layout shift (→ §20.7 CLS).

## 20.6 Binary size, battery & thermal

Performance isn't only latency — it's the download you ask for, the battery you drain, and the heat you generate. All three convert to churn.

- **App / binary size & download cost** — bigger download = lower install conversion, especially on slow networks and limited storage. Android: ship an **App Bundle (.aab)**, not a universal APK, so Play delivers per-device splits (ABI, density, language); Google Play's compressed-download limit is **200MB** for the base+config (overflow via asset packs / Play Asset Delivery, up to ~2–4GB). iOS: **app thinning** (slicing + On-Demand Resources) ships only the assets the device needs; the **App Store cellular-download prompt fires above 200MB** (raised from 150MB in 2019). Trap: bundling every localization, every `@3x` asset, and a dozen rarely-used SDKs into the base download.
- **Battery cost of animation/GPU** — continuous animation, video, and heavy compositing keep the GPU and display awake and drain battery; an always-animating screen is a battery complaint waiting to happen. Pause off-screen and idle animation, cap frame rate when nothing's moving (let the adaptive panel drop to 10–24Hz), and respect the system: iOS throttles background work; Android Doze / App Standby Buckets penalize background drain; web `Page Visibility API` + pausing `requestAnimationFrame` when hidden.
- **Thermal throttling** — sustained GPU/CPU load heats the device; the OS responds by *downclocking*, which tanks your frame rate — a performance problem caused by a performance problem. iOS exposes `ProcessInfo.thermalState` (`.nominal`→`.critical`); back off expensive effects when it rises. Android: `PowerManager.getThermalHeadroom()` / thermal status listeners. Common trigger: gratuitous shaders, particle systems, or stacked blur / Liquid-Glass layers running continuously.
- **Reduce-motion as a performance + a11y lever** — honoring "Reduce Motion" (iOS `UIAccessibility.isReduceMotionEnabled` / Android `Settings.Global.ANIMATOR_DURATION_SCALE` & `prefers-reduced-motion` / web `@media (prefers-reduced-motion)`) both serves vestibular-sensitive users **and** cuts GPU/battery cost. Accessibility veto on heavy parallax/auto-play motion: it must have a reduced-motion path (→ §19 accessibility, → §11 motion).
- **Network as battery** — radio wakeups are expensive (the cellular radio stays in a high-power state for seconds after each transfer); batch requests, coalesce polling, prefer push over poll. Predictive prefetch (§20.4) must be metered-network-aware (`NWPathMonitor` / `ConnectivityManager` / `navigator.connection`) or it's a battery *and* data regression.

## 20.7 Web-app / PWA performance

Web apps inherit the browser's instrumentation and the search engine's ranking pressure; the budgets are public and measured in the field.

- **Core Web Vitals (field metrics)** — Google's user-centric trio: **LCP (Largest Contentful Paint) ≤ 2.5s** (load), **INP (Interaction to Next Paint) ≤ 200ms** (responsiveness), **CLS (Cumulative Layout Shift) ≤ 0.1** (visual stability). "Good" = the **75th percentile** of real users clears the bar. *[dated: **INP replaced FID** as a Core Web Vital on **March 12, 2024** — FID is retired. INP is stricter: it measures *every* interaction's full input-delay→processing→presentation, not just the first input's delay.]*
- **LCP** — time until the largest above-the-fold element (usually the hero image or headline) paints. Preload it; don't lazy-load it; don't hide it behind a render-blocking font or script. Use `fetchpriority="high"` on the LCP image.
- **INP** — worst-case interaction latency across the session. Killed by long main-thread tasks blocking the event loop. Fix by breaking up long JS tasks (yield with `scheduler.yield()` / `isInputPending()`), deferring non-critical work, and shrinking hydration cost. The web analog of the native main-thread rule (§20.2).
- **CLS** — sum of unexpected layout shifts. Reserve space for images (`width`/`height` or `aspect-ratio`), ads, embeds, and async-injected content; size skeletons to match real content; use `font-display: optional`/`swap` carefully to avoid FOIT/FOUT shift. The most common own-goal: a late-loading banner shoving content down.
- **RAIL budget (web.dev)** — per-phase targets: **Response** to input in **100ms** (work in ≤50ms chunks so input isn't queued); **Animation** frame in **10ms** (16ms budget minus ~6ms browser overhead); **Idle** work in ≤50ms blocks so it doesn't starve the next input; **Load** to interactive in **≤5s** on a mid-range phone over slow connectivity. Older than Core Web Vitals but still the cleanest mental model for *which* budget applies to *which* moment.
- **Offline-first as perceived speed** — a service worker serving an app shell + cached data from Cache Storage / IndexedDB makes a PWA feel instant and survive flaky networks; the cache hit beats any network round-trip (→ §21 offline & sync). Trap: caching aggressively without a revalidation/versioning strategy → users stuck on a stale shell after deploy (always ship a `skipWaiting` + cache-version bust).
- **Hydration cost** — server-rendered HTML paints fast (good LCP) but is inert until JS hydrates; a heavy hydration pass blocks the main thread and wrecks INP. Mitigate with islands / partial / progressive hydration, React Server Components, or streaming SSR so interactivity arrives incrementally. *[dated 2026: the field is converging on "ship less JS, hydrate selectively" — load-bearing, not hype.]*

## 20.8 Measurement & tooling

You cannot fix what you don't measure, and synthetic numbers lie — field data from real devices is the truth.

- **Instruments (iOS/macOS, Xcode)** — the profiler suite. **Time Profiler** (CPU hot paths), **App Launch** template (cold-start breakdown), **Animation Hitches** (scroll/hitch ratio), **Allocations/Leaks** (memory), **Energy Log** (battery). First stop for any native-Apple perf bug.
- **MetricKit (iOS, `MXMetricManager`)** — on-device **field** metrics from real users: launch time, hang rate, hitch ratio, memory, energy, disk writes — delivered daily. The way to catch regressions you can't reproduce on your fast dev device.
- **Android Studio Profiler + Macrobenchmark / Microbenchmark** — CPU, memory, energy, network profilers plus the **Jetpack Macrobenchmark** library for repeatable startup + scroll (frame-timing) measurement in CI, and Microbenchmark for tight loops. Pair with **Perfetto / System Tracing** for system-wide vsync/Choreographer traces.
- **Android Vitals (Play Console)** — the **field** dashboard: ANR rate, slow/frozen frames, excessive startup, wakeups, all percentiled across real installs. The store ranks and surfaces apps partly on these — a distribution lever, not just a debug tool.
- **Lighthouse / PageSpeed Insights (web/PWA)** — **lab** (Lighthouse, synthetic) + **field** (CrUX real-user data) for Core Web Vitals, PWA installability, and a prioritized opportunities list. Run it in CI (`lhci`) to gate regressions. Trap: optimizing the lab score while field (CrUX) stays poor — lab runs one fast machine; users don't. For INP specifically, lab can't see real interactions — instrument with the field `web-vitals` JS library / RUM.
- **Performance budgets in CI** — encode the targets above as failing thresholds (bundle size, LCP, startup ms, hitch ratio) so a regression blocks the build instead of shipping. A budget nobody enforces is a wish.
- **Field over lab, p75 over mean** — always weight real-user, slow-device, slow-network data over your dev machine, and judge at the 75th/95th percentile, not the average — the tail is where users churn.

*Sources: [Apple — Reducing your app's launch time](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/xcode/reducing-your-app-s-launch-time); [Android — App startup time & Vitals](https://developer.android.com/topic/performance/vitals/launch-time); [Android — Slow rendering (frames/jank)](https://developer.android.com/topic/performance/vitals/render); [web.dev — INP is now a Core Web Vital (Mar 12, 2024)](https://web.dev/blog/inp-cwv-launch); [web.dev — Measure performance with the RAIL model](https://web.dev/articles/rail).*

---

Specs confirmed. The draft's facts (15-min WorkManager floor, `allowsConstrainedNetworkAccess`/`NWPath.isConstrained`, Background Sync `sync` event, Periodic Background Sync Chromium-only, `navigator.storage.persist()`, Android 15 6-hour FGS cap) are accurate. Here is the revised, strictly-more-complete section.

# 21. Offline, sync & data freshness

Apps run on subways, in basements, on airplanes, behind captive portals — flaky connectivity is a dimension web pages mostly dodge by reloading. The governing principle: **the local copy is the source of truth the user sees; the network is an eventual-consistency detail behind it.** Never block the UI on a request, never lie about sync state, never silently drop a write.

## 21.1 Architecture postures

- **Offline-first / local-first** — the app reads and writes a local store (SQLite, Core Data/SwiftData, Room, IndexedDB/OPFS) and treats the network as a background sync job, not a request/response gate. Every interaction succeeds instantly against local state; sync reconciles later. Reach for it whenever the app holds user-authored data (notes, tasks, messages) or is used on the move. Trap: bolting offline onto a request/response app late is a rewrite, not a feature — the data model, ID strategy (→ §21.2 client IDs), and conflict story have to be designed in from the start.
- **Local-first (the stronger claim)** — goes beyond "works offline": the local copy is canonical and durable, the network is optional, and the user keeps their data even if the server dies. CRDTs (→ §21.3) are the usual engine. Dated 2025–2026: genuinely load-bearing for collaborative tools (Linear's sync engine; ElectricSQL, Zero, PowerSync, Yjs/Automerge); hype when slapped on a CRUD app that needs a server round-trip for auth/billing anyway.
- **Cache-then-network (stale-while-revalidate)** — render cached data immediately, fire the fetch, swap in fresh data when it lands. The web's `stale-while-revalidate` Cache-Control directive and the service-worker SWR pattern formalize it; native apps hand-roll it (and SwiftUI/Compose data layers and React Query/SWR/Apollo do it for you). Default posture for feeds and read-heavy screens. Trap: silent swap mid-scroll yanks content under the user's thumb — diff and reconcile, don't blow away the list and reset scroll position.
- **Network-only** — no local copy; every screen needs a live request. Acceptable only for genuinely live, security-sensitive, or unbounded data (balance transfers, live auctions, a 50k-row admin table). Trap: shipping network-only by default because it's easier, then watching every screen spinner on the train.
- **Sync-engine substrates** — don't reinvent a sync engine when the platform or an off-the-shelf one gives you one. **iOS:** CloudKit / `NSPersistentCloudKitContainer` (Core Data ⇄ iCloud), SwiftData's CloudKit backing. **Android:** Room as the offline cache + WorkManager for sync is the Google-blessed pairing; Firestore offline persistence (on by default on mobile). **Web/cross-platform:** IndexedDB + a sync library (Dexie, Yjs, Replicache/Zero, PowerSync, ElectricSQL, WatermelonDB). The discipline: the local DB *is* the API the UI talks to; the UI never calls the network directly.

## 21.2 Optimistic updates & rollback

- **Optimistic update** — apply the user's change to local state and re-render *before* the server confirms, on the bet it'll succeed. Makes the app feel instant; mandatory for likes, toggles, reorders, sends. Trap: the rollback path is the hard part and the part everyone skips — if the request fails you must revert the UI *and* tell the user, or you've shown a lie. (→ §13 error states)
- **Client-generated IDs** — generate the entity's ID on-device (UUIDv4/v7, ULID, KSUID) at creation, not on the server. Lets optimistic items have a stable identity, be referenced/edited/deleted before sync, and survive a round-trip without a swap. Time-ordered IDs (UUIDv7/ULID) also keep insert order without a separate sort key. Trap: server returns its *own* ID and you now have two records — agree on client-authoritative IDs (or a reconciliation key) up front.
- **Rollback / reconciliation** — on failure, restore the pre-mutation value (keep the captured old value, don't re-derive it) and surface the failure non-destructively: a retry affordance, not a dialog that nukes the user's text. On success with a server-transformed payload, merge fields back in rather than overwriting local edits the user made in the meantime.
- **Optimistic concurrency control** — send the version/`updatedAt`/`ETag` you read; server rejects (`409 Conflict` / `412 Precondition Failed` with `If-Match`) if it changed underneath you. Turns "last write silently wins" into a detectable conflict you can surface (→ §21.3). Trap: optimistic *UI* without optimistic *concurrency* on the wire = silent overwrite of someone else's change.
- **Debounce vs. queue** — for rapid repeated mutations (slider drag, toggle spam), debounce/coalesce to the last value; for distinct actions (three messages sent offline), queue each (→ §21.5). Don't debounce a queue or you drop intent; don't queue a slider or you ship 200 PATCHes.

## 21.3 Conflict resolution

The unavoidable question of any sync system: two devices edited the same thing offline — now what.

- **Last-write-wins (LWW)** — newest timestamp (or version) clobbers the rest. Trivial to implement, lossy by design. Acceptable for single-user-multi-device settings, presence, "current screen". Trap: clock skew makes "newest" a lie, and LWW silently eats the loser's edit — the canonical silent-data-loss bug. Use a server-assigned monotonic version or a hybrid logical clock (HLC), never raw device wall-clock.
- **CRDT (Conflict-free Replicated Data Type)** — data structures that merge deterministically without coordination, so concurrent edits converge to the same state on every replica. Yjs/Automerge for rich text and JSON; the engine behind local-first collaboration. Reach for it when two users genuinely co-edit the same document offline. Trap: CRDTs converge to *a* consistent state, not necessarily the *intended* one — two people inserting at the same spot both survive, which can read as garbled; they also carry tombstone/metadata overhead that grows over time. Not a free lunch for every data shape.
- **Operational Transform (OT)** — the older Google-Docs-era approach: transform concurrent operations against each other through a central server. Powerful, notoriously hard to get right; mostly superseded by CRDTs for new builds. Cite it; rarely choose it fresh in 2025–2026.
- **Three-way merge / field-level merge** — diff each field against the common ancestor; auto-merge non-overlapping fields, conflict only on the same field. Dramatically fewer conflicts than whole-record LWW. The pragmatic middle ground for structured records (a contact, a task).
- **Merge UI (manual resolution)** — when auto-merge can't decide, show both versions and let the user pick or combine (git-style "keep mine / keep theirs / both"). The honest fallback. Trap: dumping a merge dialog on a non-technical user mid-task is hostile — reserve it for high-value, low-frequency conflicts, and make the diff legible. (→ §19 — both versions must be screen-reader-distinguishable and keyboard-navigable, not just color-coded; a red/green diff alone fails WCAG 1.4.1 *Use of Color*.)

| Strategy | Loss risk | Complexity | Use when |
|---|---|---|---|
| Last-write-wins | High (silent) | Low | Settings, presence, single-user |
| Field-level / 3-way merge | Low | Medium | Structured records |
| CRDT | None (converges) | High | Real-time co-editing |
| Manual merge UI | None | Medium (UX cost) | Rare high-value conflicts |

## 21.4 Sync-status indicators

Visibility of system status (NN/g heuristic #1) applied to data: the user must always be able to tell whether what they see is saved, saving, or stranded.

- **Per-item sync state** — surface state at the granularity of the thing that can fail: **queued → syncing → synced → failed**. A messaging app marks each message (clock = pending, single check = sent, double = delivered); a notes app shows per-note "saving…/saved". Trap: one global "syncing" spinner hides *which* item is stuck — when sync partially fails, the user can't find the casualty.
- **Synced / "all changes saved"** — the steady-state confirmation. Quiet, ambient, not a toast every keystroke. Google-Docs "All changes saved in Drive" is the archetype. Trap: a persistent loud "SAVED" badge trains the eye to ignore it, so the one time it says "NOT saved" nobody notices.
- **Delivery / read receipts** — a sync-state subspecies for messaging: *sent* (left device) vs *delivered* (reached recipient's device) vs *read* (recipient opened). iMessage and WhatsApp double-check / blue-tick conventions are the reference. Trap: conflating "sent" (your outbox drained) with "delivered" (their device has it) — they're different network legs and failing to distinguish them produces the "did my message go through?" anxiety the receipt exists to kill.
- **Failed / error state** — distinct, persistent (does *not* auto-dismiss), and actionable — tap to retry. Never a 2-second toast for a write that didn't land. (→ §13 destructive-failure handling, → §21.6 retry.)
- **Honest states only** — the cardinal sin is the **lying sync indicator**: showing "synced" when the write is sitting in a local queue, or a green check the instant the user taps before the request even fires. State must reflect the *actual* network outcome, not optimistic hope. A check that turns out false is worse than an honest spinner. (→ §19 — announce state changes via an `aria-live`/accessibility-announcement channel; a silent icon swap is invisible to screen-reader users.)
- **Platform conventions** — **iOS** leans on subtle inline text + SF Symbols (`arrow.triangle.2.circlepath`, `checkmark.icloud`, `exclamationmark.icloud`); the Files/Photos "uploading to iCloud" progress pie is the reference. **Material 3 (Android)** uses inline status + a `LinearProgressIndicator` for active sync and an error state with a retry action. **Desktop (macOS/Windows)** uses a menu-bar/tray sync glyph (Dropbox/OneDrive: green check / blue rotating / red X) plus per-file overlay badges in Finder/Explorer.
- **Watch / wearable** — screen real estate forbids verbose status; a single glyph or a haptic on sync-failure (→ §11) is the channel. watchOS commonly shows only the synced/failed terminal state, not the intermediate spinner. Don't replicate a phone's status list on a sub-2-inch screen.

## 21.5 Queued actions & the outbox

- **Outbox / action queue** — durable, persisted-to-disk log of mutations made while offline (or while a request is in flight), drained in order when connectivity returns. The backbone of "send works on the subway". Must survive app kill and reboot — an in-memory queue that dies with the process is the classic silent-loss trap. Platform substrates: **Web** — the **Background Synchronization API** (`sync` event; the browser wakes the service worker when connectivity returns — **Chromium-only**, absent on Safari/Firefox, so treat it as enhancement over a foreground-drained queue). **iOS** — a persisted queue drained on foreground + `BGProcessingTask`. **Android** — WorkManager (work survives process death and reboot by design).
- **Visible outbox** — let the user *see* pending actions ("3 messages waiting to send"; an Outbox folder à la Mail). Gives agency: cancel, edit, force-retry. Trap: a queue with no UI is a black hole — when it's stuck the user has no idea anything is pending.
- **Ordering & idempotency** — drain FIFO where order matters (messages), and make each queued request idempotent (client-supplied `Idempotency-Key`) so a retry after an ambiguous failure (request sent, response lost) doesn't double-post. Trap: retrying a non-idempotent POST on flaky networks creates duplicate charges/records — the single most expensive offline bug.
- **Queue poisoning** — one permanently-failing item (deleted server-side, validation rejection) must not block everything behind it. Detect, surface as failed, skip, keep draining. Trap: a poison message that retries forever burns battery and freezes the outbox.
- **Conflict on drain** — a queued edit may hit a server state that changed since it was queued; route the `409`/`412` into the conflict path (→ §21.3), don't just mark it "synced".

## 21.6 Retry, backoff & refresh

- **Exponential backoff with jitter** — on transient failure, retry after ~1s, 2s, 4s… plus randomized jitter to avoid a thundering herd of every client retrying in lockstep. WorkManager (`BackoffPolicy.EXPONENTIAL`, 10s floor), iOS background tasks, and resilient fetch wrappers bake this in. Honor `Retry-After` on `429`/`503` when the server sends it. Trap: fixed-interval hammering of a downed server slows its recovery and drains the device.
- **Manual retry** — every auto-retried failure also needs a user-initiated "Try again" — users won't wait out a backoff curve they can't see. Pair with honest state (→ §21.4).
- **Cap retries; distinguish permanent failures** — `4xx` (except `408`/`429`) won't fix themselves; stop retrying and surface. Only retry `5xx`, timeouts, and network errors. Trap: infinitely retrying a `403`/`422` burns battery forever and never succeeds.

### Pull-to-refresh

User-initiated foreground refresh: drag the top of a scrollable list past a threshold to trigger a fetch. **iOS** `UIRefreshControl` (UIKit) / SwiftUI `.refreshable`; **Android** Material `SwipeRefreshLayout` (Views) / Compose `PullToRefreshBox` (Material 3); **Web/PWA** browsers own native overscroll pull-to-refresh, so a custom one needs `overscroll-behavior-y: contain` to avoid double-triggering. Maps cleanly to feeds and lists. Traps: (1) it's *the only* refresh path on many screens — provide an alternative for users who can't perform the gesture (→ §19; SwiftUI exposes `.refreshable` to the VoiceOver rotor, but a custom implementation may strand VoiceOver/TalkBack users); (2) don't put pull-to-refresh on a non-scrollable or naturally-fresh screen — it implies a staleness that isn't there; (3) the spinner must reflect the *real* fetch, not a fixed 1.5s animation theater.

### Background refresh

- **iOS — `BGTaskScheduler`** — `BGAppRefreshTask` (short, seconds, for "keep content current" pulls) and `BGProcessingTask` (longer, for maintenance/large sync, can require charging). The *system*, not you, chooses when to run them based on battery, usage patterns, and Low Power Mode. SwiftUI exposes `.backgroundTask(.appRefresh)`. The user can disable **Settings → General → Background App Refresh** entirely — design for it never running. For large downloads use a **background `URLSession`** (handed to the `nsurlsessiond` daemon; completes even if the app is suspended or killed).
- **Android — WorkManager** — the canonical deferrable-sync API. Periodic work has a hard **15-minute minimum interval** (request less and it's clamped). Gate with `Constraints` (`NetworkType.CONNECTED`/`UNMETERED`, `setRequiresCharging`, `setRequiresBatteryNotLow`). Android 15 imposes a **6-hour cumulative runtime cap on `dataSync` foreground services**; sustained user-visible transfers should use a **user-initiated data-transfer (UIDT) job** (Android 14+). Doze and App Standby Buckets defer background work further — never assume timely execution.
- **Web — Periodic Background Sync API** (`periodicsync`) — refreshes content while a PWA is closed; **Chromium-only, requires an installed PWA, and is gated on a site-engagement score**; absent on iOS Safari and Firefox. Treat fresh-on-launch as a progressive enhancement, never a guarantee. (Don't confuse with one-shot **Background Sync**, §21.5, which fires once on reconnect.) **Background Fetch API** handles long downloads (large files, AI models) with OS-level progress UI and survives the page closing — where plain Background Sync would be killed.
- **Universal rule** — background refresh is best-effort on every platform; the OS can starve it indefinitely. The foreground experience must stand alone, with a visible "last updated" timestamp and a manual refresh.

## 21.7 Freshness, staleness & low-data modes

- **Cached / stale / fresh** — three distinct states the UI should be able to express. *Fresh* = just fetched; *cached* = shown from local store, possibly current; *stale* = known-old, beyond your freshness window. Trap: rendering stale data identically to fresh data, so the user trusts a number that's hours old.
- **"Last updated" labeling** — timestamp the data's age explicitly ("Updated 2 min ago", "Last synced 3:14 PM") on any screen where staleness has consequences — prices, balances, dashboards, transit ETAs. NN/g: showing the data's last-refresh time is a core visibility-of-system-status move for analytics/data UIs. Trap: relative timestamps ("2 min ago") that don't tick or re-render go stale themselves; refresh them on a timer or fall back to absolute time. (→ §19 — pair relative time with a machine-readable absolute timestamp so assistive tech and i18n locales render it correctly.)
- **Stale-data banner** — when showing cached content offline or after a failed refresh, a quiet inline banner ("You're offline — showing saved data from 9:41 AM") beats either lying (looks live) or blocking (empty screen). Trap: a modal that blocks the cached content the user *can* still use.
- **"You're offline" vs. "server's down"** — distinguish *no device connectivity* (radio off, airplane mode, no signal) from *server unreachable* (`5xx`, DNS, timeout). The messages and remedies differ: "Check your connection" vs. "We're having trouble — retrying". `navigator.onLine` / reachability only proves a *local link* exists, not that your server answers — confirm with a real request outcome before deciding which message to show. (→ §13 error taxonomy.)
- **Partial connectivity** — captive portals (hotel/airport Wi-Fi that intercepts requests), one-bar 3G, asymmetric reachability. `onLine === true` while every request 302s to a login page is the captive-portal trap (iOS/Android run their own captive-portal probe and surface a "Sign in" sheet; the web has no equivalent signal). Treat connectivity as a spectrum proven by successful requests, not a boolean from the radio.
- **Data-saver / low-data mode** — respect the OS signal: **iOS Low Data Mode** (`URLRequest.allowsConstrainedNetworkAccess`, `NWPath.isConstrained`; requests that violate it fail with `URLError` reason `.constrained`), **Android Data Saver** (`ConnectivityManager.getRestrictBackgroundStatus()`), **Web** `navigator.connection.saveData` (Save-Data header / Network Information API — Chromium-only). Under it: defer prefetch, drop image quality, pause autoplay, skip non-essential background sync. Trap: ignoring the flag and burning a metered/roaming plan — a trust-and-cost violation, not just a perf nit.
- **Adaptive fetching by network quality** — `navigator.connection.effectiveType` (web, `2g`/`3g`/`4g`), `NWPath.isExpensive`/`isConstrained` (iOS), `NetworkCapabilities` (Android) let you scale payload to a metered/slow link. Reach for it on media-heavy feeds. Trap: over-engineering it on a text app where it doesn't move the needle.

## 21.8 Download-for-offline

- **Explicit "Download" / "Make available offline"** — user pins specific content (a playlist, map region, episode, document) for guaranteed offline access, distinct from incidental caching. Spotify/Netflix downloads and Google Maps offline regions are the archetypes. Reach for it for large, deliberately-consumed media. Trap: conflating eviction-eligible cache with user-pinned downloads — auto-evicting something the user explicitly saved (because storage got tight) is a betrayal; pinned content is sacred until the user removes it.
- **Download state & progress** — queued → downloading (with %) → downloaded → (failed/paused). Resumable across app launches and network drops. Substrates: **iOS** background `URLSession` (resumable via `resumeData`), **Android** WorkManager / `DownloadManager`, **Web** **Background Fetch API** (OS progress UI, cancelable, survives page close). Trap: a download that restarts from 0% after a blip on a 2GB file — implement range-request resume.
- **Storage transparency & eviction** — show how much offline content occupies, let the user manage/remove it, and warn before downloads fail on a full disk. **Web** caches/IndexedDB/OPFS are subject to **eviction under storage pressure** unless you request **persistent storage** (`navigator.storage.persist()`); query headroom with `navigator.storage.estimate()`. **iOS/Android** can purge app caches the user hasn't pinned, and iOS "Offload App" removes the binary but keeps documents. Trap: assuming the browser keeps your "offline" data forever — by default it doesn't.
- **Expiry / licensed content** — DRM/licensed downloads (video, audiobooks) expire; show the countdown and re-validate the license on reconnect. Trap: a silently-expired download that fails at the exact moment the user is finally offline and wanting it.

## 21.9 Local-store migration & data-loss safety

- **On-device schema migration** — the local DB (Core Data/SwiftData, Room, SQLite, IndexedDB) has its own schema that must migrate on app update, often while older app versions still write the *old* shape against the *same* synced backend. Plan migrations to be additive/backward-compatible (expand-contract) — a destructive local migration on launch can wipe unsynced data. Trap: shipping a migration that drops a column holding queued-but-unsynced writes; they're gone before they ever reached the server. (Room requires explicit `Migration`s or `fallbackToDestructiveMigration` — the latter literally deletes data; IndexedDB upgrades run in `onupgradeneeded` and block other tabs.)
- **Undo / time-travel** — durable local history (CRDT op-log, event sourcing) enables multi-step undo and version history that survives offline edits, beyond a single in-memory undo stack. Reach for it in editors and note apps. (→ §19 — undo must be keyboard- and screen-reader-reachable, not gesture-only.)
- **Conflict-safe deletes** — a delete that races a concurrent offline edit needs a tombstone, not a hard row removal, or the edit "resurrects" the record on next sync. Soft-delete + tombstone is the standard fix.

*Sources: [Apple — Using background tasks (BGTaskScheduler / BGAppRefreshTask)](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/backgroundtasks); [Apple — allowsConstrainedNetworkAccess / NWPath.isConstrained](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/foundation/nsurlrequest/allowsconstrainednetworkaccess); [Android — Define work requests (WorkManager periodic 15-min floor & constraints)](https://developer.android.com/develop/background-work/background-tasks/persistent/getting-started/define-work); [MDN — Background Synchronization API](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Background_Synchronization_API) & [web.dev — Periodic Background Sync](https://web.dev/articles/periodic-background-sync); [MDN — StorageManager.persist() (persistent storage & eviction)](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/StorageManager/persist); [NN/g — Visibility of System Status](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/visibility-system-status/).*

---

Good. I have confirmed iOS 26/visionOS 26 spatial widgets, Android 16 Live Updates timeline, and the App Clip facts. I have enough to write the section precisely. Here is the complete revised section.

# 22. Platform extensions & ambient surfaces

The app is not the only surface it owns. Modern OSes let an app project glanceable state and one-tap actions into the home screen, lock screen, status bar, search, the car, the wrist, and the system share pipe — without the user ever opening it. This section catalogs those off-window surfaces, what each platform calls them, and the failure mode each invites. Governing principle: **an ambient surface earns its place by being faster than opening the app; if it isn't, it's clutter.** Every one is glanceable-first, latency-sensitive, and a privacy surface — it renders without authentication, in front of whoever holds the device (→ §19 accessibility, §20 privacy).

## 22.1 Home & lock-screen widgets

Persistent, mostly non-interactive tiles that render app state on the home screen, lock screen, or today view. Read-mostly: a widget is a *view into* the app, refreshed by the system on its own budget — not a place to run the app.

- **Widget (iOS WidgetKit) / App Widget (Android) / Live Tile (Windows, retired)** — a tile the OS renders from a snapshot your app supplies; the app process isn't running while it's on screen. iOS widgets are SwiftUI `TimelineProvider` snapshots (the system asks for a timeline of future states and renders them on schedule); Android widgets are `RemoteViews` (a restricted, serializable subset of the view hierarchy — no custom views, limited layout set). Windows killed Live Tiles in Windows 11; the desktop "widget board" is now a walled Microsoft surface, not an open app extension point. Trap: treating a widget as a live mini-app — it's a static snapshot between refreshes, and the system, not you, decides when it redraws.
- **iOS widget sizes** — `systemSmall` (2×2), `systemMedium` (4×2), `systemLarge` (4×4), and `systemExtraLarge` (iPad / StandBy, ~4×8). Provide every size you declare; the user picks. Trap: cramming the small size with what only fits in large. Each size is a distinct design, not a scaled crop.
- **Android widget sizing** — declare `targetCellWidth`/`targetCellHeight` (launcher grid cells, Android 12+) with `minWidth`/`minHeight` in dp as the pre-12 fallback. Legacy cell math is `70 × n − 30` dp for an `n`-cell span. Ship responsive layouts via a `SizeF → RemoteViews` map (`addSizeMappedRemoteView` / `RemoteViews(Map<SizeF, RemoteViews>)`, API 31+) so one widget reflows across launcher grids. Trap: a single fixed layout that clips on dense launchers or strands whitespace on sparse ones.
- **Interactive widgets** — buttons and toggles inside the widget that run an action without launching the app. iOS 17+ wires SwiftUI `Button`/`Toggle` to an **App Intent** (→ §22.5); Android has had compound buttons via `RemoteViews` + `PendingIntent` for years and added `setOnCheckedChangeResponse` / `RemoteViews.RemoteResponse` for inline toggles. Trap: putting a destructive or multi-step action behind a widget tap — there's no confirmation surface and no auth gate on the home screen (→ §20 privacy).
- **Lock Screen widgets (iOS 16+)** — three constrained families: `.accessoryCircular` (gauges/rings), `.accessoryRectangular` (a few lines of text), `.accessoryInline` (one line beside the clock). Rendered in a desaturated vibrant material — design for monochrome, not color. Android's nearest equivalent is the lock-screen notification/AOD, not a true widget. Trap: relying on color or brand fills to carry meaning; the lock screen flattens them (→ §19 — never color as the sole signal).
- **StandBy (iOS 17+)** — landscape, charging, always-on display showing large widgets and Live Activities at a distance. Night mode tints everything red; readability at 2–3 m is the bar. Trap: small type or dense data — StandBy is read across a room, not in the hand.
- **Spatial / persistent widgets (visionOS 26, 2025)** — widgets become 3D objects pinned into the user's physical room, persisting across sessions, with WidgetKit depth/elevation parameters and a recessed-vs-surface mounting style. Reuse the same `TimelineProvider`; the new axis is depth and physical placement. *Date-flag:* shipped fall 2025, API surface still settling — treat depth/mounting specifics as volatile through 2026.
- **Tinted & Liquid Glass widgets (iOS 18 / iOS 26, 2025)** — iOS 18 added a system-wide dark/tinted home-screen mode that recolors widget content; iOS 26's Liquid Glass restyles widget chrome with refractive translucency. Supply a template/monochrome rendering so tinting doesn't mangle your art. *Date-flag:* Liquid Glass is new and still settling — treat its exact material API as volatile through 2026; the tinting requirement is load-bearing now. Trap (accessibility): translucent glass chrome erodes text contrast — verify against WCAG on real backdrops, don't trust the simulator (→ §19).
- **Generated previews (Android 15+)** — the picker renders a live preview from your provider (`AppWidgetProviderInfo.generatedPreviews` / `setWidgetPreview`) instead of a stale static screenshot. Trap: shipping only the legacy `previewImage` so users pick blind.
- **Refresh budget** — neither platform lets a widget update freely. iOS meters WidgetKit timeline reloads (roughly 40–70 per widget per day; frequent reloads get throttled); Android's `updatePeriodMillis` floor is **30 minutes** (use `WorkManager` or a `PendingIntent` for faster, event-driven pushes). Trap: designing a widget that *needs* second-by-second freshness — that's a Live Activity, not a widget.

## 22.2 Live Activities & Dynamic Island (iOS) / Live Updates (Android)

Real-time, time-bounded status for one ongoing event — a ride, a game, a delivery, a timer — on the lock screen and in the Dynamic Island. Long iOS-exclusive; Android 16 ships a first-class analog.

- **Live Activity (iOS 16.1+)** — a glanceable, auto-updating lock-screen card backed by **ActivityKit**, pushed via APNs (`liveactivity` push type) or updated locally. Budget: stays live up to **8 hours** of active updates, then the system stales it; removed from the lock screen up to **12 hours** after. Trap: using one as a generic notification feed — it's for a *single, finite, user-anticipated* event with a clear end state.
- **Dynamic Island (iPhone 14 Pro+)** — the pill around the front camera that hosts active Live Activities. Four presentations: **compact leading** + **compact trailing** (the two slivers flanking the pill), **minimal** (a single circle when multiple activities contend), and **expanded** (long-press / glance — a full custom layout). You must design all four; the system chooses based on context and contention. Trap: designing only the expanded state and letting compact/minimal fall back to a clipped mess.
- **Live Updates (Android 16, 2025)** — Android's Live-Activity answer: a promoted, progress-style ongoing notification (`Notification.ProgressStyle` + the `LIVE` / ongoing-activity treatment) that surfaces on the lock screen, AOD, and status-bar chip for ride/delivery/navigation. Phones/foldables in 2025; Wear OS follows in 2026. Trap: assuming parity with ActivityKit — it's still a notification under the hood, with notification-grade interactivity and ranking, not a custom-rendered card. *Date-flag:* rolling out through late 2025; behavior varies by OEM skin.
- **Interactivity (iOS 17+)** — Live Activities take App Intent buttons (pause a timer, advance a step) inline. Same rule as widgets: no auth gate, so don't gate irreversible or financial actions here (→ §20).
- **Broadcast / channel push (iOS 18+, 2025)** — channel-based pushes fan one update out to many subscribers (live scores) without per-device sends. *Date-flag:* newer, evolving; reach for it only at broadcast scale.

## 22.3 Watch & wearable surfaces

The wrist is the most glanceable surface there is — sub-second, low-density, almost always read at arm's length. Design for a single datum.

- **Complication (watchOS) / Tile (Wear OS)** — the small data slots on a watch face (watchOS) vs. the swipeable cards beside the face (Wear OS). watchOS complications are built with **WidgetKit** (the same `.accessory*` families as Lock Screen widgets, watchOS 9+) — **ClockKit** is the retired predecessor. Wear OS **Tiles** are a separate Jetpack library (Protolayout); Wear OS **Complications** are a *distinct* API (`ComplicationData` / `ComplicationDataSourceService`) that feeds watch-face slots. Trap: conflating Wear OS Tiles (full swipeable cards) with Wear OS Complications (tiny watch-face data points) — different APIs, different real estate.
- **Watch face** — third parties can't ship custom faces on watchOS (Apple-only); Wear OS exposes the **Watch Face Format** (XML-declared, 2024+) so faces ship as data, not code, and **WFF becomes mandatory for new faces** going forward. Trap: assuming parity — a "custom watch face" is a non-starter on Apple, code-built faces are a dead end on new Wear OS.
- **Smart Stack / widget stack (watchOS 10+)** — a scrollable, system-ranked stack of widgets surfaced by a Digital Crown turn (and auto-surfaced contextually in watchOS 11). Relevance-ranked by time/location; you supply `RelevanceContext` / `TimelineEntryRelevance`. Trap: assuming yours is always on top — earn placement with relevance signals.
- **Glance budget** — a wearable view is read in under two seconds. Trap: porting a phone layout to the wrist. Strip to one number, one icon, one tap — and note hit targets are even more critical at this size (→ §19).

## 22.4 Instant experiences — App Clips & Instant Apps

A slice of the app that runs without a full install, invoked from a link, code, or tag. The pitch is zero-friction first use; the constraint is a hard size ceiling.

- **App Clip (iOS, iOS 14+)** — a lightweight, install-free slice: ≤ **15 MB** (uncompressed) for physical invocations like NFC and **App Clip Codes**, ≤ **50 MB** for digital invocations like Safari/Messages links (raised from 10 MB in iOS 16). Invoked by App Clip Code, NFC, QR, Maps, Safari Smart App Banner, location suggestions, or Messages. Scoped to one task (pay for parking, order a coffee), then a prompt to install the full app. Trap: trying to fit a whole flow under the ceiling, or asking for sign-in/permissions a one-shot user won't grant.
- **Instant App / Google Play Instant (Android)** — **deprecated; Google Play removes Instant App support in December 2025.** Was a ≤ 15 MB install-free module launched from a link or a Play Store "Try now." Trap: building anything new on it — migrate to a fast-installing app, a deep link, or a web flow. *Date-flag:* the sunset is current and load-bearing; do not author new Instant Apps.
- **Instant net effect** — with Android's path closing, "install-free trial" is now largely an iOS-only native pattern. Cross-platform, the durable equivalent is a fast **PWA** / web landing reached by the same link (→ §22.6 deep links). Trap: assuming an App Clip strategy ports to Android — it doesn't anymore; design the web fallback as the primary on Android.

## 22.5 Intents, assistants & shortcuts

Exposing app actions to the system so they're invokable by voice, Spotlight, automation, or a long-press — without opening the UI. This is where the app becomes a verb the OS can call.

- **App Intent (iOS 16+) / App Actions & `shortcuts.xml` (Android)** — a declared, parameterized app capability the system can invoke. iOS **App Intents** (the modern replacement for **SiriKit** custom intents) power Shortcuts, Spotlight, widgets, controls, and Siri from one definition; Android exposes capabilities to **Google Assistant / Gemini** via `shortcuts.xml` Capabilities bound to **Built-In Intents (BIIs)**. Trap: vague titles/parameters — the assistant can't disambiguate "open it" across ten apps. Provide typed, well-named parameters and synonyms.
- **App Shortcut / Quick Action** — zero-setup phrases (iOS `AppShortcut` / `AppShortcutsProvider`, auto-surfaced in Spotlight/Siri) and **dynamic/pinned shortcuts** (Android `ShortcutManager`). Android shows roughly **5** dynamic+pinned at a time and rate-limits `pushDynamicShortcut`. Trap: exposing every action instead of the 2–3 that matter.
- **Shortcuts app (iOS/macOS) / Routines & scripting (Android)** — user-built automations chaining App Intents across apps. Your job is clean, composable intents with typed inputs/outputs. Trap: side-effectful intents with no return value — they can't chain.
- **Home-screen quick actions (iOS Home Screen Quick Actions / Android long-press app-icon shortcuts)** — the menu from long-pressing the app icon. iOS: static (`Info.plist`) + dynamic (`UIApplicationShortcutItem`); Android: the same `ShortcutManager` static/dynamic/pinned model surfaced by the launcher. Trap: stale dynamic shortcuts pointing at content the user already cleared.
- **Siri / Google Assistant / Gemini invocation** — voice entry into the above intents. Provide natural phrases and synonyms; handle the no-screen case (a spoken confirmation, not a silent UI mutation). Trap: an intent that only makes sense with the screen on — voice and CarPlay users get nothing back (→ §19 — voice-only is an accessibility path, not a fallback).
- **Control Center controls (iOS 18+, 2025)** — `ControlWidget`s you contribute to **Control Center**, the **Lock Screen** control slots, and the **Action button** — built on WidgetKit, backed by an App Intent. `ControlWidgetButton` (discrete action) and `ControlWidgetToggle` (boolean state, with a `ControlValueProvider` for live state). Trap: a toggle whose displayed state lags reality — supply the current value or it lies on the lock screen. *Date-flag:* iOS 18+; API still maturing.
- **Quick Settings tile (Android)** — your `TileService` in the notification-shade quick-settings panel (toggle VPN, start a scan). Android 13+ lets the app *request* the user add the tile (`requestAddTileService`). The closest cross-platform cousins are iOS Control Center controls (above). Trap: firing the add-tile prompt aggressively on launch — it's a one-shot goodwill ask.

## 22.6 Linking, search & continuity

Getting *into* the right place from outside the app — a URL, a search hit, a handoff from another device — landing on the exact screen, not the home tab.

- **Deep link / Universal Link (iOS) / App Link (Android)** — a URL that opens a specific in-app destination. **Universal Links** (iOS, `apple-app-site-association` file served from `/.well-known/`) and **Android App Links** (`assetlinks.json` + `android:autoVerify`) are the *verified* HTTPS variety — they open the app with no disambiguation dialog and fall back to the website if the app's absent. Custom-scheme links (`myapp://`) are the legacy, unverified, hijackable kind. Trap: shipping a custom scheme where a Universal/App Link is required — the unverified scheme can be claimed by any installed app and breaks the web fallback (→ §20 — scheme hijack is a real attack surface).
- **Deferred deep link** — the link survives an install: tap → install → first launch lands on the intended content, not a cold home screen. The OS doesn't carry intent across the install boundary, so it needs an attribution layer (iOS pairs this with App Clips, SKAdNetwork, and clipboard/`AppClip` handoff constraints). Trap: assuming the link "just works" post-install — without deferred handling the context is lost at install.
- **Spotlight / App indexing (iOS Core Spotlight / Android AppSearch)** — making in-app content findable in system search. iOS: `CSSearchableItem` (Core Spotlight) + App Intents donations + `NSUserActivity`; Android: **AppSearch** (Jetpack) — Firebase App Indexing is **shut down**, don't build on it. Trap: indexing content the user can't reach (stale, deleted, or auth-gated) — a search hit that dead-ends erodes trust.
- **Handoff / Continuity (Apple) / Cross-device (Android)** — resume an activity on another device. **Handoff** uses `NSUserActivity` (start on iPhone, finish on Mac). Android's story is the **Cross-device SDK** + **Nearby Connections** — no single Handoff equivalent. Trap: declaring a Handoff activity without restoring full state — the receiving device must reconstruct the exact context, not just open the app.
- **Share sheet / Activity view (iOS `UIActivityViewController`) / Sharesheet (Android `Intent.ACTION_SEND` + chooser)** — the system UI for sending content out of (or into) your app. Provide a **Share Extension** (iOS) / handle `ACTION_SEND` (Android) to *receive* shared content, plus rich `LPLinkMetadata` (iOS) / `ChooserAction` previews (Android) so a shared payload looks like more than a bare URL. Android 13+ pins **Direct Share / sharing shortcuts** (people targets) into the top row. Trap: rolling a custom share UI instead of the system sheet — you lose every installed target the user already trusts, plus the OS's privacy framing.
- **Notification Service / Content Extension (iOS) / custom & expanded notifications (Android)** — modifying or enriching a notification before display: decrypt a payload, attach media, build a custom expanded layout. iOS: `UNNotificationServiceExtension` (mutate, ~30 s budget) + `UNNotificationContentExtension` (custom UI); Android: `RemoteViews` custom/expanded layouts and `MessagingStyle`/`CallStyle` templates.
