Trap: heavy work in the service extension — it has a tight time/memory budget and gets killed, delivering the un-mutated fallback (→ §11 notifications, §19 — custom layouts must still expose accessible text).

## 22.7 In-vehicle & embedded surfaces

The app projected onto a car's head unit under a templated, driving-safe UI the platform fully controls. You supply data and structure; the OS renders, throttles, and locks down interaction.

- **CarPlay (Apple) / Android Auto (projected) & Android Automotive OS (embedded)** — **CarPlay** and **Android Auto** *project* from the phone onto the head unit; **Android Automotive OS (AAOS)** runs natively *in* the car with no phone. Both projected platforms restrict you to **template** apps (lists, grids, maps, now-playing, messaging, point-of-interest) from an approved category via Apple's CarPlay app templates / Android's **Car App Library** — you don't draw freeform pixels. Trap: assuming you can ship arbitrary UI — categories are gated (navigation, audio, EV charging, parking, messaging, …) and freeform drawing is rejected in review.
- **Driver-distraction guidelines** — both platforms enforce hard limits: capped list lengths (often ~6 items before paging), no video while moving, minimum text sizes, voice-first text entry, and per-task interaction ceilings. Trap: porting phone density to the dash — the car UI is deliberately sparse, and exceeding interaction limits fails review. Automotive is a safety/accessibility regime, not a styling choice (→ §19).
- **Now Playing / messaging templates** — audio and messaging are the most permissive categories; both expose standardized now-playing and read-aloud/reply-by-voice templates wired to steering-wheel and voice controls. Trap: custom transport controls — use the template so hardware buttons and voice commands map correctly.

---

**Cross-cutting traps.** (1) *No auth gate:* widgets, Live Activities, lock-screen controls, and complications render in front of whoever holds the device — never surface PII or one-tap destructive/financial actions on them (→ §20 privacy). (2) *Snapshot ≠ live:* most of these are system-refreshed snapshots on a metered budget; design for staleness and show a timestamp when freshness matters. (3) *Glanceable-first:* every surface here is read in seconds at arm's length — one datum, one action; if it needs scrolling or reading, it belongs in the app. (4) *Each is a distinct canvas, not a reflow:* the small widget, the compact Dynamic Island, the inline lock-screen complication, the spatial widget, and the CarPlay list are separate designs — drawing one and letting the rest clip is the dominant failure mode. (5) *Contrast survives the material:* tinted, vibrant, and Liquid-Glass renderings flatten color and erode contrast — verify legibility against WCAG on real backdrops (→ §19).

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Widgets](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/widgets); [Apple HIG — Live Activities](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/live-activities); [Apple — App Clips overview](https://developer.apple.com/app-clips/); [WidgetKit — Creating controls (Control Center, iOS 18)](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/widgetkit/creating-controls-to-perform-actions-across-the-system); [Android — App widgets overview & responsive layouts](https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/appwidgets); [Android — Live updates (Android 16)](https://developer.android.com/about/versions/16/features/progress-centric-notifications); [Android — App Links verification](https://developer.android.com/training/app-links); [Apple — visionOS 26 spatial widgets](https://www.apple.com/newsroom/2025/06/visionos-26-introduces-powerful-new-spatial-experiences-for-apple-vision-pro/).*

---

My draft facts hold up. The figures and Tahoe details are confirmed. I have enough to finalize. Here is the revised, strictly-more-complete section.

# 23. Desktop app patterns

Desktop is the high-density, pointer-first, keyboard-heavy end of app design: large displays, precise cursors, real hover state, persistent chrome, and power users who live in the app all day. The governing principle: **earn the screen** — desktop users tolerate and expect more controls visible at once than any phone affords, but only when each control has a precise hit target, a keyboard path, and a home that doesn't move. macOS and Windows diverge on nearly every primitive below; name both, and never port one platform's chrome wholesale onto the other.

## 23.1 Menus & the menu model

- **Menu bar (macOS) / menu bar (Win32) / app menu (Material, GNOME)** — the horizontal list of pull-down menus. On macOS it lives at the **top of the screen**, owned by the OS, always present, showing the frontmost app's menus — never inside the window. On Windows it sits **inside the window** (or collapses into a hamburger/`CommandBar` in WinUI/Fluent apps); GNOME moved to a header-bar hamburger, KDE keeps a classic in-window bar. Trap: building a custom in-window menu bar on macOS — users reach for the screen-top bar and find nothing; ship a real `NSMenu`/`MainMenu`.
- **App menu (macOS)** — the bold menu named after the app (right of the  menu), home to *About*, *Settings…*, *Services*, *Hide* (⌘H), and *Quit* (⌘Q); Settings is **⌘,**. Trap: burying preferences under a toolbar gear instead of *App ▸ Settings…* — Mac users muscle-memory ⌘, and the App menu.
- **Standard menu set (macOS)** — File, Edit, View, Window, Help, in that order, with expected contents (Edit: Undo/Redo/Cut/Copy/Paste/Select All; Window: Minimize/Zoom + window list; Help: a searchable field). Honor the convention; users scan by position, not by reading.
- **Menu item states & affordances** — enabled/disabled (greyed), checkmark for toggles, dash/dot for mixed state, submenu disclosure (▸), trailing shortcut glyphs. macOS renders symbols (⌘⇧⌥⌃); Windows spells `Ctrl+Shift+S` and underlines the mnemonic. Trap: disabling a menu item with no explanation — keep it enabled and explain why it can't run, or users assume the app is broken.
- **Mnemonics / access keys (Windows) vs none (macOS)** — Windows exposes `Alt+F` then `S` underlined access keys, an accessibility requirement reachable without a mouse; macOS has no menu mnemonics, only full key equivalents. Don't fake underlined-letter mnemonics on Mac.
- **Help menu search (macOS)** — the Help field searches *menu commands themselves* and highlights where each lives. A free discoverability win — keep menu titles descriptive so search finds them.
- **Overflow / "more" menus** — when a command bar can't fit, Fluent collapses extras into a `…` overflow `CommandBar`; macOS toolbars collapse into a `»` chevron. Order by frequency, not alphabetically.

## 23.2 Window chrome & title bars

- **Traffic lights (macOS) vs caption buttons (Windows)** — macOS puts close/minimize/zoom as three colored dots **top-left**; Windows/Linux put minimize/maximize/close **top-right**. Red = close, yellow = minimize, green = *zoom* (fit-to-content, ⌥-click or full-screen), **not** maximize. Trap: a cross-platform app hardcoding control position or behavior — green ≠ maximize, left ≠ right, and Windows Snap layouts live on hover of the maximize button.
- **Unified toolbar (macOS)** — the default: window controls, title, and toolbar share **one row**, icons-only by default; **Unified Compact** shrinks its height. As of **macOS Tahoe 26** (Sept 2025), toolbars render in the **Liquid Glass** layer above content, refracting what scrolls beneath, and AppKit groups buttons onto one glass capsule (override with `NSToolbarItemGroup`/spacers). *(Tahoe 26 — load-bearing for apps recompiled against the SDK 26; apps built against older SDKs render the flat pre-26 chrome.)*
- **Title bar customization (Windows)** — Win11 lets apps **extend content into the title bar** and draw custom controls, but you must preserve the caption-button drag region and respect system colors/`InputNonClientPointerSource`. **Mica** (opaque, wallpaper-tinted) is the recommended base window/title-bar material; **Acrylic** (translucent blur) is for *transient* surfaces — flyouts, context menus, command bars — not the base window. *(Win11 Fluent — Mica is the load-bearing default; Acrylic-everywhere kills legibility and battery.)*
- **Custom title bars (Electron/Tauri)** — hiding native chrome (`titleBarStyle:'hidden'`, `frame:false`, Tauri `decorations:false`) buys brand consistency but you re-implement drag regions, double-click-to-zoom, Snap, and per-OS control placement. Use Electron's `titleBarOverlay` to keep native caption buttons while theming the bar. Trap: shipping macOS traffic lights on Windows (or vice versa) because the web frame doesn't know the OS — detect platform and place controls natively.
- **Window materials & vibrancy** — frosted/vibrant backdrops are platform-owned: macOS `NSVisualEffectView` / SwiftUI `.regularMaterial` + Liquid Glass; Windows Mica/Acrylic. All auto-adapt to light/dark and accent. Trap: text over vibrancy fails contrast and must honor *Reduce Transparency*; needs a solid scrim (→ §19 accessibility, → §5 glassmorphism).
- **Window snapping & tiling** — Windows Snap Assist / `Win`+arrow, macOS Tahoe window tiling (drag-to-edge, `fn`+arrows), Linux WM tiling. Respect minimum window sizes so tiling can't crush your layout; persist restored vs tiled state.

## 23.3 Toolbars, sidebars, inspectors & panels

- **Toolbar (unified, macOS) / command bar (Fluent) / app bar (Material)** — the row of primary actions. macOS toolbars are **user-customizable** (right-click ▸ *Customize Toolbar…* → drag items, icon/text/both, save sets); Fluent `CommandBar` has primary + overflow. Provide *Customize Toolbar* on Mac for power users; skip it on Windows where it's non-idiomatic.
- **Sidebar / source list (macOS) vs navigation pane (Windows) vs navigation rail/drawer (Material)** — the leading-edge nav column (mailboxes, folders, sections). macOS calls the tinted icon+label list a **source list**, collapsible via a leading toolbar toggle. Material's adaptive layout promotes a **navigation rail** (~80dp icon strip) at medium widths and a full **navigation drawer** at expanded widths. Trap: a fixed, non-collapsible sidebar on a laptop — desktop users resize aggressively; let it collapse.
- **Inspector (macOS/iPadOS) / properties pane / details pane** — the *trailing*-edge contextual panel showing attributes of the current selection. Per HIG the **inspector toggle is the trailing-most toolbar item**, anchored so it never moves; sidebar toggle anchors leading. Inspector *edits the selection*; sidebar *navigates*. Trap: swapping sides — users expect navigate-left, inspect-right (RTL mirrors both, → §19).
- **Three-column layout (sidebar / list / detail)** — the canonical "Mail" shape with sensible minimums (sidebar ~180–220pt, detail ~300pt). SwiftUI `NavigationSplitView` and WinUI `NavigationView` formalize it. Don't force three columns on a narrow window — collapse to two, then one (→ §13 adaptive layout).
- **Resizable split panes** — draggable dividers; double-click to collapse/reset. Persist sizes per window. Trap: panes with no minimum width draggable to zero and "lost," with no keyboard way to restore (→ §19).

## 23.4 Command palette, shortcuts & accelerators

- **Command palette (⌘K / ⌃⇧P)** — fuzzy launcher for every command, file, and setting; popularized by VS Code, Sublime, Linear, Raycast, Spotlight. Desktop's antidote to deep menus: keyboard-only users find anything by typing. **⌘K** is the de-facto "command/quick-action" binding (Linear, Slack, Notion); **⌘P / ⌃P** is "quick open file"; VS Code reserves **⌘⇧P** for the command palette. Trap: a palette listing only a curated subset — it must mirror the full command surface or users stop trusting it. *(Convention, not OS-owned — a baseline expectation in serious desktop/web apps, 2024–2026.)*
- **Keyboard shortcuts / accelerators (Windows) / key equivalents (macOS)** — direct bindings for frequent commands. macOS leans on **⌘**; Windows on **Ctrl** plus function keys (F2 rename, F5 refresh). Map the *same intent* to each platform's modifier — never ship ⌘ on Windows.

| Action | macOS | Windows |
|---|---|---|
| Settings/Preferences | ⌘, | menu (no OS standard; often `Ctrl+,`) |
| Quit app | ⌘Q | `Alt+F4` |
| Close window/tab | ⌘W | `Ctrl+W` |
| Find | ⌘F | `Ctrl+F` |
| Redo | ⌘⇧Z | `Ctrl+Y` |
| Rename (in lists) | Return | F2 |
| App switcher | ⌘Tab | `Alt+Tab` |
| Cycle app windows | ⌘\` | `Ctrl+Alt+Tab` / app-specific |

- **Shortcut discoverability** — show the shortcut *next to its menu item* (the single most reliable teaching surface), in tooltips, and in the palette. CheatSheet (hold ⌘) and Fluent `KeyTips` (`Alt`) reveal bindings on demand. Trap: powerful shortcuts with no on-screen trace — undiscoverable shortcuts may as well not exist (→ §19).
- **Chords & modal bindings** — multi-key sequences (`⌘K ⌘S` in VS Code). Powerful but invisible; reserve for expert features and always also expose via palette/menu. Honor OS-reserved shortcuts (don't override ⌘Q, ⌘Tab, Win+L).

## 23.5 Contextual menus, pointer & density

- **Right-click / context menu (both) / secondary-click (macOS) / contextual menu** — selection-specific commands at the cursor (Control-click or two-finger click on Mac). Both platforms expect it on any selectable object. Keep it short, ordered by frequency, labels matching the menu bar. Trap: hiding *only-here* commands in a context menu with no menu-bar/palette equivalent — keyboard and screen-reader users can't reach them (→ §19); both OSes also expose a keyboard menu key (`Shift+F10` / `≣ Menu`).
- **Hover affordances** — desktop has a true hover state (no touch equivalent): reveal-on-hover row actions, tooltips, resize cursors, drag handles, disclosure. Use hover to *reduce* persistent chrome. Trap: putting a *primary* action behind hover-only reveal — invisible to touch, keyboard, and discovery; hover may *enhance* but never *gate* (→ §19, WCAG 2.2 **1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus** requires hover content be dismissable, hoverable, persistent).
- **Pointer precision & hit targets** — mouse pixels are precise, so desktop tolerates smaller controls than touch's 44pt (iOS) / 48dp (Android) minimum — but don't drop a clickable area below ~24×24px (WCAG 2.2 **2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum)**), and respect the OS cursor set (resize, I-beam, grab, not-allowed) to signal affordance. Trackpad gestures (pinch-zoom, two-finger swipe) still apply on laptops.
- **Density (desktop is dense)** — desktop packs more per screen: compact rows, multi-column tables, persistent panels. macOS HIG and Fluent both offer **compact/comfortable** density tiers; pro apps (IDEs, DAWs, finance) skew compact. Offer a density toggle for data-heavy apps. Trap: shipping phone-sized touch spacing on desktop — wastes the display and makes power users feel they're running a blown-up phone app.

## 23.6 Multi-window, documents & drag-and-drop

- **Document-based apps (macOS) vs MDI/SDI (Windows)** — macOS treats each document as its own window with a **proxy icon**/title in the title bar (⌘-click the title for the file path; drag the proxy icon to move/share the file) plus native versioning/autosave. Windows historically used MDI (child windows inside one frame); modern Fluent apps favor SDI + tabs. Trap: a single-window Mac app opening documents in a modal stack — breaks the Window menu and ⌘\` cycling.
- **Window tabs** — macOS provides **system-level window tabbing** (⌘T / *Merge All Windows*, governed by *Prefer tabs* in System Settings); apps get it nearly free via `NSWindow`. Browsers/editors roll their own. Trap: re-implementing tab UI on macOS and fighting the system tab bar.
- **Multi-window state restoration** — restore window count, size, position, and per-window state on relaunch (macOS *Resume* / *Reopen windows when quitting*, Windows session restore). Identify windows in the Window menu and taskbar/Dock. Web-apps: the Multi-Window / `getScreenDetails()` APIs are nascent and permissioned — don't assume parity with native.
- **Drag-and-drop** — a desktop superpower: drag files in/out, reorder lists, drag the proxy icon, drop onto the Dock/taskbar icon. Support **drag-out** (export by dragging to Finder/Explorer), not just drop-in. Always show a drop-target highlight and copy/move/link cursor badge. Trap: drag-only operations with no menu/keyboard alternative — inaccessible; WCAG 2.2 **2.5.7 Dragging Movements** requires a single-pointer alternative (→ §19).
- **Spring-loading / drag-hover** — hovering a drag over a folder/tab auto-opens it (macOS spring-loading). A pro nicety; mirror with a hover-expand delay on Windows tree views.

## 23.7 Tray, badges & background presence

- **Menu bar extra (macOS) / system tray / notification area (Windows)** — a status item in the top-right menu bar (Mac) or bottom-right tray (Windows) for background/utility apps (VPN, clipboard, time tracker). Mac menu-bar extras are squeezed by the notch and can be hidden — design for *icon-only* and graceful overflow. Trap: a menu-bar/tray app with no main window and no onboarding — first-run users can't find it; point to the new icon on launch.
- **Dock badge (macOS) / taskbar badge & overlay icon (Windows)** — numeric/dot count for unread/pending. macOS `dockTile.badgeLabel`; Windows taskbar overlay icons + badges. Keep counts honest and clearable. Web-apps use the **Badging API** (`navigator.setAppBadge()`) for installed PWAs.
- **Jump lists (Windows) / Dock menu (macOS)** — right-clicking the taskbar/Dock icon surfaces recent documents, frequent tasks, quick actions. Populate with real recents + top tasks; an underused discoverability surface.
- **Progress in taskbar/Dock** — long operations should reflect progress on the taskbar button (Windows) or Dock icon (macOS) so users can switch away and still see status.

## 23.8 Preferences & settings windows

- **Settings window (macOS) vs Settings page (Fluent)** — macOS convention is a **separate, non-document Settings window** opened by ⌘, , historically toolbar-tabbed (General / Accounts / Advanced…), now often a sidebar list mirroring System Settings. Windows/Fluent favor an **in-app settings page** with a `NavigationView`. Trap: a giant single-scroll settings page on macOS where users expect ⌘, to open a focused, categorized window.
- **Settings persistence & scope** — be explicit about per-window vs per-document vs global. macOS settings **apply live** (rarely OK/Cancel); Windows mixes live-apply with Apply/OK. Trap: silent destructive settings with no undo or confirmation.

## 23.9 Native vs Electron/Tauri trade-offs

- **The core trade** — web-tech reuse and cross-platform speed vs bundle size, memory, and native fidelity. **Electron** bundles Chromium + Node: installers ~85–150 MB, idle memory ~200–300 MB, identical rendering everywhere, deepest ecosystem. **Tauri 2** uses the OS WebView (WebView2/Chromium on Windows, WebKitGTK on Linux, WebKit on macOS) + a Rust core: installers often **<10 MB**, idle memory ~30–40 MB, faster cold start — but WebKit-vs-Chromium rendering drift across platforms. *(2025–2026 figures; Hoppscotch's Electron→Tauri move cut ~165 MB → ~8 MB and ~70% memory.)*
- **Native-feel checklist (what web frameworks skip)** — real menu bar, correct traffic-light/caption placement, ⌘-vs-Ctrl bindings, system tabs, drag-out, proxy icon, dark-mode + accent following, VoiceOver/Narrator support, secondary-click menus, and respecting *Reduce Motion / Reduce Transparency*. Trap: shipping a web app in a window and calling it a desktop app — users feel the missing menu bar, wrong shortcuts, and footprint immediately.
- **When to go native** — pro tools needing low latency, deep OS integration (Finder/Explorer extensions, daemons, hardware), or Mac/Microsoft Store entitlements. **When Electron/Tauri wins** — one codebase across web + desktop, fast iteration, rich web rendering (rich text, canvas, charts). Pick Tauri when footprint/security/native-feel lead; Electron when ecosystem depth and cross-platform rendering consistency lead.
- **Accessibility veto** — web-in-a-window must still expose a real accessibility tree to VoiceOver/Narrator and honor OS contrast/motion/transparency. Many Electron apps fail this by default; verify, don't assume (→ §19).

*Sources: [Apple HIG — Toolbars](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/toolbars); [Apple HIG — The menu bar](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/the-menu-bar); [Microsoft Learn — Mica material](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/design/style/mica); [Microsoft Learn — Title bar customization](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/title-bar); [Material 3 — Canonical layouts](https://m3.material.io/foundations/layout/canonical-layouts/overview); [WCAG 2.2 — 2.5.7 Dragging Movements](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/dragging-movements.html); [Tauri vs Electron trade-offs (gethopp)](https://www.gethopp.app/blog/tauri-vs-electron).*

All facts verified. Key corrections: Play Billing v7 deadline is **Aug 31 2026** (draft wrongly said 2025), BLL **9** is current (released May 19 2026, not v8), and the iOS 3-prompts/365-days limit is Apple-specific — Play's quota is undisclosed/dynamic (draft wrongly claimed "both platforms" at exactly 3/365). Here is the revised section.

# 24. Monetization, growth & store UX

Where business meets UX — ethically. The system owns the money moment (the StoreKit/Play Billing purchase sheet can't be restyled), so your design work is everything *around* it: framing value, timing the ask, and making exit as easy as entry. Governing principle: every revenue pattern that survives is the one a regulator, a platform reviewer, and the user would all sign off on. Deception is a short-term loan against your install base.

## 24.1 Paywalls

The paywall is your store-within-the-app: the screen that converts intent into a transaction. It is yours to design freely *up to* the purchase button — past that, the OS takes over.

- **Hard paywall (gate / wall)** — blocks all (or core) functionality until purchase. Common in utility/pro tools with obvious value (VPN, photo editors). Converts high-intent traffic well; murders top-of-funnel. Trap: a hard paywall on first launch with no app preview is the #1 driver of App Store **Guideline 3.1.2 / 2.1** rejections and one-star "scam" reviews — reviewers must be able to *see* what they're paying for.
- **Soft paywall (dismissible)** — shows the offer with a visible close/"Maybe later" affordance; user can proceed into a limited free tier. Standard for freemium. The dismiss control must be a real ≥44×44pt (iOS) / 48×48dp (Android) target, not a 12px gray "×" hidden in a corner (→ §19; small/low-contrast dismiss = accessibility + dark-pattern failure).
- **Metered / freemium paywall** — N free actions (3 free edits, 5 free articles), then gate. The meter must be *visible and honest* ("2 of 3 free edits left"), not a surprise wall. Trap: resetting the meter silently to re-trap users reads as deceptive.
- **Onboarding paywall** — shown during/right after first-run onboarding, before the user has used anything. Maximizes trial starts (you've primed value with onboarding screens); maximizes refunds and churn (user hasn't felt the value yet). A/B this against a contextual paywall before committing.
- **Contextual / feature-gated paywall** — triggered at the moment the user reaches for a locked feature ("Export 4K" → sheet). Highest *quality* conversions because intent is real. The pattern to default to for retention-driven products.
- **Paywall anatomy** — value props (3–5, benefit-led not feature-led), plan selector, the system-owned CTA, and the *required* legal furniture: per Apple Guideline 3.1.2, an auto-renewable subscription paywall must show on the same screen as the buy button — title, length, price per period, a functional **Terms of Use (EULA)** link, and a **Privacy Policy** link. Missing any = rejection. Material/Play has no pixel-level mandate but Play policy requires equivalent disclosure before the Play purchase flow.
- **Native StoreKit paywall views** — iOS 17+ ships declarative SwiftUI `SubscriptionStoreView` / `ProductView` / `StoreView` that render the offer, the legal links, and the buy button for you with system styling. Trap: convenient but rigid — you trade brand control for compliance-by-default; most teams still hand-roll the paywall and call `purchase()` directly. Android has no equivalent declarative paywall view (you build the UI, `BillingClient` launches only the sheet).
- **Platform divergence**

  | Surface | iOS | Android | Web/PWA | Desktop |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | Paywall UI | Yours; or native `SubscriptionStoreView`/`ProductView` (iOS 17+) | Yours; Play `BillingClient` launches the system sheet | Fully yours (Stripe/Paddle) — no platform sheet | macOS uses StoreKit (same sheet); Windows uses MS Store or your own checkout |
  | Purchase sheet | System Apple Pay / StoreKit sheet — **unstyleable** | System Google Play sheet — **unstyleable** | Your checkout — style freely, but you own PCI/SCA | Mac App Store sheet unstyleable; direct-distribution Mac apps may use own payment |
  | Off-platform link-out | Reader apps + (post-2025 US injunction) external-purchase links with entitlement | External Offers Program / link-outs with disclosure | n/a | Direct-distribution + notarized Mac apps bypass IAP entirely |

- **Trap — the unstyleable sheet:** designers mock a beautiful custom card-entry form, then discover IAP forbids it. On iOS/Android, digital goods *must* go through the platform sheet; you design the run-up, not the checkout. Only physical goods/services may use your own payment UI.

## 24.2 IAP & subscription mechanics

The plumbing dictates the UX. Know what the API can and can't express before you design the screen.

- **StoreKit 2 (iOS) / Google Play Billing Library (Android)** — the purchase APIs. StoreKit 2 is async/await Swift with on-device `Transaction.currentEntitlements` for entitlement checks. Play Billing **7** is the publishing floor for new apps/updates as of **Aug 31 2025**; **v7 itself is now deprecated** — you must be on **v8+** to ship after **Aug 31 2026** (extension window to Nov 1 2026), and **BLL 9** is the current track (released May 2026, target SDK 35). Trap: shipping on a deprecated billing library = Play rejects the *update* (already-published binaries keep transacting — it's a publishing gate, not a kill switch).
- **Product types** — *consumable* (coins, used up), *non-consumable* (one-time unlock, restorable forever), *auto-renewable subscription*, *non-renewing subscription*. Android mirror: in-app products (one-time) vs subscriptions with **base plans + offers**. Get the type right at creation — you can't change it later.
- **Subscription groups (iOS) / base plans + offers (Android)** — one group = mutually exclusive tiers the user upgrades/downgrades/crossgrades within. Android splits a subscription into base plans (the recurring backbone, incl. **prepaid** plans, BLL 5+) and offers (trials/intro pricing) layered on top. Design the tier ladder as a group, not as separate products, or you'll double-charge a switcher.
- **System purchase sheet** — Apple Pay-style sheet (iOS) / Play sheet (Android) renders price, term, Face ID/biometric confirm. You cannot restyle, reorder, or pre-check it. Your job ends at `purchase()`. Don't fake a sheet to "match your brand" — it's a rejection and a phishing pattern (→ §24.9).
- **Server-side validation & entitlements** — verify with App Store Server API / `Transaction` signed payloads (iOS) and Play `purchases.subscriptionsv2` + **Real-time Developer Notifications** (Android) server-side; never trust the client alone (jailbreak/root spoofs receipts). Reflect entitlement state in UI optimistically but reconcile on launch. Web/PWA: your own webhook from Stripe/Paddle is the source of truth.
- **`Transaction.updates` / purchase listener** — subscribe to async transaction streams so Ask-to-Buy (Family Sharing) approvals, renewals, and cross-device purchases update the UI without a relaunch. Trap: handling purchases only in the button callback drops Family-Sharing-approved and offline-completed transactions on the floor.

## 24.3 Trials, intro offers & framing

Honest framing converts better over the lifetime than fear framing. The platforms now build the honesty in — work with it.

- **Free trial** — Apple "free" introductory offer / Android free-trial offer phase. One intro offer per subscriber per subscription group (Apple) — returning users see standard price. Eligibility is platform-enforced; don't promise "free trial" copy to ineligible users (the system sheet will contradict you = trust break).
- **"No payment due now" framing** — surfaces that the card isn't charged until the trial ends. Apple's *recommended* honest framing (echoed in their subscription UX guidance and system sheet copy). Pair with a **trial-end reminder** (Apple auto-sends a notice; Android via Play). Trap: hiding the auto-convert-to-paid is the textbook "forced continuity" dark pattern regulators target (→ §24.9).
- **Intro offer types** — *pay-as-you-go* (discounted price for N periods), *pay-up-front* (one discounted block), *free*. Android equivalents via offer phases. Choose by goal: free → max trial starts; pay-up-front → max committed revenue.
- **Win-back offers (Apple, 2024+) / re-engagement offers (Android)** — discounts shown to lapsed/churned subscribers, surfaceable *on the App Store product page itself* and in-app via `Transaction`/StoreKit. Date-sensitive [2024–2026]: Apple Win-Back Offers are load-bearing for resurrection campaigns, not hype.
- **Anchor pricing** — show annual vs monthly with the per-month equivalent and a "Save 40%" badge. Legitimate when the math is real. Trap: a struck-through "original" price that was never charged is illegal in many jurisdictions (EU Omnibus Directive, FTC) (→ §24.9 bait-and-switch).
- **Trap — confirmshaming:** decline buttons worded to guilt ("No thanks, I don't want to save money"). A recognized deceptive pattern; the decline option must be neutral and equally prominent (→ §24.9, → §19).

## 24.4 Upsell, cross-sell & gating

Gating is a UX contract: the user should always understand *what's locked, why, and what unlocking costs* before they hit the wall.

- **Feature gating** — lock badge (crown/lock/"PRO" pill) on premium features. Make the locked state legible and tappable-to-paywall, not silently disabled. A grayed-out control with no explanation fails discoverability and reads as a bug (→ §19 — and screen readers must announce the locked state, not just a dimmed label).
- **Upsell (tier-up)** — prompt to a higher tier at the moment of need (hit storage cap → "Upgrade to Pro for 1TB"). Contextual upsells beat blanket ones. iOS: model as an upgrade *within* a subscription group so proration is automatic; Android: a base-plan change with the prorated `replacementMode`.
- **Cross-sell** — offer a *different* product (companion app, add-on pack). Keep out of critical task flows; an upsell interrupting a save is hostile.
- **Consumption / paywall placement** — the best gate sits at the value-realization moment (export, share, unlock result), where willingness-to-pay peaks.
- **Trap — nagging:** re-prompting the upsell every session trains users to reflexively dismiss (banner blindness) and tanks store ratings. Cap frequency; respect a dismissal for a meaningful window (→ §24.9).

## 24.5 Restore, regional pricing & compliance furniture

Non-negotiable plumbing. Skipping any of these is a guaranteed rejection or a legal exposure.

- **Restore Purchases — required.** Apps with non-consumables or auto-renewable subscriptions **must** offer a clearly labeled "Restore Purchases" control (Apple Guideline 3.1.1; reviewers actively test it). StoreKit 2: iterate `Transaction.currentEntitlements`; legacy `AppStore.sync()` only when truly needed. Android: query `queryPurchasesAsync` on launch. Place it on the paywall *and* in settings. Trap: relying on a silent background restore with no visible button = rejection, even if entitlements actually sync.
- **Regional / localized pricing** — use App Store **price points / per-territory pricing** and Play's local pricing; never hardcode "$9.99". Show the user's local currency and tax-inclusive price where the storefront requires (EU). The system sheet localizes for you — your *paywall* copy must match, or the displayed price won't equal the charged price (rejection + trust break). Pull the localized price string from the product object (`displayPrice` / `ProductDetails`), never a hardcoded label.
- **Price-change consent** — Apple: subscribers must *actively consent* to price increases above a threshold (else auto-canceled at renewal); Apple shows a system prompt + email. Android: BLL **9** adds in-app messaging for opt-in price increases; material increases require a consent flow. Don't surprise-raise; surface the change in-app with the new price and renewal date.
- **Account deletion ↔ subscription** — Apple requires in-app account deletion (Guideline 5.1.1(v)); make clear that deleting the account does **not** cancel an Apple-billed subscription (only the App Store can), or users get charged post-deletion. Same caveat for Play-billed subs.
- **Tax & receipts** — the platform is merchant-of-record for IAP and handles VAT/sales tax + receipts. On web/PWA *and direct-distribution desktop* you are MoR: you own SCA (PSD2 3-D Secure in the EU), tax, dunning, and chargeback UX.

## 24.6 Store listing, ratings & growth loops

The listing is the highest-leverage screen you'll ever design and the one most teams neglect. Conversion happens before install.

- **Screenshots** — up to 10 per localization; users rarely scroll past the first 3, so front-load the value. Apple's 2025 baseline: **6.9″ iPhone (1290×2796)** and **13″ iPad (2064×2752)**; Apple now scales these across smaller device classes so you needn't upload every size. Play uses phone + 7″/10″ tablet sets (min 320px, max 3840px per side, 2:1 max aspect). Design captioned "marketing" screenshots, not raw screen grabs.
- **App preview video** — iOS: up to **3 per localization**, **15–30s**, **resolution must match the screenshot size for that device** (6.9″ portrait = 886×1920 or matching), `.mov/.m4v/.mp4` (H.264 / Apple ProRes 422 HQ), portrait or landscape. Play: a YouTube-hosted promo video + feature graphic (1024×500). First few seconds carry it (autoplay, muted). Adds ~20–30% to install conversion when done well.
- **Custom Product Pages (Apple) / custom store listings (Play)** — Apple allows up to **35 CPPs** per app, each with its own screenshots, up to 3 previews, 45-char promo text, and a unique deep-link URL for paid/organic campaigns; Play offers custom listings by country/language/pre-registration/install-state. Match the ad creative to the CPP to keep scent intact.
- **ASO basics** — Apple separates **app name (30) / subtitle (30) / keyword field (100, hidden)**; Play indexes the **title (30) + short description (80) + full description (4000)** text. Don't keyword-stuff the Play description (policy violation); don't repeat words across Apple's fields (wasted index space).
- **Ratings stars + reviews** — the star average is a primary conversion lever. Apple lets you **reset the summary rating** on a new version (use sparingly); both stores surface developer replies, and a thoughtful reply to a 1-star can flip it. Respond to reviews.
- **Referral / invite flows** — share-sheet-driven invites (iOS `UIActivityViewController` / Android `Intent.ACTION_SEND`) with a reward both sides can see. Use **deferred deep links** (Branch/AppsFleet/Firebase Dynamic Links successor) so a non-installed invitee lands on the right content post-install. Keep the reward honest and the terms one tap away (→ §19 — share sheets are system UI and accessible by default; don't reimplement them).
- **Promo codes / offer codes** — Apple legacy IAP promo codes **end Mar 26 2026** [dated]; **offer codes** become the path for all IAP types (consumable/non-consumable/non-renewing/auto-renewable), with configurable single-use, multi-use, or unlimited-in-window redemption, redeemable in-app via `presentCodeRedemptionSheet` (StoreKit) or a redemption URL. (App-download promo codes survive — only *IAP* promo codes are retired.) Android uses promo codes + the Play redemption flow. Design a visible "Redeem code" entry point.

## 24.7 In-app review prompts (time it right)

The single most-abused growth surface. The platforms deliberately took control away from you because developers nagged. Respect the constraint and it works; fight it and you get nothing.

- **`SKStoreReviewController.requestReview(in:)` / SwiftUI `requestReview` env action (iOS) / Play In-App Review API (`ReviewManager`)** — render a *system* rating card in-context. You **cannot** guarantee it appears, cannot restyle it. iOS rate-limits to **at most 3 prompts per user per 365 days**; Play enforces an **undisclosed, dynamic quota** (don't assume the iOS number — calling `launchReviewFlow` may show nothing, by design). Treat the call as a *request*, not a command.
- **Timing** — fire only after a *success moment* (task completed, level cleared, file exported) and only for engaged/returning users (Apple's guidance: established usage, not first run). Never on launch, never mid-task, never after an error.
- **No gating, no pre-questions** — Play explicitly **forbids** asking "Do you like the app?" before the prompt, and forbids triggering it from a button or in response to it. The old "thumbs up → store, thumbs down → email" funnel violates both stores' policies. Apple's HIG: don't interrupt or beg.
- **Manual fallback** — keep a passive "Rate us" link in settings: iOS opens a `write-review` deep link (`https://apps.apple.com/app/id<APPID>?action=write-review`); Android opens the Play listing. This is the *explicit-seek* path the system card can't serve.
- **Trap — nag-for-rating:** custom non-system rating modals on every session to dodge the rate limit. Users punish it with 1-stars, and review-gating funnels risk app removal (→ §24.9).

## 24.8 Churn, cancellation & win-back

The exit is part of the product. Make it dignified; the law increasingly requires it and users remember who let them leave cleanly.

- **Cancellation must be easy — and is partly out of your hands.** Apple-billed subscriptions are canceled in **Settings → [your name] → Subscriptions** (or App Store → account → Subscriptions); your app can't intercept it, but you **must** tell users where to go and deep-link them (`https://apps.apple.com/account/subscriptions`). Android: Play Store → Payments & subscriptions → Subscriptions, deep-linkable (`https://play.google.com/store/account/subscriptions`). Web/PWA: you own the cancel UI entirely — that's where roach-motel patterns hide, so build it clean. Don't pretend you can't help users cancel.
- **Regulatory floor** — "as easy to cancel as to subscribe" is the durable global norm (EU Consumer Rights / Omnibus; California, NY, and other US state auto-renewal laws; ROSCA), even though the **FTC federal "Click-to-Cancel" / Negative Option Rule was vacated by the Eighth Circuit on Jul 8 2025 on procedural grounds** [dated — the *principle* survives in state law, ROSCA, FTC Act §5, and platform policy; the specific federal rule does not]. Design for the strict version regardless; platforms enforce it independently.
- **Cancellation save flow** — a *single*, honest "before you go" offer (pause, downgrade, discount, or "what went wrong?") is acceptable. A multi-screen maze, a phone-only cancel, or a hidden cancel button is a forbidden "roach motel" dark pattern. One screen, neutral copy, exit always one tap away.
- **Pause / downgrade as churn-softeners** — Android supports native subscription **pause** (Play, configurable durations); Apple supports downgrade/crossgrade within a group (no native indefinite pause). Offer the smaller commitment before the full cancel — but only *offer*, never trap.
- **Win-back / dunning** — for *involuntary* churn (failed card), use the platform **billing-retry / grace period** (Apple billing grace period; Play account hold/grace) + a gentle in-app "update payment" banner; for *voluntary* churn, the win-back/re-engagement offers in §24.3. Don't dark-pattern a payment-update banner into looking like a required blocking action.
- **Trap — confirmshaming the cancel:** "Are you sure? You'll lose all your progress 😢" with an oversized "Stay" button and a tiny "Cancel" link. Recognized deceptive pattern; both stores and consumer regulators treat asymmetric cancel/stay prominence as a violation (→ §19 — asymmetric target size + contrast is also an accessibility failure).

## 24.9 Dark patterns — the forbidden list

These are not "aggressive growth tactics." They are the patterns named in regulation (EU Omnibus/DSA, FTC, state UDAP) and platform policy. Shipping one is a reviewer rejection, a legal exposure, and a trust loss that outlives the quarter's revenue.

- **Forced continuity** — auto-converting a trial to paid without clear "no payment due now" + trial-end notice.
- **Roach motel** — easy to get in, engineered-hard to get out (multi-step or off-channel cancel).
- **Confirmshaming** — guilt-worded decline/cancel options.
- **Disguised ads / fake system UI** — a fake purchase sheet, a fake "system" alert, or an ad styled as a native control.
- **Bait-and-switch pricing** — paywall price ≠ charged price; fake anchor prices; mid-flow plan swap.
- **Sneak-into-basket** — a pre-checked add-on or auto-added upsell in the buy flow.
- **Nagging** — relentless re-prompts for upsell or rating that ignore dismissal.
- **Hidden costs** — surfacing fees/auto-renew terms only after commitment.
- **Obstruction / preselection** — burying the cheaper plan, defaulting the annual tier pre-checked without disclosure.

Rule of thumb: if the pattern depends on the user *not noticing* or *not understanding*, it's a dark pattern, not a design decision (→ §19 accessibility is a veto: any of these that also degrades target size, contrast, focus order, or comprehensibility fails twice over — a low-contrast tiny "cancel" link is both a dark pattern *and* a WCAG failure).

*Sources: [Apple App Store Review Guidelines (3.1.x, 5.1.1)](https://developer.apple.com/app-store/review/guidelines/); [Apple — App Store Connect screenshot specifications](https://developer.apple.com/help/app-store-connect/reference/screenshot-specifications/); [Apple — requestReview(in:) / SKStoreReviewController](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/storekit/skstorereviewcontroller/requestreview(in:)); [Android — Play Billing Library deprecation FAQ & release notes](https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing/deprecation-faq); [Android — Play In-App Review API](https://developer.android.com/guide/playcore/in-app-review); [WilmerHale — Eighth Circuit vacates FTC "Click-to-Cancel" Rule (Aug 2025)](https://www.wilmerhale.com/en/insights/client-alerts/20250801-eighth-circuit-vacates-the-ftcs-click-to-cancel-rule-but-federal-and-state-regulators-likely-to-remain-active).*

---

The 17%/73% stats are accurate. I have enough verified grounding. Here is the complete revised section.

# 25. Emerging & AI-era app interfaces

*Dated June 2026. Highest-volatility section in the document — OS-level AI features ship, rename, and merge on a quarterly cadence. Re-audit annually; treat every named feature as a moving target and every cited OS version as a floor, not a fact-of-nature.* This section's job: catalog where AI shows up inside apps, who owns each surface (the OS vs your app), and which patterns are load-bearing vs hype. Governing principle: **AI is a capability, not a feature** — surface it where it removes work, name it plainly, keep the human in the loop, and never let a sparkle stand in for a benefit (→ §19 accessibility, → §24 trust & provenance).

## 25.1 The system-service vs in-app split

The defining architectural question of app AI: does intelligence belong to the OS (every app inherits it) or to your app (you build and brand it)? Get this wrong and you rebuild a wheel the platform already ships — or you cede a differentiator to a generic system sheet.

- **System-service AI (OS-owned)** — intelligence the platform injects into *every* app through standard text/image controls and intents, no per-app code. iOS: **Writing Tools** (in any `UITextView`/`TextEditor`), **Genmoji**, **Image Playground**, system **Siri**. Android: the **Gemini** assistant overlay, **Circle to Search**, **Smart Reply**, **Magic Compose**. Desktop: **Copilot** in Windows (system + Office), **Apple Intelligence** Writing Tools across macOS. Web-app: no OS injection inside the sandbox — the browser tab gets none of this for free; you wire your own or use the nascent **Prompt API / built-in AI** (Chrome, Gemini Nano in-browser, origin-trial as of 2026). Reach for system AI when the task is generic (rewrite, summarize, proofread). Trap: you don't control its quality, latency, or refusals — and users blame *your* app when the system sheet fumbles.
- **In-app AI (you-owned)** — a copilot/feature you build against an on-device or cloud model, branded and tuned to your domain. Reach for it when the value is domain-specific (a legal-doc assistant, a code agent, a CRM summarizer) and generic system tools can't encode your data or workflow. Trap: you now own evals, hallucination handling, cost, latency, and the trust contract end to end.
- **The hybrid default (2026)** — most serious apps do both: inherit free system AI for commodity text/image tasks, build in-app AI only where domain knowledge or proprietary data justifies the cost. iOS's **Shortcuts "Use Model" action** (iOS 26) blurs the line — users pipe on-device, Private Cloud Compute, or ChatGPT output into your App Intents without you shipping a model.
- **The "✨ everywhere" anti-pattern** — bolting a sparkle button onto every toolbar because the org mandated "ship AI this quarter." NN/g (Sept 2024): ~17% of users read a lone sparkle icon as "favorite/save" because it collides visually with the star, which 73% read as favoriting; the icon has *no* standardized meaning. If you can't state the user benefit as a verb ("Summarize", "Rewrite", "Find"), you don't have a feature, you have a slop generator. Label the *action*, not the *technology* (→ §19 — a bare ✨ is also unlabeled for screen readers).

## 25.2 On-device & hybrid models

The 2024–2026 shift: foundation models small enough to run on the phone, with cloud as escalation. This is a privacy, latency, offline, and cost story before it's a capability story.

- **Apple Foundation Models framework (iOS/iPadOS/macOS 26)** — Swift API exposing the ~3B-parameter on-device Apple Intelligence model in as few as three lines. Defining traits: **guided generation** (the `@Generable` macro + `@Guide` constraints return typed Swift structs via constrained decoding — no JSON-parsing roulette), **tool calling** (the model invokes your Swift functions; the framework orchestrates serial/parallel calls), and **LoRA adapters** for task fine-tuning. Runs only on Apple-Intelligence-capable devices (A17 Pro / M-series and later) with the feature enabled. Trap: the 3B on-device model is tuned for summarization, extraction, classification, and short rewrites — *not* world knowledge or multi-step reasoning. Don't expect GPT-class output; capability-check and degrade.
- **Private Cloud Compute (PCC)** — Apple's escalation tier: a larger server model on hardware that extends the device's security guarantees (stateless, attested, independently verifiable images, no data retention). The OS decides on-device vs PCC; you mostly don't. Pitch to privacy-sensitive users as "your data isn't training anyone's model" — but don't claim "fully on-device" if PCC can fire (→ §24 provenance).
- **Gemini Nano + AICore (Android)** — Google's on-device model running inside the **AICore** system service (auto-updated, hardware-accelerated, isolated via **Private Compute Core** — restricted package binding, internet access only through the Private Compute Services APK). You don't bundle the model; AICore distributes and shares one copy across apps. The latest multimodal Nano shipped first on Pixel 10 (Aug 2025); coverage spans Tensor, Snapdragon, and Dimensity flagships but is *not* universal — gate every feature on a runtime availability check.
- **ML Kit GenAI APIs (Android)** — the high-level wrapper over Gemini Nano: `Summarization`, `Proofreading`, `Rewriting`, and `Image Description`, plus the lower-level `Prompt` API for custom requests. Streaming and non-streaming variants. Reach for these before raw model access — they handle safety filtering, the AICore handshake, and model download/feature-status. Trap: availability varies by device and the model may need a one-time download; always ship a graceful "not supported here" path.
- **Built-in AI in the browser (web-app)** — Chrome's `Summarizer`, `Writer`, `Rewriter`, `Translator`, `LanguageDetector`, and `Prompt` (`LanguageModel`) APIs run Gemini Nano *in the browser*; origin-trial / progressively shipping through 2025–2026. The web's answer to on-device AI. Trap: download size, gated availability (`availability()` returns `unavailable`/`downloadable`/`available`), and no cross-browser support yet — feature-detect and fall back to a server route.
- **On-device vs cloud decision** — on-device wins for privacy, offline, zero marginal cost, and sub-100ms latency; cloud wins for capability, freshness, and large context. Trap: don't silently round-trip sensitive content to a server the user didn't consent to — and don't promise "private/on-device" then fall back to cloud without saying so.

| Concern | iOS / macOS | Android | Web-app |
|---|---|---|---|
| On-device model | Apple Foundation Models (~3B) | Gemini Nano | Gemini Nano (in Chrome) |
| System service host | OS (Apple Intelligence) | AICore | none (browser sandbox) |
| Cloud escalation | Private Cloud Compute / ChatGPT | Gemini cloud (Vertex / Gemini API) | your server / provider API |
| High-level text APIs | Writing Tools | ML Kit GenAI | Summarizer / Writer / Rewriter |
| Structured output | `@Generable` / guided generation | response schema / function calling | JSON-schema / function calling |

## 25.3 In-app assistants & copilots

The dominant 2024–2026 surface pattern: a conversational helper docked to the app. Form follows how disruptive the answer is and how much screen there is.

- **Copilot sidebar / panel (desktop & tablet)** — a persistent side rail (Microsoft Copilot, Notion AI, IDE assistants like Copilot/Cursor) that reads the current document/context and acts on it. Best when the user works *alongside* the AI continuously. Trap: a phone has no room for a side rail — it must collapse to a sheet or full screen; never ship a cramped 280px rail on a 390pt-wide iPhone. Ensure the rail is keyboard-reachable and not a focus trap (→ §19).
- **Assistant sheet / bottom sheet (mobile)** — the phone equivalent: a swipe-up panel (Android **bottom sheet**, iOS `.sheet` with **detents** — `.medium`/`.large` or custom) holding the conversation. Half-detent for a quick answer, full-detent to dig in. Trap: don't trap critical app navigation behind it; respect the safe-area and keyboard avoidance.
- **Inline / contextual AI (in-place)** — generate or rewrite *right where the cursor is* (editor slash-commands, "rewrite this paragraph", spreadsheet cell-fill). Lowest friction, highest trust because the user sees exactly what changed against the original. Reach for this over a chatbot whenever the action targets a specific selection. iOS Writing Tools and Android Smart Reply are the OS-level versions.
- **Command-palette AI (⌘K / Ctrl-K)** — natural-language command bar routing to actions or answers (Raycast, Linear, Superhuman, VS Code). Desktop-native muscle memory; pairs with App Intents / system actions as the execution layer. Weak on touch — no physical keyboard, so it's a desktop/web-app pattern, not a phone-first one.
- **Conversational vs. direct-manipulation** — chat is the *fallback* UI for AI, not the goal. Users need **legibility**: see what's happening, intervene, understand why. A button that does the thing beats a chatbox that describes doing the thing. Trap: don't conversationalize a task that's faster as a tap, and never make a plain free-text box the *only* path to a core action (it's a discoverability and accessibility dead end).

## 25.4 Generative & adaptive UI

Where the *interface itself* is model-generated or model-arranged — the frontier, and the thinnest ice.

- **Generative UI** — the model emits UI (forms, cards, controls), not just text. iOS **interactive snippets** (App Intents, iOS 26) are the sanctioned version: small typed pop-up UI the system composes into Siri/Spotlight contexts and can confirm actions inline. Trap: free-form generated layouts fail accessibility predictably — undefined focus order, unlabeled controls, no Dynamic Type / contrast guarantee (→ §19, accessibility veto). Constrain generation to a vetted component set; never let a model emit raw views.
- **Adaptive / predictive UI** — same components, AI-reordered by predicted intent (Siri Suggestions surfacing the next likely action, a reordered share sheet, smart-sorted inbox, Android **App Actions** in the launcher). Lower risk than generative because the vocabulary is fixed. Trap: a layout that reshuffles under the user destroys spatial memory and breaks muscle memory — stabilize the top/pinned items, adapt only the tail, and never reorder destructive controls into where a safe one just was.
- **Snippet & entity views (iOS 26)** — `AppIntent` snippets + `AppEntity` view annotations control how your data renders inline inside system surfaces. Load-bearing, shipping, low-hype. Use to make your content first-class in Siri/Spotlight/Visual Intelligence results.
- **Hype check** — "the UI generates itself per user" demos beautifully and ships rarely. As of 2026 it's load-bearing only inside tight rails (snippets, suggested actions, server-driven UI with a fixed component catalog). Treat full generative UI as research, not roadmap.

## 25.5 Voice, multimodal & visual intelligence

Point-and-ask is the genuinely new app interaction of this era — camera and screen as query inputs.

- **Visual Intelligence (iOS, OS-owned)** — point the camera (Camera Control on iPhone 16+, or the Action button / Control Center) or act on a **screenshot**, then ask; the system identifies objects and routes to actions, including *your* app's image-search results via App Intents (iOS 26). Surface to claim: register the relevant **assistant schema** so your app appears in "search where this was seen." Trap: it's an OS funnel — you get the traffic only if you wire the intent.
- **Circle to Search (Android, OS-owned)** — long-press the home handle (or nav button) to circle, highlight, or scribble anything on screen and query it via Google/Gemini. Converging into the Gemini overlay's "ask about this screen" through 2025–2026. You don't build it; you benefit when your content is the thing circled. Trap: don't fight it with a custom screenshot flow users won't discover.
- **Multimodal in-app capture** — your own "point camera + ask" (plant ID, receipt scan, "what wine pairs with this"). Build it when your domain model beats the generic OS one. Trap: camera-as-input must degrade gracefully in poor light and **announce results accessibly**, not only visually — a vision-only result is unusable for blind users (→ §19 veto).
- **Voice + on-device transcription** — live dictation/transcription is now an on-device primitive (iOS **SpeechAnalyzer** / `SpeechTranscriber`, iOS 26; Android ML Kit / `SpeechRecognizer`; Web Speech API in browsers). Reach for it for hands-free capture. Trap: always offer a typed path — voice fails in noise, in public, under privacy constraints, and for many disabled, non-native, and accented speakers (→ §19).
- **Wear & ambient voice** — on watch/earbuds, voice + raise-to-speak is often the *primary* input; there's no room for a sidebar or palette. Keep responses glanceable and short (→ §11 haptics, → §wearables).

## 25.6 Agentic actions & App Intents as AI surface area

The most strategically important, least flashy entry: AI is becoming an *actuator*, and your app's exposed actions are how it acts.

- **App Intents (iOS) / App Actions & shortcuts (Android)** — declare your app's actions (`AppIntent`) and data (`AppEntity`) so the OS assistant can *invoke*, chain, and predict them. This is the contract that lets Siri/Gemini "string together complex actions" without your app shipping an NLP pipeline. The single highest-leverage AI investment for most apps in 2026. Web-app parallel: no OS assistant reaches into a tab — your equivalent is a documented tool/function schema (MCP, function-calling) exposed to *your* model.
- **Assistant schemas / SiriKit domains** — typed categories (mail, photos, media, file management, …) that slot your intents into the assistant's semantic understanding so the LLM-era Siri can reason about them. Map your core actions to schemas; unmapped intents still work but the assistant reasons about them less reliably.
- **`PredictableIntent` & proactive suggestions** — mark intents predictable so the system learns user patterns and surfaces them (Siri Suggestions, predicted next action, Android App Actions). Trap: prediction that's wrong-but-prominent is worse than absent — a confidently-suggested wrong action erodes trust fast and, if it's a one-tap destructive action, causes real harm.
- **Agentic in-app actions** — the model takes multi-step action on the user's behalf (book, send, reschedule, file). Defining trait: it crosses from *answering* to *acting*. Trap (non-negotiable): destructive or irreversible actions — send money, purchase, delete, message a person, share externally — require **explicit human confirmation**; never let an agent execute them autonomously. Apple's financial-action guidance and platform norms both draw this line. Show the action before committing; make undo real (→ §25.9 reversibility).
- **Tool calling as in-app agency** — your in-app model invokes your own functions (Foundation Models tool calling, Android/Vertex function calling, MCP on web) to act inside the app. Keep tools narrow, validate every argument, gate side effects behind confirmation, and log what the agent did so the action is auditable.

## 25.7 AR / spatial features inside apps

Spatial as a *feature inside a phone app*, not a standalone visionOS app — the pragmatic 2026 surface.

- **AR try-on / placement (iOS ARKit / Android ARCore; web-app WebXR / `<model-viewer>`)** — furniture-in-room, glasses/makeup try-on, measure. Mature, load-bearing for commerce. Web parallel exists but is weaker (no LiDAR, permission-gated, Safari WebXR gaps). Trap: needs a real-world surface and good light; always ship a 2D fallback and an explicit "exit AR."
- **Room & object scan (RoomPlan / LiDAR; ARCore Depth / Scene Semantics)** — capture a space or object as geometry; AI now cleans, labels, and segments the scan. Trap: gate on hardware — LiDAR is iPhone Pro / iPad Pro only — and capability-check ARCore Depth rather than assuming it.
- **visionOS spatial AI (Apple Vision Pro)** — the same Apple Intelligence + App Intents surface, rendered spatially; the same Foundation Models framework is available. Still niche by install base in 2026; build here only with a concrete reason. Dated, high-volatility — re-audit.
- **Trap across AR** — motion, occlusion, vestibular discomfort, and "hold your phone up in public" are real adoption frictions; AR is a *mode*, not a home screen. Provide a non-AR equivalent for users who can't or won't enter it (→ §19).

## 25.8 Wearable & health AI

Small screen, high-stakes data, ambient sensing — the strictest design constraints in the document.

- **On-wrist suggestions & smart replies (watchOS / Wear OS)** — AI's job here is *fewer taps*: one-line summaries, suggested replies, predicted complications/Tiles. No room for chat. Trap: a wrong smart-reply sent in one tap is embarrassing and uncorrectable — bias toward a confirm step for any send, and keep reply candidates short and unambiguous.
- **Health & wellness inference** — trend detection, anomaly flags, coaching from sensor data (heart-rate, sleep, activity). Defining trait: it brushes regulated medical claims. Trap: never present an inference as a diagnosis — use hedged language, cite the underlying signal, and route to a clinician. This is a safety *and* accessibility veto; get copy reviewed (→ §19, → §24).
- **Proactive / ambient nudges** — "time to stand", "you usually leave now." Powerful when right, creepy when wrong or over-frequent. Trap: respect quiet hours / Do Not Disturb, cap frequency, and let users tune or kill any nudge — an un-silenceable notification is an accessibility and trust failure.

## 25.9 Trust, provenance & the AI-slop veto

AI's failure mode is confident wrongness; the interface's job is to make that legible and recoverable. Mirrors the web doc's trust section — same principles, app-specific surfaces.

- **Provenance & disclosure** — mark AI-generated/edited content. System outputs increasingly carry metadata (Genmoji and many Apple/Google generated images embed it; **C2PA Content Credentials** are the emerging cross-platform standard). Trap: don't pass off generated content as human-authored where it matters (reviews, support replies, news, legal).
- **Confidence & legibility** — show sources, mark uncertainty, make the "why" inspectable. Don't fake certainty; a confidently-wrong answer with no citation is the most damaging output an app can ship. Surface the model's working set (which doc, which field) so the user can verify in-place.
- **Human-in-the-loop by default** — generate *into* an editable state, not a committed one. iOS Writing Tools previews a diff before replacing; your features should too. The model drafts; the user commits.
- **Reversibility** — every AI action gets a real undo, not a toast that vanishes. No silent mutation of user data. For agentic actions, show the action before it fires and keep it undoable after.
- **Move beyond the sparkle** — name the benefit, not the engine. "Summarize" > "✨". Reserve any AI glyph for genuinely model-powered surfaces, never let it collide with favorite/save semantics, and always pair it with a text label or `accessibilityLabel` (→ §19).
- **AI-slop in apps** — auto-generated filler (empty-state blurbs, fake-personalized greetings, gratuitous chat, summaries nobody asked for) that adds nodes without removing work. The test: does this remove a step or add one? If it adds one, cut it.

*Sources: [Apple — Foundation Models framework](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/FoundationModels); [Apple — Foundation Models framework unlocks new app experiences (newsroom)](https://www.apple.com/newsroom/2025/09/apples-foundation-models-framework-unlocks-new-intelligent-app-experiences/); [Apple — Integrating actions with Siri and Apple Intelligence (App Intents)](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/appintents/integrating-actions-with-siri-and-apple-intelligence); [Android Developers — ML Kit GenAI APIs (Gemini Nano + AICore)](https://developers.google.com/ml-kit/genai); [Chrome — Built-in AI APIs](https://developer.chrome.com/docs/ai/built-in); [Nielsen Norman Group — The Proliferation and Problem of the ✨ Sparkles ✨ Icon](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ai-sparkles-icon-problem/).*
